Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa.

Homage to the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One.

Sīmavisodhanī

The Purification of Boundaries

Varadaṃ [M 0.0005] varadaṃ aggaṃ, diccaṃ diccaṃva vanajaṃ;

Bodhakaṃ bodhakaṃ pāṇaṃ, deva’devaṃ namāma’haṃ.

The supreme giver of boons, like a celestial lotus; the Awakener of beings, the God of gods—to him I pay homage.

Santaṃ santaṃ sadā vaṭṭaṃ, pajaṃ’bhave sutaṃ;

Nibbudaṃ nibbudaṃ tāraṃ, dhammavaraṃ namāma’haṃ.

The peaceful, ever pacifying the round of existence, heard among beings in the world; the extinguished, the savior, the excellent Dhamma—to it I pay homage.

Sutaṃ sutaṃ bhavane yaṃ, natanaṃ natanā’rahaṃ;

Narā’narā sadā’kaṃsu, saṅghavaraṃ namāma’haṃ.

That which is well-heard in the world, worthy of veneration, which humans and non-humans have always venerated; the excellent Sangha—to it I pay homage.

Icceva’maccantanatassaneyyaṃ, natayyamāno ratanattayaṃ yaṃ;

Puññābhivaḍḍhiṃ mahagghaṃ alatthaṃ, tassānubhāvena hatantarāyo.

Thus, bowing to the Triple Gem, which is worthy of endless veneration, I have attained a great and precious growth of merit. By its power, may obstacles be destroyed.

Vinaye sati ṭhite yaṃ, sāsanaṃ saṇṭhitaṃ bhave;

Vinaye sati naṭṭhe yaṃ, vinaṭṭhaṃ sāsanaṃ bhave.

When the Vinaya is established, the Dispensation is established; when the Vinaya is destroyed, the Dispensation is destroyed.

Tasmā dittaṃ mame’dāni, jotayitvāna sāsane;

Bhinditvā dveḷhakaṃ vādaṃ, ekībhāvaṃ karoma’haṃ.

Therefore, now, having caused the Dispensation to shine and having shattered divisive doctrines, I shall bring about unity.

Attukkaṃsana-bhāvañca, paravambhanakampica;

Tesu cittaṃ apesetvā, sāsanassa suvuḍḍhiyā.

Having removed the mind from self-exaltation and disparagement of others, for the good growth of the Dispensation.

Yadi me īdisaṃ cittaṃ, bhaveyyuṃ pāpasaṅkappā;

Paccissaṃ niraye bhayaṃ, hīna’jjhāsayakāraṇā.

If my mind were such that evil thoughts were to arise, I would suffer torment in hell on account of ignoble intentions.

Sāsanassa [M 0.0006] sulabhaṃ’va patthayanto satā’cāro;

Saṃsandissamahaṃ dāni, ciraṃ saddhamma’suddhiyā.

Being of mindful conduct and desiring what is beneficial for the Dispensation, I shall now strive for a long time for the purity of the true Dhamma.

Sudullabhaṃ labhitvāna, pabbajjaṃ jinasāsane;

Sambuddhassa varaṃ vādaṃ, katvā dveḷhaka’mekikaṃ.

Having obtained the very rare going forth in the Conqueror's Dispensation, and having unified the excellent teaching of the Fully Enlightened One which had become divided,

Sīmavisodhaniṃ nāma, nānāganthasamāhaṭaṃ;

Karissaṃ me nisāmentu, sādhavo kavipuṅgavā.

I shall compose 'The Purification of Boundaries,' a work compiled from various texts. May the virtuous and the most excellent of poets listen to me.

Pāḷiṃ aṭṭhakathañceva, mātikaṃ padabhājaniṃ;

Ogāhetvāna taṃ sabbaṃ, punappunaṃ asesato.

Having immersed myself in all of that—the Pāḷi, the commentaries, the matrices, and the word-by-word analyses—again and again, completely and without remainder.

Attanomatiganthesu, ṭīkāgaṇṭhipadesuca;

Vinicchayavimatīsu, mātikā’ṭṭhakathāsupi.

In texts based on personal opinion, in the sub-commentaries and knotty passages, in decisions and points of doubt, and also in the matrices and commentaries.

Sabbaṃ asesakaṃ katvā, saṃsanditvāna ekato;

Pavattā vaṇṇanā esā, tosayantī vicakkhaṇeti.

Having made all complete and without remainder, having compared and unified them, this exposition is set forth, delighting the discerning.

Buddhuppādo hi dullabho, tato pabbajjā ca upasampadā ca, vuttañhetaṃ

For the arising of a Buddha is indeed rare, and rarer still are the going forth and the higher ordination. As it has been said:

Buddho ca dullabho loke, saddhammasavanampi ca;

Saṅgho ca dullabho loke, sappūrisā’tidullabhā.

A Buddha is rare in the world, and hearing the true Dhamma is also rare; the Sangha is rare in the world, and virtuous persons are exceedingly rare.

Dullabhañca manussattaṃ, buddhuppādo ca dullabho;

Dullabhā khaṇasampatti, saddhammo paramadullabho’ti.

Rare is the human state, and rare is the arising of a Buddha; rare is the attainment of an opportune moment, and the true Dhamma is supremely rare.

1. Upasampadākaṇḍo

1. The Chapter on Higher Ordination

Imasmiñhi ṭhāne ṭhatvā buddhaguṇapaṭisaṃyuttāya kathāya vuccamānāya sādhūnaṃ cittasampahaṃsanañceva, imasseva pakaraṇassa anummattavacanabhāvo ca bhaveyya, anummattavacanatte ca siddhe sotabbaṃ maññissanti, tasmā buddhaguṇaṃ vaṇṇayissāma. Amhākaṃ kira bhagavā brahmadevabuddhamādiṃ katvā yāva porāṇasakyagotamā manopaṇidhivasena, kaṭṭhavāhanajātakaṃādiṃkatvā yāva majjhimadīpaṅkarā vācaṅgavasena majjhimadīpaṅkarapādamūlato paṭṭhāya kāyavācaṅgavasena sabbaṃ sampiṇḍetvā yāva vessantarattabhāvā vīsati asaṅkhyeyyāni pāramiyo pūrentassa sabbaññutaññāṇatthāya yeva alaṅkataṃ sīsaṃ chinditvā dentassa sabbaññubodhisattassa sīsaṃ jambudīpavāsīnaṃ uddhanatopi bahutaraṃ, añjitanayanaṃ uppāṭetvā dentassa nayanaṃ ajaṭākāse gaganatale tārāgaṇatopi bahutaraṃ, hadayamaṃsaṃ vā dakkhiṇabāhuṃ [M 0.0007] vā chinditvā dentassa maṃsaṃ chanahuta aṭṭhasa hassādhikasatasahassapamāṇasinerurājatopi bahutaraṃ, lohitaṃ uppāṭetvā dentassa ruhiraṃ catūsu mahāsamuddesu udakatopi bahutaraṃ, maddīsadise ca bhariye jālīkaṇhājinasadise ca putte paresaṃ dāsatthāya dentassa gaṇanapathaṃ vītivattā. Evaṃ pāramiyo pūrentasseva.

For, if at this point a discourse connected with the virtues of the Buddha were to be delivered, it would both gladden the hearts of the virtuous and establish the sober character of this treatise. And when its sober character is established, they will consider it worth listening to. Therefore, we shall extol the virtues of the Buddha. Indeed, our Blessed One—from Brahmadeva Buddha up to the ancient Sakyagautama by way of mental aspiration; from the Kaṭṭhavāhana Jātaka up to Majjhima Dīpaṅkara by way of verbal expression; and from the feet of Majjhima Dīpaṅkara up to the Vessantara existence by way of bodily and verbal expression—in total, for twenty incalculable aeons, fulfilled the perfections for the sole purpose of attaining the knowledge of omniscience. The heads of the Bodhisatta, who gave his own adorned head after having it cut off, were more numerous than the hearths of the inhabitants of Jambudīpa. The eyes he gave, having plucked out his own collyrium-adorned eyes, were more numerous than the host of stars in the clear expanse of the sky. The flesh he gave, having cut off his heart-flesh or his right arm, was greater in mass than the king of mountains, Sineru. The blood he gave, having let it flow, was more abundant than the water in the four great oceans. The times he gave his wife, like Maddī, and his children, like Jālī and Kaṇhājina, into servitude to others surpassed all reckoning. Thus it was that he fulfilled the perfections.

Cintitaṃ sattasaṅkhyeyyaṃ, navasaṅkhyeyya’vācakaṃ;

Kāyavācā catukhyātaṃ, buddhattaṃ samupāgamīti.

For seven incalculable aeons he resolved in mind, for nine incalculable aeons he declared in speech; for four, renowned for action in body and speech, he attained Buddhahood.

Jātattakīsotattakiyā vuttanayena asūro hutvā buddhattaṃ cintentasseva sattaasaṅkhyeyyāni vītivattāni. Atisūro ahutvā vācāmattameva nava asaṅkhyeyyāni vītivattāni. Atisūro hutvā kāyaṅgavācaṅgavasena pūrentassa cattāri asaṅkhyeyyāni vītivattāni. Imāni cattāri asaṅkhyeyyāni dīpaṅkarapādamūlato paṭṭhāya veditabbāni. Satasahassakappamatthake pana ajjhattapāramī pūritāti veditabbā. Vessantarattabhāvena ca sattakamahādānaṃ datvā sattakkhattuṃ pathaviṃ kampetvā vaṅkapabbataṃ gantvā nayanasadise dve putte brāhmaṇassa dāsatthāya datvā dutiyadivase brāhmaṇavaṇṇena āgatassa sakkassa devarañño attasamaṃ maddiṃ nāma bhariyaṃ datvā tasmiṃ bhave aparimeyyāni puññāni katvā tato tusitapuraṃ gantvā tattha yāvatāyukaṃ ṭhatvā dasahi cakkavāḷasahassehi āgantvā devatāvisesehi.

In the manner spoken of in the Jātattakī and Sotattakī, being one who was not yet a hero, seven incalculable aeons passed for him while merely contemplating Buddhahood. Not yet being an exceeding hero, nine incalculable aeons passed while he fulfilled the perfections by speech alone. Being an exceeding hero, four incalculable aeons passed while he fulfilled them by way of body and speech. These four incalculable aeons should be understood as beginning from the time at the feet of the Buddha Dīpaṅkara. Furthermore, it should be understood that at the culmination of one hundred thousand aeons, the internal perfections were fulfilled. And in his existence as Vessantara, having given the seven great gifts and caused the earth to quake seven times, he went to Mount Vaṅka. There he gave his two sons, who were like his own eyes, into servitude to a brahmin. On the second day, to Sakka, king of the gods, who had come in the guise of a brahmin, he gave his wife Maddī, who was equal to himself. Having performed immeasurable meritorious deeds in that existence, he then went to the city of Tusita. Having dwelt there for his full lifespan, he was approached by the foremost deities who had come from ten thousand world-systems.

Kālo deva mahāvīra, uppajja mātukucchiyaṃ;

Sadevakaṃ tārayanto, bujjhassu amataṃ padanti.

It is time, O Deva, O great hero! Be born in a mother's womb. Causing the world with its gods to cross over, awaken to the deathless state!

Yāciyamāno kālaṃ dīpañca desañca kulaṃ mātaramevacāti imāni pañca mahāvilokanāni viloketvā tusitapurato cavitvā mahāsammatavaṃsajassa okkākarañño puttānaṃ kusalambhedabhayena bhaginīhiyeva saddhiṃ āvāhakaraṇaṃ sutvā sakyā vata bho rājakumārāti okkākamahārājena vuttavacanaṃ pavattanimittaṃ katvā sakyāti laddhanāmānaṃ rājūnamabbhantare pavattassa suddhodanamahārājassa jeṭṭhamahesiyā sirimahāmāyāya kucchimhi nibbattitvā dasamāsaccayena devadahanagarassa kapilavatthussa ca majjhe lumbinīvane mātukucchito nikkhamitvā jātakkhaṇeyeva [M 0.0008] uttarābhimukhaṃ sattapadavītihārena gantvā achambhivācaṃ nicchāretvā pitaraṃ vandāpetvā anukkamena soḷasavassuddesikakāle rajjasiriṃ anubhavitvā rāhulabhaddassa jātadivase nikkhamitvā chabbassāni dukkaracariyaṃ caritvā vesākhapuṇṇamadivase anuttaraṃ sammāsambodhiṃ abhisambujjhitvā pañcacattālīsa vassāni devamanussānaṃ ratanadāmaṃ ganthento viya dhammaratanavassaṃ vassāpetvā yāva subhaddaparibbājakavinayanā katabuddhakicce kusinārāyaupavaṭṭane mallānaṃ sālavane vesākhapuṇṇamadivase paccūsasamaye anupādisesāya nibbānadhātuyā parinibbute bhagavatilokanāthe subhaddena vuḍḍhapabbajitena “alaṃ āvuso mā socittha mā paridevittha, sumuttā mayaṃ tena mahāsamaṇena, upaddutā ca homa’idaṃ vo kappati idaṃ vo na kappatī’ti. Idāni pana mayaṃ yaṃ icchissāma taṃ karissāma, yaṃ na icchissāma na taṃ karissāmā”ti vuttavacanaṃ sallakkhetvā gaṇapāmokkhānaṃ sattannaṃ bhikkhusatasahassānaṃ saṅghatthero āyasmā mahākassapo dhammasaṅgītiṃ katvā parisuddhe dhammavinaye tato aparabhāge vassasatassa accayena vesāliyā vajjiputtakā dasavatthūni dassetvā sāsanavipattiṃ karonte disvā āyasmatā yasattherena kākaṇḍakaputtena samussāhiyamāno dutiyasaṅgītiṃ katvā tato aparabhāge tathāgatassa parinibbānato dvinnaṃ vassasatānaṃ upari aṭṭhārasame vasse dhammāsokonāma rājā sakalajambudīpatale ekarajjābhisekaṃ pāpuṇitvā buddhasāsane mahantaṃ lābhasakkāraṃ pavattetvā parihīnalābhasakkārā titthiyā antamaso ghāsacchādanamattampi alabhantā lābhasakkāraṃ patthayamānā sāsane pabbajitvā sakāni sakāni diṭṭhigatāni dīpetvā sāsane mahantaṃ abbudañca malañca pavattesuṃ. Tadā dhammāsokarañño pattābhisekato sattarasame vasse saṭṭhisatasahassa bhikkhūsu sahassabhikkhuṃ gahetvā moggaliputtatisso nāma saṅghatthero mahāpavāraṇāya parappavādaṃ bhinditvā kathāvatthuppakaraṇaṃ desetvā tatiyadhammasaṅgītiṃ katvā parisuddhe dhammavinaye sāṭṭhakathebuddhavacane dharanteva pāḷiyaṭṭhakathāsu atthacchāyaṃ gahetvā attano mativasena nānāganthavisesāni karonti, tesu attanomativasena atthacchāyaṃ gahetvā kāraṇapatirūpakaṃ katvā [M 0.0009] vuttavacanaṃ sārato sallakkhetvā sutta-suttānulomaācariyavāde muñcitvā pāḷiyā aṭṭhakathaṃ, aṭṭhakathāya ca pāḷiṃ tabbivaraṇabhūtaṃ sāratthadīpaniñca aññāni ca ganthavisesāni asaṃsanditvā attanomatiyāpi pubbāparaṃ asamānetvā vādappakāsanamattameva ṭhapetvā garukalahukesu garukeyeva ṭhātabbepi garuke aṭṭhatvā keci bhikkhū nadiyā udakukkhepaṃ akatvā karonti. Evaṃ sāsane dvidhā bhinnā honti. Idañca bhindanaṃ kosambakakkhandhake bhinnatopi sataguṇena sahassaguṇena balavatarameva sāsanassa vipattikāraṇattā, tathā hi na idaṃ manussaloke bhikkhūnaṃyeva hoti, bhikkhūnaṃ, upāsakamanussānaṃ, ārakkhakadevatānampi, tāsaṃ sahāyakānaṃ bhummanissitadevatānampi tāsaṃ sahāyakānaṃ rukkhanissitadevatānampi tāsaṃsahāyakānaṃ ākāsaṭṭhakadevatānampi, tāsaṃ sahāyakānaṃ cātumahārājikadevatānampi, tāsaṃ sahāyakānaṃ tāvatiṃsadevatānampi, evaṃ mittaparamparavasena chakāmāvacaradevatānampi tāsaṃ mittānaṃ brahmapārisajjānampīti evaṃ yāvaakaniṭṭhabrahmalokā ariyapuggale ṭhapetvā sabbe puthujjanā devamanussā bhinnā honti, tesaṃ bhinnattā bahu ca pāpaṃ pasavati, tasmā pāḷiyā aṭṭhakathaṃ, aṭṭhakathāya ca pāḷiṃ saṃsanditvā tāni tāni ganthavisesāni ca ekato katvā gaṅgodake na yamunodakaṃ viya missitvā ekībhāvaṃ katvā sīmavisodhaniṃ nāma karissāmi, taṃ suṇātha sādhukaṃ manasikarotha dhammarājassa sāvakāti.

Being requested, after considering the five great reflections—time, the continent, the region, the family, and the mother—he descended from the Tusita heaven. Hearing that the sons of King Okkāka, of the Mahāsammata lineage, for fear of breaking the integrity of the lineage, had married their own sisters, and taking as the originating sign the words spoken by Mahārāja Okkāka, 'Capable (sakyā), indeed, are the royal princes!', he was reborn among those kings who had thus received the name 'Sakyas', in the womb of Queen Sirimahāmāyā, the chief consort of King Suddhodana. After ten months, he emerged from his mother's womb in the Lumbinī grove, between the cities of Devadaha and Kapilavatthu. At the moment of birth, he took seven steps northward, uttered a fearless proclamation, and caused his father to pay homage to him. In due course, at the age of sixteen, he enjoyed royal glory. On the day his son Rāhula was born, he renounced the world, practiced severe austerities for six years, and on the full moon day of Vesākha, attained unsurpassed perfect enlightenment. For forty-five years, as if stringing a garland of jewels, he caused the rain of the jewel of Dhamma to fall upon gods and humans. Having performed the Buddha's duties up to the taming of the wanderer Subhadda, then, on the full moon day of Vesākha, at dawn, in the Sāla grove of the Mallas near Kusinārā, when the Blessed One, the Lord of the world, had attained final Nibbāna in the element of Nibbāna without remainder, the elder Subhadda, who had gone forth in old age, said, 'Enough, friends! Do not grieve or lament. We are well freed from that great ascetic. We were oppressed by his rules—"This is allowable for you, this is not." Now we shall do as we wish and refrain as we wish.' Reflecting on these words, the Elder Mahākassapa, chief of the assembly of seven hundred thousand monks, convened the First Council to purify the Dhamma and Vinaya. Later, after the passing of a hundred years, seeing the Vajjiputtaka monks in Vesāli proclaiming ten points, causing a decline in the Dispensation, the Elder Yasa, son of Kākaṇḍaka, inspired by this, convened the Second Council. Further, in the eighteenth year after two hundred years from the Parinibbāna of the Thus Gone One, King Dhammāsoka, having obtained consecration as sole sovereign over all Jambudīpa, established great gain and honor in the Buddha's Dispensation. Heretics, deprived of gain and honor, not obtaining even as much as food and clothing, desiring gain and honor, entered the Dispensation, proclaiming their own views, causing a great blemish and impurity in the Dispensation. Then, in the seventeenth year after King Dhammāsoka's consecration, Elder Moggaliputtatissa, taking a thousand monks from among six million monks, having refuted the doctrines of others during the Great Pavāraṇā, expounded the Kathāvatthu-pakaraṇa and convened the Third Council to purify the Dhamma and Vinaya. Yet, while the Buddha's words, together with their commentaries, were still present, some monks, taking a superficial understanding of the meaning from the Pāli and commentaries, composed various special treatises according to their own views. In these, taking a superficial understanding of the meaning according to their own views, making a semblance of reason, and abandoning the teachings of the teachers that are in conformity with the Suttas, without harmonizing the Pāli with the commentary, and the commentary with the Pāli, and the Sāratthadīpanī which is its explanation, and other special treatises, and without bringing their own views into accord with what precedes and follows, they merely established the proclamation of their own doctrines. Some monks, even when weighty matters should be given weight, gave them no weight, acting without performing the necessary preliminaries. Thus, the Dispensation split into two. This division, being a hundredfold or a thousandfold more powerful than the division in the Kosambaka dispute, is indeed a cause for the Dispensation's decline. For this division was not confined to the monks in the human world; monks, lay followers, guardian deities, and through a succession of friendships, earth-dwelling deities, tree-dwelling deities, sky-dwelling deities, the deities of the Four Great Kings, the Tāvatiṃsa gods, and so up to the six sense-sphere deities and their friends, the retinue of Brahmā—thus, excluding the noble ones, all ordinary beings, gods and humans, became divided, all the way up to the Akaniṭṭha Brahmā world. Because of their division, much evil arises. Therefore, reconciling the Pāli with its commentary, and the commentary with the Pāli, and bringing those various special treatises together into one, mixing them like the water of the Yamunā into the water of the Ganges to make them one, I shall compose the Sīmavisodhanī. Listen to that, pay good heed, O disciples of the Dhamma King!

Tattha buddhuppādo dullabho ti buddhassa uppādakālo dullabho tathā hi anuppannakāle buddhe buddhoti saddampi asutvā vītivattānaṃ kappānaṃ gaṇanapathaṃ vītivattā. Buddhakāraṇassa dukkarattaṃ heṭṭhā vuttameva.

Here, 'the arising of a Buddha is rare' means the time of a Buddha's arising is rare. For indeed, in times when a Buddha has not arisen, even the word 'Buddha' is unheard, and aeons have passed beyond reckoning. The difficulty of the cause for a Buddha has already been stated below.

Tato pabbajā ca upasampadācā tiettha topaccayo lāmakatthe, pañcamyatthe vā. Buddhuppādo dullabho, tamanupabbajjā ca upasampadā ca dullabhāyevāti adhippāyo. Yathā ca loke kiñcideva mahagghaṃ alabhitabbaṃ labhitvā vā tena sahakārīkāraṇabhūtaṃ appagghampi mahagghaṃ mahagghaṃ dullabhaṃ dullabhanti vuccati, evameva atidullabhaṃ buddhuppādaṃ āgamma tamanupabbajitampi dullabhanti vuccati, yathā ca nidiyā uparimabhāge sattāhavaṭṭalikādibhāvena udakapūraṇe heṭṭhā kismiñci ṭhāne tarituṃ avisahantā idameva ṭhānaṃ [M 0.0010] dukkaranti vuccati, evameva buddhakāraṇassa dukkarabhāve na tamanupabbajjupasampadāpi dukkaraṃ dullabhanti vuccati. Aññathā buddhabhagavato pi pabbajjupasampadabhāvoyeva seṭṭho bhaveyya. Athavā kāraṇūpacāravasenāpi evaṃ vuttanti daṭṭhabbaṃ, yathā semhoguḷoti tathā hi guḷassa pi vanapaccayā semho ussanno, taṃ jano guḷenasemhoti vattabbepi kāraṇasmiṃ guḷe phalabhūtaṃ semhaṃ ropetvā semho guḷoti vuccati, evaṃ kāraṇassa dukkarattā dullabhe buddhuppāde tamanukriyā pabbajjāpi taṃ mūlakāraṇabhūte buddhuppāde ropetvā dullabhā pabbajjāti vuccati. Buddhuppādakālepi buddhadassanaṃ dullabhameva, anuppannakāle pana pageva, sabbakhaṇesu navamakhaṇattā ca tathā hi milakkhudesaarūpabhūmiasaññasattanirayapetatiracchānabhūmīsu jāyamānesu vā majjhimadese jāyamānopi micchādiṭṭhibhūtā vā, sammādiṭṭhikule jāyamānāpi cakkhusota vikalabhāvena aṅgavikalā vā buddhadassanaṃ na arahanti, dassanampi dassanamattameva. Supaṇṇanāgarājādīnaṃ tasmiṃ bhave maggaphalabhāgino na honti, honti cettha.

Then, regarding 'the going forth and the higher ordination': here the suffix 'to' (in 'tato') is used in a depreciative sense, or in the sense of the ablative case. The meaning is that the arising of a Buddha is rare, and thus the going forth and higher ordination that follow it are also rare. Just as in the world, when something very precious and unobtainable is obtained, even something of little value that serves as an auxiliary cause for it is called precious and rare; in the same way, because the arising of a Buddha is exceedingly rare, even the going forth that follows him is called rare. And just as when a river is filled with water in its upper part, and due to the formation of a whirlpool or the like, those unable to cross at some lower point call that very spot difficult; in the same way, because the cause for a Buddha is difficult, even the going forth and higher ordination following him are called difficult and rare. Otherwise, the very state of going forth and higher ordination of the Blessed Buddha would be the most excellent. Or, it should be understood that this is said by way of a figurative expression based on the cause, like 'phlegm-sugar.' For when phlegm arises due to the condition of sugar, people, when they should say 'phlegm from sugar,' instead attribute the effect, phlegm, to the cause, sugar, and say 'phlegm-sugar.' Similarly, because the arising of a Buddha is difficult and rare, the going forth, being rooted in that causal arising, is also called a rare going forth. Even during the time of a Buddha's arising, seeing the Buddha is rare, how much more so when a Buddha has not arisen. And further, because it is the ninth unfavorable moment, that is, those born in the borderlands, the formless realms, the realm of unpercipient beings, the hells, the realm of hungry ghosts, or the animal realm, or even those born in the middle country who are of wrong view, or even those born in a family of right view who are physically impaired with defective eyes or ears, are not capable of seeing the Buddha. Even seeing is just seeing only. Garuḍas and Nāga kings, and others, in that existence, are not partakers of the path and its fruits, though they may be present.

“Paccantajo arūpino, vikalaṅgo asaññajo;

Micchādiṭṭhi tiracchāno, peto nerayikopica.

Those born in borderlands, the formless beings, the crippled, the unperceiving beings, those with wrong view, animals, hungry ghosts, and hell-beings;

Ete aṭṭhakkhaṇā vuttā, buddhenādiccabandhunā;

Buddhuppādo khaṇo eko, navamoti pavuccatīti”.

These eight unfavorable moments are declared by the Buddha, kinsman of the sun. The arising of a Buddha is one moment, the ninth, so it is called.

Pabbajjā ticettha sāmaññena vuttepi sāmaṇerāva adhippetā, upasampadā cāti visuṃ vuttattā. Sāmaṇerā ca nāma buddhaṃ saraṇaṃ gacchāmityādinā tikkhattuṃ vatvā ubhatosuddhivaseneva sāmaṇerabhūmiyaṃ tiṭṭhanti. Dasasīlāni pana tesaṃ sikkhāpadamattameva. Tāni pāḷivasena asakkonto atthavasena “idañca samādāya vattehī”ti ācikkhitumpi vaṭṭatiyeva. Idha vattabbaṃ natthi. Upasampadā pana aṭṭhavidhā honti ehibhikkhūpasampadā, saraṇagamanūpasampadā, ovāda paṭiggahaṇūpasampadā, pañhābyākaraṇūpasampadā, aṭṭhagarudhammapaṭiggahaṇūpasampadā, dūtenūpasampadā, aṭṭhavācikūpasampadā, ñatticatutthūpasampadā cāti. Tattha bhagavā ehi bhikkhubhāvāya upanissayasampannaṃ puggalaṃ disvā rattapaṃsukūlantarato suvaṇṇavaṇṇaṃ dakkhiṇahatthaṃ nīharitvā brahmaghosaṃ nicchārento “ehi bhikkhu cara brahmacariyaṃ [M 0.0011] sammā dukkhassa antakiriyāyā”ti vadati. Tassānantarameva gihiliṅgaṃ antaradhāyitvā.

Here, although the term 'going forth' (pabbajjā) is used in a general sense, it refers to novices (sāmaṇeras), as the higher ordination (upasampadā) is mentioned separately. Novices, indeed, upon saying three times, 'I go to the Buddha for refuge,' and so forth, and by having purity on both sides, stand on the footing of a novice. For them, the ten precepts are merely training rules. If one is unable to recite them in Pāli, it is permissible to explain them by their meaning, saying, 'Undertake these and conduct yourself accordingly.' Here, there is nothing further to be said. Higher ordination, however, is of eight kinds: ordination by 'Come, monk,' ordination by taking refuge, ordination by acceptance of instruction, ordination by answering questions, ordination by acceptance of the eight weighty rules, ordination by messenger, ordination by eight recitations, and ordination by a motion and three announcements. In this regard, when the Blessed One sees a person endowed with the supporting conditions for monkhood, he draws forth his golden-hued right hand from beneath his reddish-brown rag-robe and, emitting a Brahma-like voice, says, 'Come, monk, live the holy life for the complete ending of suffering.' Immediately afterward, the signs of a householder disappear.

Ticīvarañca patto ca, vāsi sūcica bandhanaṃ;

Parissāvanamaṭṭhete, yuttayogassa bhikkhunoti.

The three robes, the bowl, the razor, the needle, the belt, and the water strainer—these eight are the requisites for a monk engaged in striving.

Evaṃ vuttehi aṭṭhahiparikkhārehi sarīre paṭimuttoyeva vassasatikatthero viya iriyāpathasampanno buddhācariyako buddhupajjhāyako sammāsambuddhaṃ vandamānova tiṭṭhati. Ayaṃ ehi bhikkhūpasampadā nāma. Bhagavā hi paṭhamabodhiyaṃ etasmiṃ kāle ehi bhikkhupasampadāya eva upasampādeti. Tāni pana pañcavaggiyādayo aṅgulimālattherapariyosānā.

Upon this being said, with his body equipped with the eight requisites, he stood like an elder of a hundred years, possessed of proper deportment, with the Buddha as his teacher and the Buddha as his preceptor, as if venerating the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One. This is called the 'Come, Bhikkhu' ordination. Indeed, during the first twenty years of his Buddhahood, the Blessed One ordained with this 'Come, Bhikkhu' ordination. Those ordained in this way ranged from the group of five monks to the Elder Aṅgulimāla.

Tīṇisataṃ sahassañca, cattālīsaṃ punāparo;

Eko ca thero sappañño; Sabbe te ehi bhikkhukāti.

Three hundred thousand, and forty more, and one wise elder; all of them were 'Ehi Bhikkhus'.

Ete vinayapiṭake niddiṭṭhaehibhikkhū nāma vinayapiṭake aniddiṭṭhā pana tisataparivāraselabrāhmaṇādayo.

These are the 'Ehi Bhikkhus' specified in the Vinaya Piṭaka. However, those not specified in the Vinaya Piṭaka are, for example, the brahmin Sela and his retinue of three hundred.

Sattavīsa sahassāni, tīṇiyeva satāni ca;

Ete hi sabbe saṅkhātā, sabbe te ehibhikkhukāti.

Twenty-seven thousand, and three hundred; all these were reckoned, all were 'Ehi Bhikkhus'.

Taṃ sabbaṃ sampiṇḍetvā aṭṭhavīsasahassāni chasatāni ekacattālīsuttarāni ca honti. Paṭhamabodhica nāmesā vīsativassāni honti.

All that combined amounts to twenty-eight thousand, six hundred and forty-one. This period of twenty years is called the First Bodhi.

Bhagavā hi anuttaraṃ dhammacakkaṃ pavattetvā aṭṭhārasakoṭibrāhmaṇānaṃ amataṃ pāyetvā paṭhamavassaṃ vasi. Dutiyatatiyacatutthavassesu rājagahe veḷuvanavihāre vassaṃ vasi, bhagavā hi āsāḷhinakkhattapuṇṇamiyaṃ bārāṇasiyā isipatane migadāye paṭhamavassaṃ vasitvā phagguṇamāse phagguṇapuṇṇamiyaṃ rājagahaṃ patvā bimbisāraraññā kārite veḷuvana vihāre ekamāsamattaṃ vasitvā citramāse vīsatikhīṇāsavasahassaparivuto kapilavatthuṃ gantvā asītiyā ñātisahassānaṃ majjhe vippaṭipannānaṃ rājūnaṃ matthake paṃsurajaṃ okiritvā te vandāpetvā pokkharavassañca vassāpetvā tamāgamma asītiyā ñātisahassānaṃ vessantara jātakaṃ kathetvā rāhulamātaramāgamma candakinnarījātakañca kathetvā pitaraṃ anāgāmiphale patiṭṭhāpetvā rājagahameva āgantvā vāsaṃ kappesi, tena vuttaṃ dutiya tatiya catuttha vassesu rājagahe veḷuvanavihāre vassaṃ vasī”ti[M 0.0012]. Pañcame pana vesāliyaṃ ekavāsaṃ vasi. Chaṭṭhe makuḷapabbate ekavāsaṃ vasi. Puṇṇo vā tattha. Tatrāyaṃ anupubbikathā sunāparantaraṭṭhe kira ekasmiṃ vāṇijakagāme dve bhātaro. Tesu kadāci jeṭṭho pañcasakaṭasatāni gahetvā janapadaṃ gantvā bhaṇḍaṃ āharati, kadāci kaniṭṭho. Imasmiṃ pana samaye kaniṭṭhaṃ ghare ṭhapetvā jeṭṭhabhātiko pañca sakaṭasatāni gahetvā janapadacārikaṃ caranto anupubbena sāvatthiṃ patvā jetavanassa avidūre pañcasakaṭasatāni ṭhapetvā bhuttapātarāso parijanaparivuto phāsukaṭṭhāne nisīdi. Tena ca samayena sāvatthivāsino bhuttapātarāsā uposathaṅgāni adhiṭṭhāya suṭṭhuttarāsaṅgā gandhapupphādihatthā yenabuddho, yenadhammo, yenasaṅgho, tanninnā tappoṇā tappabbharā hutvā dikkhiṇadvārena nikkhamitvā jetavanaṃ gacchanti. So te disvā “kahaṃ ime gacchantī”ti ekaṃ manussaṃ pucchi. Kiṃ tvaṃ ayyo na jānāsi, loke buddhadhammasaṅgharatanāni nāma uppannāni, icceva mahājano satthusantike dhammakathaṃ sotuṃ gacchatīti. Tassa “buddho”ti vacanaṃ chavicammādīni chinditvā aṭṭhimiñjaṃ āhacca aṭṭhāsi. Atha attano parijanaparivuto tāya parisāyasaddhiṃ vihāraṃ gantvā satthāmadhurassarena dhammaṃ desentassa parisa pariyante ṭhito dhammaṃ sutvā pabbajjāya cittaṃ uppādesi. Atha tathāgatena kālaṃ viditvā parisāya uyyojitāya satthāraṃ upasaṅkamitvā vanditvā svātanāya nimantetvā dutiyadivase maṇḍapaṃ karetvā āsanāni paññāpetvā buddhappamukhassa saṅghassa mahādānaṃ datvā bhattakiccāvasāne bhagavā anumodanaṃ katvā pakkami. Bhuttapātaraso uposathaṅgāni adhiṭṭhāya bhaṇḍāgārikaṃ pakkosāpetvā “ettakaṃ bhaṇḍaṃ vissajjitaṃ ettakaṃ pana na vissajjita”nti sabbaṃ ācikkhitvā “imaṃ sāpateyyaṃ mayhaṃ kaniṭṭhassa dehī”ti sabbaṃ niyyotetvā satthu santike pabbajitvā kammaṭṭhāna parāyaṇo ahosi. Athassa kammaṭṭhānaṃ manasikarontassa kammaṭṭhānaṃ na upaṭṭhāti, tato cintesi “ayaṃ janapado mayhaṃ asappāyo, yaṃnūnāhaṃ satthu santike kammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvā sakaṭṭhānameva gaccheyya”nti. Atha pubbaṇhasamaye piṇḍāya caritvā sāyanhasamaye paṭisallānā vuṭṭhito bhagavantaṃ upasaṅkamitvā kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathāpetvā satthā sīhanādaṃ naditvā pakkami. Atha kho āyasmā puṇṇo sūnāparantaraṭṭhaṃ [M 0.0013] patvā sammatapabbataṃ nāma pavisitvā vāṇijakagāmaṃ piṇḍāya pāvisi. Atha naṃ kaniṭṭhabhātā sagehaṃ netvā bhikkhaṃ datvā “bhante aññattha agantvā idheva vasathā”ti paṭiññaṃ kāretvā vasāpesi. Tato samuddagirivihāraṃ nāma agamāsi. Tattha samuddavīciyo āgantvā ayakaṇṭapāsāṇesu paharitvā mahāsaddaṃ karonti. Thero kammaṭṭhānaṃ manasikaronto na phāsuvihāro hotīti samuddaṃ nisaddaṃ katvā adhiṭṭhāsi. Tato mātulagiriṃ nāma āgamāsi. Tattha sakuṇasaṅgho ussanno rattiñca divā ca saddo eko baddho hoti. Thero “idaṃ ṭhānaṃ aphāsukanti tato makuḷakārāmavihāraṃ nāma gato. So vāṇijagāmassa nātidūro nāccāsanno gamanāgamanasampanno vivitto appasaddo. Thero “idaṃ ṭhānaṃ phāsuka”nti tattha rattiṭṭhānadivāṭhāne caṅkamanādīni kāretvā vassaṃ upagacchati. Evaṃ catūsu ṭhānesu vihāsi. Athekadivasaṃ tasmiṃyeva antovasse pañca vāṇijakasatāni “parasamuddaṃ gacchāmā”ti nāvāya bhaṇḍaṃ pakkhipiṃsu. Nāvārohanadivase therassa kaniṭṭhabhātā theraṃ bhojetvā therassa santike sikkhāpadāni gahetvā gacchanto “bhante mahāsamuddo nāma assaddheyyo cānekantarāyo ca, amhe āvajjeyyāthā”ti vatvā nāvaṃ ārūyhi. Nāvā ūmijavena gacchamānā aññataraṃ dīpaṃ pāpuṇi. Manussā “pātarāsaṃ karissāmā”ti dīpake otiṇṇā. Tasmiṃ dīpake aññaṃ kiñci natthi, candanavanameva ahosi. Atheko vā siyā rukkhaṃ ākoṭetvā lohitacandanabhāvaṃ ñatvā “bho mayaṃ lābhatthāya parasamuddaṃ gacchāma, ito ca uttari lābhā nāma natthi, caturaṅgula mattāpi ghaṭikā satasahassaṃ agghati, saṃhāretabbakayuttaṃ bhaṇḍaṃ hāretvā gacchāmā”ti. Te tathā kariṃsu candanavane adhivatthā amanussā kujjhitvā imehi amhākaṃ candanavanaṃ nāsitaṃ, ghātessāma ne”ti cintetvā na imesu ghāṭitesu sabbaṃ ekakuṇapaṃ bhavissati, samuddamajjhe nesaṃ nāvaṃ osīdessāmā”ti āhaṃsu. Atha tesaṃ nāvaṃ ārūyha muhuttaṃ gatakāleyeva uppādikaṃ upaṭṭhāpetvā sayampi te amanussā bhayānakāni rūpāni dassayiṃsu. Bhītā manussā attano devatā namassanti. Therassa kaniṭṭho cūḷapuṇṇakuṭumbiko “mayhaṃ bhātā avassayo hotū”ti therassa namassamāno aṭṭhāsi. Thero pi kira tasmiṃyeva khaṇe [M 0.0014] āvajjetvā tesaṃ byasanappattiṃ ñatvā vehāsaṃ uppatitvā abhimukho aṭṭhāsi. Amanussā theraṃ disvāva “ayyapuṇṇatthero ehī” ti pakkamiṃsu. Uppādikaṃ sannisīdi. Thero “mā bhāyathā”ti tesaṃ assāsetvāva kahaṃ gantukāmatthā”ti pucchi. “Bhante amhākaṃ sakaṭṭhānameva gacchāmā”ti thero nāvaṃ phale akkamitvā “etesaṃ icchitaṭṭhānaṃ gacchatū”ti adhiṭṭhāti. Vāṇijā sakaṭṭhānaṃ gantvā taṃ pavattiṃ puttadārassa ārocetvā “etha theraṃ saraṇaṃ gacchāmā”ti pañcasatāni attano attano pañcamātugāmasatehi saddhiṃ tīsu saraṇesu patiṭṭhāya upāsakattaṃ paṭivedesuṃ. Tato nāvāya bhaṇḍaṃ otāretvā therassa ekaṃ koṭṭhāsaṃ katvā “ayaṃ bhante tumhākaṃ koṭṭhāso”ti āhaṃsu. Thero “mayhaṃ visuṃ koṭṭhāsa kiccaṃ natthi”. Satthā pana tumhehi diṭṭhapubbo’ti. Na diṭṭhapubbo bhante’ti, tenahi iminā satthu maṇḍalamāḷaṃ karotha. Evaṃ satthāraṃ passissathāti te sādhu bhante’ti tena ca koṭṭhāsena attano ca koṭṭhāsena maṇḍalamāḷaṃ kātuṃ ārabhiṃsu. Satthā kirassa āraddhakālato paṭṭhāya paribhogaṃ akāsi. Tatho manussā rattiṃ obhāsaṃ disvā “mahesakkhā devatā atthī”ti saññaṃkariṃsu. Upāsakā maṇḍalamāḷañca bhikkhusaṅghassa senāsanāni ca niṭṭhapetvā dānasambhāraṃ sajjetvā “kataṃ bhante amhehi attanokiccaṃ, satthāraṃ pakkosathā”ti therassa ārocesuṃ. Thero sāyanhasamaye iddhiyāsāvatthiṃ patvā “bhante vāṇijagāmavāsino tumhe daṭṭhukāmā, tesaṃ anukampaṃ karothā”ti. Bhagavā adhivāsesi. Thero bhagavato adhivāsanaṃ viditvā sakaṭṭhānameva paccāgato. Bhagavā ānandattheraṃ āmantesi “ānanda sve sūnāparante vāṇijagāme piṇḍāya carissāma, tvaṃ ekūnapañcasatānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ salākaṃ dehī”ti. Thero sādhu bhante’ti bhikkhusaṅghassa tamatthaṃ ārocetvā “cārikā salākaṃ gaṇhantū”ti āha. Taṃ divasaṃ kuṇḍadhānatthero paṭhamaṃ salākaṃ aggahoti. Vāṇijagāmavāsinopi “sve kira satthā āgamissatī”ti gāmamajjhe maṇḍapaṃ katvā dānaggaṃ sajjayiṃsu. Bhagavā pātova sarīrapaṭijagganaṃ katvā gandhakuṭiṃ pavisitvā phalasamāpattiṃ appetvā nisīdi. Sakkassa paṇḍukambalasilāsanaṃ uṇhaṃ ahosi. So kiṃ idanti āvajjetvā satthu sūnāparantagamanaṃ disvā visukammaṃ āmantesi [M 0.0015] “tāta ajja bhagavā tīṇimattāni yojanasatāni piṇḍācāraṃ gamissati, pañca kūṭāgārasatāni māpetvā jetavanadvāra koṭṭhamatthake gamanasajjāniṃ katvā ṭhapehī”ti, so tathā akāsi. Bhagavato kūṭāgāraṃ catumukhaṃ ahosi, dvinnaṃ aggasāvakānaṃ dvimukhaṃ, sesānaṃ ekamukhaṃ, satthā gandhakuṭito nikkhamma paṭipāṭiyā ṭhapita kūṭāgāresu dhurakūṭāgāraṃ pāvisi. Dve aggasāvake ādiṃ katvā ekūnapañcabhikkhusatānipi kūṭāgāraṃ gantvā nisinnā ahesuṃ, ekaṃ tucchakūṭāgāraṃ ahosi. Pañcapi kūṭāgārasatāni ākāse uppatiṃsu. Satthā saccabandhapabbataṃ nāma patvā kūṭāgāraṃ ākāse ṭhapetvā tasmiṃ pabbate saccabandho nāma micchādiṭṭhi, so mahājanaṃ micchādiṭṭhiṃ uggaṇhāpento lābhaggayasaggappatto hutvā vasati. Abbhantare cassa antocāṭiyaṃ padīpo viya arahattassa upanissayo jalati, taṃ disvā “dhammamassa kathessāmī”ti gantvā dhammaṃ deseti. Tāpaso desanāpariyosāne arahattaṃ pāpuṇi. Maggenevassa abhiññā āgatā, ehi bhikkhu hutvā iddhimayapattacīvaro kūṭāgāraṃ pāvisi. Bhagavā kūṭāgāragatehi pañcahi bhikkhusatehisaddhiṃ vāṇijagāmaṃ gantvā kūṭāgārāni adissamānāni katvā vāṇijagāmaṃ pāvisi. Vāṇijā buddhappamukhassa bhikkhusaṅghassa mahādānaṃ datvā sasatthāraṃ makuḷakārāmaṃ nayiṃsu. Satthā maṇḍalamāḷaṃ pāvisi. Mahājano yāva satthā bhattadarathaṃ paṭippassambheti, tāva pātarāsaṃ katvā uposathaṅgāni samādāya gandhañca pupphañca ādāya dhammasavanatthāya ārāmaṃ paccāgamāsi. Satthā dhammaṃ desesi. Mahājanassa bandhanā mokkho jāto, mahanta buddhakolāhalaṃ ahosi. Satthā mahājanassa saṅgahatthaṃ katipāhaṃ tattheva vasi, aruṇaṃ pana mahāgandhakuṭiyaṃyeva uṭṭhapesi. Tattha katipāhaṃ vasitvā vāṇijagāme piṇḍāya caritvā “tvaṃ idheva vasāhī”ti puṇṇattheraṃ nivattetvā antare nammadānadī nāma atthi, tassā tīraṃ agamāsi. Nammadānāgarājā satthu paccuggantvā nāgabhavanaṃ pavesetvā tiṇṇaṃ ratanānaṃ sakkāraṃ akāsi. Satthā tassa dhammaṃ kathetvā nāgabhavanā nikkhami. So “mayhaṃ bhante paricaritabbaṃ dethā”ti yāci. Bhagavā nammadānadītīre padacetiyaṃ dassesi. Taṃ vīcīsu āgahāsu pidhiyati vīcīsu gatāsu vivarati, mahāsakkārappattaṃ ahosi. Satthā tato nikkhamma saccabandhapabbataṃ gantvā [M 0.0016] saccabandhaṃ āha “tayā mahājano apāyamagge otārito. Tvaṃ idheva vasitvā etesaṃ laddhiṃ vissajjāpetvā nibbānamagge patiṭṭhāpehī”ti. Sopi paricaritabbaṃ yāci. Satthā ghanapiṭṭhipāsāṇe allamattikapiṇḍamhi lañchanaṃ viya padacetiyaṃ dassesi. Evaṃ sūnāparantaraṭṭheyeva dve cakkaratanacetiyāni yāvasāsanantaradhānaṃ patiṭṭhāpesi. Sattame pana vasse sāvatthiyaṃ kaṇḍambarukkhamūle titthi maddanaṃ karonto yamakapāṭihāriyaṃ katvā dasahi cakkavāḷa sahassehi āgatānaṃ devatānaṃ mātaraṃ kāyasakkhiṃ katvā abhidhammaṃ desente tāvatiṃsabhavane pāricchattakamūle paṇḍukambalasilāyaṃ vassaṃ vasi. Aṭṭhame saṃsumāranagare ekavassaṃ vasi. Navame kosambiyaṃ ekavassaṃ vasi. Tadā bhaddiyo, kimilo, bhagu, ānando, anuruddho, devadatto, cāti cha khattiyā pabbajiṃsu. Tesaṃ vitthāro dhammapade āgato. Dasame pana vasse pālileyyāgāme dakkhiṇavanasaṇḍe bhaddasālamūle pālileyyakena hatthinā upaṭṭhiyamāno phāsukaṃ vassāvāsaṃ vasi. Ekādasame nālandhabrāhmaṇagāme ekavāsaṃ vasi. Dvādasame verañjāyaṃ ekavāsaṃ vasi duccaritassa vipākaṃ anubhavamāno, bhagavatā kira phussassa bhagavato kāle sāvakānaṃ vacīduccaritavasena katupacitassa akusalakammassa tadā laddhokāsavasena upaṭṭhitattā verañjabrāhmaṇena nimantitopi samāno mārāvaṭṭana vasena asallakkhetvā assavāṇijakānaṃ nibaddhavattasaṅkhepena paññattaṃ patthapatthamūlakaṃ yavataṇḍulameva temāsaṃ bhuñjati, vuttañhetaṃ apadāne.

The Blessed One, having set in motion the unsurpassed Wheel of Dhamma and given the deathless nectar to eighteen crore brahmins, spent the first rains retreat. In the second, third, and fourth rains retreats, he stayed at the Bamboo Grove Monastery in Rājagaha. Indeed, the Blessed One spent the first rains retreat in the Deer Park at Isipatana near Bārāṇasī on the full moon day of Āsāḷha. Then, in the month of Phagguna, on the full moon day of Phagguna, he arrived in Rājagaha. King Bimbisāra had the Bamboo Grove Monastery built, where the Blessed One stayed for about one month. In the month of Citra, accompanied by twenty thousand arahants, he went to Kapilavatthu. Amidst eighty thousand relatives, he scattered dust upon the heads of the quarreling kings, made them pay homage, and caused a rain of lotuses to fall. Based on this, he taught the Vessantara Jātaka to the eighty thousand relatives. Then, approaching Rāhula’s mother, he taught the Candakinnarī Jātaka. He established his father in the fruit of non-returning and returned to Rājagaha to reside there. Hence it is said that in the second, third, and fourth rains retreats, he stayed at the Bamboo Grove Monastery in Rājagaha. In the fifth rains retreat, he stayed once in Vesālī. In the sixth, he stayed once on Mount Makuḷa. Or, Puṇṇa was there. Now, this is the sequential story: In the Sunāparanta country, it is said, there were two brothers in a merchant village. Sometimes the elder brother took five hundred carts and traveled to other regions to bring back goods, and sometimes the younger brother. On this occasion, the elder brother, leaving the younger brother at home, took five hundred carts on a journey through the country. Gradually, he arrived in Sāvatthī, parked the five hundred carts not far from Jetavana, and after his morning meal, sat in a comfortable place surrounded by his retinue. At that time, the residents of Sāvatthī, having finished their morning meal, undertook the Uposatha observances. With their upper robes well-adjusted and carrying perfumes, flowers, and other offerings in their hands, they headed toward the Buddha, the Dhamma, and the Saṅgha. With minds inclined, sloping, and directed toward them, they left through the southern gate and went to Jetavana. Seeing them, he asked a certain person, "Where are these people going?" The man replied, "Noble sir, do you not know? In the world, the jewels named Buddha, Dhamma, and Saṅgha have arisen. Thus the great populace is going to hear a Dhamma discourse in the Teacher's presence." For him, the word "Buddha" pierced through his skin and hide, struck his very bone marrow, and there it stood. Then, surrounded by his own retinue, he went with that assembly to the monastery. Standing at the edge of the assembly, he heard the Dhamma as the Teacher was teaching in his sweet voice, and a desire for ordination arose in his mind. Then, when the Tathāgata, knowing the time was right, dismissed the assembly, the merchant approached the Teacher, paid homage, and invited him for the next day. On the following day, he had a pavilion made, prepared seats, and gave a great offering to the Saṅgha with the Buddha at its head. At the conclusion of the meal, the Blessed One gave a blessing and departed. Having eaten his morning meal and undertaken the Uposatha observances, the merchant had his treasurer summoned and explained everything, saying, "This much merchandise was disposed of, but this much was not." Then he entrusted everything to him, saying, "Give this property to my younger brother." He went forth in the Teacher's presence and became devoted to his meditation subject. But as he was attending to his meditation subject, it did not become clear to him. He thought, "This region is not suitable for me. I should take a meditation subject from the Teacher and go to my own place." Then, in the morning, he went for alms. In the evening, having arisen from seclusion, he approached the Blessed One and had the meditation subject explained. After the Teacher had roared the lion's roar, he departed. Then the Venerable Puṇṇa arrived in the Sunāparanta country, entered a mountain called Sammata, and went for alms in the merchant village. His younger brother took him to his own home, offered him almsfood, and had him stay, making him promise, "Venerable sir, do not go elsewhere; please stay right here." Later, he went to a monastery called Samuddagiri. There, ocean waves, coming and striking against iron-thorn rocks, made a great noise. The Elder, while attending to his meditation subject, was not dwelling comfortably, so he made a determination, making the ocean silent. Then he went to a place called Mātulagiri. There, the flock of birds was abundant, and day and night there was one continuous sound. The Elder thought, "This place is not comfortable," and went to the Makuḷakārāma Monastery. It was neither too far from nor too close to the merchant village, suitable for coming and going, secluded, and quiet. The Elder thought, "This place is comfortable," and there he had places for night and day dwelling, walking paths, and other things made, then entered the rains retreat. Thus, he dwelt in four places. One day during that very rains retreat, five hundred merchants loaded goods onto a ship, intending, "We will go to the further shore of the ocean." On the day of embarking, the Elder’s younger brother fed the Elder, took the training rules in his presence, and as he was leaving, said, "Venerable sir, the great ocean is untrustworthy and has many dangers. Please pay attention to us." Then he boarded the ship. The ship, going with the speed of the waves, reached a certain island. The men disembarked on the island, thinking, "We will make our morning meal." On that island, there was nothing else; there was only a sandalwood forest. Then a certain man, having struck a tree with an axe and realizing it was red sandalwood, thought, "Sirs, we go to the further shore of the ocean for the sake of profit, but there is no profit greater than this. A piece of just four fingers' measure is worth a hundred thousand. Let's take away the goods that should be taken and go." They did so. The non-humans dwelling in the sandalwood forest became angry and thought, "Our sandalwood forest has been destroyed by these men. We will kill them!" But then they said, "No, if these are killed, the whole thing will become one corpse. In the middle of the ocean, we will sink their ship." Then, after the merchants had boarded their ship and only a moment had passed, the non-humans created a sudden storm, and those non-humans themselves showed frightening forms. The frightened men paid homage to their own deities. The Elder’s younger brother, the householder Cūḷapuṇṇa, stood paying homage to the Elder, thinking, "May my brother be my refuge." At that very moment, the Elder, it is said, having adverted and known their attainment of disaster, rose into the air and stood facing them. As soon as they saw the Elder, the non-humans departed, saying, "Here comes the noble Elder Puṇṇa!" The sudden storm subsided. The Elder, having reassured them, "Do not be afraid," asked, "Where do you wish to go?" They replied, "Venerable sir, we wish to go to our own place." The Elder, having stepped on the plank of the ship, made a determination, "May it go to their desired place." The merchants, having gone to their own place, reported the event to their children and wives and said, "Come, let us go for refuge to the Elder." The five hundred of them, together with their own five hundred womenfolk, having been established in the three refuges, declared their state as lay followers. Then, having unloaded the goods from the ship, they made one share for the Elder and said, "Venerable sir, this is your share." The Elder replied, "I have no need for a separate share. But have you seen the Teacher before?" "No, venerable sir." "Then with this, build a circular hall for the Teacher. Thus you will see the Teacher." They agreed, saying, "Good, venerable sir," and with that share and with their own shares, they began to make the circular hall. The Teacher, it is said, made use of it from the time it was started. Then the people, seeing a light at night, perceived, "There is a deity of great power." The lay followers completed the circular hall and the lodgings for the Saṅgha of monks, prepared the requisites for the offering, and informed the Elder, "Venerable sir, our own duty has been done by us. Please summon the Teacher." In the evening, the Elder, by means of his psychic power, reached Sāvatthī and said, "Venerable sir, the residents of the merchant village are desirous of seeing you. Please have compassion on them." The Blessed One consented. Knowing the Blessed One’s consent, the Elder returned to his own place. The Blessed One addressed the Venerable Ānanda, "Ānanda, tomorrow we will wander for alms in the merchant village in Sunāparanta. Give a ticket to four hundred and ninety-nine monks." The Elder agreed, saying, "Good, venerable sir," informed the Saṅgha of monks of this matter, and said, "Let those going on the journey take a ticket." That day, the Venerable Kuṇḍadhāna took the first ticket. The residents of the merchant village also, hearing, "The Teacher will come tomorrow," built a pavilion in the middle of the village and prepared the best of offerings. At dawn, the Blessed One attended to his bodily needs, entered the perfumed chamber, entered the fruition attainment, and sat. Sakka’s Paṇḍukambala stone throne grew warm. He adverted, "What is this?" and seeing the Teacher’s journey to Sunāparanta, summoned Vissakamma, saying, "Dear one, today the Blessed One will travel about three hundred yojanas on his alms round. Have five hundred peaked-roof houses created and place them prepared for the journey on top of the gatehouse of Jetavana." He did so. The Blessed One’s peaked-roof house had four faces, those of the two chief disciples had two faces, and the rest had one face. The Teacher, having emerged from the perfumed chamber, entered the foremost peaked-roof house among those placed in order. Beginning with the two chief disciples, the four hundred and ninety-nine monks also went to their peaked-roof houses and were seated. One peaked-roof house was empty. All five hundred peaked-roof houses rose into the sky. The Teacher reached a mountain called Saccabandha and placed the peaked-roof houses in the sky. On that mountain was a holder of wrong views named Saccabandha; he lived, having reached the peak of gain and fame, causing the great populace to learn wrong views. And inside him, like a lamp inside a pot, the supporting condition for arahantship was burning. Seeing that, the Buddha thought, "I will teach him the Dhamma," and went and taught the Dhamma. At the end of the discourse, the ascetic attained arahantship. With the path itself his higher knowledges came. Having become an "ehi bhikkhu" monk, with bowl and robes created by psychic power, he entered the peaked-roof house. The Blessed One, together with the five hundred monks in the peaked-roof houses, went to the merchant village. Making the peaked-roof houses invisible, he entered the merchant village. The merchants offered a great donation to the Saṅgha of monks with the Buddha at its head and escorted the Teacher and his disciples to the Makuḷakārāma Monastery. The Teacher entered the circular hall. While the Teacher was allaying the fatigue from the meal, the great multitude, having eaten their morning meal, undertaken the Uposatha observances, and taking perfumes and flowers, returned to the monastery to hear the Dhamma. The Teacher taught the Dhamma. For the great multitude, a release from bonds arose, and there was a great "Buddha-uproar." For the sake of gathering the great multitude, the Teacher stayed there for some days, but at dawn, he would arise in the great perfumed chamber itself. Having stayed there for some days and having wandered for alms in the merchant village, he sent the Venerable Puṇṇa back, saying, "You stay right here," and went to the bank of the Nammadā River, which was on the way. The Nāga king of the Nammadā came out to meet the Teacher, led him into the nāga abode, and made offerings to the three jewels. The Teacher taught him the Dhamma and left the nāga abode. The Nāga king requested, "Venerable sir, please give me something to be venerated." The Blessed One showed him a footprint shrine on the bank of the Nammadā River. It is covered when the waves come and uncovered when the waves have gone. It came to receive great honor. Leaving there, the Teacher went to Saccabandha Mountain and told Saccabandha, "You have led the great populace down the path of woe. Stay right here, have them give up their doctrine, and establish them on the path to Nibbāna." He also requested something to be venerated. The Teacher showed him a footprint shrine on a solid-backed stone, like a seal on a lump of wet clay. Thus, in the Sunāparanta country itself, he established two Wheel-Jewel shrines to last until the disappearance of the Dispensation. In the seventh rains retreat, in Sāvatthī, at the foot of the Kaṇḍamba tree, while crushing the heretics, he performed the Twin Miracle. Then, having made his mother a personal witness for the devas who had come from ten thousand world-systems, he spent the rains retreat in the Tāvatiṃsa heaven, seated on the Paṇḍukambala stone at the foot of the Pāricchattaka tree, while teaching the Abhidhamma. In the eighth, he spent one rains retreat in the city of Saṃsumāragiri. In the ninth, he spent one rains retreat in Kosambī. At that time, six khattiyas—Bhaddiya, Kimila, Bhagu, Ānanda, Anuruddha, and Devadatta—went forth. The detailed account of them is found in the Dhammapada Commentary. In the tenth rains retreat, being attended by the elephant Pālileyyaka, he spent a comfortable rains retreat at the foot of an auspicious sal tree in a forest grove to the south of the village of Pālileyya. In the eleventh, he spent one rains retreat in the brahmin village of Nālandā. In the twelfth, he spent one rains retreat in Verañjā, experiencing the result of misconduct. It is said that this was because the unwholesome kamma done and accumulated by him and his companions through verbal misconduct in the time of the Blessed One Phussa came to fruition when the opportunity arose. Thus, although invited by the brahmin of Verañjā, not noticing it due to Māra's influence, for three months he ate only barley-rice from the measure of a pattha, which was provided as a regular allowance in the manner of horse-traders. This is told in the Apadāna.

“Phussassāhaṃ pāvacane, sāvake paribhāsissaṃ;

Yavaṃ khādatha bhuñjatha, māca bhuñjatha sālino.

In the dispensation of Phussa, I spoke disparagingly of the disciples: ‘Eat barley and consume it, but do not eat fine rice!’

Tena kammavipākena, temāsaṃ khāditaṃ yavaṃ;

Nimantito brāhmaṇena, verañjāyaṃ vasiṃ tadāti”.

By the result of that kamma, for three months I ate barley; invited by a brahmin, I then dwelt in Verañjā.

Tena vuttaṃ “dvādasame verañjāyaṃ ekavāsaṃ vasi duccaritassa vipākaṃ anubhavamāno”ti. Terasame jāliyapabbate ekavāsaṃ vasi. Catuddasame sāvatthiyaṃ anāthapiṇḍikassa ārāme jetavanavihāre paṭhamavāsaṃ vasitvā pannarasame vasse ñātijanasaṅgahatthaṃ kapilavatthuṃ gantvā ekameva vāsaṃ kappesi[M 0.0017]. Soḷasame vasse āḷavakayakkhadamanatthaṃ aggāḷavīnagare ekavāsaṃ vasi. Sattarasame vasse rājagahameva nivattitvā vāsaṃ kappesi. Aṭṭhārasame vasse jāliyapabbatameva nivattitvā vasi. Ekūnavīsatimepi vasse tattheva jāliyapabbatameva vasi. Vīsatime vasse rājagahassa avidūre bhīsanake bhesakalavanasaṇḍe vāsaṃ kappesī”ti imāni vīsati vassāni paṭhamabodhīti veditabbāni. Etasmiṃ kāle ehibhikkhūpasampadāyaeva upasampādesīti yebhuyyena bhagavantaṃyeva uddissa ācariyupajjhāyaṃ katvā ehibhikkhu bhāvamāpanne puññavantapuggaleyeva sandhāya vuttaṃ, aññesampi ariyabhāvamanāpannānaṃ sudinnādīnaṃ vā yamakapāṭihāriye pasīditvā āyasmato sāriputtattherassa santike pabbajitānaṃ pubbe kassapabuddhakāle vaggulibhūtapubbānaṃ pañcannaṃ bhikkhusatānaṃ therasseva santike pabbajitabhāvassa dissanato tena vuttaṃ “bhagavā hi paṭhamabodhiyaṃ etasmiṃ kāle ehi bhikkhūpasampadāya eva upasampādesī”ti. Ito paresu pana pañcavīsativassesu anāthapiṇḍikena kārite jetavanamahāvihāre ekūnavīsativassā vāsaṃ vasi. Visākhāya sattavīsati koṭidhanapariccāgena kārite pubbārāme chabbassāni vasi. Dvinnaṃ kulānaṃ guṇamahattaṃ paṭicca sāvatthiyaṃ nissāya pañcavīsati vassāni vāsaṃ vasi. Antime pana vasse beḷuvagāme vasitvā maraṇantikā vedanā uppajji, āyusaṅkhārossajjanañca tattha akāsi. Imāni idha nādhippetāni, bhagavato vassakkamajānanatthaṃyeva vuttanti.

Therefore it was said: ‘In the twelfth year, he stayed one rainy season in Verañjā, experiencing the result of misconduct.’ In the thirteenth year, he stayed one rainy season on Jāliya Mountain. In the fourteenth year, he spent his first rainy season at Jetavana Monastery in Anāthapiṇḍika’s Park in Sāvatthī, and in the fifteenth year, he went to Kapilavatthu to support his relatives and spent only one rainy season there. In the sixteenth year, he stayed one rainy season in the town of Aggāḷava to tame the yakkha Āḷavaka. In the seventeenth year, he returned to Rājagaha and spent the rainy season there. In the eighteenth year, he again returned to Jāliya Mountain and stayed there. In the nineteenth year, he also stayed on Jāliya Mountain. In the twentieth year, he spent the rainy season in the Bhesakalā forest grove near Bhīsanaka, not far from Rājagaha. These twenty years are to be understood as the first period after enlightenment. During this time, he ordained only through the ‘Come, monk’ (ehi bhikkhu) ordination. This was said generally with reference to those meritorious individuals who attained monkhood through the ‘Come, monk’ method, having made the Blessed One himself their teacher and preceptor. And since it is seen that others, such as Sudinna and his companions who had not yet attained the noble state, and the five hundred monks who, inspired by the Twin Miracle, went forth under the Venerable Sāriputta Thera—these having formerly been bat-like ascetics in the time of Kassapa Buddha—were ordained by the Elder, therefore it was said: ‘During the first period after enlightenment, the Blessed One gave ordination only through the “Come, monk” method.’ In the following twenty-five years, he spent nineteen rainy seasons in the great Jetavana Monastery built by Anāthapiṇḍika. He spent six years in the Pubbārāma built by Visākhā with a donation of twenty-seven crores of wealth. Considering the great virtues of these two families, he spent twenty-five years residing in dependence on Sāvatthī. In his final year, he stayed in the village of Beḷuva, where a deadly affliction arose, and there he relinquished the life-determination. These details are not the main subject here; they are mentioned only to clarify the sequence of the Blessed One’s rains-retreats.

Saraṇagamanūpasampadā nāma “buddhaṃ saraṇaṃ gacchāmi”tyādinā tikkhattuṃ vācaṃ bhinditvā vuttehi tīhi saraṇagamanehi upasampanno.

Ordination by going for refuge is the name for being ordained through the threefold refuge, declared by uttering three times the words, ‘I go to the Buddha for refuge,’ and so on.

Ovādapaṭiggahaṇūpasampadā nāma “tasmā tiha te kassapa evaṃ sikkhitabbaṃ tibbameva hirottappaṃ paccupaṭṭhitaṃ bhavissati theresu navesu majjhimesu cāti, evañhi te kassapa sikkhitabbaṃ tasmā tiha te kassapa evaṃ sikkhitabbaṃ yaṃ kiñci dhammaṃ sossāmi kusalūpasañhitaṃ sabbantaṃ aṭṭhiṃ katvā manasikatvā sabbameva cetaso samannāharitvā ohitasoto dhammaṃ sossāmīti, evañhi te kassapa sikkhitabbaṃ tasmā tiha te kassapa evaṃ sikkhitabbaṃ sātasahagataṃ me kāyagatā satiṃ na jahissatīti[M 0.0018], evañhi te kassapa sikkhitabbanti iminā ovādapaṭiggaṇena mahākassapattherassa anuññātaupasampadā nāma.

The ordination by acceptance of advice is the name for the ordination granted to the Elder Mahākassapa by means of this acceptance of advice: ‘Therefore, Kassapa, you should train yourself thus: “Keen shame and fear of wrongdoing shall be established in me towards the elders, the newly ordained, and those of middle standing.” Thus indeed, Kassapa, should you train yourself. Therefore, Kassapa, you should train yourself thus: “Whatever teaching I hear that is connected with the wholesome, I will grasp its essence, attend to it, comprehend it with my whole mind, and listen to the Dhamma with an attentive ear.” Thus indeed, Kassapa, should you train yourself. Therefore, Kassapa, you should train yourself thus: “Mindfulness directed to the body, accompanied by pleasure, shall not be abandoned by me.” Thus indeed, Kassapa, should you train yourself.’

Pañhābyākaraṇūpasampadā nāma sopākassa anuññātaupasampadā, bhagavā kira pubbārāme anucaṅkamanto sopākasāmaṇeraṃ “uddhumātakasaññāti vā sopāka rūpasaññāti vā ime dhammā nānatthā nānābyañjanā, udāhu ekatthā byañjanameva nāna”nti dasaasubhanissite pañhe pucchi. So te byākāsi. Bhagavā tassa sādhukāraṃ datvā “kativassosi tvaṃ sopākā”ti pucchi. “Sattavasso ahaṃ bhagavā”ti, “sopāka tvaṃ mama sabbaññutaññāṇena saddhiṃ saṃsanditvā pañhe byākāsī”ti āraddhacitto upasampadaṃ anujānāti, ayaṃ pañhābyākaraṇūpasampadā.

The ordination by answering questions is the name for the ordination granted to Sopāka. It is said that the Blessed One, while walking up and down in the Pubbārāma, asked the novice Sopāka questions based on the ten kinds of foulness: ‘Sopāka, these phenomena—such as the perception of a bloated corpse or the perception of form—are they different in meaning and different in phrasing, or are they one in meaning and only the phrasing is different?’ He answered them. The Blessed One, giving him his approval, asked, ‘How old are you, Sopāka?’ ‘I am seven years old, Blessed One.’ ‘Sopāka, you have answered these questions in correspondence with my omniscient knowledge.’ Then, with a pleased mind, he granted him ordination. This is the ordination by answering questions.

Aṭṭhavācikūpasampadā nāma bhikkhuniyā bhikkhunisaṅghato ñatticatutthena bhikkhusaṅghato ñatticatutthenāti imehi dvīhi kammehi upasampadā. Ñatticatutthakammūpasampadā nāma bhikkhūnaṃ etarahi upasampadā, ayameva idhādhippetā, sampattivipattivasena sambhavato. Kasmā hesa upasampanno nāmāti. Samaggena saṅghena ñatticatutthena kammena akuppena ṭhānārahena uparibhāvaṃ samāpanno pattoti upasampanno, seṭṭhabhāvaṃ pattotiattho tathā hi gihibhāvato sāmaṇerabhāvo seṭṭho nāma, tatopi upasampannabhāvoyeva seṭṭho. Tattha samaggena saṅghenāti sabbantimena pariyāyena pañcavaggakaraṇīye kamme yāvatikā bhikkhū kammappattā, tesaṃ āgatattā chandārahānaṃ chandassa āhaṭattā, sammukhībhūtānañca appaṭikkosanato etasmiṃ kamme samaggabhāvaṃ upagatena. Ñatticatutthenāti tīhi anusāvanāhi ekāya ca ñattiyā kattabbena. Ettha ca kiñcāpi ñatti sabbapaṭhamaṃ vuttā, tissannaṃ pana anusāvanānaṃ atthabyañjanabhedābhāvato atthabyañjanabhinnā ñatti tāsaṃ catutthanti katvā ñatticatutthanti vuccati. Kammenā ti dhammikena kammena, vinayakammenātiattho. Akuppenā ti vatthu ñatti anusāvana sīma parisasampatti sampannattā akopetabbataṃ appaṭikkositabbatañca upagatena. Ṭhānārahenā ti kāraṇārahena, satthusāsanārahenāti attho. Etehi kāraṇehi seṭṭhabhāvamāpannoti attho. Tattha sampattivipattivasena sambhavato ti vatthusampatti ñattisampatti anusāvanasampattisīmasampattiparisasampattivasena [M 0.0019] pañcahi sampattīhi upasampadakammassa sijjhanatoti attho. Tattha vatthu sampattināma paripuṇṇavīsativassabhāvo idāni purisattabhāvo ca. Unnavīsativassaṃ, antimavatthuajjhāpannapubbaṃ, paṇḍako, theyyasaṃvāsako, titthiyapakkantako, tiracchānagato, mātughātako, pitughātako, arahantaghātako, bhikkhunidūsako, saṅghabhedako, lohituppādako, ubhatobyañjanako, ti ime terasa puggalā upasampadāya avatthu. Tattha ūnavīsativassanti sāmaññena vuttepi mātukucchito nikkhamanato paṭṭhāya ekūnavīsaparipuṇṇo purisapuggalo upasampadāya vatthu, tato oraṃ na upasampadāya vatthu tatho hi ekasmiṃ saṃvacchare hemantagimhavassavasena tiṇṇaṃ utūnaṃ sambhavattā tayo utū honti, honti cettha.

The eightfold verbal act of higher ordination for a bhikkhunī is the ordination performed by both the bhikkhunī Saṅgha with a motion and three announcements and the bhikkhu Saṅgha with a motion and three announcements. The ordination with a motion and three announcements is the current ordination for bhikkhus, and this is what is intended here, as it can occur through success or failure. Why is he called 'one who is ordained'? Because he has attained the higher state through an unshakeable act, worthy of its position, performed by a harmonious Saṅgha with a motion and three announcements, he is called 'one who is ordained'. The meaning is that one has attained the superior status. For indeed, the state of a novice is superior to that of a householder, and the state of one who is fully ordained is even more superior. Here, 'by a harmonious Saṅgha' means: by a Saṅgha that has attained a state of harmony in this formal act—which, at a minimum, is to be performed by a chapter of five—because as many bhikkhus as are eligible for the act have arrived, the consent of those eligible to give consent has been brought, and those who are present do not object. 'With a motion and three announcements' means it is to be done with one motion and three proclamations. Here, although the motion is stated first, because the three proclamations do not differ in meaning or wording, the motion, being distinct in meaning and wording, is counted as the fourth of those; hence it is called 'a motion and three announcements.' 'By a formal act' means by a lawful act, that is, an act in accordance with the Vinaya. 'Unshakeable' means that because the requisites—the object, motion, proclamation, boundary, and assembly—are complete, it cannot be overturned or objected to. 'Worthy of its position' means worthy of the cause, worthy of the Teacher’s dispensation. The meaning is that one has attained the superior status through these reasons. Here, 'as it can occur through success or failure' means that the formal act of ordination succeeds through five kinds of completeness: completeness of the object, completeness of the motion, completeness of the proclamation, completeness of the boundary, and completeness of the assembly. Here, completeness of the object means being fully twenty years of age and presently being a male. The following thirteen individuals are not a valid basis for ordination: one under twenty years of age, one who has previously committed a final offense, a eunuch, one who lives in communion by theft, one who has gone over to another sect, an animal, a matricide, a patricide, a slayer of an Arahant, a defiler of a bhikkhunī, a schismatic, one who has caused a Buddha's blood to flow, and one with both male and female characteristics. Here, although 'under twenty years of age' is said generally, a male person who has completed nineteen years from leaving the mother’s womb is a valid basis for ordination, but one younger than that is not a valid basis for ordination. For indeed, in one year, due to the existence of the winter, summer, and rainy seasons, there are three seasons, and here there are...

“Kattikantikapakkhamhā, hemaṃ phagguṇapuṇṇamā;

Tassanti kapakkhamhā, gimhaṃ āsāḷhipuṇṇamā;

Vassakālaṃ tato sesaṃ, catuvīsatūposathā.

From the waning half of the month of Kattika, the cold season lasts until the full moon of Phagguṇa; from the waning half of the month of Citta, the hot season lasts until the full moon of Āsāḷha; the remainder is the rainy season. There are twenty-four Uposatha days.

Cātuddasī cha etesu, pakkhā tatiyasattamā;

Sesā pannarasī ñeyyā, aṭṭhārasa uposathāti”.

Of these, six are fourteenth-day Uposathas, occurring in the third and seventh fortnights. The remaining eighteen should be known as fifteenth-day Uposathas.

Ettha ca kattikassa kāḷapakkhapāṭipadato paṭṭhāya yāva phagguṇapuṇṇamā cattāro māsā hemautu nāma. Phagguṇassa kāḷapakkhapāṭipadato paṭṭhāya yāva āsāḷhīpuṇṇamā cattāro māsā gimhautu nāma. Asāḷhakāḷapakkhapāṭipadato paṭṭhāya yāva aparakattikapuṇṇamā cattāro māsā vassautu nāma. Evaṃ ekasmiṃ saṃvacchare tayo utū honti. Tattha ekasmiṃ utumhi pakkhassa tatiyasattamesu dve dve katvā cha catuddasikā, paṭhamadutiyacatutthapañcamachaṭṭhaaṭṭhamesu cha cha katvā aṭṭhārasa pannarasikāti evaṃ ekasaṃvacchare catuvīsati uposathā honti. Imesu catuvīsatuposathesu cātuddasiyā ekūnatiṃsa divasā hontīti katvā ekasaṃvacchare cha ūnā divasā honti. Iminā nayena ekūnavīsatime vasse chamāsādhikāni aṭṭhārasavassāni honti. Tasmiṃ mātukucchimhi nibbattakāle dasamāse pakkhipitvā cattāri māsādhikāni ekūnavīsavassāni paripuṇṇāni. Rājāno pana tiṇṇaṃ tiṇṇaṃ vassānaṃ accayena māsaṃ ākaḍḍhanti[M 0.0020], divasena saha māsampi, tasmā aṭṭhārasavasse yeva cha māsādhikāni honti. Taṃ heṭṭhācatumāsādhika ekūnavīsatime vasse pakkhipitvā ekamāsādhikāni vīsativassāni sabbaso paripuṇṇāni honti. Evaṃ ekūnavīsativassato paṭṭhāya gabbhavīso upasampadāya vatthūti veditabbo. Idāni purisattabhāvo ti bhikkhuniyā abhāvato purisapuggalova idāni vaṭṭatīti adhippeto tathā hi mātugāmassa pabbajitatthā pañcavassasatāni saddhammo tiṭṭheyya, paññattattā pana aparāni pañcavassasatāni ṭhassatīti evaṃ vassasahassameva paṭisambhidāppattasaddhammassa patiṭṭhitabhāvo vutto. Mātugāmā ca nāma duppaññā tibbakilesā lāmakā ca. Bhagavatā ca paṭhamabodhiyaṃ ādito paṭṭhāyameva mahāpajāpatiyā gotamiyā aṭṭhagarudhammapaṭiggahaṇena upasampadatte anuññātepi sāsanassa aciraṭṭhitikabhāvo vutto. Duppaññattā itthiyo sāsane ciraṃ na tiṭṭhanti, tibbakilesattā yathā paññatta sikkhāpadānurūpaṃ saṃvarampi rakkhituṃ na sakkonti. Lāmakattāpi tā aṭṭhasamāpattiṃ labhantāpi brāhmapārisajjaṃ nātikkamanti, evaṃ bhikkhuniyā abhāvato idāni purisapuggaloti vuttaṃ. Imasmiṃ ṭhāne ṭhatvā cattāri khettāni veditabbāni tathā hi mātugāmassa pabbajitattā vassasahassameva ṭhassatīti cetaṃ paṭisambhidāppattakhīṇāsavavasena vuttaṃ, tato paraṃ uttaripi sukkhavipassakakhīṇāsavavasena vassasahassaṃ, anāgāmivasena vassasahassaṃ, sakadāgāmivasena vassasahassaṃ, sotāpannavasena vassasahassanti evaṃ pañcavassasahassāni paṭivedhasaddhammo tiṭṭhati. Pariyattisaddhammopi tāniyeva na hi pariyattiyā asati paṭivedho atthi. Nāpi pariyattiyā sati paṭivedho na hoti. Liṅgaṃ pana pariyattiyā antarahitāya ciraṃ pavattissati, idaṃ khandhakabhāṇakānaṃ matena vuttanti veditabbaṃ. Dīghanikāyaṭṭhakathāyaṃ pana evaṃ vuttaṃ “paṭisambhidāppattehi vassasahassaṃ aṭṭhāsi, chaḷabhiññehi vassasahassaṃ, tevijjehi vassasahassaṃ, sukkhavipassakehi vassasahassaṃ, pātimokkhena vassasahassaṃ aṭṭhāsī”ti. Idampi dīghabhāṇakattherānaṃ matena vuttaṃ. Aṅguttaranikāyaṭṭhakathāyampi buddhānaṃ parinibbānato vassasahassameva paṭisambhidā nibbattetuṃ sakkonti, tato paraṃ cha abhiññā[M 0.0021], tato tāpi asakkontā tisso vijjā nibbattanti. Gacchantekāle tānipi nibbattetuṃ asakkontā sukkhavipassakā honti. Eteneva upāyena anāgāmino sakadāgimino, sotāpannāti vuttaṃ. Idampi aṅguttaranikāye vuttaṃ. Saṃyuttanikāyaṭṭhakathāyaṃ pana paṭhamabodhiyaṃ bhikkhū paṭisambhidāppattā. Atha kāle gacchante paṭisambhidā pāpuṇituṃ na sakkhiṃsu, chaḷabhiññā ahesuṃ. Tato chapi abhiññā pattuṃ asakkontā tisso vijjā pāpuṇiṃsu. Idāni kāle gacchante tisso vijjā pāpuṇituṃ asakkontā āsavakkhayaṃ pāpuṇissanti, tampi asakkontā anāgāmiphalaṃ, tampi asakkontā sakadāgāmiphalaṃ, tampi asakkontā sotāpattiphalaṃ. Gacchantekāle sotāpattiphalampi pattuṃ na sakkhissantī’ti vuttaṃ. Yasmā ce te ācariyā bhinnavādā, tasmā tesaṃ ācariyānaṃ bhāṇakānaṃ matameva buddhaghosācariyena tattha tattha likhitanti gahetabbaṃ, aññathā ācariyasseva pubbāparavirodhappasaṅgo siyā ācariyabuddhaghoseneva hi sīhaḷabhāsaṃ apanetvā māgadhabhāsāya sāṭṭhakathaṃ buddhavacanaṃ likhitaṃ, na aññena ācariyena tasmā aṭṭhakathāya vuttavacanameva pamāṇanti gahetabbaṃ. Nanu cattāropi nikāyaṭṭhakathāsaṅgītiṃ ārūḷhā, atha kasmā evaṃ bhinnāti. Saccaṃ, tathāpi kesañci therānaṃ vādappakāsanatthaṃ vuttaṃ. Na saṅgāhakattherānaṃ, tasmā aṭṭhakathāvacanameva pamāṇanti vuttaṃ.

Here, starting from the first day of the dark fortnight of Kattika until the full moon of Phagguna, four months are called the winter season. Starting from the first day of the dark fortnight of Phagguna until the full moon of Āsāḷhī, four months are called the summer season. Starting from the first day of the dark fortnight of Āsāḷhā until the full moon of the following Kattika, four months are called the rainy season. Thus, in one year, there are three seasons. Therein, in one season, the third and seventh fortnights have fourteenth-day observances, and the first, second, fourth, fifth, sixth, and eighth have fifteenth-day observances; thus, in one year, there are twenty-four Uposatha days. Among these twenty-four Uposatha days, since the months with a fourteenth-day observance have twenty-nine days, there are six days fewer in one year. By this method, in the nineteenth year, there are eighteen years and six additional months. Adding the ten months of gestation in the mother’s womb, nineteen years and four additional months are completed. Kings, however, add a month after every three years, and also a month along with a day; therefore, in eighteen years there are six additional months. Adding that to the previously mentioned nineteen years with four additional months, twenty years and one additional month are fully completed. Thus, it should be understood that from nineteen years onwards, the age of twenty including gestation is the basis for higher ordination. Now, 'the state of being a man' is intended to mean that only male individuals are currently suitable due to the absence of bhikkhunīs. For, because women went forth, the true Dhamma would have lasted for five hundred years; but because of the promulgation of the eight weighty rules, it will last for another five hundred years. Thus, the establishment of the true Dhamma attained with analytical knowledge is said to be for one thousand years. And women are indeed of weak wisdom, with intense defilements, and are inferior. And by the Blessed One, from the very beginning at his first enlightenment, even though higher ordination was permitted through Mahāpajāpatī Gotamī’s acceptance of the eight weighty rules, the short duration of the Dispensation was stated. Due to their weak wisdom, women do not long remain in the Dispensation. Because of their intense defilements, they cannot even maintain the restraint in accordance with the precepts as promulgated. And because of being inferior, even if they attain the eight meditative attainments, they do not transcend the retinue of Brahmā. Thus, due to the absence of bhikkhunīs, it is said that now only male individuals are suitable. Standing in this context, four fields should be understood: for, because women went forth, the dispensation would have lasted one thousand years—this is said with reference to those arahants who have attained analytical knowledge and whose taints are extinguished. Beyond that, further, by way of dry-insight arahants, a thousand years; by way of non-returners, a thousand years; by way of once-returners, a thousand years; and by way of stream-enterers, a thousand years. Thus, the Dhamma of penetration lasts for five thousand years. The Dhamma of learning also lasts for the same duration, for indeed, when there is no learning, there is no penetration; nor is it that when there is learning, there is no penetration. However, the outward signs will persist for a long time even after the Dhamma of learning has disappeared. This should be understood as stated according to the opinion of the Khandhaka reciters. In the Dīghanikāya commentary, however, it is said: “It lasted a thousand years with those who attained analytical knowledge; a thousand years with those with the six supernormal knowledges; a thousand years with those with the threefold knowledge; a thousand years with dry-insight practitioners; and a thousand years with those who uphold the Pātimokkha.” This too is said according to the opinion of the Dīgha reciters. In the Aṅguttaranikāya commentary too, it is said that for a thousand years after the Buddha’s Parinibbāna, they are able to produce analytical knowledge. After that, the six supernormal knowledges; then, those unable to attain even those develop the threefold knowledge. As time passes, those unable to develop even those become dry-insight practitioners. By this same method, non-returners, once-returners, and stream-enterers are mentioned. This is also stated in the Aṅguttaranikāya. In the Saṃyuttanikāya commentary, however, at the first enlightenment, bhikkhus attained analytical knowledge. Then, as time passed, they were unable to attain analytical knowledge and became possessors of the six supernormal knowledges. Then, unable to attain even the six supernormal knowledges, they attained the threefold knowledge. Now, as time passes, unable to attain the threefold knowledge, they will attain the destruction of taints. If unable to do that, they will attain the fruit of non-returning; if not that, the fruit of once-returning; if not that, the fruit of stream-entry. As time passes further, they will not even be able to attain the fruit of stream-entry. And since those teachers hold differing views, therefore, the opinion of those teachers, the reciters, as written here and there by the venerable Buddhaghosa, should be taken as authoritative. Otherwise, there would be the fault of contradiction between earlier and later statements of the teacher himself. For indeed, it was by the venerable Buddhaghosa himself that the Sinhalese language was removed and the Buddha’s word with its commentaries was written in the Māgadha language, not by any other teacher. Therefore, the statements found in the commentaries should be taken as authoritative. But were not all four Nikāya commentaries included in the council? Why then are they so different? True, but it was stated for the purpose of proclaiming the views of some elders, not of the elders who compiled the texts. Hence, the commentary statements are to be taken as authoritative.

Antimavatthuajjhāpannapubbanti pārājikabhikkhu, so abhabbo sāsane upasampadakammassa laddhuṃ.

One who has previously fallen into the last offense is a monk who has committed a pārājika offense; he is incapable of receiving the act of higher ordination in the Dispensation.

Paṇḍakoticetta pañcavidho hoti āsittapaṇḍako, ussūyapaṇḍako, opakkamikapaṇḍako, napuṃsako, pakkhapaṇḍakoti. Tattha yassa paresaṃ aṅgajātaṃ mukhena gaṇhitvā asucinā āsittassa pariḷāho vūpasamati, ayaṃ āsittapaṇḍako nāma. Yassa paresaṃ ajjhācāraṃ passato ussūyāya uppannāya pariḷāho vūpasamati, ayaṃ ussūyapaṇḍako nāma. Yassa upakkamena bījāni apanītāni honti, ayaṃ opakkamikapaṇḍako nāma. Yo pana paṭisandhiyaṃ yeva abhāvako uppanno hoti, ayaṃ napuṃsako nāma. Ekacco pana akusalavipākānubhāvena kāḷapakkhe paṇḍako hoti, juṇhapakkhe panassa pariḷāhovūpasamati[M 0.0022], ayaṃ pakkhapaṇḍako nāma, keci pana yo kāḷapakkhe itthī hoti, juṇhapakkhe puriso, ayaṃ pakkhapaṇḍakoti vadanti, taṃ tesaṃ matimattameva. Etesu āsittapaṇḍakassa ca ussūyapaṇḍakassa ca pabbajjā na vāritā. Opakkamikanapuṃsakapakkhapaṇḍakānaṃ pana vāritā. Tesupi pakkhapaṇḍakassa yasmiṃ pakkhe paṇḍako hoti, tasmiṃyeva pabbajjā vāritā. Ettha ca apaṇḍakapakkhe pabbajitvā paṇḍakapakkhe nāsetabboti tīsupi gaṇṭhipadesu vuttaṃ. Keci pana “apaṇḍakapakkhepabbajito sato kilesakkhayaṃ pāpuṇāti, na nāsetabbo”ti vadanti, taṃ tesaṃ matimattameva, paṇḍakassa kilesakkhayā sambhavato khīṇakilesassa ca paṇḍakabhāvānupapattito ahetuka paṭisandhikathāyañhi avisesena paṇḍakassa ahetukapaṭisandhi katā vuttā, āsitta ussūyapakkhapaṇḍakānañca paṭisandhito paṭṭhāyeva paṇḍakabhāvo, na pavattiyaṃyevāti vadanti. Teneva ahetukapaṭisandhiniddese jaccandha-jaccabadhirādayo viya paṇḍako jātisaddena visesetvā niddiṭṭho. Catutthapārājikasaṃvaṇṇanāyaṃ pana abhabbapuggale dassentena paṇḍakatiracchānagataubhato byañjanakā tayo vatthuvipannā ahetukapaṭisandhikā, tesaṃ saggo avāritoti avisesena vuttaṃ, evaṃ pañcavidhā paṇḍakā te paṇḍakasāmaññena avisesetvā “paṇḍakāti evameva vatvā paṇḍako bhikkhave na upasampādetabbo”ti vuttanti daṭṭhabbaṃ.

A paṇḍaka, it is said, is of five kinds: the āsitta-paṇḍaka, the ussūya-paṇḍaka, the opakkamika-paṇḍaka, the napuṃsaka, and the pakkha-paṇḍaka. Herein, one whose burning sensation is relieved when he takes the genital organ of another into his mouth and is sprinkled with impurity—this is called an āsitta-paṇḍaka. One whose burning sensation is relieved when, seeing the misconduct of others, envy arises in him—this is called an ussūya-paṇḍaka. One whose generative seeds have been removed by means of an operation—this is called an opakkamika-paṇḍaka. One who is born without generative faculty from the moment of rebirth-linking—this is called a napuṃsaka. Another, however, due to the influence of unwholesome kamma-result, is a paṇḍaka during the dark fortnight, but his burning sensation is relieved during the bright fortnight—this is called a pakkha-paṇḍaka. Some, however, say that one who is a woman during the dark fortnight and a man during the bright fortnight is called a pakkha-paṇḍaka—that is merely their opinion. Among these, the going forth is not prohibited for the āsitta-paṇḍaka and the ussūya-paṇḍaka. However, it is prohibited for the opakkamika-paṇḍaka, the napuṃsaka, and the pakkha-paṇḍaka. Even among these, for the pakkha-paṇḍaka, ordination is prohibited only during the fortnight in which he is a paṇḍaka. Here, it is said in all three knotty passages that one who has gone forth during the non-paṇḍaka fortnight should be expelled during the paṇḍaka fortnight. Some, however, say, “One who, having gone forth during the non-paṇḍaka fortnight, attains the destruction of defilements should not be expelled”—that is merely their opinion, for the destruction of defilements is impossible for a paṇḍaka, and the state of a paṇḍaka is not possible for one whose defilements are destroyed. Indeed, in the discussion of rootless rebirth-linking, it is said that the paṇḍaka has a rootless rebirth-linking without distinction. And they say that for the āsitta-paṇḍaka, ussūya-paṇḍaka, and pakkha-paṇḍaka, the state of being a paṇḍaka exists from the moment of rebirth-linking, not merely in the course of existence.
