Therefore, in the exposition of rootless rebirth-linking, the paṇḍaka is specified by the word ‘birth’ (jāti), like the congenitally blind and congenitally deaf, etc. However, in the commentary on the fourth pārājika, when showing incapable persons, it is said without distinction that the paṇḍaka, the animal, and the one with both sexual characteristics—these three having defective substance and rootless rebirth-linking—are not barred from heaven. Thus, it should be understood that these five kinds of paṇḍakas are mentioned without distinction by the general term ‘paṇḍaka,’ saying, “A paṇḍaka, monks, should not be given higher ordination.”

Theyyasaṃvāsako ti yo sayameva pabbajjāliṅgaṃ gahetvā bhikkhuvassaṃ gaṇetvā bhikkhūti paṭiññaṃ sampaṭicchati, yathāvuḍḍhaṃ vandanaṃ sādiyati, so liṅgassa saṃvāsassa ca thenattā ubhayatthenako nāma. Yo pana yathāvuḍḍhaṃ vandanaṃ na sādiyati, asuddhacittavasena liṅgassathenattāliṅgatthenako nāma. Yo vā pana sāmaṇerabhūmiyaṃ ṭhatvā musāvādena bhikkhuti paṭiññaṃ gahetvā bhikkhuvassaggena vandanādiṃ sādiyati, so bhikkhūhi dinnaliṅgattā liṅgatthenako na hoti, saṃvāsassa thenattā saṃvāsatthenako nāma. Ettha ca ubhayatthenakopi asuddhacittena liṅgatthenakeeva paviṭṭhoti veditabbo. Yopi sāmaṇero vuḍḍhasāmaṇerānaṃ vandanaṃ sādiyati, daharabhikkhūpi vuḍḍhabhikkhūnaṃ vandanaṃ sādiyati, sopi theyyasaṃvāsako nāpi hoti, athenattā. Yo vā pana bhikkhupārājikamāpannova bhikkhuliṅge [M 0.0023] ṭhito yāva paṭijānāti, tāva attheva tassa bhikkhubhāvo, na so anupasampannasaṅkhyaṃ gacchati, tathā hi so saṃvāsaṃ sādiyantopi theyyasaṃvāsako na hoti, so sahaseyyādiāpattimpi na janeti, omasavāde pācittiyañca na janeti teneva “asuddho hoti puggalo aññataraṃ pārājikaṃ dhammaṃ ajjhāpanno, tañce suddhadiṭṭhi samāno okāsaṃ kārāpetvā akkosādhippāyo vadati āpatti omasavādassā”ti omasavāde pācittiyaṃ vuttaṃ. Asati hi bhikkhubhāve dukkaṭaṃ vadeyya. Yo pana sāmaṇero sāmaṇeraliṅgeneva pāṇātipātādipañcavidhaṃ silavipattiṃ pāpuṇāti, sopi āgato liṅganāsanāya nāsetvā saraṇasīlaṃ dātabbaṃ, athenattā upasampadāya avatthubhāvampi na pāpuṇāti. Vikālabhojanādikaṃ karontassa pana “accayo maṃ bhante accāgamā”tyādinā saṅghamajjhe accayaṃ desāpetvā vā daṇḍakammaṃ vā katvā sīlaṃ dātabbameva. Titthiyapakkantako uttānoyeva.

A thief in communion is one who, having taken the outward signs of a monk by himself, counts the rains as a monk, and accepts the declaration of being a monk. He enjoys being venerated according to seniority—this is called a thief in both respects: in outward signs and in communion, because of the theft in both. But one who does not enjoy being venerated according to seniority is called a thief of the sign, on account of his impure mind. Or, one who, while on the level of a novice, falsely declares himself a monk and then enjoys veneration and the like with the seniority of a monk—he is not a thief of the sign, since the outward signs were given by monks, but is called a thief in communion, because of the theft of communion. Herein, it should be understood that even one who is a thief in both respects is included as a thief of the sign on account of his impure mind. Moreover, a novice who enjoys venerating senior novices, or a junior monk who enjoys venerating senior monks, is not a thief in communion, as there is no theft. Or, one who has committed a pārājika offense but remains in the outward signs of a monk—as long as he does not confess, his status as a monk remains. He does not fall into the category of an unordained person. Thus, even while enjoying communion, he is not a thief in communion. He does not incur offenses such as sharing a dwelling, nor does he incur a pācittiya for insulting speech. For this reason it is said regarding insulting speech: “If an impure person has committed some pārājika offense, and another of pure view, having been given an opportunity, speaks with intent to insult, there is an offense of insulting speech.” For if the monkhood were absent, he would be speaking a dukkaṭa. But if a novice, while still in the outward signs of a novice, commits one of the five kinds of moral failure, such as killing living beings, he, having come, should be expelled by the destruction of his sign, and the refuges and precepts should be given. Since there is no theft, he does not become an invalid basis for higher ordination. For one who eats at the wrong time and the like, the precepts should be given only after having him confess the transgression in the midst of the Saṅgha with words like, “Venerable sirs, a transgression has overcome me,” or after a disciplinary action has been carried out. The case of one who has gone over to the sectarians is self-evident.

Tiracchānagatoti yokoci amanussabhūto cattāro apāyikāpi upasampadāya avatthuyeva. Te paṭisandhiyā lāmakattā abhabbā sāsane pabbajituṃ. Yadi cattāro apāyikā upasampadāya avatthu bhaveyya, atha kasmā sese anapadisitvā tiracchānagatova avatthūti vuttanti. Sesānaṃ apākaṭavatthuttā, tathā hi sambuddhakāle tiracchānayoniyaṃ nibbatto eko nāgarājā nāgayoniyā aṭṭīyati harāyati jigucchati. Athakho tassa nāgassa etadahosi kenanukho ahaṃ upāyena nāgayoniyā parimucceyyaṃ khippañca manussattaṃ labheyyanti. Athakho tassa nāgassa etadahosi “ime kho samaṇāsakyaputtiyā dhammacārino samacārino brahmacārino saccavādino sīlavanto kalyāṇadhammā sace kho ahaṃ samaṇesu sakyaputtiyesu pabbajeyyaṃ, evāhaṃ nāgayoniyā parimucceyyaṃ khippañca manussattaṃ paṭilabheyya”nti. Atha kho so nāgo māṇavakavaṇṇena bhikkhū upasaṅkamitvā pabbajjaṃ yāci. Taṃ bhikkhū pabbājesuṃ upasampādesuṃ. Tena kho pana samayena so nāgo aññatarena bhikkhunā saddhiṃ paccantime vihāre paṭivasati. Atha kho so bhikkhu rattiyā paccūsasamayaṃ paccūṭṭhāya ajjhokāse [M 0.0024] caṅkamati. Atha kho so nāgo tassa bhikkhuno nikkhante vissaṭṭho niddaṃ okkamesi. Sabbo vihāro ahinā puṇṇo, vātapānehi bhogā nikkhantā honti. Athakho so bhikkhu “vihāraṃ pavisissāmī”ti kavāṭaṃ paṇāmento addasa sabbaṃ vihāraṃ ahināpuṇṇaṃ vātapānehi bhoge nikkhante. Disvāna bhīto vissaramakāsi. Bhikkhū upadhāvitvā taṃ bhikkhuṃ etadavocuṃ “kissa tvaṃ āvuso vissaramakāsī”ti. Ayaṃ āvuso sabbo vihāro ahinā puṇṇo vātapānehi bhogā nikkhantāti. Atha kho so nāgo tena saddena pabujjhitvā sake āsane nisīdi. Bhikkhū evamāhaṃsu “koci tvaṃ āvuso”ti. “Ahaṃ bhante nāgo”ti. “Kissa pana tvaṃ āvuso evarūpaṃ akāsī”ti. Atha kho so nāgo bhikkhūnaṃ etamatthaṃ ārocesī. Bhikkhū bhagavato etamatthaṃ ārocesuṃ. Atha kho bhagavā etasmiṃ nidāne etasmiṃ pakaraṇe bhikkhusaṅghaṃ sannipātāpetvā taṃ nāgaṃ etadavoca “tumhe khvattha nāgā avirūḷhidhammā imasmiṃ dhammavinaye, gaccha tvaṃ nāga, tattheva cātuddase pannarase aṭṭhamiyā ca pakkhassa uposathaṃ upavasa, evaṃ tvaṃ nāgayoniyā parimuccissasi khippañca manussattaṃ paṭilabhissasī”ti. Atha kho so nāgo “avirūḷhadhammo kirāhaṃ imasmiṃ dhammavinayeti dukkhī dummano assūni pavattayamāno vissaraṃ katvā pakkami. Atha kho bhagavā bhikkhū āmantesi “dve me bhikkhave paccayā nāgassa sabhāvapātukammāya. Yadā ca sajātiyā methunaṃ dhammaṃ paṭisevati, yadā ca vissaṭṭho niddaṃ okkamati. Ime kho bhikkhave dve paccayā nāgassa sabhāva pātukammāya. Tiracchānagato bhikkhave na upasampādetabbo, upasampanno nāsetabbo”ti evaṃ tiracchānasseva pākaṭavattuttāti. Ettha ca pavattiyaṃ abhiṇhaṃ sabhāvapātukammadassanavasena “dve paccayā”ti vuttaṃ. Nāgassa pana pañcasu kālesu sabhāvapātukammaṃ hoti paṭisandhikāle, tacapajahananakāle, sajātiyā methunakāle, vissaṭṭha niddokkamanakāle, cutikāleti. Nanu ca devāpi upasampadāya avatthuyeva, atha kasmā cattāro apāyikāva upasampadāya avatthūti vuttanti. Saccaṃ, tathāpi devānaṃ abhabbattā paṭivedhasāsanassa ṭhitattā te abhabbato visuṃ karaṇatthāya evaṃ vuttanti daṭṭhabbaṃ. Vakkhati hi samantapāsādikāya vinayaṭṭhakathāya mahāvagge “tiracchānagato bhikkhaveti [M 0.0025] ettha nāgo vā hotu supaṇṇamāṇavakādīnaṃ vā aññataro antamaso sakkaṃ devarājānaṃ upādāya yo koci amanussajātiyo sabbova imasmiṃ atthe tiracchānagatoti veditabbo”ti, tena “tiracchānagatoti yokoci amanussabhūto”ti vuttanti. Ettha cāha yadi nāgasupaṇṇā paṭisandhiyā lāmakā bhaveyya, atha kasmā vidhurajātake cattāro janā nāgasupaṇṇasakkadhanañcayakorabyesu bhavanesu taṃ tadeva ṭhānaṃ patthentā dānādīni puññāni karonti, tesu eko āyupariyosāne saputtadāro nāgabhavane nāgarājā hutvā nibbatti, eko supaṇṇabhavane simbalivimāne supaṇṇarājā hutvā nibbatti, eko tāvatiṃsabhavane sakko hutvā nibbatti, eko dhanañcayakorabyarañño aggamahesiyā kucchimhi nibbattīti catunnampi janānaṃ paṭisandhiyaṃ kusalakammeneva pavattabhāvo vutto yadi kusalakammena bhaveyya, “kusalā cetanā vipākānaṃ khandhānaṃ kaṭattā ca rūpānaṃ kammapaccayena paccayo”ti imissāpi paṭṭhānapāḷiyā ahetukānampi paṭisandhikkhaṇe kammapaccayabhāvo vutto bhaveyya, na panevaṃ vattabbaṃ atha kathañcidaṃ paccetabbanti. Vuccate, nikantiyā balavattā sattavidhamethunasaṃyogattā ca aparisuddhā tesaṃ kusalacetanā, aparisuddhāyeva sugatibhūmiyaṃ janetuṃ na sakkonti. Duggatibhūmiyaṃ pavattamānāpi kammā yūhanakāle taṃ taṃ bhūmipatthanā taṇhāsampayuttāpubbabhāgacetanā balena tesu tiracchānabhūmīsu nibbattanti, na aññena akusalena. Evañhi patthitapatthanā siddhāpi bhaveyya. Yadi aññena akusalakammena bhaveyya, icchitapatthanāpi asiddhāva bhaveyya. Nikantica nāmesā balavajhānasampayuttacetanāpi paṭibāhituṃ sakkā tathā hi gopakadevadattassa ācariyasamaṇo jhānabalena brahmabhūmīsu anibbattitvā jhānaṃ paṭibāhitvā aññakammena gandhabbadevesuyeva uppajjati “sīlavato hi bhikkhave cetopaṇidhi samijjhati visuddhattā”ti tesaṃ vitthāro dīghanikāye āgato, atthikehi tattha oloketabbo. Sattavidhamethunasaṃyogavasenāpi sattā dukkhato na muccanti vuttañhi bhagavatā “idha brāhmaṇa ekacco samaṇo vā brāhmaṇo vā sammā brahmacārī paṭijānamāno na heva kho mātugāmena saddhiṃ dvayaṃdvaya samāpattiṃ samāpajjati, api [M 0.0026] ca kho mātugāmassa ucchādanaṃ nhāpanaṃ parimaddanaṃ sambāhanaṃ sādiyati, so tadassādeti taṃ nikāmeti, tenacavittiṃ āpajjati. Idampikho brāhmaṇabrahmacariyassa khaṇḍampi chiddampi sabalampi kammāsampi. Ayaṃ vuccati brāhmaṇa aparisuddhaṃ brahmacariyaṃ carati. Saṃyutto methunasaṃyogena na parimuccati jātiyā jarāmaraṇena…pe… na parimuccati dukkhasmāti vadāmi. Punaca paraṃ brāhmaṇa idhekacco samaṇovā…pe… paṭijānamāno. Na heva kho mātugāmenasaddhiṃ dvayaṃdvayasamāpattiṃ samāpajjati, na pi mātugāmassa ucchādanaṃ…pe… sādiyati, api ca kho mātugāmena saddhiṃ saṃjagghati saṃkīḷati saṃkeḷāyati, so tadassādeti…pe… dukkhasmāti vadāmi. Punaca paraṃ brāhmaṇa idhekacco samaṇo vā…pe… na heva kho mātugāmena saddhiṃ samāpajjati. Napi mātugāmassa ucchādanaṃ sādiyati. Napi mātugāmena saddhiṃ saṃjagghati saṃkīḷati saṃkeḷāyati. Apica kho mātugāmassa cakkhuṃ upanijjhāyati pekkhati, so tadassādeti…pe… dukkhasmāti vadāmi. Punaca paraṃ brāhmaṇa idhekacco samaṇovā brāhmaṇo vā…pe… na heva kho mātugāmena, na mātugāmassa, napi mātugāmena pekkhati. Api ca kho mātugāmassa saddaṃ suṇāti tirokuṭṭaṃvā tiropākāraṃvā hasantiyā vā bhaṇantiyā vā gāyantiyā vā rodantiyā vā, so tadassādeti…pe… dukkhasmāti vadāmi. Punaca paraṃ brāhmaṇa idhekacco samaṇo vā brahmaṇo vā…pe… naheva kho mātugāmena, napi mātugāmassa, napi mātugāmena, napi mātugāmassa rodantiyā vā…pe… api ca kho yānitāni pubbe mātugāmena saddhiṃ hasitalapitakīḷitāni anussarati. So tadassā deti…pe… dukkhasmāti vadāmi. Punaca paraṃ brāhmaṇa idhekacco samaṇo vā brahmaṇo vā…pe… na heva kho mātugāmena, napi mātugāmassa…pe… na pi yāni tāni pubbe mātugāmena saddhiṃ hasitalapitakīḷitāni anussarati, api ca kho gahapatiṃ vā gahapatiputtaṃ vā pañcahi kāmaguṇehi sahitaṃ samaṅgībhūtaṃ paricārayamānaṃ, so tadassādeti…pe… dukkhasmāti vadāmi. Punaca paraṃ brāhmaṇa…pe… na heva kho mātugāmena…pe… napi anussarati gahapatiṃ vā gahapatiputtaṃ vā paricārayamānaṃ, api ca kho aññataraṃ devanikāyaṃ paṇidhāya brahmacariyaṃ carati imināhaṃ sīlena vā vatena vā tapenavā brahmacariyena vā devo vā bhavissāmi devaññataro vāti, so tadassādeti[M 0.0027], taṃ nikāmeti, tena ca vittiṃ āpajjati. Idaṃ kho brāhmaṇa brahmacariyassa khaṇḍampi chiddampi sabalampi kammāsampī”ti evaṃ lābhādihetukena bhedena ca sattavidhamethunasaṃyogena khaṇḍādibhāvo saṅgahitoti veditabbo.

An animal being, that is, any non-human being, even those in the four woeful states, is unfit for ordination. Due to the inferiority of their rebirth-linking, they are incapable of going forth in the Dispensation. If those in the four woeful states were unfit for ordination, why then, without mentioning the others, was only the animal being declared unfit? It is because the cases of the others are not evident. For instance, during the time of the Buddha, a certain nāga king, born in the animal realm, was oppressed, ashamed, and disgusted by his nāga existence. Then it occurred to that nāga: “By what means can I be freed from the nāga birth and quickly obtain human status?” Then it occurred to that nāga: “These ascetics, the Sakyan disciples, live righteously, live equitably, live the holy life, speak truth, are virtuous, and are of good qualities. If I were to go forth among the Sakyan ascetics, I would thus be freed from the nāga birth and quickly obtain human status.” So that nāga, in the guise of a young man, approached the monks and requested the going forth. The monks gave him the going forth and the full admission. At that time, that nāga was dwelling with another monk in a border monastery. Then that monk, having risen at dawn in the latter part of the night, was walking in the open air. Then that nāga, when that monk had gone out, relaxed and fell asleep. The entire dwelling was filled with a snake, and its coils had come out through the windows. Then that monk, intending to enter the dwelling, opened the door and saw the entire dwelling filled with a snake, its coils having come out through the windows. Seeing this, he screamed in terror. The monks rushed up and said to that monk, “Why did you scream, friend?” “Friend, this entire dwelling is filled with a snake, and its coils have come out through the windows.” Then that nāga, awakened by that sound, sat on his own seat. The monks said, “Who are you, friend?” “Venerables, I am a nāga.” “Why, friend, did you do such a thing?” Then that nāga reported this matter to the monks. The monks reported this matter to the Blessed One. Then the Blessed One, on this occasion, in this connection, convened the Sangha of bhikkhus and said to that nāga: “You nāgas are of a nature not to grow in this Dhamma and Discipline. Go, nāga. There, observe the Uposatha on the fourteenth, fifteenth, and eighth days of the fortnight. Thus, you will be freed from the nāga realm and quickly obtain human status.” Then that nāga, thinking, “Indeed, I am of a nature not to grow in this Dhamma and Discipline,” unhappy and dejected, shedding tears, made a loud cry and departed. Then the Blessed One addressed the bhikkhus: “Monks, there are these two conditions for the manifestation of a nāga’s true nature: when he engages in sexual intercourse with his own kind, and when he is relaxed and falls asleep. Monks, these are the two conditions for the manifestation of a nāga’s true nature. Monks, an animal being should not be given full ordination; if ordained, he should be expelled.” Thus, the case of the animal being is evident. And here, in this account, it is said “two conditions” by way of frequently showing the manifestation of his true nature. A nāga’s true form appears at five times: at the time of rebirth-linking, at the time of shedding skin, at the time of sexual intercourse with his own kind, at the time of relaxed falling asleep, and at the time of death. Now, one might argue that even devas are unfit for ordination, so why are only those in the four woeful states mentioned as unfit for ordination? True, but it should be understood that they are mentioned thus for the purpose of distinguishing them from the other incapable ones, due to the devas' own incapability and the established nature of the teaching of penetration. Indeed, it will be said in the Mahāvagga of the Samantapāsādikā Vinaya commentary: “‘Animal being’ here is to be understood as any non-human being whatsoever, be it a nāga, or one of the Supaṇṇa youths and so forth, even including Sakka, the king of devas.” Therefore, it is said, “An animal being, that is, any non-human being.” And here it is said: If nāgas and supaṇṇas were inferior by rebirth-linking, why then in the Vidhura Jātaka do four persons, aspiring for that very state in the realms of nāgas, supaṇṇas, Sakka, and Dhanañjaya Korabya, perform merits such as giving? Of them, one, at the end of his life, was reborn as a nāga king with his children and wives in a nāga realm; one was reborn as a supaṇṇa king in the Simbali mansion in the supaṇṇa realm; one was reborn as Sakka in the Tāvatiṃsa realm; and one was reborn in the womb of the chief queen of King Dhanañjaya Korabya. It is stated that the rebirth-linking of all four persons occurred only through wholesome kamma. If it were through wholesome kamma, then, as stated in this Paṭṭhāna text, “Wholesome volition is a condition by way of kamma for resultant aggregates and for kamma-produced material form,” the state of being conditioned by kamma would be stated even for rootless rebirth-linking moments. But it should not be said thus. How then is this to be believed? It is said: Due to the strength of craving (nikanti) and the sevenfold sexual bond, their wholesome volition is impure. Being impure, they are unable to generate rebirth in a happy destination. Although this kamma operates within the sphere of miserable destinations, at the time of accumulation, by the power of the preceding volition associated with craving for aspiration to those respective realms, it causes them to be reborn in those animal realms, not by any other unwholesome kamma. Thus, the desired aspiration would also be fulfilled. If it were by other unwholesome kamma, the desired aspiration would indeed not be fulfilled. Indeed, this craving (nikanti) is capable of obstructing even volition associated with powerful jhāna. For example, the ascetic teacher of Gopaka Devadatta, despite his jhāna power, was not reborn in the Brahmā realms, but having obstructed his jhāna, was reborn only among the gandhabba devas by other kamma. As it is said, “Indeed, monks, the mental aspiration of one who is virtuous succeeds due to its purity.” The detailed account of this is found in the Dīgha Nikāya; those interested should look there. Even by way of the sevenfold sexual bond, beings are not freed from suffering. For it was said by the Blessed One: “Here, brahmin, some ascetic or brahmin, claiming to live the pure holy life, does not indeed engage in two-by-two intimacy with women, yet he enjoys the rubbing, bathing, massaging, and pressing of women. He delights in that, is pleased by that, and comes to satisfaction through that. This, brahmin, is a broken, perforated, spotted, and variegated holy life. This, brahmin, is called practicing an impure holy life. Bound by the sexual bond, he is not freed from birth, aging, and death… I say he is not freed from suffering. Again, brahmin, some ascetic or brahmin… claiming to live the pure holy life, does not indeed engage in two-by-two intimacy with women, nor does he enjoy the rubbing… of women, yet he laughs, plays, and sports with women. He delights in that… I say he is not freed from suffering. Again, brahmin, some ascetic or brahmin… does not indeed engage in two-by-two intimacy with women, nor does he enjoy the rubbing… nor does he laugh, play, and sport with women, yet he gazes intently at women’s eyes. He delights in that… I say he is not freed from suffering. Again, brahmin, some ascetic or brahmin… does not indeed engage in two-by-two intimacy with women, nor does he enjoy… nor does he gaze intently… yet he hears the sound of women from behind a wall or a fence, whether they are laughing, speaking, singing, or weeping. He delights in that… I say he is not freed from suffering. Again, brahmin, some ascetic or brahmin… does not indeed engage in two-by-two intimacy with women, nor does he enjoy… nor does he gaze intently… nor does he hear the sound of women… yet he recollects past laughter, speech, and play with women. He delights in that… I say he is not freed from suffering. Again, brahmin, some ascetic or brahmin… does not indeed engage in two-by-two intimacy with women, nor does he enjoy… nor does he gaze intently… nor does he hear the sound of women… nor does he recollect past laughter, speech, and play with women, yet he sees a householder or a householder’s son enjoying and endowed with the five cords of sensual pleasure. He delights in that… I say he is not freed from suffering. Again, brahmin… does not indeed engage in two-by-two intimacy with women… nor does he recollect past laughter, speech, and play with women, nor does he see a householder or a householder’s son enjoying and endowed with the five cords of sensual pleasure, yet he practices the holy life with the aspiration for a certain class of devas, thinking: “By this virtue, or by this vow, or by this ascetic practice, or by this holy life, I shall become a deva or one of the devas.” He delights in that, is pleased by that, and comes to satisfaction through that. This, brahmin, is a broken, perforated, spotted, and variegated holy life.’ Thus, it should be understood that the state of being broken and so forth is included by the sevenfold sexual bond and by the distinction due to reasons such as gain.

Mātughātake pana yena manussitthibhūtāpi ajanikā posā vanikā mātā vā mahāmātā vā cūḷamātā vā janikāpi amanussitthibhūtā mātā ghātitā, tassa pabbajjā na vāritā, na ca anantariko hoti. Tiracchānitthiyopi manussapurisamāgamma manussabhūtaṃ puttaṃ vijāyanti kontaputtā dvebhātikattherā viya tepi hi aṇḍajāyeva, tathā hi petaloke tiracchāne manusse cāti imesu tīsu bhūmīsu catasso yoniyo sambhavanti. Manussesu panettha kecideva opapātikā honti, seyyathāpi mahāpadumakumārādayo. Aṇḍajāpi konta puttā dvebhātikattherā viya. Vitthāro pana sīhaḷavatthusmiṃ oloketabbo. Ettha ca kontāti kinnarī, tassā puttā kontaputtā, sā hi rañño asokadhammarājassa kāle vanacarakena labhitvā manussasaṃvāsamāgamma dve putte vijāyitvā “idāni dārake pahāya na gamissatī”ti saññāya vanacarako tassā potthaṃ adāsi, sā potthaṃ labhitvā pakkhanditvā sakaṭṭhānameva gatā kinnariyo hi potthaṃ vinā pakkhandituṃ na sakkonti, teneva tāpaso “mā potthaṃ assā adāsī”ti āha. Kammapaccaya utusamuṭṭhānañhi tāsaṃ potthaṃ, rañño cakkavattissa cakkaratanaṃ viya, tathā hi cakkavattino vehāsagamanādi puññavato iddhināma, na tena cakkaratanaṃ vā hatthiassaratanaṃ vā vinā attano sabhāveneva gantuṃ sakkonti. Tattha cakkaratanaṃ rañño cakkavattissa kammabalena nibbattattā samuddamajjhe jāyamānampi kammapaccayautusamuṭṭhānaṃ nāma. Hatthiassaratanāni pana kammapaccayakammasamuṭṭhānāni nāma. Pakkhīnaṃ vehāsagamanādikā kammavipākajā iddhi. Kinnariyo pana potthaṃ vinā attano sabhāvena pakkhandituṃ na sakkonti, tasmā tāsaṃ potthaṃ kammapaccayautusamuṭṭhānanti daṭṭhabbaṃ. Keci pana devānaṃ alaṅkārā viya paṭisandhiyā saha āgatoti vadanti, na taṃ paccetabbaṃ, aṇḍajajalābujānaṃ tathā asambhavato. Kecipi pavatti kāle katanti vadanti, tampi ayuttameva, saṃsedajāpi padumagabbhevā veḷugabbhe [M 0.0028] vā nibbattā pokkharasātibrāhmaṇapadumavatīveḷuvatīdevīādayo viya. Jalābujā pana pākaṭāyeva. Yena sayaṃ tiracchānabhūtena manussitthibhūtā mātā ghātitā, sopi ānantariko na hoti tiracchānagatattā panassa pabbajjā paṭikkhittā, kammaṃ panassa bhāriyaṃ hoti, ānantariyaṃ āhacca tiṭṭhati. Manussitthiyopi tiracchānamāgamma gabbhaṃ janenti, seyyathāpi bhūridattassa mātā itthiyo hi aṭṭhahi kāraṇehi gabbhaggahaṇaṃ hoti - ajjhācārena, kāyasaṃsaggena, coḷaggahaṇena, asucipānena, nābhiparāmasanena, rūpadassanena, saddena gandhenāti. Tattha ajjhācārena sudinnassa purāṇadutiyikā gabbhaṃ gaṇhitvā pacchimabhavikaṃ suvaṇṇabimbasadisaṃ bījakaṃ nāma puttaṃ vijāyi. Kadāci ekaccā utusamaye chandarāgarattā purisānaṃ hatthagāhaveṇigāhaaṅgapaccaṅgaparāmasanaṃ sādiyantiyopi gabbhaṃ gaṇhanti. Evaṃ kāyasaṃsaggena gabbhaggahaṇaṃ hoti. Udāyittherassa pana purāṇadutiyikā bhikkhunī taṃ asuciṃ ekadesaṃ mukhena aggahesi, ekadesaṃ coḷakeneva saddhiṃ aṅgajāte pakkhipi, sā tena gabbhaṃ gaṇhi. Evaṃ coḷaggahaṇena gabbhaggahaṇaṃ hoti. Migasiṅgatāpasassa mātā migī utusamaye tāpasassa passāvaṭṭhānaṃ āgantvā sasambhavaṃ pivi, sā tena gabbhaṃ gaṇhitvā migasiṅgaṃ nāma tāpasadārakaṃ vijāyi. Evaṃ asucipānena gabbhaggahaṇaṃ hoti. Sāmassa pana bodhisattassa mātā utusamaye nābhiparāmasanena gabbhaṃ gaṇhitvā sāmatāpasadārakaṃ vijāyi, evaṃ maṇḍabyassa ca caṇḍapajjo tassa ca mātā utusamaye nābhiparāmasanavasena, tattha ca diṭṭhamaṅgalikāya nābhiparāmasanena maṇḍabyassa nibbatti ahosi. Caṇḍapajjotassa mātu nābhiyaṃ vicchiko pharitvā gato, tena caṇḍapajjotassa nibbatti ahosi. Idhekaccā itthī utusamaye purisasaṃsaggaṃ alabhamānā chandarāgavasena antogehegatāva purisaṃ upanijjhāyati, rājorodho viya, sā tena gabbhaṃ gaṇhāti. Evaṃ rūpadassanena gabbhaggahaṇaṃ hoti. Balākāsu pana purisonāma natthi, tā utusamaye meghasaddaṃ sutvā gabbhaṃ gaṇhanti. Gāvīeva vā kadāci usabhagandhena gabbhaṃ gaṇhanti. Evaṃ gandhena gabbhaggahaṇaṃ hoti. Evaṃ aṭṭhahi kāraṇehi itthiyo gabbhaṃ gaṇhanti. Ettha ca pitusadisāyeva [M 0.0029] puttā honti, tasmā bhūridattādayo dhataraṭṭhaṃ nāgarājānaṃ paṭicca nāgāyeva honti. Caṇḍapajjotassa pana vicchikaṃ paṭicca manusso jāto, sambhavena ajātattā. Pitughātakepi eseva nayo.

Regarding the killer of one's mother: If a human woman who was not his birth mother—such as a foster mother, a merchant mother, a grandmother, or an aunt—or even a non-human female being who was his birth mother was killed, his ordination is not prohibited, nor does he become one who has committed an immediate offense. Even female animals, through union with a human male, may give birth to a human offspring, like Kontaputta and the two Bhātika Theras, who were indeed born from eggs. Similarly, in the realms of ghosts, animals, and humans, these three planes give rise to the four types of birth. Among humans here, some are spontaneously born, such as the great Padumakumāra and others. Those born from eggs, like Kontaputta and the two Bhātika Theras, are also examples. For further details, one should examine the Sinhalese narratives. Here, 'Kontā' refers to a kinnarī. Her sons are called Kontaputta. During the time of King Asokadhammarāja, a forest dweller captured her and, through living with her, she gave birth to two sons. Thinking, 'Now she will not abandon the children and leave,' the forest dweller gave her her wings. Upon receiving them, she flew away to her own abode, for kinnarīs cannot fly without their wings. That is why the ascetic said, 'Do not give her the wings!' The wings of kinnarīs arise from kamma as a condition and from the physical elements, just as the wheel-treasure of a universal monarch appears by the power of his merit. For indeed, the universal monarch's ability to travel through the air and so forth is a psychic power born of merit, but he cannot go by his own nature without that wheel-treasure or elephant-and-horse treasure. Therein, the wheel-treasure, though arising in the middle of the ocean due to the kammic power of the universal monarch, is called 'arising from kamma as a condition and from the physical elements.' The elephant and horse treasures, however, are called 'arising from kamma as a condition and from kamma.' The ability of birds to travel through the air and so forth is a psychic power born of kamma-result. Kinnarīs, however, cannot fly by their own nature without their wings, so their wings must be understood as arising from kamma as a condition and from the physical elements. Some say that their wings appear at the moment of rebirth, like the ornaments of deities, but this should not be accepted, as it is impossible for egg-born and womb-born beings. Others claim they are formed during the course of life, but that too is improper, as in the case of moisture-born beings like Pokkharasāti, the Brahmin, and the goddesses Padumavatī and Veḷuvatī, who were born in lotus calyxes or bamboo culms. Womb-born beings are clearly evident. If one, being an animal oneself, kills one's mother who was a human woman, one does not become one who has committed an immediate offense due to being in the animal realm, but one's ordination is prohibited. However, one's kamma is grave, and it stands close to an immediate offense. Human women may also conceive through union with animals, as in the case of Bhūridatta’s mother. Women conceive through eight causes: misconduct, physical contact, grasping of clothing, consuming impurities, touching the navel, seeing a form, hearing a sound, or smelling a scent. Therein, by misconduct, Sudinna’s former wife conceived and gave birth to a son named Bījaka, who was destined for his last existence and resembled a golden image. Sometimes, some women, being impassioned by desire during their fertile period, conceive even by accepting the grasping of a man's hand, hair, or the touching of limbs. Thus conception occurs through physical contact. The former wife of the Elder Udāyi, a bhikkhunī, took some of that impurity into her mouth, and put some into her private parts along with a piece of cloth; she conceived thereby. Thus conception occurs through grasping of clothing. The mother of the ascetic Migasiṅga, a doe, during her fertile period, came to the ascetic's place of urination and drank his semen. She conceived thereby, and gave birth to the ascetic’s son, Migasiṅga. Thus conception occurs through consuming impurities. The mother of the Bodhisatta Sāma, during her fertile period, conceived by the touching of the navel, and gave birth to the ascetic’s son Sāma. Similarly, the mothers of Maṇḍabya and Caṇḍapajjota conceived during their fertile period by way of touching the navel. In Maṇḍabya’s case, his birth occurred through the touching of the navel by his mother, who had seen an auspicious sight. For Caṇḍapajjota, a scorpion crawled over his mother’s navel, and thereby Caṇḍapajjota’s birth occurred. Here, some women, unable to obtain sexual intercourse with a man during their fertile period, merely gaze intently at a man from inside their house due to desire, like a royal harem woman; she conceives thereby. Thus conception occurs through seeing a form. Among Balākā cranes, there are no males; they conceive upon hearing the sound of thunder during their fertile period. Or cows may sometimes conceive by the scent of a bull. Thus conception occurs through smelling a scent. Thus women conceive through these eight causes. And here, the offspring are like their fathers. Therefore, Bhūridatta and others, being dependent on the Nāga king Dhataraṭṭha, were Nāgas themselves. Caṇḍapajjota, however, was born human due to the scorpion, not being born by way of generation. The same principle applies to the killer of one’s father.

Arahantaghātako pana amanussa arahantadevataṃ vā manussajātiyaṃ vā avasesaṃ ariyapuggalaṃ ghātetvā anantariyo na hoti, pabbajjāpissa na vāritā, kammaṃ pana balavaṃ hoti. Tiracchāno pana amanussaarahantampi ghātetvā ānantariko na hoti, kammaṃ pana bhāriyaṃ tiracchānagatattā panassa pabbajjā vāritā. Bhikkhunidūsakasaṅghabhedakāni pākaṭāyeva.

Regarding the killer of an Arahant: one who kills a non-human Arahant deity, or any other noble person in the human realm, does not become one who has committed an immediate offense, nor is his ordination prohibited, but the kamma is powerful. However, an animal, even if it kills a non-human Arahant, does not become one who has committed an immediate offense, but the kamma is grave; due to its animal existence, however, its ordination is prohibited. The cases of violating a bhikkhunī and causing schism in the Sangha are self-evident.

Lohituppādako pana yo tathāgatassa abhejjakāyatāya parūpakkamena cammaṃ chinditvā lohitaṃ paggharituṃ na sakkoti, sarīrassa pana antoyeva ekasmiṃ ṭhāne lohitaṃ samosarati, āghātena patubbanamānaṃ sañcitaṃ hoti, tassa bahu apuññaṃ pasavati, ānantariko ca hoti, pabbajjāpissa vāritā, seyyathāpi devadatto. Yo pana jīvako viya rogavūpasamanatthaṃ phāletvā pūtimaṃsañca lohitañca nīharitvā phāsuṃ karoti, ayaṃ lohituppādako na hoti, bahupuññaṃ pasavati brahmapuññaṃ pasavati, seyyathāpi jīvakokomārabhacco.

Regarding the producer of blood: whoever, due to the Tathāgata’s indivisible body, by means of an assault by another, is unable to make blood flow by cutting the skin, yet blood gathers inside the body in one place, manifesting due to impact and accumulated—such a person generates much demerit, becomes one who has committed an immediate offense, and his ordination is prohibited, like Devadatta. However, one who, like Jīvaka, cuts open for the alleviation of illness, removes putrid flesh and blood, and brings ease—such a person is not a producer of blood. They generate much merit, even Brahma-merit, like Jīvaka Komārabhacca.

Ubbhatobyañjanako pana itthi-purisavasena duvidho tattha itthiubhatobyañjanakassa itthinimittaṃ pākaṭaṃ, purisanimittaṃ paṭicchannaṃ. Purisaubhatobyañjanakassa purisanimittaṃ pākaṭaṃ, itthinimittaṃ paṭicchannaṃ. Itthiubhatobyañjanakassa itthīsu purisattaṃ karontassa itthinimittaṃ paṭicchannaṃ hoti, purisanimittaṃ pākaṭaṃ hoti. Purisaubhatobyañjanakassa purisānaṃ itthibhāvaṃ upagacchantassa purisanimittaṃ paṭicchannaṃ hoti, itthinimittaṃ pākaṭaṃ. Itthiubhatobyañjanako sayañca gabbhaṃ gaṇhāti, parañca gaṇhāpeti. Purisa ubhato byañjanako pana sayaṃ na gaṇhāti, paraṃ gaṇhāpeti. Idameva tesaṃ nānākaraṇaṃ. Ubhatobyañjananti cettha “byañjananti nimittaṃ. Ubhato byañjanaṃ assa atthīti viggahena “ubhatobyañja”nti vakkhatha, ekassa dve indriyāni honti, atha ekamevāti. Ekameva na dve, “yassa itthindriyaṃ uppajjati, tassa purisindriyaṃ uppajjatīti no. Yassa vā pana purisindriyaṃ [M 0.0030] uppajjati, tassa itthindriyaṃ uppajjatīti noti ekasmiṃ santāne indriya dvayassa paṭisevitattā. Tañca kho itthiubhatobyañjanakassa itthindriyaṃ, purisaubhatobyañjanakassa purisindriyamevāti. Yadi ekameva indriyaṃ, evaṃ ubhatobyañjanakabhāvo kathaṃ siyā. Indriyaṃ byañjanakāraṇanti vuttaṃ, tañca tassa natthīti. Vuccate na itthindriyaṃ purisabyañjanassa kāraṇaṃ, tathā purisindriyampi itthibyañjanakassa, kasmā sadā abhāvatoti, tathā hi itthi ubhatobyañjanakassa yadā itthiyā rattaṃ hoti, tadā purisabyañjanaṃ pākaṭaṃ hoti, itthibyañjanaṃ paṭicchannaṃ tathā purisaubhatobyañjanakassapi. Yadi tesaṃ indriyadvayaṃ byañjanānaṃ kāraṇaṃ bhaveyya, sadā byañjana dvayaṃ ekato tiṭṭheyya na pana tiṭṭhati, tasmā ekameva indriyanti niṭṭhametthāva gantabbaṃ. Byañjanakāraṇaṃ indriyaṃ pahāya rāgacittamevettha byañjanadvayassa kāraṇanti daṭṭhabbaṃ, tasmā itthiubhatobyañjanako sayampi gabbhaṃ gaṇhāti, parampi gaṇhāpeti, purisaubhatobyañjanako paraṃ gaṇhāpeti, sayaṃ pana na gaṇhāti, itthindriyasamaṅgisseva gabbhasambhavatoti. Kurundiyaṃ pana “yadi paṭisandhiyaṃ purisaliṅgaṃ, pavatte itthiliṅgaṃ nibbattati, yadi paṭisandhiyaṃ itthiliṅgaṃ, pavatte purisaliṅgaṃ nibbatī”ti paṭisandhiyaṃ liṅgasambhavo vutto, so ayuttova. Kasmā, pavattiyaṃyeva itthiliṅgādīni samuṭṭhahanti. Paṭisandhiyameva samuṭṭhāti, na liṅgādīni. Indriyameva liṅganti na sakkā vattuṃ, indriyaliṅgānaṃ bhinnasabhāvattā, yathā ca bīje sati rukkho sambhavati, nāsati, evameva indriye sati liṅgādīni sambhavanti bījasadisañhi indriyaṃ, rukkhasadisāni liṅgādīni evameva tesu puggalesu paṇḍakādayo ekādasapuggalā tasmiṃ yeva bhave niyāmaṃ okkamituṃ na arahanti, tasmā abhabbā nāma. Ūnavīsati antimavatthuajjhāpannapubbā dve upasampadāya avatthumattameva, upanissaye sati tasmiṃyeva bhave niyāmaṃ okkamituṃ arahanti, tasmā bhabbānāma honti.

A hermaphrodite (ubhatobyañjanaka) is of two kinds, female and male. For a female hermaphrodite, the female characteristic is evident, while the male characteristic is concealed. For a male hermaphrodite, the male characteristic is evident, while the female characteristic is concealed. When a female hermaphrodite acts as a male among females, her female characteristic is concealed, and her male characteristic is evident. When a male hermaphrodite assumes the state of a female among males, his male characteristic is concealed, and his female characteristic is evident. A female hermaphrodite can both conceive herself and cause another to conceive. A male hermaphrodite, however, cannot conceive himself but can cause another to conceive. This is the distinction between them. Here, regarding 'ubhatobyañjana,' 'byañjana' means 'characteristic.' The term 'ubhatobyañjana' is explained by the analysis: 'one who has both characteristics.' Some say there are two faculties for such a person, but in fact, there is only one. There is only one, not two. It is not the case that when the female faculty arises, the male faculty also arises, nor that when the male faculty arises, the female faculty also arises, because the two faculties are not found together in one continuum. Indeed, for a female hermaphrodite, it is the female faculty, and for a male hermaphrodite, it is the male faculty. If there is only one faculty, how can the state of being a hermaphrodite exist? It is said that the faculty is the cause of the characteristics, but that [dual characteristic] is not present in it. It is said that the female faculty is not the cause of the male characteristic, nor is the male faculty the cause of the female characteristic. Why? Because of their constant absence. For instance, when a female hermaphrodite is aroused by a female, then the male characteristic becomes evident, and the female characteristic is concealed. The same applies to a male hermaphrodite. If both faculties were the cause of the characteristics, both characteristics would always be present simultaneously, but this is not the case. Therefore, it must be concluded here that there is only one faculty. Abandoning the faculty as the cause of the characteristics, it should be understood that the mind influenced by lust is the cause of the dual characteristics here. Thus, a female hermaphrodite can both conceive herself and cause another to conceive, while a male hermaphrodite can only cause another to conceive but cannot conceive himself, because conception is possible only for one endowed with the female faculty. However, in the Kurundi commentary, it is stated: 'If at rebirth the male organ arises, then in the course of life the female organ manifests; if at rebirth the female organ arises, then in the course of life the male organ manifests.' This assertion of the arising of organs at rebirth is indeed incorrect. Why? Because female organs and so forth arise only in the course of life. At rebirth, only the faculty arises, not the organs. It cannot be said that the faculty itself is the organ, because the nature of the faculty and the organs are different. Just as a tree arises when there is a seed, but not without it, so too the organs and so forth arise when there is a faculty. For the faculty is like the seed, and the organs and so forth are like the tree. Similarly, among these individuals, the eleven types, including eunuchs, are not capable of attaining fixed destiny in that very existence. Therefore, they are called 'incapable.' The nineteen who have previously fallen into the ultimate offense are merely without a basis for higher ordination. But when there is a strong supporting condition, they are capable of attaining fixed destiny in that very existence. Therefore, they are called 'capable.'

Vatthusampattikathā niṭṭhitā.

The discourse on the accomplishment of the basis is concluded.

Ñattisampatti nāma.

This is called the accomplishment of the motion.

“Sithilaṃ dhanitañca dīgharassaṃ, garukaṃ lahukañca niggahitaṃ;

Sambandhaṃ vavatthitaṃ vimuttaṃ, dasadhā byañjanabuddhiyā pabhedo”ti.

“Loose and firm, long and short, heavy and light, and the nasal sound; connected, distinct, and detached—these are the ten distinctions in understanding consonants.”

Evaṃ [M 0.0031] vuttaṃ dasavidhaṃ byañjanaṃ apekkhitvā vā evaṃ asakkontenāpi sithiladhanita vimuttaniggahitavasena vuttāni byañjanāni akopetvā vā duruttaṃ vā akatvā vuttaṃ ñattisampatti nāma. Ñattivipatti nāma sabbaso ñattiṃ aṭṭhapetvā vā vatthusaṅghapuggalañattīnaṃ aparāmasanāni pacchā ñattiṭṭhapanañcāti ime tāva pañca ñattidosā. Tattha ayaṃ itthanāmoti upasampadāpekkhassa api akittanaṃ vatthuaparāmasanaṃ nāma. Suṇātu me bhante saṅghoti ettha suṇātu me bhanteti vatvā saṅghoti abhaṇanaṃ saṅghaaparāmasanaṃ nāma. Itthannāmassa upasampadāpekkhoti upajjhāyassa akittanaṃ puggalaaparāmasanaṃ nāma. Sabbena sabbaṃ ñattiyā anuccāraṇaṃ ñattiaparāmasanaṃ nāma. Paṭhamaṃ kammavācaṃ niṭṭhāpetvā esā ñattīti vatvā khamati saṅghassāti evaṃ ñatti kittanaṃ pacchā ñattiṭṭhapanaṃ nāma. Iti ime pañca ñattidosā ñattivipatti nāma. Imehi dosehi vimuttāya ñattiyā sampannaṃ ñattisampatti nāma.

The accomplishment of the motion is said to be when the tenfold consonant, as thus stated, is observed, or even if one is unable to do so, when the consonants are uttered without corrupting them, whether they are loose, firm, detached, or nasal, and without making any mispronunciation or incorrect utterance. The failure of the motion is when the motion is not established at all, or when there is a failure to refer to the basis, the Sangha, the individual, or the motion itself, or when the motion is established afterwards. These, then, are the five faults of the motion. Among these, not mentioning the name of the candidate for higher ordination, 'this one named so-and-so,' is called the fault of neglecting the basis. Here, in 'May the Sangha hear me, venerable sirs,' saying 'May the venerable sirs hear me' but not saying 'the Sangha' is called the fault of neglecting the Sangha. Not mentioning the preceptor of the candidate for higher ordination, saying 'for so-and-so, the candidate for higher ordination,' is called the fault of neglecting the individual. Not reciting the entire motion is called the fault of neglecting the motion. After completing the first formal act, to then announce the motion by saying, 'This is the motion,' and 'It is approved by the Sangha,' is called establishing the motion afterward. Thus, these five faults of the motion are called the failure of the motion. The accomplishment of the motion is a motion free from these faults.

Ñattisampattikathā niṭṭhitā.

The discourse on the accomplishment of the motion is concluded.

Anusāvanasampatti nāma vatthu saṅghapuggalānaṃ aparāmasanāni, sāvanāya hāpanaṃ, akāle sāvananti ime pañca anusāvanadose vajjetvā kathanaṃ anusāvanasampatti nāma. Tattha vatthādīnaṃ aparāmasanāni ñattiyaṃ vuttasadisāneva. Tīsu pana anusāvanāsu yattha katthaci etesaṃ aparāmasanaṃ aparāmasanameva. Sabbena sabbaṃ pana kammavācaṃ avatvā catukkhattuṃ ñattikittanameva vā kammavācabbhantarepi cattāri byañjanāni kopetvā vā akkharassa vā padassa vā duruccāraṇaṃ vā katvā sāvanaṃ sāvanāya hāpanaṃ nāma, tathā hi sithiladhanitavimuttaniggahitavasena cattāri byañjanāni antokammavācāya kammaṃ kopenti. Tattha “suṇātu me ti sithile vattabbe suṇāthu me”ti vacanaṃ sithile dhanitaṃ nāma. “Bhante”ti vattabbe bhanteti vacanaṃ dhanite sithilaṃ nāma. “Suṇātu esā ñattī”ti vattabbe “suṇantu esaṃ ñattī”ti vacanaṃ vimutte niggahitaṃ nāma. “Pattakalla”nti vattabbe “pattakallā”ti vacanaṃ niggahite vimuttaṃ nāma. Evaṃ imāni cattāri byañjanāni antokammavācāya kammaṃ dūsenti. Imāni cattāri akopetvā vadantenāpi aññasmiṃ akkhare [M 0.0032] vattabbe aññaṃ akkharaṃ vadanto duruttaṃ karoti nāma. Itaresu dīgharassādīsu chasu ṭhānesu dīghaṭṭhāne dīghaṃ, rassaṭṭhāne rassamevāti evaṃ yathāṭhāne taṃ tadeva akkharaṃ bhāsantena anukkamāgataṃ paveṇiṃ avināsetvā kammavācā kātabbā. Sace pana evaṃ akatvā dīghe vattabbe rassaṃ, rasse vattabbe dīghaṃ vadati, tathā garuke vattabbe lahukaṃ, lahuke vattabbe garukaṃ vadati. Sambandhe vā vattabbe vavatthitaṃ, vavatthite vā vattabbe sambandhaṃ vadati, evampi kammavācā na kuppati. Imāni cha byañjanāni kammaṃ na kopenti. Idameva ācariyānaṃ samānakathā. Apare pana idāni byañjanāni antokammavācāya kammaṃ dūsentīti vadanti, evaṃ sati anupasampannāva bahutarā bhaveyyuṃ, vīmaṃsitvā pana gahetabbaṃ. Suṭṭhutarā vā kammavācā sikkhitabbā. Sāvanāya anokāse paṭhamaṃ ñattiṃ aṭṭhapetvā anusāvanaṃ akāle sāvanaṃ nāma. Iti imehi dosehi vimuttāya anusāvanāya sampannaṃ anusāvanasampattisampannaṃ nāma. Yadi pattacīvararahitaṃ puggalaṃ upasampādeti, “paripuṇṇassa pattacīvara”nti anusāvanāya katattā kammakopo na hoti, kārakasaṅgho pana sātisāro tenevāha vimativinodaniyampi pattacīvarānaṃ abhāvepi’paripuṇṇassa pattacīvara’nti kammavācāya sāvitattā kammakopaṃ avatvā dukkaṭameva vuttanti. Sace anupajjhāyako upasampādeti, “itthannāmassa upasampadāpekkho, itthannāmena upajjhāyenā”ti vā upajjhāyaṃ sāveti, sūpasampannova hoti. Upajjhāyaṃ na sāveti nupasampanno. Vakkhati hi vimativinodaniyaṃ “kammaṃ na kuppatīti idaṃ upajjhāyābhāvepi itthannāmassa upasampadāpekkho itthannāmena upajjhāyenā”ti matassa vā vibbhamantassa vā purāṇaupajjhāyassa vā yassa kassaci avijjamānassāpi nāmena sabbattha upajjhāyakittanassa katattā vuttaṃ. Yadi hi upajjhāyakittanaṃ na kareyya,’puggalaṃ na parāmasatī’ti vuttakammavipattieva siyā, teneva pāḷiyaṃ “anupajjhāyakanti vutta”nti. Sace saṅghupajjhāyena gaṇupajjhāyena upasampādeti, kārakasaṅgho sātisāro, kammaṃ pana na kuppati, vakkhati hi vimativinodaniyaṃ “saṅghena upajjhāyenā”ti ayaṃ itthannāmo saṅghassa upasampadāpekkho, itthannāmo saṅghaṃ upasampadaṃ yācati. Saṅghena upajjhāyenā”ti evaṃ kammavācāya saṅghameva upajjhāyaṃ kittetvāti attho. Evaṃ’gaṇena upajjhāyenā’ti etthāpi ayaṃ itthannāmogaṇassa upasampadāpekkho’ti ādinā yojanā veditabbā. Evaṃ vuttepi kammaṃ na kuppati eva, dukkaṭasseva vuttattā, aññathā so ca puggalo anupasampannoti vadeyyā’ti. Sace paṇḍakādinā anupasampannakena upajjhāyena upasampādeti, te vajjetvā pañcavaggādigaṇo hatthapāsaṃ avijahitvā pūrati, kammaṃ na kuppati, kārakasaṅgho pana sātisāro tena vuttaṃ vimativinodaniyaṃ “paṇḍakādīhi upajjhāyehi kariyamānesu paṇḍakādikena vināva pañcavaggādigaṇo pūrati, kammaṃ na kuppati. Itarathā kuppatī”ti. Sace cattāro ācariyā ekato anusāventi, ettha pana kathanti vuccate, sabbaaṭṭhakathāsu dvīhi vā tīhi vā ācariyehi visuṃ visuṃ ekena ekassāti ekappahāreneva dve tisso vā kammavācā kātabbā. Sace pana nānācariyā nānupajjhāyā honti, tissatthero sumanattherassa saddhivihārikaṃ, sumanatthero tissattherassa saddhivihārikaṃ anusāveti, gaṇapūrakā honti vaṭṭati. Sace pana nānupajjhāyā honti, eko ācariyo hoti, natveva’nānupajjhāyenā’ti paṭikkhittattā na vaṭṭatīti ettakameva vuttaṃ. Catunnaṃ pana janānaṃ vicāraṇakathāya nāpi kammavipattichāyā dissati, tathāpi na kattabbameva, sabbaaṭṭhakathāsu avicāritattā. Sace vatthālaṅkārādisahitaparūḷhakesamassuṃ upasampādeti, sūpasampannova hoti, pacchā kesamassuṃ ohāretvā kāsāyāni vatthāni acchādetuṃ vaṭṭati terasavatthuvimuttattā sampatti yevetthapamāṇattā ca.

The excellence of proclamation is the declaration made by avoiding these five faults of proclamation: not grasping the subject, the Sangha, or the individuals; omission in the proclamation; and proclamation at the wrong time. This is called the excellence of proclamation. Here, the non-grasping of the subject, etc., is just as stated in the motion. But in the three proclamations, wherever this non-grasping occurs, it is indeed non-grasping. However, omission in the proclamation means not reciting the entire procedural text, or only reciting the motion four times, or even within the procedural text, corrupting four syllables, or mispronouncing a letter or a word, and then proclaiming. For thus, four syllables corrupt the act within the procedural text by way of being loose, firm, released, and constricted. Therein, when 'suṇātu me' should be pronounced loosely, pronouncing 'suṇāthu me' is called a firm sound in a loose place. When 'bhante' should be pronounced firmly, pronouncing 'bhante' is called a loose sound in a firm place. When 'suṇātu esā ñattī' should be pronounced released, pronouncing 'suṇantu esaṃ ñattī' is called a constricted sound in a released place. When 'pattakallaṃ' should be pronounced constricted, pronouncing 'pattakallā' is called a released sound in a constricted place. Thus, these four syllables corrupt the act within the procedural text. Even if one does not corrupt these four, but pronounces a different letter where another letter should be spoken, it is called making a mispronunciation. In the other six places, such as long and short, one should pronounce the same letter in its proper place, such as long in the place of long, and short in the place of short, thus performing the procedural text without destroying the traditional sequence. But if, having not done so, one pronounces a short where a long should be, or a long where a short should be; similarly, if one pronounces a light where a heavy should be, or a heavy where a light should be; or if one pronounces a disconnected where a connected should be, or a connected where a disconnected should be, even then the procedural text is not corrupted. These six characteristics do not corrupt the act. This is the common teaching of the teachers. Others, however, now say that these syllables corrupt the act within the procedural text. If this were so, the unordained would be more numerous. Therefore, one should examine and accept what is proper. Alternatively, the procedural text should be learned very well. Proclaiming in an unsuitable place for the proclamation, or making the proclamation without having first established the motion, is called proclamation at the wrong time. Thus, a proclamation endowed with the excellence of proclamation is called one that is accomplished by a proclamation free from these faults. If one ordains a person without bowl and robe, because it is stated in the proclamation, 'he is complete with bowl and robe,' the act is not corrupted, but the performing Sangha is at fault. Therefore, it is also said in the Vimativinodanī that even in the absence of bowl and robe, because it is proclaimed in the procedural text, 'he is complete with bowl and robe,' it is said to be merely a dukkaṭa offense, without the act being corrupted. If one ordains without a preceptor, but proclaims the preceptor, saying, 'So-and-so seeks ordination, with So-and-so as preceptor,' he is indeed well-ordained. If he does not proclaim the preceptor, he is not ordained. For the Vimativinodanī will state: 'This statement, "the act is not corrupted," is said because even in the absence of a preceptor, the naming of a preceptor is done everywhere with the name of someone, whether deceased, confused, or a former preceptor, or anyone else who is not present, by saying, "So-and-so seeks ordination, with So-and-so as preceptor." For if the naming of a preceptor were not done, it would be the same as the stated corruption of the act, "not grasping the individual." Therefore, it is said in the Pāli, "without a preceptor." If one ordains with the Sangha as preceptor or the group as preceptor, the performing Sangha is at fault, but the act is not corrupted. For the Vimativinodanī will state: 'When it is said, "with the Sangha as preceptor," the meaning is that by the procedural text, "This So-and-so seeks ordination from the Sangha; So-and-so requests ordination from the Sangha as preceptor," the Sangha itself is named as preceptor.' Similarly, in 'with the group as preceptor,' the explanation should be understood starting with 'This So-and-so seeks ordination from the group.' Even if it is stated thus, the act is not corrupted, because only a dukkaṭa offense is stated. Otherwise, that person would be said to be unordained. If one ordains with an unordained preceptor such as a eunuch, etc., but excluding them, if the group of five or more is complete without leaving arm's reach, the act is not corrupted, but the performing Sangha is at fault. Therefore, it is stated in the Vimativinodanī: 'If ordination is performed by preceptors such as eunuchs, etc., but the group of five or more is complete even without the eunuch, etc., the act is not corrupted. Otherwise, it is corrupted.' If four teachers proclaim together, here it is said how: In all commentaries, it is said that two or three procedural texts should be performed at once, by two or three teachers, each for one person, separately. If there are different teachers and different preceptors, such as Elder Tissa proclaiming for Elder Sumana’s pupil, and Elder Sumana proclaiming for Elder Tissa’s pupil, they complete the group, and it is valid. But if the preceptors are different and there is only one teacher, it is not valid because it is rejected as 'with different preceptors,' only this much is stated. However, regarding the discussion of four individuals, not even a shadow of a corrupted act is seen, yet it should not be done, as it is not discussed in all commentaries. If one ordains someone with overgrown hair and beard, adorned with clothes and ornaments, etc., he is indeed well-ordained. Afterwards, it is permissible to shave the hair and beard and wear saffron robes, because it is free from the thirteen matters, and excellence is indeed the standard here.

Anusāvanasampattikathā niṭṭhitā.

The Discussion on the Excellence of Proclamation is concluded.

Idāni sīmavisodhaniṃ nāma, nānāganthasamāhanti imissā gāthāya saṃvaṇṇanākkamo anuppatto.

Now, the turn for the commentary on this verse, 'a compilation of various texts', in the section named 'The Purification of Boundaries', has arrived.

Paññāva sabbañeyyesu, dayā yassa mahesino;

Anantesupi sattesu, pavattittha yathāruci.

Wisdom is supreme among all knowables, and compassion belongs to the Great Sage; even toward infinite beings, it arose according to his inclination.

Tāya ussāhito satthā, bhikkhūnaṃ hitakāraṇaṃ;

Yaṃ sīmaṃ anujānāti, tattha pañho pavattati.

Inspired by that, the Teacher, for the welfare of the bhikkhus, allows a boundary; there a question arises.

Dveḷhakā [M 0.0034] saṃsayā honti, dese sabbattha bhikkhavo;

Ganthāpi visamā honti, attanomatikāraṇā.

Twofold doubts and uncertainties arise, O bhikkhus, everywhere; texts, too, become inconsistent, due to personal opinions.

Yadi me īdisaṃ vādaṃ, sutvāvupekkhako bhave;

Pabbajjā nipphalā mayhaṃ, vare sambuddhasāsane;

Gambhīro nipuṇo attho, sabbaññujinagocaro.

If, upon hearing such a teaching from me, one were to remain indifferent, my going forth would be fruitless in the excellent Dispensation of the Perfectly Enlightened One. Profound and subtle is the meaning, the domain of the All-Knowing Conqueror.

Sāriputto mahāpañño, therassapi avisayo;

Kathaṃ tvaṃ sakkuṇeyyāsi, sabbaññujinagocare;

Iti me upavadeyyuṃ, dharateva mahāmuni.

Sāriputta is of great wisdom, a domain beyond even that Elder; how then could you comprehend the domain of the All-Knowing Conqueror? Thus they would disparage me, while the Great Sage is still present.

Pāliphullo mahāsālo, ucco merunagūpamo;

Parinibbānakālamhi, sambuddho iccamabravi.

Like a great sāl tree in full bloom, lofty like Mount Meru, at the time of final Nibbāna, the Perfectly Enlightened One spoke thus:

Mā tvaṃ ānanda rodasi, mā tvaṃ ānanda socasi;

Ahamekova nibbāyi, dharanteva bahū buddhā.

“Do not weep, Ānanda; do not grieve, Ānanda. Though I alone attain Nibbāna, many Buddhas are still present.”

Sambuddhā caturāsīti, sahassāni imāni te;

Ovadissanti tvaṃ bhikkhu, katapuññosi hohisi.

“These eighty-four thousand Perfectly Enlightened Ones will advise you, O bhikkhu: ‘You have performed meritorious deeds; you shall be accomplished.’”

Vare sāṭṭhakathe pāḷi, ṭhite sabbaññugocare;

Nibbutopi sambuddho, asuññova pajaṃ imaṃ.

While the excellent Pāli with its commentary stands in the domain of the Omniscient One, though the Perfectly Enlightened One is extinguished, he does not leave this world empty.

Evāhaṃ cintayitvāna, rattidivaṃ atandito;

Dharanteyeva sambuddhe, bhinnavādo avisayo.

Reflecting thus, day and night, untiring, while the Perfectly Enlightened One was still present, I was a dissenter, outside the fold.

Taṃ vādaṃ bhindayitvāna, jahitvā vattanomatiṃ;

Vissajjissamahaṃ dāni, ciraṃ saddhammasuddhiyāti.

Having shattered that argument and abandoned my habitual way of thinking, I will now relinquish it for the long-lasting purity of the true Dhamma.

Tattha sīmavisodhani'nti sīmavipattijahanaṃ sīmavipattiṃ pahāya sampattilakkhaṇappakāsakaṃ pakaraṇaṃ karissanti attho. Tattha sīmāti pannarasavidhā sīmā. Katame pannarasa. Khaṇḍasīmā, upacārasīmā, samānasaṃvāsasīmā, avippavāsasīmā, lābhasīmā, gāmasīmā, nigamasīmā, nagarasīmā, abbhantarasīmā, udakukkhepasīmā, janapadasīmā, raṭṭhasīmā, rajjasīmā, dīpasīmā, cakkavāḷasīmā’ti. Tattha khaṇḍasīmā nāma pabbajjupasampadādīnaṃ saṅghakammānaṃ sukhakaraṇatthaṃ mahāsīmāya vā gāmakhette vā khaṇḍitvā paricchinditvā sammatā. Tassāvitthāro ''paṭhamaṃ nimittā kittetabbā''ti ādinā dvāsattatippabhedāya samantapāsādikāya vinayaṭṭhakathāya mahāvagge vutto, idha vuttopi tatheva vutto bhaveyya, tasmā na [M 0.0035] vakkhāma, duviññeyyaṭṭhānameva kiñciṭhānaṃ nīharitvā pākaṭaṃ karissāma. Tattha sace pana heṭṭhā uparimassa sīmāparicchedassa parato anto leṇaṃ hoti, bahi sīmā na otarati. Athāpi uparimassa sīmāparicchedassa orato bahi leṇaṃ hoti, antosīmā na otaratīti ime dve ekatthā byañjanameva nānaṃ. Ayañhetthatthouparimassa sīmāparicchedassa orimabhāgato heṭṭhā pabbatapāde umaṅgasaṇṭhānena pabbatapasse vijjhitvā leṇaṃ hoti, sīmāparicchedassa parato leṇe ca leṇassa bahibhūte heṭṭhā pabbate ca bhūmibhāge sīmā na otarati, leṇassa upariyeva sīmā hotīti. Tassa uparimassa sīmāparicchedassa orimabhāgato bahibhūte heṭṭhā pabbatapāde umaṅgasaṇṭhānena vijjhitvā pabbatapasse leṇaṃ athāpi hoti, leṇe ca leṇassa parabhūte heṭṭhā pabbate ca bhūmibhāge sīmā na otarati, leṇassa uparibhāge va sīmā hotīti. Esā ca vicāraṇā vimativinodaniyampi katā. Anto ti pabbatassa anto, pabbatamūleti attho. Tameva antosaddaṃ sīmāparicchedena visesetuṃ “uparimassa sīmāparicchedassa parato”ti vuttaṃ. Pabbatapādaṃ pana apekkhitvā “orato”ti vattabbepi sīmānissayaṃ pabbataggaṃ sandhāya “parato”ti vuttanti daṭṭhabbaṃ teneva bahileṇanti ettha bahisaddaṃ visesento “uparimassa sīmāparicchedassa orato”ti āha. Bahi sīmā na otaratīti ettha bahī ti pabbatapādeleṇaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Leṇassa bahibhūte upari sīmāparicchedassa heṭṭhābhāge sīmā na otaratīti attho. Antosīmāti leṇassa ca pabbatapādassa ca anto attano otaraṇārahaṭṭhāne na otaratīti attho. Bahi sīmā na otaratīti attho, sīmā na otaratī ti cettha attano otaraṇārahaṭṭhāne leṇabhāvena sīmā sabbathā “anotaraṇameva dassitanti gahetabbaṃ. Tattha pi anotaraṇanti uparieva sīmā hotī”ti vimativinodanī. Keci pana veḷususiro viya susirameva leṇanti vadanti, taṃ ayuttaṃ.

Here, 'sīmavisodhanī' means the purification of boundaries; that is, a treatise will be made clarifying the characteristics of a proper boundary by eliminating boundary defects. Here, 'sīmā' refers to fifteen types of boundaries. What are the fifteen? Khaṇḍasīmā (fragmented boundary), upacārasīmā (proximity boundary), samānasaṃvāsasīmā (shared residence boundary), avippavāsasīmā (non-separation boundary), lābhasīmā (gain boundary), gāmasīmā (village boundary), nigamasīmā (town boundary), nagarasīmā (city boundary), abbhantarasīmā (inner boundary), udakukkhepasīmā (water-throwing boundary), janapadasīmā (country boundary), raṭṭhasīmā (state boundary), rajjasīmā (kingdom boundary), dīpasīmā (island boundary), and cakkavāḷasīmā (world-sphere boundary). Among these, 'khaṇḍasīmā' refers to a boundary demarcated by dividing a large boundary or a village area, approved for the convenience of Saṅgha acts such as ordination and higher ordination. Its detailed explanation is given in the Samantapāsādikā Vinaya Commentary in the Mahāvagga, beginning with "First, the markers should be declared," and comprising seventy-two kinds. Since it would be explained similarly here, we will not elaborate further. Instead, we will extract and clarify only certain obscure points. Here, if there is a cave inside, beyond the upper boundary demarcation, below, the boundary does not descend outside. Similarly, if there is a cave outside, on this side of the upper boundary demarcation, the boundary does not descend inside. These two cases differ only in wording but have the same meaning. The meaning here is this: if a cave penetrates the mountainside like a tunnel at the mountain’s base, from the hither part of the upper boundary demarcation, the boundary does not descend into the cave beyond the boundary demarcation, nor into the ground at the base of the mountain outside the cave. The boundary remains only above the cave. If a cave penetrates the mountainside like a tunnel at the mountain’s base, outside the hither part of the upper boundary demarcation, the boundary does not descend into the cave, nor into the ground at the base of the mountain beyond the cave. The boundary remains only above the cave. This analysis is also made in the Vimativinodanī. 'Anto' means inside the mountain, that is, at the base of the mountain. To specify this word 'anto' in relation to the boundary demarcation, it is said, "beyond the upper boundary demarcation." Although 'orato' (on this side) could be said with reference to the mountain’s base, it should be understood that 'parato' (beyond) is used with reference to the mountain peak, which is the boundary’s support. Therefore, when specifying the word 'bahi' in 'bahi leṇaṃ' (outer cave), it says "on this side of the upper boundary demarcation." Here, 'bahi' in 'bahi sīmā na otarati' refers to the cave at the mountain’s base. The meaning is: the boundary does not descend into the lower part of the upper boundary demarcation, which is outside the cave. 'Antosīmā' means the boundary does not descend into the cave and the mountain’s base, in the place where it is fit to descend. The meaning of 'bahi sīmā na otarati' is that the boundary does not descend. Here, it should be understood that the boundary is shown to not descend at all due to the presence of the cave in the place where it is fit to descend. In that case, the Vimativinodanī says, 'Because of non-descent, the boundary remains only above.' Some say a cave is merely a hollow like a bamboo tube, but that is incorrect.

Upacārasīmā nāma gāmāraññanigamādivasā yāva brahmalokā tesaṃ tesaṃ sattānaṃ nivāsagehassa vā devavimānakapparukkhādīnaṃ vā upacārārahaṭṭhānameva upacārasīmā te idha nādhippetā, bhikkhūnaṃ avisayatāya[M 0.0036]. Yaṃ pana padesaṃ paricchinditvā pariveṇaṃ katvā pākārādiparikkhittaṃ vā katvā aparikkhittepi upacārārahaṭṭhānaṃ paricchinditvā vā yasmiṃ padese bhikkhū vasanti, ayameva upacārasīmāti adhippetā. Bhikkhūnaṃ vassūpagamanakathinatthārakaraṇaṭṭhānattā, tathā hi “kathinatthatasīmāya”nti upacārasīmaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ, khandhasīmāya tatruppādābhāvatoti tīsupi gaṇṭhipadesu vuttaṃ. Tatruppādenā bhatanti vihārasantakena khettavatthuādinā ābhataṃ kiñci saṅghikaṃ cīvaraṃ uppajjati, taṃ tesaṃ kathinatthārakabhikkhūnaṃ yeva bhavissatīti yojanā kātabbā. Khettavatthuādinā ti ettha ādisaddena matakacīvaraṃ vā hotu saṃghaṃ uddissa dinnaṃ vā saṅghikena vā ti mahāaṭṭhakathāyaṃ vuttaṃ. Tasmiṃ upacārasīme pavattacīvaraṃ sabbaṃ saṅgaṇhāti, teneva “tena kho pana samayena aññatarena upāsakena saṅghaṃ uddissa vihāro kārito hoti, so tassa vihārassa kate ubhatosaṅghassa akālacīvaraṃ dātukāmo hoti. Tena kho pana samayena ubhatosaṅghassa kathinaṃ atthataṃ hoti. Atha kho so upāsako saṅghaṃ upasaṅkamitvā kathinuddhāraṃ yācī”ti imasmiṃ vatthusmiṃ ānisaṃsapasambhanatthaṃ kathinuddhāraṃ bhagavatā paññattaṃ, teneva thullanandā bhikkhunī ānisaṃsaṃ appaṭippassambhentā cīvaraṃ amhākaṃ bhavissatīti kathinuddhāraṃ paṭibāhi, tasmā kathinatthatasīmāyaṃ anuddhaṭe kathine yāva phagguṇapuṇṇamā pañca māsā etthantare matakacīvaraṃ vā hotu cātuddisaṃ saṅghaṃ uddissa matānaṃ ñātijanānaṃ vā, bhikkhāpaññattiyā vā vihāramahādikiccena vā yenakenaci ākārena saṅghikaṃ cīvaraṃ dinnaṃ hoti, taṃ sabbaṃ tesaṃ kathinatthārakabhikkhūnaṃyeva hoti. Chinnavassānaṃ vā pacchimikāya upagatānaṃ vā tattha sampattānaṃ sīmaṭṭhakabhikkhūnaṃ vā na hoti. Ajānitvā ce gaṇhanti dātabbameva. Yadi taṃ cīvaraṃ taṇḍulādikhādanīyabhojanaṃ karoti, itaresampi hotiyeva. Kasmāti ce ānisaṃsa vigatattā. Teneva gaṇapūraṇavasena sampattānaṃ chinnaṃ vassānaṃ vā pacchimikāya upagatānaṃ vā aññasmiṃ vihāre vutthavassanaṃ vā khādanīyabhojanīyādīni pāpuṇanti. Kathinatthatasāṭakassa dānakammavācā pana khaṇḍasīmāyameva vaṭṭasi. Tasmiṃ vihāre asati aññavihāre baddhasīmāya vā udakukkhepasīmādīsu vā kātabbameva.

The proximity boundary (upacārasīmā) refers to the area around villages, forests, towns, and so forth, extending as far as the Brahma world, encompassing the dwellings of beings, celestial mansions, or wish-fulfilling trees, etc., suitable for proximity. However, these are not intended here, as they are beyond the monks' domain. Instead, the intended proximity boundary is the area demarcated by enclosing a space with walls or other boundaries, or even an undemarcated space where monks reside and which is suitable for proximity. This is considered the upacārasīmā. Because it serves as the place for monks to observe the rains retreat (vassāvāsa) and perform the Kathina ceremony, it is referred to as the "boundary where the Kathina is spread." This is mentioned in all three key sections (gaṇṭhipadesu), because such an arising is not found in a fragmented boundary. Regarding 'what is brought within' (tatruppādenābhataṃ), any Sangha-owned robe material that arises within the monastery's vicinity—whether from fields, lands, or other sources—belongs exclusively to those monks who have spread the Kathina. Here, the term 'fields, lands, etc.' includes funeral robes or anything donated to the Sangha, as explained in the Mahā-aṭṭhakathā. All robe material arising within this proximity boundary is included. For example, on one occasion, a certain lay follower had a monastery built for the Sangha and wished to offer out-of-season robes to both Sanghas. At that time, the Kathina had been spread for both Sanghas. Then that lay follower approached the Sangha and requested the withdrawal of the Kathina. The Blessed One had prescribed the withdrawal of the Kathina in this story for the purpose of setting aside the special privileges. Therefore, the nun Thullanandā, not relinquishing the special privileges, obstructed the withdrawal of the Kathina, thinking, "The robes will be ours." Therefore, as long as the Kathina remains unwithdrawn within the boundary where the Kathina was spread—until the full moon of Phagguna (five months)—any robe material, whether funeral robes, those offered to the four-directional Sangha by relatives of the deceased, or given by way of alms-food prescription or a great monastery undertaking or in any other way given to the Sangha, all of that belongs solely to those monks who have spread the Kathina. Monks who have broken the rains-residence, or those who undertook the later rains-residence, or resident monks within the boundary who were present there, have no share. If they unknowingly take it, it must be given back. If that robe material is converted into rice or other food, it belongs to others as well. Why? Because the special entitlement has ceased. For that reason, food and other provisions accrue to those who arrived to complete the group number, or those who have broken the rains-residence, or those who undertook the later rains-residence, or those who observed the rains retreat in another monastery. However, the formal act of giving the Kathina cloth is valid only within a fragmented boundary (khaṇḍasīmā). If there is no such sīmā in that monastery, it must be performed in another monastery, within a fixed boundary (baddhasīmā), or within a water-throwing boundary (udakukkhepasīmā), etc.

Kasmāti ce[M 0.0037], tasmiṃ vihāre purimikāya upagatā kathinatthārakusalā na honti. Atthārakusalā khandhakabhāṇakattherā pariyesitvā ānetabbā. Kammavācaṃ sāvetvā kathinaṃ attharāpetvā dānañca bhuñjitvā gamissanti, ānisaṃso pana itaresaṃ vahotī’ti. Aññasīmaṭṭhakabhikkhūnampi kammavāca sāvanassa aṭṭhakathāyaṃ vuttattā. Chinnavassāvā pacchimikāya upagatā vā aññasmiṃ vihāre vutthavassāpi vā purimikāya upagatānaṃ gaṇapūraṇamattameva labhanti, ānisaṃso itaresaṃyeva hoti. Sacepi te chinnavassādayo anumodenti, bhinno kathinatthāro, idaṃ aṭṭhakathāyaṃ vicāritaṃ. Apare pana “kathinatthatasīmanti upacārasīmaṃ sandhāya vutta”nti vuttattā upacārasīmāyameva kathinanatthāraka ñattiṃ karonti, taṃ ayuttaṃ, sodhetuṃ dukkarattā. Tathā hi upacārasīmāyameva paviṭṭhagāmasīmāyaṃ tattha otiṇṇe bhikkhū tattha pāsānayanaṃ vā bahisīmakaraṇaṃ vā ko sakkhissati. “Kathinatthatasīmāya”nti imināpipadena kathinatthatabhāvoyeva vutto, na kathinatthatasāṭakassa dānakammavācā, sā ca vicāraṇā kammacatukkena dīpetabbā. Tattha apalokanakammaṃ ñattikammaṃ ñattidutiyakammaṃ ñatticatutthakammanti. Tattha apalokanakammaṃ nāma sīmaṭṭhakasaṅghaṃ sodhetvā chandārahānaṃ chandaṃ āharitvā samaggassa saṅghassa anumatiyā saṅghaṃ kittetvā “ruccati saṅghassā”ti tikkhuttuṃ anusāvetvā kattabbaṃ vuccati. Ñattikammaṃ nāma vuttanayeneva samaggassa saṅghassa anumatiyā ekāya ñattiyā kattabbaṃ kammaṃ. Ñattidutiyakammaṃ nāma vuttanayeneva samaggassa saṅghassa anumatiyā ekāya ñattiyā ekāya ca anusāvanāyāti evaṃ ñattidutiyāya anusāvanāya kattabbaṃ kammaṃ. Ñatticatutthakammaṃ nāma vuttanayeneva samaggassa saṅghassa anumatiyā ekāya ñattiyā tīhi ca anusāvanāhīti evaṃ ñatticatutthāhi tīhi anusāvanāhi kattabbaṃ kammaṃ. Tattha apalokanakammaṃ phalabhājanakālādīsu lahukakammeyeva labbhati. Kathinatthatasāṭakadānepi anatthateyeva kathine tato bahuānisaṃsacīvarasāṭakaṃ labhitvā teneva sāṭakena attharitabbā, puna kammavācāya dānakiccaṃ natthi apalokanakammena saṅghassa anumatiyā dātabbaṃ. Kathinatthatasāṭakadānakammavācā ekā ce vaṭṭati. Puna kamma vācāyakiccaṃ [M 0.0038] natthi. Ñattikammaṃ pana dhammasaṅgāhakakāle mahākassapattherādīhi attanāva attānaṃ sammannanakāle lahukakammeyeva labbhati. Ñattidutiyakammaṃ pana sīmāsammuti, sīmāsamūhanaṃ, kathinadānaṃ, kathinuddhāro, kuṭivatthudesanā, vihāravatthudesanāti imāni cha garukammāni apaloketvā kātuṃ na vaṭṭati. Ñattidutiyakammavācaṃ sāvetvāva kātabbaṃ. Ito paresu cīvaravippavāsasammutiādīsu ñattidutiyakammesu apaloketvāpi kātabbaṃ, lahukakammattā. Ñatticatutthakammaṃ pana idāni upasampadākammaṃ idāni cattāri kammāni baddhasīmāya vā, tasmiṃ asati udakukkhepasīmāya vā sodhetuṃ asakkonte sati abaddhasīmāya vā kātabbameva. Apare evaṃ vadanti “kuṭivatthukāravihāravatthukārakāle tāni kuṭivihāravatthūni kathaṃ sīmaṭṭhā kātuṃ sakkhissanti, tasmā tattha tattheva kuṭivihārakaraṇaṭṭhāneyeva kātabba”nti, taṃ tesaṃ vacanamattameva, na sārato paccetabbaṃ. Vatthuparāmasanamevettha pamāṇaṃ, tathā hi saṅghakuṭivatthuṃ olokentaṃ yācatityādinā vatthuparāmasanaṃ katvā heṭṭhā vatthuñattidose ca vipatti lakkhaṇe ca vajjetvā ñattidutiyakammena sammatabhikkhūhi tattha kuṭivihāraṭṭhānaṃ gantvā oloketabbaṃ teneva pāḷiyaṃ “tehi sammatehi bhikkhūhi tattha gantvā kuṭivatthu oloketabba”nti vuttaṃ. Aññathā pattacīvaravirahitaṃ puggalaṃ vā anupajjhāyakaṃ vā katvā upasampādentehi “paripuṇṇassa pattacīvara”nti vā “itthannāmassa upasampadāpekkho, itthannāmena upajjhāyenā”ti kammavācāya sāvitepi kammassa asijjhanaṃ bhaveyya. Apare evaṃ vadanti “sīmāsammutikāle pana kataṃ, tathā hi sīmāsammutikāle bhikkhū tasmiṃ yeva ṭhāne ṭhatvā kammavācaṃ sāventi, tasmimpi ṭhāne abaddhasīmāva hoti. Yadi baddhasīmā bhaveyya, sīmajjhottharaṇaṃ vā sīmasambhedo vā siyā”ti. Saccaṃ, tathāpi taṃ sīmāsammutiṭṭhānaṃ bhujissaṃ katvā sīmasammutikammassakatattā visuṃgāme sammatānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ gāmasīme ṭhitā bhikkhū kammaṃ kopetuṃ na sakkonti. Yadi sīmasammutiṭṭhānaṃ bhujissaṃ katvāva sīmaṃ sammanneyya, kathaṃ bhagavato dharamānakāle chabbaggiyā bhikkhu yathāsukhaṃ sīmaṃ sammannissantīti na panevaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ tathā hi vāsākhā migāramātāpi navakoṭīhi bhūmiṃ gaṇhitvā pubbārāmaṃ nāma mahā vihāraṃ [M 0.0039] kāresi, tathā anāthapiṇḍiko kahāpaṇasanthatena jetassa rājakumārassa uyyānaṭṭhānaṃ kiṇāpetvā gaṇhitvā jetavanaṃ nāma mahāvihāraṃ kāresi evaṃ puññakāmā pariveṇaṃ katvā vihāraṃ karonti tasmā chabbaggiyā bhikkhū yathāsukhaṃ sīmaṃ bandhantīti daṭṭhabbanti. Ayaṃ upacārasīmāya vicāraṇā.

Why is that? If in that monastery, those who completed the earlier rains-residence are not skilled in spreading the Kathina, then those skilled in spreading should be sought among the elders who recite the Khandhaka and brought there. After reciting the formal act, the Kathina should be spread, and having received the offering, they will depart. The benefit, however, accrues to the others. Because it is said in the commentary that the formal act should be announced even to monks within another boundary. Those whose rains-residence is broken, or who arrived later, or who completed the rains-residence in another monastery, only serve to complete the quorum for those who completed the earlier rains-residence; the benefit accrues to the others. If even those whose rains-residence is broken and others express approval, the spreading of the Kathina is considered invalid. This is examined in the commentary. Others say, "The term 'Kathina-spread boundary' refers to the vicinity boundary," and thus they make the motion for spreading the Kathina only within the vicinity boundary. This is improper, as it is difficult to rectify. For how, if the vicinity boundary itself has entered into the village boundary, who will be able to establish a stone boundary or exclude it from the boundary there? By the phrase "Kathina-spread boundary," only the state of having spread the Kathina is meant, not the formal act of giving the Kathina cloth. That discussion should be explained by the fourfold act. There are the apalokana-kamma, the ñatti-kamma, the ñatti-dutiya-kamma, and the ñatti-catuttha-kamma. There, the apalokana-kamma means purifying the Sangha within the boundary, obtaining the consent of those entitled to consent, proclaiming the harmonious Sangha, and it is said to be done by announcing three times, "It is agreeable to the Sangha." The ñatti-kamma means performing the act with one motion, with the agreement of the harmonious Sangha, in the manner stated. The ñatti-dutiya-kamma means performing the act with one motion and one announcement, with the agreement of the harmonious Sangha, that is, by a motion followed by one announcement. The ñatti-catuttha-kamma means performing the act with one motion and three announcements, with the agreement of the harmonious Sangha, that is, by a motion followed by three announcements. There, the apalokana-kamma is only permissible for minor acts, such as during the time for distributing fruits, etc. Even in giving the Kathina cloth, if the Kathina has not been spread, after receiving a robe cloth with much benefit, it should be spread with that very cloth. There is no need for another formal act of giving; it should be given with the Sangha’s consent through the apalokana-kamma. If one formal act for giving the Kathina cloth is valid, there is no need for another formal act. The ñatti-kamma, however, is permissible for minor acts, such as when the great elder Mahā Kassapa and others appointed themselves during the compilation of the Dhamma. The ñatti-dutiya-kamma, however, is not permissible for the six weighty acts—establishing a boundary, removing a boundary, giving the Kathina, removing the Kathina, designating a hut site, or designating a monastery site—without an apalokana. The ñatti-dutiya-kamma must be done after reciting the motion. For other matters, such as agreements on robe seasons, etc., the ñatti-dutiya-kamma may be performed even after an apalokana, as they are minor acts. The ñatti-catuttha-kamma must now be performed for the higher ordination act and the four acts within a fixed boundary, or, if that is not possible, within a water-bound boundary, or, if it is not possible to purify even that, within an unfixed boundary. Others say, "When constructing huts or monasteries, how can those hut and monastery sites be made within a boundary? Therefore, they should be constructed right there in the intended place." This is merely their opinion and should not be accepted as substantial. The examination of the site is the authority here. For when the Sangha inspects a hut site, after examining the site by requesting, etc., and avoiding defects in the motion regarding the site and signs of failure mentioned below, monks appointed by the ñatti-dutiya-kamma should go there and inspect the hut site. As stated in the text, "Those appointed monks should go there and inspect the hut site." Otherwise, if someone is ordained without bowl and robe or without a preceptor, even if the formal act declares, "He has a complete bowl and robe," or "So-and-so seeks ordination, with So-and-so as preceptor," the act would not succeed. Others say, "At the time of establishing a boundary, monks perform the formal act while standing right there, so that place remains an unfixed boundary. If it were a fixed boundary, there would be an overlapping of boundaries or a breaking of boundaries." True, but since that place is treated as independent due to the act of boundary establishment, monks standing in the village boundary cannot invalidate the act of the monks who have agreed in a separate village. If the boundary-establishing place were treated as independent when establishing the boundary, how could the group-of-six monks establish boundaries as they pleased during the Buddha’s time? It should not be seen thus. For instance, Visākhā Migāramātā acquired land for ninety million (coins) and built the great monastery called Pubbārāma. Similarly, Anāthapiṇḍika purchased Prince Jeta’s park with a spread of kahāpaṇas (gold coins) and established the great monastery called Jetavana. Thus, those desiring merit build residences and monasteries. Therefore, it should be understood that the group-of-six monks did bind boundaries as they pleased. This is the discussion on the vicinity boundary.

Samānasaṃvāsasīmā nāma diṭṭhisīlasāmaññasaṅghātasaṅkhātehi bhikkhugaṇehi uposathādisaṅghakammena samānaṃ ekībhāvaṃ hutvā vasituṃ sammatā sīmā. Avippavāsasīmā nāma ticīvarena vippavasituṃ sammatā sīmā, etā dvesīmā tiyojanaparamatāyapi sambhavato mahāsīmā tipi nāmaṃ labhanti, tāsaṃ vicāraṇā mahāaṭṭhakathāyaṃ sabbaso paripuṇṇaṃ katvā vuttā, tasmā idha na vakkhāma vuccamānampi avisesetvā vuttaṃ bhaveyya, tasmā na vakkhāma.

The boundary of common residence is that which is designated for groups of bhikkhus, reckoned as having commonality of view and commonality of virtue, to dwell in unity, having become one, through Sangha acts such as the Uposatha. The boundary of non-separation is that which is designated for not separating from the three robes. These two boundaries, because they can extend up to three yojanas, also obtain the name 'great boundary.' Their discussion is fully and completely given in the Great Commentary (Mahā-aṭṭhakathā). Therefore, we will not elaborate on it here; for even if it were mentioned, it would be stated without distinction. Hence, we will not discuss it further.

Lābhasīmā nāma yaṃ gāmaṃ vā nigamaṃ vā pokkharaṇītaḷākavanādikaṃ vā yaṃ yaṃ padesaṃ saṅghassa catuccappayatthāya rājarājamahāmattehi paricchinditvā ṭhapitā, esā lābhasīmā nāma. Neva sammāsambuddhena anuññātā, na dhammasaṅgahakattherehi ṭhapitā, api ca kho lābhadāyakehi ṭhapitāva. Mātikāṭṭhakathāya līnatthappakāsaniyampi lābhasīmāti yaṃ rājarājamahāmattādayo vihāraṃ kāretvā gāvutaṃ vā aḍḍhayojanaṃ vā yojanaṃ vā samantā paricchinditvā “ayaṃ amhākaṃ vihārassa lābhasīmā, yaṃ etthantare uppajjati, taṃ sabbaṃ amhākaṃ vihārassa demā”ti ṭhapenti, ayaṃ lābhasīmā nāmā’ti vuttaṃ. Imasmiṃ lābhasīmādhikāre rañño mahācetiyadāyakassa divaṅgatakālato ekasmiṃ saṃvacchare atīte tassa jeṭṭhaputtassa dhammarañño kāle īdisaṃ puññaṃ bhūtapubbaṃ. Yadi hi rājarājamahāmattādīsu yo koci yassa vihārassa yāni taḷākakhettavatthādīni datvā taṃ sahitavihāraṃ puggalassa deti, evaṃ sati kiṃ tassa puggalassa taṃ sahitavihāraṃ puggalikabhāvena paṭiggahetuṃ vaṭṭati, udāhu saṅghikabhāvenevāti taḷākakhettavatthādivirahito pana kevalo puggalikavihāro aññesaṃ dammīti adatvā puggale mate saṅghiko hoti, udāhu puggalikoyevāti.
