Evañcapana vadeyya ye udakukkhepena saha ijjhanti, tepi “sacepi hi bhikkhusahassaṃ tiṭṭhati, tassa ṭhitokāsassa bāhirantato paṭṭhāya bhikkhūnaṃ vaggakammaparihāratthaṃ sīmāpekkhāya uppannāya tāya saha sayameva sañjātā sattabbhantarasīmā samānasaṃvāsakāti adhippāyo. Yattha pana khuddake araññe mahantehi vā bhikkhūhi paripuṇṇatāya vaggakammasaṅkābhāvena sattabbhantarasīmāpekkhā natthi, tattha sattabbhantarasīmā na uppajjati, kevalāraññasīmāyameva tattha saṅghena kammaṃ kātabbaṃ nadīādīsupi esevanayo”ti vimati vinodaniyaṃ vuttavacanaṃ bhindanti nāmāti. Taṃ na, tena no kā hāni evampi amhākaṃ vāde koci virodho na vijjateva. Kasmāti ce, ime dve saddaracanāpi asambandhāva bhinnalakkhaṇā bhinnavisayā cetā sīmā. Kathaṃ saddaracanā asambandhā. Yathā araññe tattha sattabbhantarasīmā na uppajjati, kevalāraññamevāti vuccati, eva meva “nadiyāpi sabbattha saṅgho nisīdati, udakukkhepasīmā nuppajjatī”ti avatvā “udakukkhepasīmā kammaṃ natthī”ti kriyāparāmasanavasena vuttaṃ. Karitabbaṃ kammaṃ. Kiṃ taṃ, khipanaṃ. Evampi saddaracanā asambandhāva. “Vaggakammaparihāratthaṃ…pe… udakukkhepasīmā sattabbhantarasīmā uppajjati, nāsatī”ti ettha “nāsatī”ti imassa udakukkhepena vināpīti atthopi yujjateva. Kasmāti ce “sīmāpekkhāya satievā”ti iminā anulomanayavasena “sīmāpekkhāya asati [M 0.0064] nuppajjatī”ti attho yujjateva. Kecipana “samantā abbhantaraṃ minitvā paricchedakaraṇeneva sīmā sañjāyati, na sayamevā”ti vadanti taṃ na gahetabbaṃ. Yadi hi…pe… yathā cettha, evaṃ udakukkhepasīmāyapi nadīādīsupi tatthāpi hi majjhimapuriso na ñāyati, tathā sabbathāmena khipananti imināpi vacanena ācariyavarassa udakukkhepena saheva sīmāpekkhāya sati udakukkhepasīmā uppajjati, nāsatīti adhippāyo ñāyati. Kathaṃ bhinnalakkhaṇā, gāmasīmasattabbhantaraudakukkhepasīmā kiñcāpi abaddhasīmasāmaññena samānā, na pana samānalakkhaṇā tathā hi “asammatāya bhikkhave sīmāya aṭṭhapitāya yaṃ gāmaṃ vā nigamaṃ vā upanissāya viharati, yā tassa gāmassa vā gāmasīmā, nigamassa vā nigamasīmā, ayaṃ tattha samānasaṃvāsā ekūposathā”ti imasmiṃ sikkhāpade khuddako vā mahanto vā sabbopi gāmapadeso gāmasīmāti anuññātā, na sattabbhantarasīmāya viya samantā leḍḍupātukkhepena anuññātā abbhantarasīmāyapi agāmake ce araññe yaṃ nissāya viharati, tassa araññassa araññasīmāti nānuññātā tathā nadīsamuddajātassaresupi gāmasīmāyamiva sabbo nadīsamuddajātassarapadeso nadīsamuddajātassa rasīmāti evameva na anuññātā tathā abbhantarasīmāyamiva samantā sattabbhantarāti vā nānuññātā evamimā gāmasīmasattabbhantaraudakukkhepasīmā bhinnalakkhaṇā, tasmā bhagavatā paññattasikkhāpadānusāreneva gāmasīmāyapi khuddako vā mahanto vā sabbo gāmapadeso gāmasīmāva hoti, sattabbhantarasīmāyapi “samantā sattabbhantarā”ti paññattasikkhāpadānusārena samantā sattabbhantarasīmā sīmāpekkhāya saha sayameva uppajjatī”ti minanāminanavicāraṇā pana niratthakāva tathā udakukkhepasīmāyapi “majjhimassa purisassa samantā udakukkhepā”ti paññattasikkhāpadānurūpaṃ udakukkhepena saheva uppajjatīti natthi khipanākhipana vicāraṇāya payojananti. Kathaṃ bhinnavisayā sattabbhantarasīmā araññavisayā, udakukkhepasīmā nadīsamuddajātassaravisayā, evampi etā gāmasīmasattabbhantaraudakukkhepasīmā bhinnavisayāva tasmā “nadīādīsu eseva nayo”ti vuttepi sattabbhantarasīmā apekkhāya saheva attano sabhāveneva sattabbhantarasīmā uppajjati yathā. Evameva udakukkhepasīmāyapi [M 0.0065] apekkhāya saha udakukkhepaṃ katvāva vaggakammaparihāratthaṃ udakukkhepasīmā uppajjati, na vināti sanniṭṭhānaṃ kātabbaṃ. Nanu ca mātikāṭṭhakathāyaṃ paricchedabbhantare hatthapāsaṃ vijahitvā ṭhitopi, paricchedā bahi aññaṃ tattakaṃyeva paricchedaṃ anatikkamitvā ṭhitopi kammaṃ kopeti, idaṃ sabbaaṭṭhakathāsu sinniṭṭhāna’nti vuttavacane navirujjhatīti ce. Taṃ na, mātikāṭṭhakathāyaṃ “sace pana dvesaṅghā visuṃvisuṃ uposathādikammaṃ karonti, dvinnaṃ udakukkhepānaṃ antare añño eko udakukkhepo upacāratthāya ṭhapetabbo”ti dvinnaṃ saṅghānaṃ visuṃ visuṃ kammakaraṇādhikāre vuttattā. Sāratthadīpaniyampi “tattha aññaṃ tattakaṃyeva paricchedaṃ anatikkamitvā ṭhitopi kammaṃ kopetīti idaṃ neva pāḷiyaṃ, na aṭṭhakathāyaṃ upalabbhati. Yadi cetaṃ dvinnaṃ saṅghānaṃ visuṃvisuṃ uposathādikammakaraṇādhikāre vuttattā udakukkhepato bahi aññaṃ udakukkhepaṃ anatikkamitvā uposathādikaraṇatthaṃ. Ṭhito saṅgho sīmāsambhedasambhavato kammaṃ kopetīti iminā adhippāyena vuttaṃ siyā. Evaṃ sati yujjeyya, teneva mātikāṭṭhakathāyaṃ līnatthappakāsaniyaṃ aññaṃ tattakaṃyeva paricchedanti dutiyaṃ udakukkhepaṃ anatikkamantopi kopeti. Kasmā, attano udakukkhepasīmāya paresaṃ udakukkhepasīmāya ajjhotthatattā sīmāsambhedo hoti, tasmā kopetī’ti “idaṃ sabbaaṭṭhakathāsu sanniṭṭhāna”nti ca iminā adhippāyena vuttanti gahetabbaṃ. Sabbāsupi aṭṭhakathāsu sīmāsambhedassa anicchitattā teneva attano ca aññesañca udakukkhepaparicchedassa antarā añño udakukkhepo sīmantarikatthāya ṭhapetabboti vuttaṃ. Aññe panettha aññathāpi papañcenti, taṃ na gahetabba’nti vatvā paṭikkhittaṃ. Tassa “antohatthapāsaṃ vijahitvā ṭhito kammaṃ kopetī”ti iminā paricchedato bahi yatthakatthaci ṭhito kammaṃ na kopetīti dīpetīti sāratthadīpanīvacanenāpi ekasmiṃ saṅghasannipāte paricchedato bahi aññaṃ tattakaṃyeva paricchedaṃ anatikkamitvā ṭhitopi kammaṃ na kopetīti veditabbametaṃ sāratthadīpaniyaṃyeva imamatthaṃ daḷhikaraṇavasena udakukkhepappamāṇā sīmantarikā suviññeyyatarā hoti, sīmāsambhedasaṅkā ca na siyāti sāmicidassanatthaṃ añño udakukkhepo sīmantarikatthāya ṭhapetabbo’ti vuttaṃ. Yattakena pana sīmāsambhedo na [M 0.0066] hoti, tattakaṃ ṭhapetuṃ vaṭṭatiyeva, tenāhu porāṇā “yattakena sīmasaṅkaro na hoti, tattakampi ṭhapetuṃ vaṭṭati. Khuddakaṃ pana na vaṭṭatī”ti idampi udakukkhepasīmāya parisavasena vaḍḍhanato sīmasambhedasaṅkā siyā. Taṃ nivāraṇatthameva vuttanti.

Concerning the strength of the ten classes of elephants that have been mentioned, it should be understood according to their respective powers. Therein, 'kāḷāvaka' means an elephant of the ordinary class; the strength of one kāḷāvaka elephant is equal to the bodily strength of ten men. The strength of ten kāḷāvaka elephants is equal to the strength of one gaṅgeyya elephant. The strength of ten gaṅgeyya elephants is equal to the strength of one paṇḍara elephant. The strength of ten paṇḍara elephants is equal to the strength of one tamba elephant. The strength of ten tamba elephants is equal to the strength of one piṅgala elephant. The strength of ten piṅgala elephants is equal to the strength of one gandhahatthī elephant. The strength of ten gandhahatthī elephants is equal to the strength of one maṅgala elephant. The strength of ten maṅgala elephants is equal to the strength of one hema elephant. The strength of ten hemavanta elephants is equal to the strength of one uposatha elephant. The strength of ten uposatha elephants is equal to the strength of one chaddanta elephant. The strength of ten chaddanta elephants is equal to the bodily strength of a Tathāgata. This is also called 'the strength designated as Nārāyana.' Therein, 'nārā' are called rays; many diverse rays arise from that, hence Nārāyana, which is vajira; therefore, it also means 'strength designated as vajira.' Thus, this is equivalent to a thousand koṭis of elephants according to the reckoning of ordinary elephants, or the strength of ten thousand koṭis of men according to the reckoning of men. This is the bodily strength of the Tathāgata. But the detailed explanation of power with regard to authority and so forth should be understood according to the respective suttas. The expression 'middle-sized man' refers to a carpenter. For he, in relation to the power of the Tathāgata, is like one measuring Mount Sineru, the king of mountains, with a mustard seed; he is incapable of measuring it, even with a hundred, a thousand, or a hundred thousand, and is even incapable of grasping so much as the big toe. Because of his great size compared to an ordinary man, he is called a 'middle-sized man,' since he exists in between. Thus, a Sugata span is three spans for a carpenter; with a carpenter's hand, it is one and a half hands. Similarly, a carpenter's span is two spans for an ordinary man; with an ordinary man's hand, it is a full hand. Thus, the Sugata span is now three spans for the middle-sized man; with a carpenter's hand, it is one and a half hands, as stated in the commentary on the training rule about building a hut. That is not what is intended here by 'middle-sized man.' Why? Because in the training rule about building a hut itself, it is stated, 'three hands' means three hands according to the carpenter's measure. A bed that conforms to the standard means a bed of nine spans according to the ordinary span. Thus, the commentary states that instead of saying, 'by a middle-sized man' here, it should be, 'by a strong middle-sized man.' An 'ordinary man' is the man who has been mentioned as having the bodily strength of ten men, which is equal to the strength of one kāḷāvaka elephant. Although some fortunate individuals, such as King Ajātasattu, Jīvaka Komārabhacca, and even the woman Visākhā Migāramātā, possess the strength of five elephants, they are not called middle-sized men; they are simply ordinary men, but are special men due to their good fortune. Here, however, an ordinary man himself is intended by 'strong middle-sized man.' 'Udakukkhepa' means delimited by a water-throw. Therefore, the commentary on the Mātikā, the Līnatthappakāsanī, says, 'Udakukkhepa is a word used in the instrumental sense.' This is the meaning of 'udakukkhepena' there: In rivers, seas, and natural lakes, the area that is delimited by a water-throw all around from the edge of the assembly by a middle-sized man—this area in those rivers and so forth, which are established in worldly usage, in those very unbound boundaries, is also a shared dwelling and a single uposatha. In the Kaṅkhāvitaraṇī, however, the statement 'that which is a water-throw by a middle-sized man all around' means that area which is delimited by a water-throw all around by a strong middle-sized man. There, just as dice-players throw a wooden ball, so too, water or sand should be taken in the hand and thrown with all one's strength by a middle-sized man. The place where the thrown water or sand falls, that is called a water-throw (udakukkhepa). This area is a shared dwelling and a single uposatha; having made this connection of meaning, it means that the boundary delimited by a water-throw in those rivers and so forth is indeed a shared dwelling and a single uposatha. This is obtained only within these rivers and so forth, not outside. Therefore, the area in a river or a natural lake that is covered by water during the four months of the normal rainy season, or the area in the sea where the natural waves recede and settle, from that point onwards, is allowable ground. Even in times of drought or in the hot season, when rivers and natural lakes are dry, that same area is allowable ground. If, however, in a dry natural lake, they dig a reservoir or make a sown field, that place becomes village land. The udakukkhepa boundary does not extend beyond what has been stated as 'allowable ground'; it extends only within. Therefore, it has been said, 'from the inner edge of the assembly of those, a water-throw delineation should be made all around.' In the Gaṇṭhipada, however, the statement, 'that which is a water-throw by a middle-sized man all around,' regarding this river, is stated to show the demarcation of the boundary at the time of performing acts that require a quorum of four or more by separate groups of monks, and also to show the delineation of the boundary for separation from the three robes, just like the delineation of the boundary of a seven-abbhantara boundary, according to the teachers. Therefore, it is written, 'it is established that even in the absence of a water-throw delineation, it is permissible to perform the act within the river.' This statement, 'even in the absence of a water-throw delineation,' does not agree with the statement in the Mahā-Aṭṭhakathā, 'delimited by a water-throw all around,' or with the statement in the Mātikāṭṭhakathā and the Kaṅkhāvitaraṇī, 'a water-throw delineation should be made all around,' or with the statements in the Vimatavinodanī, 'even there, the middle-sized man is not known, thus one throws with all one's strength,' or 'indeed, it is for this purpose that boundaries are delineated with sand and so forth.' The author, in order to avoid criticism from others, does not say, 'this is our opinion,' but instead indicates 'teachers' as the other agent, and then in the conclusion says, 'having thoroughly considered all this, having attended upon teachers in a venerable lineage, what is proper should be accepted, and what is not should be discarded.' Therefore, it should be seen as written according to the opinion of other teachers. Otherwise, the statements in the commentaries that have been established in the three councils, as well as the statements in the Kaṅkhāvitaraṇī and the Vimatavinodanī, would have to be annulled, and the texts would become mutually contradictory. Even the training rule established by the Blessed One would have to be revised according to the opinions of the disciples. But the training rule established by the Blessed One should not be annulled; one should act according to how it was established. For it will be said that for a monk who knowingly drinks intoxicants, there is a pācittiya offense; but for a novice who knowingly drinks, there is a breach of morality, not if he does so unknowingly. There, a reason should be sought, or not sought, since the establishment of training rules is the domain of the Buddhas alone; one should act according to how it was established. Therefore, 'a water-throw all around' means delimited by a water-throw all around, in accordance with the established training rule, or 'water should be thrown,' or 'the boundary is also delimited by a water-throw,' or 'a water-throw delineation should be made all around'; the words of the Elders who compiled the Dhamma are the standard, for they are knowers of the Buddha's teaching. And this statement is not the direct word of the Blessed One, nor is it the word of the compilers of the Dhamma. Alternatively, since the teachers say, 'that which is a water-throw by a middle-sized man all around,' regarding this river... is stated to show the delineation of the boundary, just like the delineation of the boundary for a seven-abbhantara boundary; therefore, it is established that even in the absence of a water-throw delineation, it is permissible to perform the act within the river. Even the compilers of the Dhamma did not enter into this connection of meaning, nor did the author enter into it. Why? Because in the Vinaya commentaries that have been established in the three councils, as well as in the sub-commentaries that are their explanations, they write about the water-throw delineation in many ways, with statements such as, 'delimited by a water-throw,' 'water should be thrown,' 'a water-throw delineation should be made.' Therefore, it is understood that even the compilers of the Dhamma did not enter into it. The author, in order to avoid criticism from others, indicates 'teachers' as the other agent; therefore, it is understood that even the author did not enter into it. And this statement is the opinion of some elders; and that opinion is the weakest of all. Who is able to annul the words of Mahākassapa, Yasa, Moggaliputtatissa, and others, who explain the meaning of the training rule 'water-throw' established by the all-knowing Buddha, and of their disciples and grand-disciples who reside in the Mahāvihāra, like one measuring Mount Sineru with a mustard seed? And because the acts beginning with ordination are weighty acts, and because they are the root of the dispensation, one should stand on what is authoritative. 'That which is a water-throw by a middle-sized man all around' was established by the Blessed One. How can it be that the place suitable for a water-throw by a middle-sized man is the water-throw boundary, or rather, that the water-throw boundary is defined only by the water-throw? To refute this objection, the commentary master says, 'But how should the water be thrown?' There, 'how' is a question of wanting to explain. How, by what means, should the water be thrown? Should it be thrown by a method of sprinkling water, or should it be thrown in the manner of throwing a clod of earth or a wooden ball? This is the meaning. Although it is said in general, 'dice-players,' it is understood from the context of the word 'throwing a wooden ball' that it refers to deceptive people who play with wooden balls, just as in the phrase 'the lion sings with its tail, the lion wags its tail,' where the meaning is understood from the proximity of the words. The word 'akkha' is well-established even for gambling. 'Wooden ball' means a wooden artifact made by turning on a lathe and fitted with an awl; this is the meaning... Just as deceptive dice-players, roguish people who play with wooden balls, take a wooden ball in their hand and throw it, showing their strength, bending down with all their might; in the same way, a strong middle-sized man, taking water or sand in his hand and throwing it, showing his strength, bending down with all his might, should walk around the edge of the assembly, either sitting or standing, and throw. Thus, the water or sand that is thrown, at whatever place it falls, that is called a single water-throw. This word, 'a single water-throw,' indicates the possibility of another water-throw because of the boundary interval at the time of performing acts by two separate groups of monks. Therefore, in the Mātikāṭṭhakathā it is stated, 'If two groups of monks perform acts beginning with the uposatha separately, another water-throw should be placed between the two water-throws for the purpose of a buffer zone.' And the Vimatavinodanī also says, 'Its inside means the inside of that area delimited by the water-throw. Not only within it, but it is also not permissible to stand outside it, yet within the range of one water-throw; that statement is made because there would be a concern about the violation of the act for one who does not understand the water-throw delineation well.' But the Sāratthadīpanī says, '“One who stands at a distance beyond the hand's reach from its inside violates the act”; by this it is indicated that one who stands anywhere outside the delineation does not violate the act,' and rejects the statement in the Mātikāṭṭhakathā, which should be seen as having been stated with reference to a single monastic gathering. Just as boundary intervals are placed in a large boundary to avoid intermingling of the divided and bound boundaries, in the same way, in a natural boundary like a village boundary by its own nature, a water-throw boundary is permitted by the Blessed One because of the extreme size of rivers, seas, and natural lakes. Nevertheless, another water-throw should be placed between the two water-throw boundaries for the purpose of a boundary interval, just as between two bound boundaries, to avoid intermingling. A bound boundary has a marker; a water-throw boundary has a water-throw; one water-throw should be seen as a boundary interval. Therefore, the Vimatavinodanī states, 'This arrangement for a boundary interval should be seen as established in accordance with the sutta that permits a boundary interval for two bound boundaries.' However, it should be seen that even one who stands outside of one water-throw in a single monastic gathering does not violate the act. For the Vimatavinodanī states, 'The commentary masters, knowing that due to the initiating cause, the overlapping and intermingling of two bound boundaries that rely on a single village boundary and are of one division, and the place of non-separation without a boundary interval, are indeed approved by the Blessed One, made an arrangement for a boundary interval even here. For even with boundaries of different divisions, the place without a boundary interval is for intermingling only when it is endowed with two factors, that is, relying on one boundary and being of one division; it is not so otherwise.' Thus, having shown the delineation by a water-throw in rivers, seas, and natural lakes, in accordance with the established training rule of 'a water-throw all around,' to show that in a river or natural lake region with a short source, it is allowable in every way by its own nature, just like a village boundary, even without a water-throw, he says, 'If, however, it is not too long, from the source onwards,' and so forth. There, 'from the source onwards' means from the upper part of the place where the river flows for a maximum of four months. 'As far as the mouth' means as far as the riverbank itself, which is intended here as the mouth. 'Everywhere' means that in every part of the river, there is no act of a water-throw boundary, as a boundary that proceeds by a water-throw is a water-throw boundary. 'To be done' refers to the act; the root is 'kara' (to do), and the suffix is 'ramma'. 'Doing' (karaṇa) is the bodily action that is a transformation of the wind element produced by the seventh wholesome impulsion-consciousness of the mind-door process; it is said that the act that should be thrown or lifted by that bodily action. But from the ultimate perspective, it is just a collection of octads that arise from the vibration of the mind's speed element (javāyodhātu), which causes arrival in another place, which is designated as bodily action. The act of the water-throw boundary is the water-throw boundary act; this is absent in a river with a short source, which is the meaning here. This is the meaning: if a river is not too long, either half a yojana, a gāvuta, or half a gāvuta, from the place of its origin up to the riverbank, the Sangha sits covering everywhere in the river region. Then, in that river region, due to the absence of a complete river all around, there is no concern about acts requiring a quorum, so there is no act of a water-throw boundary; the river is only a boundary. This same determination has been stated in the Sāratthadīpanī and the Mātikāṭṭhakathā-Līnatthappakāsanī. But the Vimatavinodanī states that where there is no need for a seven-abbhantara boundary due to the absence of concern about acts requiring a quorum in a small forest that is complete with many monks, there, a seven-abbhantara boundary does not arise; it is only the forest boundary in which the Sangha should perform the act; the same method applies to rivers and so forth. For it will be said, 'If a river is not too long, the Sangha sits everywhere from the source up to the mouth; there is no act of a water-throwing boundary,' and so forth, in both cases. In whatever direction there is no opportunity for a seven-abbhantara boundary or a water-throw, how could there be measuring or throwing there? Because of entering into village land and so forth, a boundary that has entered into a non-area, a too-large boundary would be ruined. However, in the arising of a boundary due to need, where it is possible, there a seven-abbhantara water-throw boundary arises of itself completely. But where it is not possible, there it arises only according to the size of its own area, not beyond. And here, because it is stated, 'a seven-abbhantara water-throw boundary arises of itself completely due to the need for a boundary,' some say that a water-throw boundary arises due to the need for a boundary even without a water-throw; that appears to be inappropriate. Why? Because of contradiction with the Pali method, and also because of contradiction with the statement made later in the Vimatavinodanī. And this statement might have been made by the teacher to explain the view of some individuals. Why? Because it does not fit with the statement made later, and also because it contradicts the statements in the Pali commentaries and sub-commentaries. We will explain that later.

Pāḷiṃ aṭṭhakathañceva, ṭīkāvimatiādike;

Oloketvā punappunaṃ, maññantu kavipuṅgavāti;

Ayamettha udakukkhepasīmāya vicāraṇā.

Having repeatedly examined the Pāli, the commentaries, the sub-commentaries, resolutions of doubt, and the like, let the excellent poets consider this. Herein is the investigation concerning the water-casting boundary.

Janapadasīmā nāma ekassa rañño vijite pavatto mahāmaccānaṃ nivāsabhūto ekameko padeso janapadasīmā nāma.

A district boundary, so-called, is each single region that exists within the dominion of a single king and is the dwelling place of great ministers. Each such region is called a district boundary.

Raṭṭhasīmā nāma kāsikosalādikā soḷasa mahājanapadā. “Soḷasamahānagara”ntipi tesaṃ nāmaṃ. Tattha soḷasa mahājanapadāni nāma. Aṅgaraṭṭhaṃ, magadharaṭṭhaṃ, kosalaraṭṭhaṃ, vajjiraṭṭhaṃ, cetiyaraṭṭhaṃ, kururaṭṭhaṃ, pañcālaraṭṭhaṃ, majjharaṭṭhaṃ, surasenaraṭṭhaṃ, assakaraṭṭhaṃ, avantiraṭṭhaṃ, gandhālaraṭṭhaṃ, mallaraṭṭhaṃ, kambojaraṭṭhanti imāni soḷasamahājanapadāni nāma. Ime soḷasamahājanapadā majjhimapadeseyeva pavattā mahāraṭṭhā nāma tadaññepi sunāparantaraṭṭhādikā bahutarāva. Tathā paccantavisayepi rāmaññādikā anekappabhedā mahāraṭṭhā attheva tepi sabbe raṭṭhasīmā nāma. Lokiyasatthesu pana yasmiṃ padese khattiyabrāhmaṇavessasuddavasena catuvaṇṇāni vasanti, so padeso “mahāraṭṭho”ti pavuccati. Vuttañhe taṃ porāṇehi.

A country boundary, so-called, consists of the sixteen great regions such as Kāsi and Kosala. They are also called 'the sixteen great cities.' Therein, the sixteen great regions are: the Aṅga country, the Magadha country, the Kosala country, the Vajji country, the Cetiya country, the Kuru country, the Pañcāla country, the Majjha country, the Surasena country, the Assaka country, the Avanti country, the Gandhāra country, the Malla country, and the Kamboja country—these are the names of the sixteen great regions. These sixteen great regions, existing only in the middle country, are called great countries; and besides them, there are many others such as Sunāparanta country and so forth. Similarly, in the border regions, there are also many kinds of great countries like Rāmañña and so forth. All of these are indeed called country boundaries. However, in worldly treatises, any region where the four castes—khattiyas, brāhmaṇas, vessas, and suddas—dwell is called a 'great country.' For this has been stated by the ancients.

“Pavattā catuvaṇṇānaṃ, yasmiṃ padese na vijjate;

So milakkhudeso vutto, puññabhūmi tato para”nti.

“In whatever region the four castes are not found, that is called a barbarian land; the land other than that is the land of merit.”

Rajjasīmā nāma ekassa rañño āṇāpavattaṭṭhānaṃ rajjasīmā nāma.

A kingdom boundary, so-called, is the place where the command of a single king prevails.

Dīpasīmā nāma samuddantare vā nadimajjhe vā pavattā dīpā dīpasīmā nāma. Yasmiṃ pana dīpe gāmā vasanti, so gāmasīmātveva saṅkhyaṃ gacchati, vakkhati hi sukkhe jātassare vāpiṃ vā khaṇanti vappaṃ vā karonti, taṃ ṭhānaṃ gāmakhettaṃ hotīti.

An island boundary, so-called, refers to islands existing in the midst of the ocean or a river. However, if villages dwell on an island, it is reckoned simply as a village boundary. For it will be said: “On dry land or in a natural lake, should they dig a pond or make a sown field, that place becomes a village territory.”

Cakkavāḷasīmā nāma “āyāmato ca vitthārato ca yojanānaṃ dvādasasatasahassāni tīṇisahassāni cattārisatāni paññāsañcayojanāni.

A world-system boundary, so-called, measures in length and in breadth one million, two hundred and three thousand, four hundred and fifty yojanas.

Parikkhepato

In circumference:

“Sabbaṃ [M 0.0067] satasahassāni, chattiṃsaparimaṇḍalaṃ;

Dasañceva sahassāni, aḍḍhuḍḍhāni satānicā”ti.

“Thirty-six hundred-thousands, ten thousand, and three hundred and fifty in circumference.”

Vuttaṃ ekaṃ cakkavāḷaṃ cakkavāḷasīmā nāma. Evaṃ pannarasappabhedā hoti sīmā. Tattha iddhimā puggalo yasmiṃ yasmiṃ vā sīme uposathādisaṅghakammaṃ karoti, tattha tattha gate bhikkhū hatthapāsanayanaṃ vā bahisīmakaraṇaṃ vā kātuṃ chandārahānaṃ, chandaṃ āharitvā kātuṃ sakkuṇeyyabhāvoyeva pamāṇaṃ. Evamasakkonte aniddhimapuggale sandhāya atimahantāraññe samantā sattabbhantarasīmā anuññātā. Tattha sīmāpekkhāya saha samantā sattabbhantarasīmā attano sabhāveneva jātā tathā nadīsamuddajātassaresupi majjhimassa purisassa samantā udakukkhepasīmā anuññātā tatthapi vaggakammaparihāratthaṃ sīmāpekkhaṃ katvā udakukkhepena saha udakukkhepasīmā uppajjati tathā gāmanigamajanapadanagararaṭṭharajjasīmāsupi vaggakammaparihāratthaṃ baddhasīmā anuññātā. Tato paresu pana gāmasīmasattabbhantaraudakukkhepasīmā nuññātasuttānulomanayasāmatthiyato khuddakesu gāmanigamajanapadanagaresu tattha tattha gate bhikkhū sodhetvā chandārahānaṃ chandaṃ āharitvā samaggassa saṅghassa anumatiyāva uposathādisaṅghakammaṃ kātabbameva tathā khuddake araññepi nadījātassarepi yathāsukhaṃ uposathādisaṅghakammaṃ kātabbamevāti.

The aforementioned single world-system is called a world-system boundary. Thus, the boundary is of fifteen kinds. Therein, in whatever boundary a person with psychic power performs a Sangha act such as the Uposatha, the measure is simply the ability of the monks present to perform the act after bringing the consent of those worthy of it, either by bringing them within arm's reach or by placing them outside the boundary. If they are thus unable, with reference to persons without psychic power, in a very large wilderness, a boundary of seven abbhantaras all around is permitted. Therein, a boundary of seven abbhantaras all around arises by its own nature, along with the consideration of a boundary. Similarly, in rivers, oceans, and natural lakes, a water-casting boundary all around for a person of middle stature is permitted. There too, for the purpose of avoiding incomplete formal acts, having made consideration of the boundary, a water-casting boundary arises along with the casting of water. Likewise, in village, market-town, district, city, country, and kingdom boundaries, a fixed boundary is permitted for the purpose of avoiding incomplete formal acts. However, in other small villages, market-towns, districts, and cities, by the power of the method conforming to the suttas where village, seven-abbhantara, and water-casting boundaries are permitted, wherever monks have gone, having purified the assembly and brought the consent of those worthy of consent, a Sangha act such as the Uposatha must be performed only with the approval of a united Sangha. Similarly, in small wilderness areas, rivers, and natural lakes, a Sangha act such as the Uposatha must be performed as is suitable.

Evaṃ paricchindanalakkhaṇena abhedepi baddhābaddhavasena duvidhā. Tappabhedena pannarasavidhā, sarūpavasena pana khaṇḍasīmā, upacārasīmā, samānasaṃvāsasīmā, avippavāsasīmā, lābhasīmā, gāmasīmā. Nigamasīmā, nagarasīmā, abbhantarasīmā, araññasīmā, udakukkhepasīmā, nadīsīmā, jātassarasīmā, samuddasīmā, janapadasīmā, raṭṭhasīmā, rajjasīmā, dīpasīmā, cakkavāḷasīmātiekūnavīsappabhede pi sīme vipattilakkhaṇaṃ pahāya sampattilakkhaṇappakāsakaṃ sīmavisodhanīnāma pakaraṇaṃ samattaṃ.

Thus, by the characteristic of delimiting, though it is one, it is twofold by way of being bound or unbound. By its divisions, it is fifteenfold. However, in terms of their specific nature, there are: sectional boundaries, proximate boundaries, boundaries of common residence, boundaries of non-separation, boundaries of gain, village boundaries, market-town boundaries, city boundaries, seven-abbhantara boundaries, wilderness boundaries, water-casting boundaries, river boundaries, natural lake boundaries, ocean boundaries, district boundaries, country boundaries, kingdom boundaries, island boundaries, and world-system boundaries. The boundary being of these nineteen kinds, abandoning the characteristic of failure and revealing the characteristic of success, the treatise called 'The Purification of Boundaries' is complete.

Iti

Thus.

“Sīmavisodhaniṃ nāma, nānāganthasamāhaṭaṃ;

Karissaṃ me nisāmentu, sādhavo kavipuṅgavā.

“I shall compose the ‘Purification of Boundaries,’ compiled from various texts; may the virtuous and excellent poets listen to me.

Pāḷiṃ [M 0.0068] aṭṭhakathañceva, mātikāpadabhājaniṃ;

Ogāhetvāna taṃ sabbaṃ, punappunamasesato.

Having plunged repeatedly and without remainder into all of it—the Pāli, the commentaries, and the analysis of the matrix of terms;

Attanomatiganthesu, ṭīkāgaṇṭhipadesu ca;

Vinicchayavimatīsu, mātikāṭṭhakathāsupi.

In treatises of one's own opinion, in sub-commentaries and knot-points; in resolutions of doubts and uncertainties, and also in the Mātikā and Aṭṭhakathā.

Sabbaṃ asesakaṃ katvā, saṃsanditvāna ekato;

Pavattā vaṇṇanā esā, tosayantī vicakkhaṇe”ti.

Having made all complete and compared them together into one, this explanation proceeds, delighting the discerning.”

Imesaṃ gāthāpadānaṃ attho sabbaso saṃvaṇṇito hoti.

The meaning of these verse-sections has been fully explained.

Sīmasampattikathā niṭṭhitā.

The discourse on the accomplishment of boundaries is concluded.

Parisasampattināma ekavīsativajjanīyapuggale vajjetvā etarahi ñatticatutthakammena upasampannā bhikkhū paccantimesu janapadesu pañcavaggato paṭṭhāya, majjhimesu janapadesu dasavaggato paṭṭhāya sabbe hatthapāsaṃ avijahitvā nisinnā honti, ayaṃ parisasampatti nāma. Tattha ekavīsati vajjanīyā nāma “na bhikkhave saga ṭṭhāya parisāyā”ti vacanato heṭṭhā “na bhikkhave bhikkhuniyā nisinnaparisāya pātimokkhaṃ uddisitabba”nti ādinānayena vuttā bhikkhunī, sikkhamānā, sāmaṇero, sāmaṇerī, sikkhāpaccakkhātako, antimavatthuajjhāpannako, āpattiyā adassane ukkhittako, āpattiyā appaṭikamme ukkhittako, pāpikāya diṭṭhiyā appaṭinissagge ukkhittako, paṇḍako, theyyasaṃvāsako, titthiyapakkantako, tiracchānagato, māghātutako, pitughātako, arahantaghātako, bhikkhunidūsako, saṅghabhedako, lohituppādako, ubhatobyañjanakoti te bahisīmakaraṇavasena nisinnāti attho. Yadi te hatthapāse vā sīmeyeva vā honti, te avajjetvā pañcavaggādiko pakatattagaṇo hatthapāsaṃ avijahitvā nisinno hoti, kārakasaṅgho sāvajjo, kammaṃpana na kuppati kasmā avaggārahattā. Aparepana evaṃ vadanti antimavatthuajjhāpannassa avandanīyesu avuttattā, tena saddhiṃ sayantassa sahaseyyāpattiyā abhāvato tassa ca paṭiggahaṇassa rūhanatoti tadeva yuttataranti viññāyati, kiñcāpi yāva so bhikkhubhāvaṃ paṭijānāti, tāva vanditabbo. Yadā pana “assamaṇomhī”ti [M 0.0069] paṭijānāti, tadā na vanditabboti ayamettha viseso veditabbo. Antimavatthuajjhāpannassa hi bhikkhubhāvaṃ paṭijānantasseva bhikkhubhāvo, na tatoparaṃ. Bhikkhubhāvaṃ appaṭijānanto hi anupasampannapakkhaṃ bhajatīti majjhimagaṇṭhipade sāratthadīpaniyañca vuttattā antimavatthu ajjhāpannapubbo bhikkhu attano kāraṇaṃ ajānitvāva tena saha pañcavaggagaṇo uposathādisaṅghakammaṃ karoti, kārakasaṅgho anavajjo, kammampi na kuppatīti, tesaṃ matimattameva. Kasmā sahaseyyāpatti abhāvopi tassa bhikkhusaññāya patiṭṭhitattā, paṭiggahaṇaruhaṇassa ca tasmiṃ puggale bhikkhūti saddahitattā tena puggalena sahaseyyāpattipi natthi, paṭiggahaṇañca ruhati, ekavīsativajjanīyapuggalepi tathāvidhassa avicārikattā tena saha pañcavaggagaṇo uposathādisaṅghakammaṃ karonto kārakasaṅgho asañcicca anupavajjo. Kammaṃ pana kuppatīti amhākaṃ khanti, ito yuttataro labbhamāno pariyesitabbova.

Completeness of the assembly, so-called, is when, excluding the twenty-one individuals to be avoided, monks who are now fully ordained by a motion and three announcements—starting from a group of five in the border regions, and starting from a group of ten in the middle regions—are all seated without leaving arm's reach. Therein, the twenty-one individuals to be avoided are: a bhikkhunī, a female probationer, a male novice, a female novice, one who has renounced the training, one who has committed an ultimate offense, one suspended for not recognizing an offense, one suspended for not making amends for an offense, one suspended for not relinquishing a wrong view, a eunuch, one who lives by theft, one who has gone over to another sect, an animal, a matricide, a patricide, a murderer of an arahant, a violator of a bhikkhunī, a schism-maker, one who sheds a Buddha’s blood, and a hermaphrodite. The meaning is that these individuals are to be considered as seated outside the boundary. If they are within arm’s reach or within the boundary itself, and a normal group—starting from five—is seated without leaving arm’s reach, not counting them, the acting Sangha is at fault, but the act is not invalidated. Why? Because it does not constitute an invalidating factor for the quorum. Others, however, say that since one who has committed an ultimate offense is not mentioned among those who should not be venerated, and since there is no offense of co-residence with him, and because the acceptance of offerings made to him is valid, that indeed seems more reasonable. Although he should be venerated as long as he claims to be a monk, when he declares, “I am not a recluse,” then he should no longer be venerated. This distinction should be understood here. For one who has committed an ultimate offense, his status as a monk remains only as long as he claims it, and not beyond that. For one who does not claim to be a monk falls into the category of the unordained. As stated in the Majjhimagaṇṭhipada and the Sāratthadīpanī, if a monk who has previously committed an ultimate offense, unaware of his own condition, performs a Sangha act such as the Uposatha with a group of five, the acting Sangha is blameless, and the act is not invalidated—this is merely their opinion. Why? Because of the absence of the offense of co-residence, because the perception of him as a monk is established, and because the acceptance of offerings is valid due to believing that person to be a monk, there is no offense of co-residence with that person, and the acceptance of offerings is valid. Also, because such a person is not considered among the twenty-one individuals to be avoided, if a group of five performs a Sangha act such as the Uposatha with him, the acting Sangha is unintentionally blameless. However, that the act is invalidated is our view; if a more reasonable alternative is found, it should be sought.

Parisasampattikathā niṭṭhitā.

The discourse on the completeness of the assembly is concluded.

Evaṃ vatthuñattianusāvanasīmaparisasampattivasena pañcahi aṅgehi sampannoti pañcaṅgoti vā samaggena saṅghena ñatticatutthena kammena akuppena ṭhānārahena uparibhāvaṃ samāpannoti vā dvīhi kāraṇehi laddhanāmo ñatti catutthaupasampannabhāvo atidullabhova.

Thus, the state of full ordination by a motion and three announcements is indeed extremely rare. It is called 'five-factored' (pañcaṅga) for one of two reasons: either because it is endowed with five factors—the candidate, the motion, the announcements, the boundary, and the completeness of the assembly; or because one has attained a superior state through an unshakeable and fitting act with a motion and three announcements performed by a harmonious Sangha.

Evaṃ buddhuppādo dullabho, tato pabbajjā ca upasampadā cā ti imesaṃ tiṇṇaṃ padānaṃ atto vuttoyeva hoti.

Thus, the arising of a Buddha is rare, and rarer still are going forth and full ordination. The meaning of these three terms has thus been stated.

Iti sāgarabuddhittheraviracite sīmavisodhane

Thus in the Sīmavisodhana composed by the Elder Sāgarabuddhi.

Upasampadākaṇḍo paṭhamo paricchedo.

The Chapter on Full Ordination, the First Section.

2. Kappavināsakaṇḍo

2. The Section on the Destruction of the Aeon.

Idāni buddhuppādadullabhakathā

Now, the discourse on the rarity of a Buddha's arising:

“Buddho ca dullabho loke, saddhammasavanampi ca;

Saṅgho ca dullabho loke, sappurisāti dullabhā”ti.

"A Buddha is rare in the world, as is hearing the true Dhamma; the Sangha is rare in the world, and noble persons are rare."

Imissā [M 0.0070] gāthāya saṃvaṇṇanākkamo sampatto. Tattha imasmiṃ sattaloke okāsaloke vā sabbaññusammāsambuddho dullabhova tathā hesa loko saṅkhāraloko, sattaloko, okāsalokoti tippabhedo hoti tesaṃ sampattivipatti ca evaṃ veditabbā tattha lujjati palujjatīti lokoti vacanatthena sattaloko veditabbo. Lokiyanti patiṭṭhahanti ettha sattanikāyāti vacanatthena okāsaloko veditabbo. Tathā hesa sattā avakasanti etthātiokāsoti vuccati so bhūmivasena apāyabhūmi, kāmasugatibhūmi, rūpāvacarabhūmi, arūpāvacarabhūmiceti catubbidhā hoti. Tattha nirayaṃ, tiracchānayoni, pettivisayo, asurakāyo, ti catasso apāyabhūmi nāma. Manussā, cātumahārājikā, tāvatiṃsā, yāmā, tusitā, nimmānarati, paranimmitavasavattī ceti sattavidhā hoti kāmasugatibhūmi sāpanāyaṃ ekādasavidhāpi kāmataṇhā avacarati etthāti vacanatthena kāmāvacarabhūmi nāma. Brahmapārisajjā, brahmapurohitā, mahābrahmā ca paṭhamajjhānabhūmi idaṃ agginā pariggahitaṭṭhānaṃ. Parittābhā, appamāṇābhā, ābhassarā ca dutiyajjhānabhūmi idaṃ āpena pariggahitaṭṭhānaṃ. Parittasubhā, appamāṇasubhā, subhakiṇhā ca tatiyajjhānabhūmi idaṃ vātena pariggahitaṭṭhānaṃ, tesaṃ vipattiṃ parato vaṇṇayissāma. Vehapphalā, asaññasattā, suddhāvāsā ca catutthajjhānabhūmiceti rūpāvacarabhūmi soḷasavidhā hoti. Avihā, atappā, sudassā, sudassī, akaniṭṭhāceti suddhāvāsabhūmi pañcavidhā hoti. Ākāsānañcāyatanabhūmi, viññāṇañcāyatanabhūmi, ākiñcaññāyatanabhūmi, nevasaññānāsaññāyatanabhūmiceti catubbidhā hoti arūpabhūmi. Ettāvatā ekatiṃsappabhedāpi bhūmi avakasanti ettha sattanikāyāti vacanatthena okāsoti vuccati. Tattha aṭṭha mahānirayāni apāyabhūmi nāma. Tiracchānaṃ pettivisayo asurakāyoti imesaṃ visuṃ bhūmi nāma natthi, manussabhūmiyaṃyeva yattha katthaci araññavanapatthādīsu nibaddhavāsaṃ vasanti soyeva padeso tesaṃ bhūmi. Manussabhūmito dvitālīsasahassayojano yugandharappamāṇo sineruno pañcamāḷindho cātumahārājikabhūmi. Tatupari dvitālīsasahassayojanaṃ sinerumatthakaṃ tāvatiṃsānaṃ [M 0.0071] bhūmi. Tatupari dvetālīsasahassayojanaṃ ṭhānaṃ yāmānaṃ bhūmi. Iminā nayena yāva vasavattibhūmi dvitālīsasahassayojane tiṭṭhati, tasmā manussabhūmito yāva vasavattibhūmi dvesahassānipañcana hutāni duvesatasahassāni yojanāni honti, tenetaṃ vuccati.

Now comes the explanation of this verse. Here, in this sentient world or the world of space, an All-Knowing Perfectly Self-Enlightened One is indeed rare. For this world is threefold: the world of formations, the sentient world, and the world of space. Their success and failure should be understood thus: Therein, by the verbal meaning of 'loka' (world) as 'it is destroyed, it perishes,' the sentient world is to be understood. By the verbal meaning 'sentient groups are located and established here,' the world of space is to be understood. For here, beings find space—thus it is called 'space' (okāsa). It is fourfold by way of planes: the plane of misery, the plane of sensual happiness, the plane of form, and the formless plane. Therein, hell, the animal womb, the sphere of ghosts, and the host of demons—these are the four planes of misery. Humans, the realm of the Four Great Kings, the realm of the Thirty-Three, the Yāma realm, the Tusita realm, the Nimmānarati realm, and the Paranimmitavasavattī realm—these seven constitute the plane of sensual happiness. These eleven planes are also called the sensual-sphere plane by the meaning that sensual craving roams here. The Brahmā’s Retinue, the Brahmā’s Ministers, and the Great Brahmās constitute the first jhāna plane; this is the place encompassed by fire. The realm of Limited Radiance, the realm of Immeasurable Radiance, and the realm of Streaming Radiance constitute the second jhāna plane; this is the place encompassed by water. The realm of Limited Beauty, the realm of Immeasurable Beauty, and the realm of Unwavering Beauty constitute the third jhāna plane; this is the place encompassed by wind. We will describe their destruction later. The realm of Great Fruit, the realm of non-percipient beings, and the Pure Abodes constitute the fourth jhāna plane; thus the plane of form has sixteen divisions. The Avihā, Atappā, Sudassā, Sudassī, and Akaniṭṭhā—these five constitute the Pure Abodes plane. The base of infinite space, the base of infinite consciousness, the base of nothingness, and the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception—these four constitute the formless plane. Thus far, these thirty-one kinds of planes are called 'space' by the meaning that 'sentient groups have space here.' Therein, the eight great hells are called the plane of misery. For animals, the sphere of ghosts, and the host of demons, there is no separate plane; they live a settled existence wherever in the human plane, such as in forests and wildernesses—that very region is their plane. At a distance of forty-two thousand yojanas from the human plane is the fifth terrace of Mount Sineru, which is the size of Mount Yugandhara and is the plane of the Four Great Kings. Above that, at forty-two thousand yojanas, the summit of Mount Sineru is the plane of the Thirty-Three. Above that, at a place forty-two thousand yojanas higher, is the plane of the Yāma devas. In this way, up to the Paranimmitavasavattī plane, each stands at an interval of forty-two thousand yojanas. Thus, from the human plane up to the Paranimmitavasavattī plane is two hundred and fifty-two thousand yojanas. Therefore, it is said.

“Manussabhūmito yāva, bhūmi vasavattantarā;

Duve satasahassāni, pañcanahutameva ca.

From the human plane up to the plane of the Vasavatti deities, it is two hundred thousand and also fifty thousand yojanas.

Dvesahassañca madhikaṃ, yojanānaṃ pamāṇato;

Gaṇanā nāma bhūmīsu, saṅkhyā evaṃ pakāsitā”ti.

And an additional two thousand, by measure of yojanas; thus is the number, the counting in the realms, declared.

Tatupari brahmapārisajjādayo tayo brahmuno pañcapaññāsasatasahassa aṭṭhasahassayojane samatale ṭhāne tiṭṭhanti. Dutiyatatiyabhūmībrahmunopi tappamāṇesu samatalesu tiṭṭhanti. Catutthabhūmiyaṃ pana vehapphalaasaññasattā tappamāṇe samatale ṭhāne tiṭṭhanti. Tatuparisuddhāvāsabhūmiyo taṃtaṃ pamāṇe ṭhāne uparūpari tiṭṭhanti. Catassopi arūpabhūmiyo tappamāṇe ṭhāne uparūpari tiṭṭhanti. Ettāvatā ca manussabhūmito yāva bhavaggā yojanānaṃ sattakoṭi ca aṭṭhārasalakkhā ca pañcanahutāni ca chasahassāni ca honti tenetaṃ vuccati porāṇehi.

Above that, the three [classes of] Brahmās, the Brahmapārisajjā and so forth, reside on a level plane of five million, five hundred thousand, and eight thousand yojanas. The Brahmās of the second and third jhāna realms also reside on level planes of that same measure. In the fourth jhāna realm, however, the Vehapphala and Asaññasattā beings reside on a level plane of that same measure. Above that, the Suddhāvāsa realms are situated one above the other, each in a place of its respective measure. The four formless realms also stand one above the other, each in a place of that same measure. Thus, from the human realm up to the summit of existence (Bhavagga), the distance is seven crores, eighteen lakhs, fifty thousand, and six thousand yojanas; therefore, this is said by the ancients.

“Heṭṭhimā brahmalokamhā, patitā mahatī silā;

Ahorattena ekena, uggatā aṭṭhatālīsaṃ.

From the lowest Brahma world, a great rock, having fallen, travels forty-eight in a single day and night.

Yojanānaṃ sahassāni, cātumāsehi bhūmi;

Evaṃ vuttappamāṇena, sāyaṃ heṭṭhimabhūmi.

Thousands of yojanas; in four months, the earth is reached. By the measure thus stated, such is this lowest realm.

Ito satasahassāni, sattapaññāsa cāparaṃ;

Saṭṭhi ceva sahassāni, ubbedhena pakāsitā.

From here, one hundred thousand, and a further fifty-seven thousand, and also sixty thousand, are declared in height.

Yojanesupi vuttesu, hitvā kāmappamāṇakaṃ;

Sesāni vasavattīnaṃ, pārisajjāna mantaraṃ.

Among the yojanas mentioned, having set aside the measure of the desire realm, the remainder is the interval between the Vasavattī deities and the [Brahma] attendants.

Tañca pañcahi paññāsa, satasahassāni cāparaṃ;

Aṭṭha ceva sahassāni, yojanāni pavuccare.

And that is declared to be five million, five hundred thousand, and a further eight thousand yojanas.

Ito parāsu sabbāsu, brahmabhūmīsu yojanā;

Tappamāṇāva daṭṭhabbā, nayaggāhena dhīmatā.

In all the Brahma realms beyond this, they should be understood by the wise, through grasping the principle, to be of that same measure in yojanas.

Bhūmito [M 0.0072] ābhavaggamhā, sattakoṭi aṭṭhārasa;

Lakkhā pañca nahutāni, chasahassāni sabbadāti.

From the earth to the summit of existence, it is seven crores, eighteen lakhs, fifty thousand, and six thousand in all, always.

Esā ca vicāraṇā ṭīkā cariyamatena katā;

Idameva sandhāya, yāvatā candimasūriyā.

And this investigative commentary was made according to the view of the teachers, with reference to this saying: 'As far as the moon and sun...

Pariharanti disā bhanti, virocamānā yāvatā;

Tāva sahassadhā loke, ettha te vattatī vaso”ti.

...revolve, illuminating the directions, shining as far as they go—so far extends the thousandfold world, and therein your power prevails.'

Vuttaṃ. Etthantare sattā tiṭṭhanti, tesaṃ vitthāro apubbaṃ katvā kathetuṃ asakkuṇeyyattā na vakkhāma imasmiṃ sattaloke okāsaloke ca sabbaññusammāsambuddhova dullabho, tathā hi cattāro buddhā anubuddho, sāvakabuddho, paccekabuddho, sammāsambuddhoti. Tattha bahussutaṃ bhikkhuṃ pasaṃsantena ca na so tumhākaṃ sāvako nāma, buddhonāmesa cundāti bahussutassa bhikkhuno buddhabhāvaṃ anujānantena ca.

It is said. In this interval, beings exist, but because it is impossible to describe their extent without doing so in an unprecedented way, we will not speak of it. In this world of beings and world of space, an Omniscient, Perfectly Self-Enlightened One is indeed rare. For there are four [types of] Buddhas: the Anubuddha, the Sāvakabuddha, the Paccekabuddha, and the Sammāsambuddha. Therein, when praising a learned monk, one does not say, 'He is your disciple,' but rather, by saying, 'This, Cunda, is indeed a Buddha,' one acknowledges the Buddha-state of that learned monk.

Dhammakāyo yato satthā, dhammo satthukāyo mato;

Dhammāsikoso saṅgho ca, satthusaṅkhyampi gacchatīti.

Because the Teacher has the Dhamma as his body, the Dhamma is considered the Teacher's body; and the Sangha, the treasury of the Dhamma, is also reckoned as the Teacher.

Vutte puthujjanopi upacāravasena vācanāmaggassa bodhattā anubuddho nāma. Ariyasāvako pana parato yosavasena cattāri saccāni bujjhati bujjhanamattameva vāti vacanatthena bujjhanasabhāvattā sāvakabuddho nāma. Yo pana khaggavisāṇakappo sayambhūñāṇena anaññabodhako hutvā sāmaṃ bujjhanatthena paccekabujjhattā paccekabuddho nāma. Sambuddhotipi etasseva nāmaṃ. Yo pana saṅkhatāsaṅkhatappabhedaṃ sakalampi dhammajātaṃ yāthāvasarasalakkhaṇappaṭivedhavasena sammā pakārena sayaṃ pacitupāramitāsayambhūtena sayambhūñāṇena sayameva anaññabodhito hutvā savāsanasammohaniddāya accantaṃ vigato dinakarakiraṇasamāgamena paramarucirasirīsobhaggappattiyā vikasitamiva padumaṃ aggamaggañāṇasamāgamena aparimitaguṇagaṇālaṅkatasabbaññutaññāṇappattiyā sabbadhamme bujjhi abujjhi aññāsīti sammāsambuddho, bhagavā, sova loke dullabho paccekasambuddhānampi hi ekasmiṃ kāle satasahassādivasena ekato pavattattā te dullabhāpi anacchariyajātattā paresaṃ maggaphalādhigamāya upanissayarahitattā ca sammāsambuddhova loke dullabho, ayaṃ lokasampattivicāraṇā.

When spoken of, even a worldling is called an 'anubuddha' by way of figurative speech, due to awakening to the path of speech. A noble disciple, however, is called a 'sāvakabuddha' due to having the nature of awakening, by the meaning of the word 'awakens'—that is, he awakens to the Four Truths through the instruction of another, or he simply awakens. But one who, like a rhinoceros horn, having become one who is not taught by another, awakens by himself through self-originating knowledge, is called a 'paccekabuddha' because of awakening for oneself. 'Sambuddha' is also a name for this one. But the one who—having penetrated all phenomena in their entirety, with their divisions of conditioned and unconditioned, according to their individual characteristics as they are—by means of self-originating knowledge that arose from the perfections he himself properly accumulated, having become one who awakens by himself without being taught by another, has completely overcome the sleep of delusion with its latent tendencies; who, like a lotus blossomed with supreme, lovely, glorious beauty at the touch of the sun's rays, has, through the attainment of the knowledge of the highest path, reached the knowledge of omniscience, adorned with a multitude of immeasurable qualities; who understood, fully understood, and knew all things—he is the Sammāsambuddha, the Blessed One. He alone is rare in the world. For even Paccekabuddhas, though rare, are not of a wondrous nature, since they can arise together in a single era by the hundreds of thousands, and because they lack the supporting condition for others to attain the path and its fruit. Therefore, the Sammāsambuddha alone is rare in the world. This is the investigation of worldly attainment.

Kathaṃ [M 0.0073] lokassa vipatti veditabbā tathā hesa loko vinassamāno tejenapi āpenapi vāyunāpi vinassati. Tassa vitthāro visuddhimaggādīsu vuccamānopi kesañci puggalānaṃ matena micchāgāhattā brahmūnaṃ āyunā minitepi asamānaṃ saṃvaṭṭasīmāpi viruddhā, tasmā tesaṃ vādaṃ apanetvā ganthato samānetvā vakkhāma. Tattha cattāri asaṅkhyeyyāni. Katamāni cattāri asaṅkhyeyyāni saṃvaṭṭo, saṃvaṭṭaṭṭhāyī, vivaṭṭo, vaṭṭaṭṭhāyīti. Tayo saṃvaṭṭā āposaṃvaṭṭo, tejosaṃvaṭṭo, vāyosaṃvaṭṭo. Tisso saṃvaṭṭasīmā ābhassarā, subhakiṇhā, vehapphalāti. Ayamettha uddeso. Tattha saṃvaṭṭo nāma parihāyamāno kappo. Tena saṃvaṭṭaṭṭhāyīpi gahito hoti, taṃ mūlakattā. Vaḍḍhayamāno vivaṭṭakappo nāma. Tena vivaṭṭaṭṭhāyīpi gahito taṃ mūlakattā. Tattha saṃvaṭṭoti vināso. Yadā kappo vinassamāno tejena saṃvaṭṭati, ābhassarato heṭṭhā paṭhamajjhānabhūmi agginā dayhati. Yadā āpena saṃvaṭṭati, subhakiṇhato heṭṭhā yāva dutiyajjhānabhūmi udakena vilīyati. Yadā vāyunā saṃvaṭṭati, vehapphalato heṭṭhā yāva tatiyajjhānabhūmi vātena saṃvaṭṭati. Vitthārato pana jātikhetta āṇākhettavisayakhettavasena tiṇṇaṃ buddhakhettānaṃ āṇākhettaṃ vinassati. Tasmiṃ vinaṭṭhe jātikhettampi vinassateva. Tattha jātikhettaṃ dasasahassacakkavāḷapariyantaṃ hoti. Taṃ tathāgatassa paṭisandhiggahaṇaāyusaṅkhārossajjanādikālesupi kampati. Āṇākhettaṃ koṭisatasahassacakkavāḷapariyantaṃ. Yattha ratanasuttaṃ khandhaparittaṃ dhajaggaparittaṃ āṭānāṭiyaparittaṃ moraparittanti imesaṃ parittānaṃ ānubhāvo pavattati. Visayakhettaṃ anantaṃ aparimāṇaṃ. Tattha jātikhettaāṇākhettāvasena dve khettāni ekato vinassanti. Saṇṭhahantampi ekato saṇṭhahati. Tassa vināsoca saṇṭhahantañca evaṃ veditabbaṃ yasmiṃ samaye kappo agginā nassati, tadā ādito kappavināsakamahāmegho vuṭṭhahitvā koṭisatasahassacakkavāḷaṃ ekameghavassaṃ vasi, tuṭṭhā manussā bījāni vappenti, sassesu gokhāyitamattesu jātesu gadrabharavaṃ viravanto ekabindupi na patati. Yadā pacchinnameva hoti tadā vassūpajīvino sattā kālaṃ katvā parittābhesu brahmabhūmīsu [M 0.0074] uppajjanti puññaphalūpajīvinopi devatā tattheva brahmaloke uppajjanti, evaṃ dīghassa addhuno accayena tattha tattha udakaṃ parikkhayaṃ gacchati, macchakacchapādi udakanissitā pāṇā kālaṃ katvā manussadevalokesu nibbattitvā jhānaṃ uppādetvā brahmaloke nibbattanti. Nerayikasattāpi sattamasūriyapātubhāvā vinassanti, tepi devaloke paṭiladdhajjhānavasena brahmaloke uppajjanti, tadā hi lokabyūhā nāma kāmāvacaradevā ukkhittasirā vikiṇṇakesā rudamukhā assūni hatthehi muñcamānā rattavatthanivatthā ativiya virūpavesadhārino hutvā manussapathe vicarantā evaṃ ārocenti “mārisā bho ito vassasata sahassaccayena kappavuṭṭhānaṃ bhavissati ayaṃ loko vinassissati mahāsamuddopisussissati ayañca mahāpathavī sinerupabbatarājā ḍayhissati vinassissati yāvabrahmalokā vināso bhavissati mettaṃ mārisā bhāvetha. Karuṇaṃ, muditaṃ, upekkhaṃ mārisā bhāvetha mātaraṃ upaṭṭhahatha, pitaraṃ upaṭṭhahatha, kule jeṭṭhāpacāyino hothā”ti. Tesaṃ vacanaṃ sutvā yebhuyyena manussā ca bhummadevatā ca saṃvegajātā aññamaññaṃ muducittā hutvā mettādīni puññāni karitvā devaloke nibbattanti. Tattha sudhābhojanaṃ bhuñjitvā vāyo parikammaṃ katvā jhānaṃ paṭilabhanti, evaṃ nerayikasattāpi aparāpariyakammena devalokaṃ nibbattanti. Ye pana niyatamicchādiṭṭhikā tesaṃ kammassa aparikkhayā aññacakkavāḷesu attano kammānurūpaṃ vipākamanubhonti. Teneva ahosikamme micchādiṭṭhivasena akattabbaṃ nāma pāpaṃ natthi, yato saṃsārakhāṇubhāvova nāma hotīti āha “diṭṭhi paramāni bhikkhave vajjānī”ti. “Aparāpariyavedanīyakammarahitopi saṃsaranto satto nāma natthī”ti vuttattā. “Aparāpariyakammavasena yato micchādiṭṭhisamādānato sappurisūpanissayavasena virājetvā bhavato vuṭṭhānaṃ nāma bhaveyyāti amhākaṃ khanti. Evaṃ devaloke paṭiladdhajjhānavasena sabbepi brahmaloke nibbattanti. Vassupacchedato pana uddhaṃ dīghassa addhuno accayena dutiyo sūriyo pātubhavati. Pātubhūte ca tasmiṃ neva rattiparicchedo, na divā paricchedo paññāyati, eko sūriyo uṭṭheti, eko sūriyo atthaṃ gacchati. Avicchinnasūriyasantāpova loko hoti.

How should the world's dissolution be understood? For this world, when it dissolves, perishes by fire, by water, or by wind. Although its detailed explanation is given in the Visuddhimagga and other texts, in the opinion of some individuals, due to wrong grasping, even the lifespan of Brahmās is measured incorrectly, and the boundaries of dissolution are contradictory. Therefore, setting aside their assertions and reconciling them with the texts, we shall explain. Here, there are four incalculable eons. What are these four incalculable eons? The eon of dissolution (saṃvaṭṭa-kappa), the eon of the duration of dissolution (saṃvaṭṭaṭṭhāyī-kappa), the eon of evolution (vivaṭṭa-kappa), and the eon of the duration of evolution (vivaṭṭaṭṭhāyī-kappa). There are three types of dissolution: dissolution by water (āposaṃvaṭṭa), dissolution by fire (tejosaṃvaṭṭa), and dissolution by wind (vāyosaṃvaṭṭa). There are three boundaries of dissolution: the Ābhassara Brahmā-world, the Subhakiṇhā Brahmā-world, and the Vehapphalā Brahmā-world. This is the summary here. Here, 'saṃvaṭṭa' refers to the eon that is declining. By this, the duration of dissolution (saṃvaṭṭaṭṭhāyī) is also understood, as it is its root. The eon that is expanding is called 'vivaṭṭa-kappa.' By this, the duration of evolution (vivaṭṭaṭṭhāyī) is also understood, as it is its root. Here, 'saṃvaṭṭa' means dissolution. When the eon dissolves by fire, the first jhāna plane below the Ābhassara Brahmā-world is burnt up by fire. When it dissolves by water, the realms up to the second jhāna plane below the Subhakiṇhā Brahmā-world dissolve in water. When it dissolves by wind, the realms up to the third jhāna plane below the Vehapphalā Brahmā-world are scattered by wind. In detail, among the three Buddha-fields—the field of birth (jātikhetta), the field of authority (āṇākhetta), and the field of range (visayakhetta)—it is the field of authority that perishes. When it perishes, the field of birth also perishes. Here, the field of birth extends to ten thousand world-systems. It trembles at such times as the Tathāgata’s conception, his renunciation of the life-principle, and so forth. The field of authority extends to a hundred thousand koṭis of world-systems, where the power of protective chants such as the Ratana Sutta, Khandha Paritta, Dhajagga Paritta, Āṭānāṭiya Paritta, and Mora Paritta operates. The field of range is infinite and immeasurable. Here, the two fields—the field of birth and the field of authority—perish together and re-establish together. Their dissolution and re-establishment should be understood thus: When the eon is dissolved by fire, first the great eon-dissolving rain cloud arises and rains over a hundred thousand koṭis of world-systems as a single downpour. Delighted, people sow seeds, but when the crops have grown only enough for a cow to eat, the cloud brays like a donkey, and not a single drop falls thereafter. When the rain is completely cut off, beings who depend on rain die and are reborn in the Parittābha Brahmā-world. Devas who live on the fruit of merit also arise there in the Brahmā-world. Thus, after a long time, the water gradually dries up here and there. Fish, turtles, and other water-dependent creatures die and, being reborn in the human or deva realms, develop jhāna and are reborn in the Brahmā-world. Hell beings also perish with the appearance of the seventh sun, and they too, having attained jhāna in the deva-world, are reborn in the Brahmā-world. Then, the Kāmāvacara devas called Lokabyūhā, with disheveled hair, weeping faces, wiping away tears with their hands, wearing red garments, and appearing exceedingly disfigured, wander among humans, announcing thus: 'Sirs, friends, in a hundred thousand years from now, the eon will end. This world will perish, the great ocean will dry up, and this great earth and Mount Sineru, the king of mountains, will burn and perish. There will be dissolution up to the Brahmā-world. Sirs, friends, cultivate loving-kindness, compassion, appreciative joy, and equanimity. Attend to your mothers, attend to your fathers, be respectful to the elders in your families.' Hearing their words, most humans and earth-bound devas become spiritually stirred, develop tender hearts towards each other, perform meritorious deeds such as cultivating loving-kindness, and are reborn in the deva-world. There, having eaten divine food and performed the preliminary work for the wind kasina, they attain jhāna. Similarly, hell beings, through subsequent kamma, are reborn in the deva-world. But those with fixed wrong views, due to the inexhaustibility of their kamma, experience results in other world-systems according to their deeds. For this reason, by means of wrong view, there is no evil deed that is not done, for it is indeed a stump in saṃsāra, as it is said: 'Wrong view, monks, is the worst of faults.' For it has been said: 'There is no being wandering in saṃsāra without kamma to be experienced in subsequent lives.' And because it has been said: 'By means of kamma to be experienced in subsequent lives, by abandoning the adoption of wrong views through reliance on good people, there would be an escape from becoming' — this is our conviction. Thus, all beings, having attained jhāna in the deva-world, are reborn in the Brahmā-world. However, after the cessation of rain, after a long period, a second sun appears. And when it appears, no distinction between night and day is discerned. One sun rises, and another sets. The world becomes one of uninterrupted heat from the suns.

Pakatisūriye [M 0.0075] sūriyadevaputto atthi kappavināsakasūriye pana natthi. Pakatisūriyobhāsena ākāse valāhakā dhūmasikhāpi caranti. Kappavināsakasūriyobhāsena vigatadhūmavalāhakaṃ ādāsamaṇḍalaṃ viya nimmalaṃ hoti nabhaṃ ṭhapetvā gaṅgā, yamunā, sarabhū, aciravatī, mahīti, imā pañca mahānadiyo ṭhapetvā avasesapañcasatakunnadīādīsu udakaṃ sussati tato dīghassa addhuno accayena tatiyo sūriyo pātubhavati tasmiṃ pātubhūte pañca mahānadiyopi sussanti. Tato dīghassa addhuno accayena catuttho sūriyo pātubhavati. Yassa pātubhāvā himavatī mahānadīnaṃ pabhavā sīhapatano, haṃsapatano, mandākinī, kaṇṇamuṇḍako, rathakāradaho, anotattadaho, chaddantadaho, kuṇāladahoti ime satta mahāsarā sussanti. Tato dīghassa addhuno accayena pañcamo sūriyo pātubhavati, yassa pātubhāvā anupubbena mahāsamuddo aṅgulipabbatemanamattampi udakaṃ na saṇṭhāti. Tato dīghassa addhuno accayena chaṭṭho sūriyo pātubhavati, yasmiṃ pātubhāve sakalacakkavāḷaṃ ekadhūmaṃ hoti, pariyādinnasinehadhūmena yathā ca evaṃ koṭisatasahassacakkavāḷampi. Tatopi dīghassa addhuno accayena sattamo sūriyo pātubhavati, yassa pātubhāvā sakalacakkavāḷaṃ ekajālaṃ hoti saddhiṃ koṭisatasahassacakkavāḷehi yojanasatikādibhedā sinerukūṭāni palujjitvā ākāseyeva antaradhāyanti. Sāpi aggijālā uṭṭhahitvā cātumahārājike gaṇhāti, tattha kanakavimānaratanavimānāni jhāpetvā tāvatiṃsabhavanaṃ gaṇhāti etenūpāyena yāva paṭhamajjhānabhūmi gaṇhāti. Tattha tayopi brahmaloke jhāpetvā ābhassare āhacca aṭṭhāsi. Sā yāva aṇumattampi saṅkhāragataṃ atthi, tāva nanibbāyi. Sabbasaṅkhāraparikkhayā pana sappitelajhāpanaaggisikhā viya chārikampi anavasesetvā nibbāyi. Heṭṭhā ākāsena saha upari ākāso eko hoti mahandhakāro. Evaṃ kappavināsakamahāmeghato yāvajālaparicchedā saṃvaṭṭo nāma ekamasaṅkhyeyyaṃ nāma. Atha dīghassa addhuno accayena mahāmegho vuṭṭhahitvā paṭhamaṃ sukhumaṃ vassati. Anupubbena kumudanāḷayaṭṭhimusalatālakkhandhādippamāṇāhi dhārāhi vassanto [M 0.0076] koṭisatasahassacakkavāḷesu sabbaḍaḍḍhaṭṭhānaṃ pūretvā antaradhāyati. Taṃ udakaṃ heṭṭhā ca tiriyañca vāto samuṭṭhahitvā ghanaṃ karoti. Parivaṭumaṃ paduminipatte udakabindusadisaṃ. Evaṃ aggijālanibbāyanato yāva koṭisatasahassacakkavāḷaparipūrato sampattimahāmegho, idaṃ saṃvaṭṭaṭṭhāyī nāma, dutiyamasaṅkhyeyyaṃ nāma. Kathaṃ tāva mahantaṃ udakarāsiṃ vāto ghanaṃ karotīti ce. Vivarasampadānato taṃ vā tena piṇḍiyamānaṃ ghanaṃ kariyamānaṃ parikkhayamānaṃ anupubbena heṭṭhā otarati. Otiṇṇotiṇṇe udake pubbe brahmalokaṭṭhāne brahmapāri sajja-brahmapurohitamahābrahmāvasena paṭhamajjhānabhūmi paṭhamaṃ pātubhavati. Tato otiṇṇotiṇṇe udake catukāmāvacaradevalokaṭṭhāne anukkamena paranimmitavasavattī, nimmānaratī, tusitā, yāmāti cattāro kāmāvacaradevalokā pātubhavanti. Tato purimapathaviṭṭhānaṃ otiṇṇe pana udake balavavātā uppajjanti. Te taṃ pidahitadvāre dhamakaraṇe ṭhitaudakamiva nirussāsaṃ katvā nirujjhanti. Madhurodakaṃ parikkhayaṃ gacchamānaṃ upari rasapathaviṃ samuṭṭhāpeti sā vaṇṇasampannāceva hoti gandharasasampannā ca, nirudakapāyāsassa upari paṭalaṃ viya tadā ca ābhassarabrahmaloke paṭhamatarābhinibbattā sattā āyukkhayā vā puññakkhayā vā tato cavitvā idhūpapajjanti. “Puññakkhayā vā”ti iminā brahmūnaṃ āyuparicchedaṃ appatvāva kammakkhayena maraṇaṃ viññāyati. Te honti sayaṃpabhā antalikkhacarā. Te aggaññasutte vuttanayena taṃ rasapathaviṃ sāyitvā taṇhābhibhūtā āluppakāraṃ paribhuñjituṃ upakkamanti. Atha nesaṃ sayaṃ pabhā antaradhāyati, andhakāro hoti te andhakāraṃ disvā bhāyanti. Tato nesaṃ bhayaṃ nāsetvā sūrabhāvaṃ janayantaṃ paripuṇṇapaṇṇāsayojanaṃ sūriyamaṇḍalaṃ vā pātubhavati. Te taṃ disvā “ālokaṃ paṭilabhimhā”ti haṭṭhatuṭṭhā hutvā “amhākaṃ bhayaṃ nāsetvā sūrabhāvaṃ janayanto uṭṭhito, tasmā sūriyo hotū”ti sūriyo tvevassa nāmaṃ karonti. Atha sūriye divasaṃ ālokaṃ katvā atthaṅgate “yampi ālokaṃ labhimhā, sopi no naṭṭho”ti puna bhītā honti. Tesaṃ evaṃ hoti “sādhuvatassa aññaṃ ālokaṃ labheyyāmā”ti. Tesaṃ cittaṃ ñatvā viya ekūnapaññāsayojanaṃ candamaṇḍalaṃ pātubhavati[M 0.0077]. Te taṃ disvā bhiyyosomattāya haṭṭhatuṭṭhā hutvā “amhākaṃ chandaṃ ñatvā viya uṭṭhito, tasmā cando hotū”ti candotvevassa nāmaṃ karonti. Tato pabhuti rattidivā paññāyanti. Anukkamena ca māsaddhamāsautusaṃvaccharā. Candimasūriyānaṃ pana pātubhūtadivaseyeva sinerucakkavāḷahimavantapabbatā pātubhavanti, te ca kho apubbaṃ acarimaṃ phagguṇapuṇṇamadivaseyeva pātubhavanti. Kathaṃ yathānāma kaṅgubhatte paccamāne ekappahāreneva bubbuḷakāni uṭṭhahanti. Eke padesā thūpathūpā honti, eke ninnaninnā, eke samasamā evameva thūpathūpaṭṭhāne pabbatā honti, ninnaninnaṭṭhāne samuddā, samasamaṭṭhāne dīpāti evaṃ manussaloke saṇṭhite cātumahārājikā, tāvatiṃsāti dve kāmāvacaradevalokā pacchato pātubhavanti. Bhūmanissitā nāma hete dve devalokā. Evaṃ sampattimahāmeghato yāva candimasūriyapātubhāvo, idaṃ vivaṭṭo nāma tatiyamasaṅkhyeyyaṃ nāma. Atha tesaṃ sattānaṃ rasapathaviṃ paribhuñjantānaṃ kammena ekacce vaṇṇavanto, ekacce dubbaṇṇā honti tattha vaṇṇavanto dubbaṇṇe atimaññanti. Tesaṃ mānātimānapaccayā sāpi rasapathavī antaradhāyati. Bhūmipappaṭako pātubhavati atha nesaṃ teneva nayena sopi antaradhāyati, patālatā pātubhavati, teneva nayena sāpi antaradhāyati. Akaṭṭhapāko sāli pātubhavati akaṇo athuso suddho sugandho taṇḍulapphalo. Tato nesaṃ bhājanāni uppajjanti te sālī bhājane ṭhapetvā pāsāṇapiṭṭhiyā ṭhapenti, sayameva jālasikhā uṭṭhahitvā pacati. So hoti odano sumanajātipupphasadiso, na tassa sūpena vā byañjanena vā karaṇīyaṃ atthi. Yaṃyaṃ rasaṃ bhuñjitukāmā honti, taṃtaṃ rasova hoti. Tesaṃ taṃ oḷārikaṃ āhāraṃ āharataṃ tato pabhuti muttakarīsaṃ sañjāyati. Atha nesaṃ tassa nikkhamanatthāya vaṇamukhāni pabhijjanti. Purisassa purisabhāvo, itthiyā itthibhāvo pātubhavati. Tatrasudaṃ itthī purisaṃ, puriso ca itthiṃ ativelaṃ upanijjhāyati. Tesaṃ ativelaṃ upanijjhāyanabhāvā kāmapariḷāho uppajjati. Tato methunadhammaṃ paṭisevanti. Te asaddhammapaṭisevanapaccayā viññūhi garahiyamānā viheṭhiyamānā tassa asaddhammassa paṭicchādanahetu agārāni karonti.

In the ordinary sun, there is a sun deity, but in the world-destroying sun, there is none. By the light of the ordinary sun, clouds and plumes of smoke move in the sky. By the light of the world-destroying sun, the sky becomes clear like a polished mirror, free of smoke and clouds. Leaving aside the five great rivers—the Ganges, Yamuna, Sarabhu, Aciravati, and Mahi—the water in the remaining five hundred small rivers and so on dries up. After a long time passes, a third sun appears. When it appears, the five great rivers also dry up. After another long period, a fourth sun appears. Upon its appearance, the sources of the great rivers in the Himalayas, namely the seven great lakes, dry up: Sihapatana, Hamsapatana, Mandakini, Kannamundaka, Rathakaradaha, Anotattadaha, and Chaddantadaha. After yet another long time, a fifth sun appears. When it appears, the great ocean gradually loses its water until not even a finger-joint’s depth remains. After another long period, a sixth sun appears. When it appears, the entire world-system becomes a single plume of smoke, as do a hundred thousand koṭis of world-systems. Then, after another long time, a seventh sun appears. Upon its appearance, the entire world-system becomes a single blaze, together with a hundred thousand koṭis of world-systems. The peaks of Mount Sineru, of various sizes from a hundred yojanas upwards, crumble and vanish into the sky. That flame spreads upward, seizing the realm of the Four Great Kings, and having burned their golden and jeweled mansions, it seizes the Tāvatiṃsa abode. In this way, it ascends until it seizes the first jhāna-plane. There, having burned the three Brahma worlds, it stops upon reaching the Ābhassara realm. The fire is not extinguished so long as even the smallest conditioned thing exists. But upon the complete exhaustion of all formations, it is extinguished without leaving behind even ash, like a flame consuming ghee and oil. The space below becomes one with the space above, and there is a great darkness. Thus, from the world-destroying great cloud up to the end of the conflagration, this is called the period of contraction (saṃvaṭṭa), lasting one incalculable age. Then, after a long time has passed, a great cloud arises and first rains finely. Gradually, raining with streams the size of lotus stalks, pestles, and palm trunks, it fills all the burnt places in the hundred thousand koṭis of world-systems and then vanishes. A wind arises and makes that water below and across dense, rounded like a drop of water on a lotus leaf. Thus, from the extinguishing of the fire up to the complete filling of the hundred thousand koṭis of world-systems by the great cloud, this is called the period of remaining-in-contraction (saṃvaṭṭaṭṭhāyī), lasting a second incalculable age. If it is asked how the wind makes such a vast mass of water dense: by providing an outlet, that water, being compressed by the wind, made dense, and diminished, gradually descends. As the water descends, the first jhāna-plane first appears in the former place of the Brahma world, with Brahma's Retinue, Brahma's Ministers, and the Great Brahmas. Then, as the water continues to descend, the four desire-sphere deva worlds appear in sequence in their former place: Paranimmitavasavattī, Nimmānaratī, Tusita, and Yāma. Then, when the water has descended to the former place of the earth, strong winds arise. They hold the water in check, making it breathless like water in a water-clock with a closed opening. As the sweet water diminishes, a savory earth forms on top, like the skin on top of milk-rice as it cools, endowed with color, scent, and taste. At that time, beings first reborn in the Ābhassara Brahma world, due to the exhaustion of their lifespan or the exhaustion of their merit, fall from there and are reborn here. The phrase “or due to the exhaustion of merit” indicates that Brahmas may die through the exhaustion of their kamma without having reached the limit of their lifespan. They are self-luminous, traversing the sky. As described in the Aggañña Sutta, they taste that savory earth and, overcome by craving, begin to consume it in lumps. Then their self-luminosity vanishes, and darkness arises. Seeing the darkness, they are afraid. Then, to dispel their fear and generate courage, a sun-disc, fully fifty yojanas wide, appears. Seeing it, they become joyful and delighted, thinking, “We have obtained light!” They give it the name ‘Sūriya,’ saying, “It has arisen dispelling our fear and generating courage (sūrabhāvaṃ), therefore let it be ‘Sūriya’!” Then, when the sun, having made light for the day, has set, they are afraid again, thinking, “The light we obtained is now lost to us!” It occurs to them, “Oh, would that we might get another light!” As if knowing their thought, a moon-disc, forty-nine yojanas wide, appears. Seeing it, they, being even more joyful and delighted, give it the name ‘Canda,’ saying, “It has arisen as if knowing our wish (chanda), therefore let it be ‘Canda’!” From then on, night and day are known, and in sequence, months, half-months, seasons, and years. Moreover, on the very day the moon and sun appeared, Mount Sineru, the Cakkavāḷa range, and the Himalaya mountains appear; and they appear neither before nor after, but on the very full moon day of Phagguṇa. How? Just as when millet-gruel is cooking, bubbles rise up all at once—some places become raised, some sunken, some level. Just so, in the raised places, mountains form; in the sunken places, oceans; and in the level places, continents. Thus, when the human world is established, the two desire-sphere deva worlds, Cātumahārājika and Tāvatiṃsa, appear afterwards, for these two deva worlds are called 'earth-dependent'. Thus, from the great cloud's arrival up to the appearance of the moon and sun, this is called the period of expansion (vivaṭṭa), lasting a third incalculable age. Then, as those beings consume the savory earth, through their kamma, some become fair-complexioned, some ill-complexioned. Therein, the fair-complexioned despise the ill-complexioned. Due to their conceit and contempt, that savory earth vanishes. A soil-scum appears. Then, in the same way, that too vanishes. A creeping plant appears. In the same way, that too vanishes. Self-growing rice appears—without bran or husk, pure, fragrant, with rice grains as its fruit. Then vessels appear for them. They place the rice in a vessel, set it on a stone slab, and a tongue of flame arises by itself and cooks it. It becomes cooked rice like jasmine flowers, needing no soup or curry. Whatever taste they wish to eat, it has that very taste. As they consume this coarse food, from then on, urine and feces are produced. Then, for its discharge, orifices break forth. The male state appears for the male, and the female state for the female. Thereupon a woman gazes intently at a man, and a man at a woman. Because of their intent gazing, a burning of passion arises. Then they engage in sexual intercourse. On account of practicing this wrong thing, being censured and harassed by the wise, they build houses to conceal it.

Te [M 0.0078] agāraṃ ajjhāvasamānā anukkamena aññatarassa alasajātikassa sattassa diṭṭhānugatiṃ āpajjantā sannidhiṃ karonti. Tato pabhuti kaṇopi thusopi taṇḍulaṃ pariyonaddhanti, lāyitaṭṭhānampi nappaṭivirūhati. Te sannipatitvā anuṭṭhunanti “pāpakā vata bho dhammā sattesu pātubhūtā, mayaṃ pubbe manomayā ahumhā”ti. Aggaññasutte vuttanayena vittharetabbaṃ. Tato mariyādaṃ ṭhapenti. Athaññataro satto aññassa bhāgaṃ adinnaṃ ādiyati. Taṃ dvikkhattuṃ paribhāsitvā tatiyavāre leḍḍudaṇḍehi paharanti. Te evaṃ adinnādānakalahamusāvādadaṇḍadānesu uppannesu sannipatitvā cintayanti, “yaṃnūna mayaṃ ekaṃ sattaṃ sammanneyyāma, yo no sammā khiyitabbaṃ khiyeyya, garahitabbaṃ garaheyya, pabbājetabbaṃ pabbājeyya, mayaṃ panassa sālīnaṃ bhāgaṃ anupadassāmā”ti. Evaṃ katasanniṭṭhānesu pana sattesu imasmiṃ tāva kappe ayameva bhagavā bodhisattabhūto tena samayena tesu sattesu abhirūpataro ca dassanīyataro ca mahesakkhataro ca buddhisampanno paṭibalo niggahapaggahaṃ kātuṃ te taṃ upasaṅkamitvā yācitvā sammaniṃsu. So tena mahājanena sammatotipi mahāsammato, khettānaṃ adhipatīti khattiyo, dhammena samena paresaṃ rañjetīti rājāti, tīhi nāmehi paññāyittha. Yañhi loke acchariyaṃ ṭhānaṃ bodhisattova tattha ādi purisoti evaṃ bodhisattaṃ ādiṃ katvā khattiyamaṇḍale saṇṭhite anupubbena brāhmaṇādayopi vaṇṇā saṇṭhahiṃsu. Evaṃ candimasūriyapātu bhāvato yāva puna kappavināsakamahāmegho, idaṃ vivaṭṭaṭṭhāyī nāma catutthamasaṅkhyeyyaṃ nāma. Tenetaṃ vuccati megho jālaparicchinno saṃvaṭṭoti jālā meghaparicchinno saṃvaṭṭaṭṭhāyīti vuccati. Meghā sūriyaparicchinno vivaṭṭoti vuccati. Sūriyā meghaparicchinno vivaṭṭaṭṭhāyīti vuccati. Cattāri imāni kappāni mahākappoti vuccati. Vivaṭṭaṭṭhāyikappeyeva uppajjanti buddhādayoti. Tathā hi imasmiṃyeva vivaṭṭaṭṭhāyiasaṅkhyayyakappe buddhapacceka buddhasāvakacakkavattino uppajjanti, na tīsu asaṅkhyeyyakappesu. Tañca kho asuññakappeyeva, na suññakappe. Tattha buddhapaccekabuddhasāvakacakkavattīhi puññavantapuggalehi asuññattā asuññakappo nāma. Tabbigamena suññakappo veditabbo. Tesupi asuññakappo pañcavidho hoti sārakappo[M 0.0079], maṇḍakappo, varakappo, sāramaṇḍakappo, bhaddakappoti, tesu yasmiṃ kappe ekova buddho uppajjati, so sārakappo nāma. Yasmiṃ kappe dve buddhā uppajjanti, so maṇḍakappo nāma. Yasmiṃ kappe tayo uppajjanti, so varakappo nāma. Yasmiṃ kappe cattāro buddhā uppajjanti, so sāramaṇḍakappo nāma. Yasmiṃ kappe pañca buddhā uppajjanti, so bhaddakapponāma. Imāni pañcakappāniyeva saheva samodhānetvā jātattakīsotattakiyā nidāne.

Dwelling in homes, they gradually began to imitate the ways of a certain lazy creature and started making stores. From then on, even husks and chaff covered the rice grains, and the reaped area did not grow again. Gathering together, they lamented, 'Alas, evil things have appeared among beings! Before, we were mind-made.' This should be elaborated in the way stated in the Aggañña Sutta. Then they established boundaries. Then one being took another's share without being given it. Having rebuked him twice, on the third occasion they struck him with clods and sticks. When theft, quarrels, false speech, and the giving of punishment arose, they gathered and deliberated: 'What if we were to appoint one being among us—someone who would rightly reprove what should be reproved, rebuke what should be rebuked, and banish those who should be banished? We, for our part, will give him a share of the rice.' When the beings had thus decided, at that time in this very aeon, the Blessed One, then a Bodhisatta, was the most handsome, the most pleasing to behold, the most influential, endowed with wisdom, and capable of censure and commendation. They approached him, requested him, and appointed him. He was known by three names: Mahāsammata, because he was chosen by that great multitude; Khattiya, because he was lord of the fields; and Rājā, because he pleased others by means of the Dhamma and by fairness. Indeed, in the world, wherever there is an extraordinary state, the Bodhisatta is the first person there. Thus, with the Bodhisatta as the first, the Khattiya circle was established, and gradually the other classes, such as the brahmins, also became established. From the appearance of the sun and moon up to the great cloud that destroys the aeon, this is called the fourth incalculable aeon, the Vivaṭṭaṭṭhāyī. Therefore it is said: the cloud bounded by flame is called Saṃvaṭṭa; the flame bounded by cloud is called Saṃvaṭṭaṭṭhāyī; the cloud bounded by the sun is called Vivaṭṭa; the sun bounded by the cloud is called Vivaṭṭaṭṭhāyī.
