These four aeons are called a 'great aeon'. Only in the Vivaṭṭaṭṭhāyī aeon do Buddhas, Paccekabuddhas, disciples, and wheel-turning monarchs arise, not in the other three incalculable aeons. Moreover, this occurs only in non-empty aeons, not in empty aeons. A 'non-empty aeon' is so called because it is not devoid of Buddhas, Paccekabuddhas, disciples, wheel-turning monarchs, and virtuous individuals. By contrast, an 'empty aeon' should be understood as one lacking these. Non-empty aeons are of five kinds: Sārakappa, the aeon with essence; Maṇḍakappa, the adorned aeon; Varakappa, the excellent aeon; Sāramaṇḍakappa, the aeon with essence and adornment; and Bhaddakappa, the auspicious aeon. In these, the aeon where only one Buddha arises is called Sārakappa. Where two Buddhas arise, it is Maṇḍakappa. Where three arise, it is Varakappa. Where four arise, it is Sāramaṇḍakappa. Where five arise, it is Bhaddakappa. These five aeons are thus combined in the introduction to the Jātaka commentary.

“Nando sunando pathavī, maṇḍo dharaṇī sāgaro;

Puṇḍarīko ime satta, asaṅkhyeyyā pakāsitā.

Nanda, Sunanda, Pathavī, Maṇḍa, Dharaṇī, Sāgara, Puṇḍarīka—these seven incalculable aeons are declared.

Pañca buddhasahassāni, honti nande asaṅkhyeyye;

Nava buddhasahassāni, sunandamhi asaṅkhyeyye.

Five thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Nanda; nine thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Sunanda.

Dasa buddhasahassāni, pathavimhi asaṅkhyeyye;

Ekādasa sahassāni, tamhi maṇḍe asaṅkhyeyye.

Ten thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Pathavī; eleven thousand were in the incalculable aeon of Maṇḍa.

Vīsati buddhasahassāni, dharaṇimhi asaṅkhyeyye;

Tiṃsa buddhasahassāni, sāgaramhi asaṅkhyeyye.

Twenty thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Dharaṇī; thirty thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Sāgara.

Cattālīsa sahassāni, puṇḍarīke asaṅkhyeyye;

Asaṅkhyeyyesu sattasu, ettakāti pavuccati.

Forty thousand were in the incalculable aeon of Puṇḍarīka; so many are declared in these seven incalculable aeons.

Ekaṃsatasahassāni, vīsati ca sahassañca;

Sesā pañcasahassāni, sabbabuddhehi maṇḍitā”ti.

One hundred thousand, and twenty thousand, and five thousand more, all adorned by Buddhas.

Vuttaṃ. Aparampi vuttaṃ.

Thus it was said. And further it was said.

“Bhaddo sabbaphullo, sabbaratano usabhakkhandho;

Mānitabhaddo ca padumo, usabhakkhantuttameva ca;

Sabbabhāso asaṅkhyeyyo, navamoti pavuccati.

Bhadda, Sabbaphulla, Sabbaratana, Usabhakkhandha, Mānitabhadda, Paduma, Usabha, Khantuttama, and Sabbabhāsa—these nine incalculable aeons are declared.

Paṇṇāsa buddhasahassāni, sabbabhadde asaṅkhyeyye;

Saṭṭhi buddhasahassāni, sabbaphulle asaṅkhyeyye.

Fifty thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Sabbabhadda; sixty thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Sabbaphulla.

Sattati buddhasahassāni, sabbaratane asaṅkhyeyye;

Asīti buddhasahassāni, usabhakkhandhe asaṅkhyeyye.

Seventy thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Sabbaratana; eighty thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Usabhakkhandha.

Navuti buddhasahassāni, mānitabhadde asaṅkhyeyye;

Vīsati buddhasahassāni, padumamhi asaṅkhyeyye.

Ninety thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Mānitabhadda; twenty thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Paduma.

Dasa [M 0.0080] buddhasahassāni usabhamhi asaṅkhyeyye;

Pañca buddhasahassāni, khantuttame asaṅkhyeyye.

Ten thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Usabha; five thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Khantuttama.

Dve ca buddhasahassāni, sabbabhāse asaṅkhyeyye;

Aṅkhyeye navasmiṃ, ettakāti pavuccati.

Two thousand Buddhas were in the incalculable aeon of Sabbabhāsa; this is the total number declared in these nine incalculable aeons.

Tīṇi satasahassāni, sattāsītisahassañca;

Gaṇanānañca buddhānaṃ, sabbabuddhehi maṇḍitā”ti.

Three hundred thousand and eighty-seven thousand is the count of Buddhas, adorned by all the Buddhas.

Te sabbepi sammāsambuddhe yāva arimetteyyā samodhānetvā

Having summed up all those Perfectly Self-Enlightened Buddhas up to Ariya Metteyya:

“Sambuddhe aṭṭhavīsañca, dvādasañca sahassake;

Pañcasatasahassāni, namāmi sirasā mahaṃ;

Tesaṃ dhammañca saṅghañca, ādarena namāmahaṃ.

To the Perfectly Self-Enlightened Ones—twenty-eight, and twelve thousand, and five hundred thousand—I bow my head. Their Dhamma and Sangha, I respectfully revere.

Namakkārānubhāvena, hitvā sabbe upaddave;

Anekaantarāyāpi, vinassantu asesato”ti.

By the power of this homage, having abandoned all dangers, may even numerous obstacles be utterly destroyed.

Namassanagāthā pavattā sā ūnasaṅkhyāvaseneva katāva bhavitabbaṃ, tathā hi amhākaṃ bhagavā porāṇabrahmadevabuddhaṃ ādiṃ katvā yāva porāṇasakyagotamā manasāva cintentassa sattaasaṅkhyeyyāni vītivattāni porāṇasakyagotamabuddhaṃ ādiṃ katvā yāva majjhimadīpaṅkarā vācāmattena nava asaṅkhyeyyāni vītivattāni. Majjhimadīpaṅkarato paṭṭhāya yāva padumuttarabuddhā kāyaṅgavācaṅgavasena cattāri asaṅkhyeyyāni vītivattāni. Padumuttarabuddhato yāva kakusandhā ekamasaṅkhyeyyaṃ vītivattaṃ. Evamimesu vīsatiasaṅkhyeyyesu brahmadevaporāṇasakyagotamabuddhānamantare satta asaṅkhyeyye sabbaṃ sampiṇḍetvā ekasatasahassañca vīsati sahassañca pañcasahassāni ca honti. Porāṇasakyagotamamajjhimadīpaṅkarānamantare nava asaṅkhyeyye sabbaṃ sampiṇḍetvā tīṇisatasahassāni sattāsītisahassāni ca honti. Majjhimadīpaṅkarato yāva metteyyā aṭṭhavīsāti sabbaṃ samodhānetvā aṭṭhavīsañca dvādasasahassañca pañcasatasahassāni ca honti tasmā ūnasaṅkhyāti veditabbā. Paripuṇṇasaṅkhyāvasena icchamānehi cintetabbāva. Esā ca saṅkhyāvicāraṇā nidāne vuttāva.

The verses of homage that have been recited must have been made with a reduced count. For, with regard to our Blessed One, starting from the ancient Brahmadeva Buddha up to the ancient Sakyagotama Buddha, seven incalculable aeons passed merely by contemplating in the mind. From the ancient Sakyagotama Buddha up to the middle Dīpaṅkara, nine incalculable aeons passed by mere utterance. From the middle Dīpaṅkara up to the Padumuttara Buddha, four incalculable aeons passed through bodily and verbal actions. From the Padumuttara Buddha up to Kakusandha, one incalculable aeon passed. Thus, in these twenty incalculable aeons, between the Brahmadeva and the ancient Sakyagotama Buddhas, when all in the seven incalculable aeons are summed up, there are one hundred thousand, twenty thousand, and five thousand. Between the ancient Sakyagotama and the middle Dīpaṅkara, when all in the nine incalculable aeons are summed up, there are three hundred thousand and eighty-seven thousand. From the middle Dīpaṅkara up to Metteyya, there are twenty-eight. When all are summed together, there are twenty-eight, and twelve thousand, and five hundred thousand. Therefore, it should be understood as a reduced count. Those who desire a complete count should contemplate it accordingly. This analysis of numbers has already been stated in the introduction.

Evaṃ “cintitaṃ sattasaṅkhyeyyaṃ, navasaṅkhyeyyavācakaṃ;

Kāyavācā catusaṅkhyeyyaṃ, buddhattaṃsamupāgamī”ti.

Thus: 'Seven incalculable aeons were contemplated; nine incalculable aeons were uttered; four incalculable aeons were by body and speech; thus he attained Buddhahood.'

Vuttesu [M 0.0081] vīsatiasaṅkhyeyyesu pavattaasaṅkhyeyyakappavaseneva katā. Atthato pana sārakappamaṇḍakappavarakappasāramaṇḍakappabhaddakappavasena pañcavidhāti veditabbā. Imāni pañcanāmāni buddhuppādakappeyeva labbhanti, anuppannakappe pana suññakappotveva nāmaṃ labbhati tathā hi kappasaṇṭhahanakāle sabbapaṭhamaṃ mahābodhipallaṅkaṭṭhāneyeva paduminigabbhā uppajjati. Sā yasmiṃ kāle eko buddho uppajjissati, eko paduminigabbho aṭṭhaparikkhārehi saha uppajjati. Taṃ disvā suddhāvāsabrahmuno nemittapāṭhakā arahanto imasmiṃ kappe eko buddho uppajjissatīti sañjātapītisomanassā hutvā aṭṭhaparikkhāre gahetvā brahmaloke ṭhapenti “yadā buddho uppajjissati, tadā dassāmā”ti. Evaṃ dve tayo cattāro pañca buddhā uppajjissanti, tadā dve tīṇi cattāri pañca paduminigabbhā aṭṭhaparikkhārehi saha uppajjanti. Tañca kho ekasmiṃ yeva nāḷekeka baddhā hutvā uppajjanti. Evaṃ kappasaṇṭhahanakālato paṭṭhāyeva imāni pañca nāmāni labbhantīti veditabbāni. Ettāvatā imasmiṃyeva vivaṭṭaṭṭhāyīasaṅkhyeyyakappe buddhādayo mahesakkhā puññavanto cakkavattirājāno uppajjanti, tathā āyukappantarakappānipi. Tattha āyukappo nāma tesaṃ tesaṃ sattānaṃ āyuparicchedo. Antarakappo nāma tattha sattharogadubbhikkhānaṃ aññatarasaṃvaṭṭanena bahūsu vināsamupagatesu avasiṭṭhasattasantā nappavattakusalakammānubhāvena dasavassato paṭṭhāya anukkamena asaṅkhyeyyāyukappappamāṇesu sattesu pana adhammasamādānavasena kamena parihāyitvā dasavassāyukesu jātesu rogādīnamaññatarasaṃvaṭṭanena sattānaṃ vināsappatti yāva ayameko antarakappo. Evaṃ paricchinnaantarakappavasena catusaṭṭhiantarakappo eko asaṅkhyeyyakappo vīsati antarakappappamāṇoti apare vadanti. Imāni cattāri asaṅkhyeyyakappāni eko mahākappo nāma. Evaṃ tejo saṃvaṭṭavasena tāva mahākappānaṃ antaraṃ aggināva vinassati. Yasmiṃ pana samaye kappo udakena nassati, tadā āditova kappavināsakamahāmegho vuṭṭhahitvāti pubbe vuttanayeneva vitthāretabbaṃ ayaṃ pana viseso yathā tattha dutiyasūriyo, evamidhapi kappavināsako khārudakamahāmegho vuṭṭhāti so ādito sukhumaṃ vassanto anukkamena mahādhārāhi koṭisatasahassacakkavāḷaṃ [M 0.0082] pūrento vassati. Khārudakena phuṭṭhaphuṭṭhā pathavīpabbatādayo vilīyanti taṃ udakaṃ vātena samantato dhāritaṃ pathavito yāva parittābhā appamāṇābhā ābhassarāti tayopi dutiyajjhānabhūmi udakaṃ gaṇhāti. Tattha tayopi brahmaloke vilīyāpetvā subha kiṇhe āhacca tiṭṭhati yāva aṇumattaṃ saṅkhāragataṃ atthi, tāva na vūpasamati. Udakānugataṃ pana sabbasaṅkhāragataṃ abhibhavitvā sahasā vūpasamati antaradhānaṃ gacchati, heṭṭhā ākāsena saha upari ākāso eko hoti mahandhakāroti sabbaṃ vuttasadisameva. Kevalaṃ panidha otiṇṇotiṇṇe udake ābhassarabrahmalokaṃ ādiṃ katvā lokapātubhāvo veditabbo. Subhakiṇhato cavitvā ābhassaraṭṭhānādīsu sattā nibbattanti etthāpi kappavināsakamahāmeghato yāva kappavināsakaudakaparicchedo saṃvaṭṭo nāma paṭhamamasaṅkhyeyyakappo nāmāti cattāriasaṅkhyeyyakappāni vuttasadisāni evaṃ satta mahākappāni sattakkhattuṃ agginā vilīyitvā aṭṭhame mahākappe udakenapi vināso ca saṇṭhahanañca veditabbaṃ, evaṃ sattasattamahākappo sattasattavāraṃ agginā vilīyitvā aṭṭhame aṭṭhame vāre āpena vilīyitvā anukkamena tesaṭṭhimahākappāni paripuṇṇāni tadā āpavāraṃ paṭibāhitvā catusaṭṭhime mahākappe vātena vinassati tadā ādito kappavināsakamahāvāto vuṭṭhahitvāti sabbaṃ pubbe vuttanayameva. Ayaṃ pana viseso yathā agginā vināsakappe dutiyasūriyo, evamidhāpi kappavināsanatthaṃ vāto samuṭṭhāti so paṭhamaṃ sukhumarajaṃ uṭṭhāpeti, tato thūlarajaṃ saṇharajaṃ sukhumavālikaṃ sakkharapāsāṇādayoti yāva kūṭāgāramatte pāsāṇepi visamaṭṭhāne ṭhitamahārukkheca uṭṭhāpeti, te pathavito nabhamuggatā na puna patanti, tattheva cuṇṇavicuṇṇā hutvā abhāvaṃ gacchanti. Yathānukkamena heṭṭhā mahāpathaviyā vāto samuṭṭhahitvā pathaviṃ parivattetvā uddhaṃmūlaṃ katvā ākāse khipati yojanasatappamāṇāpi pathavippadesā dviyojanatiyojanacatuyojanapañcayojanachayojanasattayojanappamāṇāpi bhijjitvā te vegakkhittā ākāseyeva cuṇṇavicuṇṇā hutvā abhāvaṃ gacchanti cakkavāḷapabbatampi vāto ukkhipitvā ākāse khipati te aññamaññaṃ ghaṭṭayantā cuṇṇavicuṇṇā [M 0.0083] hutvā vinassanti eteneva bhūmaṭṭhakavimānāni ākāsaṭṭhakavimānāni ca vināsento chakāmāvacaradevaloke nāsetvā koṭisatasahassacakkavāḷāni vināseti. Tattha cakkavāḷā cakkavāḷehi, himavantā himavantehi, sinerū sinerūhi aññamaññaṃ samāgantvā cuṇṇavicuṇṇā hutvā vinassanti. Pathavito yāva parittasubhā, appamāṇasubhā, subhaṇiṇhāti tayopi tatiyajjhānabhūmī vāto gaṇhāti tattha tayopi brahmaloke vināsetvā vehapphalaṃ āhacca aṭṭhāsi. Evaṃ sabbaṃ saṅkhāragataṃ vināsetvā sayaṃ vinassati, heṭṭhā ākāsena saha upari ākāso eko hoti mahandhakāroti sabbaṃ vuttasadisaṃ. Idha pana subhakiṇhabrahmalokaṃ ādiṃ katvā loko pātubhavati. Vehapphalato cavitvā subhakiṇhādīsu sattā nibbattanti, tatthakappavinā sakamahāmeghato yāva kappavināsakavātupacchedo, idaṃ saṃvaṭṭo nāma, paṭhamamasaṅkhyeyyaṃ nāmāti cattāri asaṅkhyeyyakappāni eko mahākappo nāmāti sabbaṃ tejosaṃvaṭṭe vuttanayameva.

The twenty incalculable aeons that have been mentioned are reckoned by way of the incalculable aeon of evolution. In terms of their substance, however, they should be understood as fivefold: the Sārakappa (Essence Aeon), Maṇḍakappa (Quintessence Aeon), Varakappa (Auspicious Aeon), Sāramaṇḍakappa (Essence-Quintessence Aeon), and Bhaddakappa (Fortunate Aeon). These five names are found only in aeons where a Buddha arises; in an aeon where no Buddha arises, it is just called a 'void aeon.' For at the time of the aeon's formation, a lotus-bud first appears at the site of the great Bodhi-tree's throne. When one Buddha is to arise in that aeon, one lotus-bud appears together with the eight requisites. Seeing this, the arahants among the Suddhāvāsa brahmās, who are readers of signs, become filled with joy and delight. Taking the eight requisites, they place them in the brahma-world, thinking: 'When the Buddha arises, we will give these to him.' Similarly, when two, three, four, or five Buddhas are to arise, then two, three, four, or five lotus-buds appear together with the eight requisites. And they arise bound together on a single stalk. Thus, it should be understood that from the time of the aeon's formation, these five names are found. Furthermore, it is in this very incalculable aeon of expansion-and-continuance that beings of great power—Buddhas and others, meritorious wheel-turning monarchs—arise, and so too do the lifespan-aeon and the in-between-aeon. Here, the lifespan-aeon is the determination of the lifespan of those beings. The in-between-aeon is when, after many have perished through one of the destructions by the sword, disease, or famine, the lineage of the remaining beings, through the power of their unwholesome karma, gradually declines from an incalculable lifespan until they are born with a lifespan of ten years; the perishing of beings through one of the destructions by disease and so on is one in-between-aeon. Thus, in terms of defined in-between-aeons, sixty-four in-between-aeons make one incalculable aeon; but others say it is a period of twenty in-between-aeons. Four such incalculable aeons are called one great aeon. Thus, by way of destruction by fire, the interval of great aeons is destroyed by fire. But when the time comes for the aeon to be destroyed by water, a great world-destroying cloud arises at the outset, which should be elaborated as stated before. This, however, is the difference: just as a second sun appears there, so here a great world-destroying cloud of caustic water arises. At first it rains gently, then with progressively larger drops it rains, filling a hundred thousand koṭis of world-systems. The earth, mountains, and so on, wherever they are touched by the caustic water, dissolve. That water, held up on all sides by wind, rises from the earth up to the three second jhana-planes: the Parittābhā, Appamāṇābhā, and Ābhassarā. Having dissolved these three brahma-worlds, it stops at the Subhakiṇha plane. It does not subside so long as even a minute amount of conditioned phenomena exists. But having overwhelmed all conditioned phenomena, the water suddenly subsides and vanishes. The space below becomes one with the space above, and there is a great darkness. All this is just as was said before. Here, however, the reappearance of the world should be understood as beginning from the Ābhassara brahma-world downwards as the waters recede. Beings who pass away from the Subhakiṇha plane are reborn in the Ābhassara and other planes. Here too, the period from the world-destroying great cloud until the end of the world-destroying water is called the 'dissolution,' which is the first incalculable aeon. The four incalculable aeons are as stated before. Thus, for seven great aeons, it is destroyed by fire seven times, and on the eighth great aeon, it is destroyed by water. In this way, after seven cycles of seven destructions by fire, on the eighth occasion each time it is destroyed by water. In sequence, when sixty-three great aeons are completed, the turn for water having passed, in the sixty-fourth great aeon it is destroyed by wind. At that time, a great world-destroying wind arises at the outset. All is as stated before. This, however, is the difference: just as in the destruction by fire a second sun appears, so here a wind arises to destroy the aeon. First, it raises fine dust, then coarse dust, fine sand, gravel, stones, and so on, up to rocks the size of gabled mansions and great trees standing on uneven ground. Having risen from the earth into the sky, they do not fall back but are pulverized there and vanish. In sequence, a wind arises from beneath the great earth, turns the earth over, and hurls it upside down into the sky. Tracts of land a hundred yojanas in size, and those two, three, four, five, six, or seven yojanas in size, are broken up, and being flung by the force of the wind, they are pulverized in the sky and vanish. The wind also lifts up the Cakkavāḷa mountain range and hurls it into the sky, where the mountains collide with one another and are pulverized and destroyed. In this way, destroying the celestial mansions on earth and in the sky, it annihilates the six sense-sphere deva-worlds and a hundred thousand koṭis of world-systems. There, world-systems collide with world-systems, Himavantā mountains with Himavantā mountains, Sinerus with Sinerus, and coming together they are pulverized and destroyed. The wind rises from the earth up to the three third jhana-planes: the Parittasubhā, Appamāṇasubhā, and Subhakiṇhā. Having destroyed these three brahma-worlds, it stops at the Vehapphala plane. Having thus destroyed all conditioned phenomena, it itself is destroyed. The space below becomes one with the space above, and there is a great darkness. All this is just as was said before. Here, however, the world reappears beginning from the Subhakiṇha brahma-world. Beings who pass away from the Vehapphala plane are reborn in the Subhakiṇha and other planes. There, the period from the world-destroying great wind until the cessation of the world-destroying wind is called the 'dissolution,' which is the first incalculable aeon. That four incalculable aeons make one great aeon—all this is just as was stated in the section on destruction by fire.

Vuttampi cetaṃ.

And this has been said:

“Satta satta gginā vārā, aṭṭhame aṭṭhame dakā;

Catusaṭṭhi yadā puṇṇā, eko vāyuvaro siyā.

Seven are the turns of destruction by fire, and on each eighth turn, by water; when sixty-four are thus complete, there is one supreme destruction by wind.

Agginā’bhassarā heṭṭhā, āpena subhakiṇhato;

Vehapphalato vātena, evaṃ loko vinassatī”ti.

By fire, all below the Ābhassarā plane is destroyed; by water, all below the Subhakiṇha; by wind, all below the Vehapphala—thus the world is destroyed.

Tasmā tiṇṇampi paṭhamajjhānatalānaṃ ekakappepi avināsābhāvato sakalakappe tesaṃ sambhavo natthīti asaṅkhyeyya kappavasena tesaṃ āyuparicchedo daṭṭhabbo. Dutiyajjhānatalato paṭṭhāya pana paripuṇṇamahākappavasena āyuparicchedo daṭṭhabbo, na asaṅkhyeyyakappavasena. Yadi dutiyajjhānatalato paṭṭhāya paripuṇṇamahākappavasena āyuparicchedo siyā, kathaṃ ābhassarādīsu brahmūnaṃ aṭṭhamahākappādivasena āyu paripuṇṇaṃ siyā, tathā hi paṭhamajjhānatalaṃ sattasu vāresu agginā vinassati. Aṭṭhame vāre yāva ābhassarā udakena vinassati puna sattavāresu agginā paṭhamajjhānatalaṃ, puna aṭṭhamevāre udakena dutiyajjhānatalaṃ vinassatīti evaṃ aṭṭhame vāre āpavāraṃ paṭibāhitvā yāva subhakiṇhā vātena vinassati evaṃ catusaṭṭhi paripuṇṇā hotīti saccaṃ[M 0.0084], heṭṭhāvivaṭṭaṭṭhāyī asaṅkhyeyyakappavasena eko satta mahākappāni cāti aṭṭha kappāni ābhassarabrahmūnaṃ āyuppamāṇaṃ hoti. Catusaṭṭhikappesupi vivaṭṭaṭṭhāyīasaṅkhyeyyakappavasena eko te saṭṭhimahākappāni cāti catusaṭṭhikappāni subhakiṇhānaṃ brahmūnaṃ āyuppamāṇaṃ hoti tena vuttaṃ porāṇehi.

Therefore, for all three first jhāna planes, since they are not exempt from destruction even within a single aeon, their existence throughout a whole aeon is impossible; hence, their lifespan should be understood as determined by way of an incalculable aeon. From the second jhāna plane onward, however, the lifespan should be reckoned in terms of full great aeons, not in terms of incalculable aeons. If the lifespan from the second jhāna plane onward were measured in full great aeons, how could the lifespan of the Brahmās in the Ābhassarā and other realms be complete, such as eight great aeons? For indeed, the first jhāna plane is destroyed by fire seven times, and on the eighth time, it is destroyed by water up to the Ābhassarā. Then again, the first jhāna plane is destroyed by fire seven times, and on the eighth time, the second jhāna plane is destroyed by water. Thus, on the eighth occasion, having warded off the water-turn, it is destroyed by wind up to the Subhakiṇhā. In this way, sixty-four are completed—this is true. For the Ābhassarā Brahmās, their lifespan is eight great aeons, consisting of one incalculable aeon of the lower evolving-and-remaining phase and seven great aeons. And for the Subhakiṇhā Brahmās, their lifespan is sixty-four great aeons, consisting of one incalculable aeon of the evolving-and-remaining phase and sixty-three great aeons, even within the sixty-four aeons. Thus it was said by the ancients.

Satta satta’gginā vārā, aṭṭhame aṭṭhame dakā;

Catusaṭṭhi yadā puṇṇā, eko vāyuvaro siyā’ti.

Seven are the turns by fire, and on each eighth turn, by water; when sixty-four are complete, there is one supreme destruction by wind.

Keci pana.

But some say:

“Agginā’bhassarā heṭṭhā, āpena subhakiṇhato;

Vehapphalato vātena, evaṃ loko vinassatī”ti.

"By fire, all below the Ābhassarā plane is destroyed; by water, all below the Subhakiṇha; by wind, all below the Vehapphala—thus the world is destroyed."

Imissā gāthāya “ābhassarāti”ca “subhakiṇhato”ti ca “vehapphalato”ti ca abhividhivasena vuttabhāvañca samatalabhāvepi seṭṭhabhūmittā paṭṭhānavasena vuttabhāvañca amaññitvā suddhāvāsabhūmīsu viya uparūparivasena bhūmikkamoti maññitvā.

Without understanding that in this verse the terms "Ābhassara," "Subhakiṇha," and "Vehapphala" are stated in the sense of pervasion, and that they are mentioned in order starting from the superior planes even though they are on the same level, some mistakenly think that, like the Suddhāvāsa realms, there is a progression of realms one above the other.

“Pañcabhūmi naṭṭhā aggi, aṭṭha bhūmi naṭṭhā dakā;

Navabhūmi naṭṭhā vātā, lokanaṭṭhā tadā siyuṃ.

Five planes are destroyed by fire, eight planes are destroyed by water; nine planes are destroyed by wind—then the world is destroyed.

Paṭhame aggi dvattiṃsa, dutiye ādi soḷasa;

Dutiyabhūme majjhe aṭṭhamaṃ, taṃ chappaññāsa vārakaṃ.

In the first, fire destroys thirty-two; in the second, sixteen at the start; in the middle of the second plane, the eighth; that is fifty-six times.

Ābhassaramhi catutthaṃ, parittasubhamhi dve jalaṃ;

Appamāṇasubhaṃ ekavāraṃ, mataṃ udakasattama”nti.

In the Ābhassara realm, the fourth; in the Parittasubha realm, twice by water; in the Appamāṇasubha realm, once—this is considered the seventh water destruction.

Vadanti tesaṃ vāde ābhassarato heṭṭhā pañcabhūmi agginaṭṭhā, subhakiṇhato heṭṭhā aṭṭhabhūmi udakanaṭṭhāti vadanti. Samataladīpanatthaṃ sandehacchedanatthaṃ evaṃ gahitāti ekabhavapariyosānaṃ sandhāya paṭisandhibhavañcāti ādi vuttanti. Imissāpi atthaṃ duggahitena gahetvā paṭisandhibhavaṅgavasena sadisabhāvameva sandhāya vuttanti parikappenti so ayuttarūpo viya dissati ativiya micchāgāho ca hoti aggaññasuttavisuddhimaggādīhi virujjhanato saṃvaṭṭasīmāpi viruddhā tattha paṭhamajjhānatalaṃ upādāya agginā, dutiyajjhānatalaṃ upādāya udakena, catutthajjhānatalaṃ upādāya vātena vinassatīti vuttavacanenāpi virujjhateva tasmā [M 0.0085] “ābhassarā”ti ca “subhakiṇhato”ti ca “vehapphalato”ti ca etthābhividhivasena attho gahetabbo. Aññathā “upari pabbatā devo vassatī”ti ettha viya ābhassarasubhakiṇhavehapphalānaṃ tena tena saṃvaṭṭena vināsopi na bhaveyya. Ayaṃ lokavipattiparicchedo. Evaṃ buddho ca dullabho loke ti imassa attho vuttoyeva.

They say that in their view, below the Ābhassara realm there are five planes destroyed by fire, and below the Subhakiṇha realm there are eight planes destroyed by water. They say that this is stated for the purpose of illuminating the level ground and dispelling doubts, and that it is said referring to the end of one existence and the rebirth-linking existence, etc. However, having grasped this meaning wrongly, they imagine it as referring to a similar state in terms of rebirth-linking and life-continuum, but this appears inappropriate and is indeed an extreme wrong grasp, conflicting with the Aggañña Sutta, the Visuddhimagga, etc. Even the limit of dissolution also contradicts this, for it is said that beginning with the first jhāna plane, it is destroyed by fire; beginning with the second jhāna plane, it is destroyed by water; and beginning with the fourth jhāna plane, it is destroyed by wind. This statement also contradicts it. Therefore, the meaning of "Ābhassara," "Subhakiṇha," and "Vehapphala" should be taken here in the sense of pervasion. Otherwise, just as in the phrase "the deva rains above the mountain," there would be no destruction of the Ābhassara, Subhakiṇha, and Vehapphala realms by that respective dissolution. This is the delimitation of the world's destruction. Thus, the meaning of "a Buddha is rare in the world" has already been stated.

Iti sāgarabuddhittheraviracite sīmavisodhane

Thus, in the Sīmavisodhana composed by the Elder Sāgarabuddhi.

Kappavināsakaṇḍo dutiyo paricchedo.

The Section on the Destruction of the Aeon, the Second Chapter.

3. Nibbānakaṇḍo

3. The Chapter on Nibbāna

Idāni “saddhammasavanampi cāti imassa saṃvaṇṇanākkamo sampatto tattha saddhammasavanampi ca loke dullabhameva tathā hi saddhammo nāma tividho hoti. Pariyattisaddhammo, paṭipattisaddhammo, paṭivedhasaddhammoti. Tattha pariyattisaddhammo nāma tepiṭakaṃ buddhavacanaṃ. Paṭipattisaddhammo nāma terasadhutaṅgāni asīti khandhakavattādayo abhisamācāravattādīni. Paṭivedhasaddhammo nāma catusaccappaṭivedho. Tesu pariyattisaddhammo dvinnaṃ saddhammānaṃ pubbaṅgamoyeva padaṭṭhānañca. Kasmā taṃmūlakattā tathā hi pariyattiyā asati paṭivedho nāma natthi. Pariyattiyā antarahitāya paṭipatti, paṭipattiyā antarahitāya adhigamo antaradhāyati. Kiṃ kāraṇā ayañhi pariyatti paṭipattiyā paccayo hoti. Paṭipattiadhigamassāpi pariyattiyeva pamāṇaṃ. Tattha paṭivedho ca paṭipatti ca hotipi nahotipi. Ekasmiñhi kāle paṭivedhadharā bhikkhū bahū honti, “esa bhikkhu puthujjano”ti aṅguliṃ paharitvā dassetabbo hoti. Imasmiṃ yeva dīpe ekavāraṃ puthujjanabhikkhunāma nāhosi. Paṭipattipūrakāpi kadāci bahū honti kadāci appā. Iti paṭivedho ca paṭipatti ca hotipi na hotipi. Sāsanassa ṭhitiyā pana pariyattiyeva pamāṇaṃ paṇḍito hi tepiṭakaṃ sutvā dvepi pūreti, yathā amhākaṃ bodhisatto āḷārassa santike pañca abhiññā satta ca samāpattiyo nibbattetvā nevasaññānāsaññāyatana samāpattiyā parikammaṃ pucchi. So “na jānāmī”ti āha. Tato udakassa santikaṃ gantvā adhigatavisesaṃ saṃsanditvā nevasaññānāsaññāyatanassa parikammaṃ pucchi. So ācikkhi, tassa vacanasamanantarameva mahāsatto taṃ [M 0.0086] sampādesi evameva paññavā bhikkhu pariyattiṃ sutvā dvepi pūreti tasmā pariyattiyā ṭhitāya sāsanaṃ ṭhitaṃ hoti yathā mahātaḷākassa pāḷiyā thirāya udakaṃ na ṭhassatīti na vattabbaṃ udake sati padumādīni pupphāni na pupphissantīti na vattabbaṃ evameva mahātaḷākassa thirapāḷisadise tepiṭake buddhavacane sati mahātaḷāke udakasadisā paṭipattipūrakā kulaputtā natthīti na vattabbaṃ. Tesu sati mahātaḷākesu padumādīni pupphāni viya sotāpannādayo ariyapuggalā natthīti na vattabbaṃ, ekantato pariyattiyeva pamāṇaṃ pariyattiyā antarahitāya paṭipattipaṭivedhānaṃ antaradhānato. Tattha pariyattināma tepiṭakaṃ buddhavacanaṃ sāṭṭhakathā pāḷiyāva sā tiṭṭhati, tāva pariyatti paripuṇṇā hoti. Gacchante kāle kaliyugarājāno adhammikā honti tesu adhammikesu rājāmaccādayo adhammikā honti tathā raṭṭhajanapadavāsinopi adhammikā etesaṃ adhammikatāya na devo sammā vassati, tato sassāni na sampajjanti. Tesu sampajjantesu paccayadāyakā bhikkhusaṅghassa paccaye dātuṃ na sakkonti. Bhikkhū paccayehi kilamantā antevāsike saṅgahetuṃ na sakkonti. Gacchante gacchante kāle pariyatti parihāyati, atthavasena dhāretuṃ na sakkonti, pāḷivaseneva dhārenti. Tato kāle gacchante pāḷimpi sakalaṃ dhāretuṃ na sakkonti paṭhamaṃ abhidhammapiṭakaṃ parihāyati. Parihāyamānaṃ matthakato paṭṭhāya pariyatti hāyati paṭhamañhi mahāpakaraṇaṃ parihāyati. Tasmiṃ parihāyamāne yamakaṃ, kathāvatthu, puggalapaññatti, dhātukathā, vibhaṅgo, dhammasaṅgahoti evaṃ abhidhammapiṭake parihīne matthakato paṭṭhāya suttantapiṭakaṃ parihāyati. Paṭhamañhi aṅguttaranikāyo parihāyati. Tasmimpi paṭhamaṃ ekādasanipāto…pe… tato ekanipātoti evaṃ aṅguttare parihīne matthakato paṭṭhāya saṃyuttanikāyo parihāyati, paṭhamaṃ mahāvaggo parihāyati. Tato paṭṭhāya saḷāyatanavaggo, khandhavaggo, nidānavaggo, sagāthāvaggoti evaṃ saṃyuttanikāye parihīne matthakato paṭṭhāya majjhimanikāyo parihāyati paṭhamañhi uparipaṇṇāsako parihāyati. Tato majjhimapaṇṇāsako, tato mūlapaṇṇāsakoti evaṃ majjhimanikāye parihīne matthakato paṭṭhāya dīghanikāyo parihāyati, paṭhamañhi pāthiyavaggo [M 0.0087] parihāyati tato mahāvaggo, tato khandhakavaggoti dīghanikāye parihīne suttantapiṭakaṃ parihīnaṃ nāma hoti. Vinayapiṭakena saddhiṃ jātakameva dhārenti. Vinayapiṭakaṃ lajjino dhārenti. Lābhakāmā pana suttante kathitepi sallakkhentā natthīti jātakameva dhārenti. Gacchante kāle jātakampi dhāretuṃ na sakkonti. Atha nesaṃ paṭhamaṃ vessantarajātakaṃ parihāyati. Tato paṭilomakkamena puṇṇakajākatakaṃ, mahānāradakassapajātakaṃ parihāyati. Vinayapiṭakameva dhārenti. Gacchante kāle tampi matthakato parihāyati paṭhamañhi parivāro parihāyati, tato khandhako bhikkhunīvibhaṅgo mahāvibhaṅgoti anukkamena uposathakkhandhakamattameva dhārenti, tadā pariyatti anantarahitāva hoti. Yāva pana manussesu catuppadikagāthāpi pavattati, tāva pariyatti anantarahitāva hoti. Yadā saddho pasanno rājā hatthikkhandhe suvaṇṇacaṅkoṭakamhi sahassatthavikaṃ ṭhapāpetvā “buddhehi kathitaṃ catuppadikaṃ gāthaṃ jānanto imaṃ sahassaṃ gaṇhatū”ti nagare bheriṃ carāpetvā gaṇhakaṃ alabhitvā ekavāraṃ carāpite na suṇantāpi honti asuṇantāpi, yāva tatiyaṃ carāpetvā gaṇhakaṃ alabhitvā rājapurisā sahassatthavikaṃ puna rājakulaṃ pavesenti, tadā pariyattiantarahitā nāma hoti. Evaṃ pariyattiyā antarahitāya paṭipattipi paṭivedhopi antarahitova hoti. Saddhammasavanassa dullabhabhāvo dhammasoṇḍakavatthunā dīpetabbo amhākaṃ kira sammāsambuddho kassapasammāsambuddhassa dhammarājassa sāsanantaradhānato nacireneva kālena bārāṇasirañño putto dhammasoṇḍakarājakumāro hutvā pituaccayena rajje patiṭṭhāya kassapadasabalena desitaṃ dhammaṃ sotukāmo hutvā māsamattaṃ rajjaṃ katvā amhākaṃ sammāsambuddho imasmiṃ yeva bhaddakappe rajjaṃ kāretvā devanagarasadise bārāṇasinagare cakkavattirajjasadisaṃ rajjaṃ karonto evaṃ cintesi… “mayhaṃ evarūpaṃ rajjaṃ kiṃ vilāsaṃ rajjānubhāvaṃ saddhammaviyogena, divākaravirahito ākāso viya, sasaṅkavirahitaratti viya, dāṭhāvirahitagajo viya, velantavirahitamahāsamuddo viya cakkhuvirahita susajjitavadanaṃ viya, sugandhavirahitapārichattapupphaṃ viya, catuakkharaniyamitadhammadesanāviyogena mayhaṃ idaṃ rajjaṃ na sobhatī”ti [M 0.0088] cintetvā suvaṇṇacaṅkoṭakena sahassatthavikaṃ bhaṇḍakaṃ susajjitaṃ maṅgalahatthikumbhe ṭhapetvā bārāṇasīnagare mahāvīthiyaṃ bheriṃ carāpeti “ekapadikaṃ vā dvipadikaṃ vā tipadikaṃ vā catuppadikaṃ vā dhammapadaṃ jānantassa dammī”ti evaṃ bheriṃ carāpetvā dhammajānanakaṃ alabhitvā punappunaṃ dvisahassaṃ tisahassaṃ yāva satasahassaṃ koṭi dve sahassakoṭi, satasahassakoṭi gāmanigamajanapadasenāpatiṭṭhānaṃ uparājaṭṭhānaṃ. Pariyosāne dhammadesakaṃ alabhitvā attano suvaṇṇapīṭhakaṃ setacchattaṃ cajitvāpi dhammadesakaṃ alabhitvā rajjasiriṃ pahāya attānaṃ cajitvā “dhammadesakassa dāso hutvāpi dhammaṃ sossāmī”ti vatvā evampi dhamma desakaṃ alabhitvā vippaṭisārī hutvā “kiṃ me saddhammaviyogena rajjenāti amaccānaṃ rajjaṃ niyyātetvā saddhammagavesako hutvā dhammasoṇḍakamahārājā mahāvanaṃ pāvisi dhammasoṇḍakamahārājassa saddhammasavanaṃ sandhāya paviṭṭhakkhake sakkadevamahārājassa vejayantapāsādo saheva kiṇṇikāya kampo ahosi, paṇḍukambalasilāsanaṃ uṇhākāraṃ ahosi. Sakkodevarājā kenakāraṇena paṇḍukambalasilāsanaṃ uṇhākāraṃ ahosī”ti cintetvā attano sahassanettena devamanussesu vitthāretvā olokento dhammagavesako hutvā vanaṃ paviṭṭhaṃ dhammasoṇḍakamahārājānaṃ disvā cintesi… “ajja me attānampi jahāya rakkhasavesaṃ māpetvā etaṃ aniccaparidīpanaṃ jātijarābyādhimaraṇaṃ sakalasarīre dosaṃ dassetvā dhammaṃ desetvā etaṃ sakarajjeyeva patiṭṭhāpetabba”nti cintetvā sakko devarājā yakkhasarūpaṃ māpetvā bodhisattassa abhimukho avidūre attānaṃ dassesi. Taṃ disvā dhammasoṇḍakamahārājā evaṃ cintesi… “evaṃrūpo nāma rakkhaso dhammaṃ jāneyyā”ti, cintetvā avidūre ṭhāne ṭhatvā pucchāmīti rakkhasena saddhiṃ sallapanto āha “sāmipuññadevarāja imasmiṃ pana vanaghane vasanakadevarājā kiṃ nukho dhammaṃ jānāsī”ti, devatā “mahārāja dhammaṃ jānāmī”ti āha. “Yadi dhammaṃ jānāsi, mayhaṃ dhammakathaṃ kathethā”ti āha. “Ahaṃ tuyhaṃ dhammaṃ kathessāmi, tvaṃ mayhaṃ kīdisaṃ dhammakathikassa sakkāraṃ karissasī”ti āha. “Evaṃ sante mayhaṃ dhammaṃ kathetvā pacchā mayhaṃ sarīre maṃsaṃ khādissasī”ti āha. “Ahaṃ mahārāja chāto hutvā dhammaṃ kathetuṃ na sakkomī”ti āha. “Yadi tumhe [M 0.0089] paṭhamaṃ maṃsaṃ khādatha, dhammaṃ ko suṇissatī”ti āha. Puna so rakkhaso “nāhaṃ dhammaṃ desetuṃ sakkomī”ti. Puna rājā “mayhaṃ dhammapaṭilābhañca tumhākaṃ maṃsapaṭilābhañca tumhe jānitvā mayhaṃ dhammaṃ desethā”ti āha. Atha sakko devarājā “sādhu hothā”ti vatvā avidūre ṭhāne ubbedhena tigāvutamattaṃ mahantaṃ añjanapabbataṃ māpetvā evamāha… “sace mahārāja imaṃ pabbatamuddhaniṃ ārūyha ākāsā uppatitvā tvaṃ mama mukhe patissasi, ahaṃ te ākāsagatakāle dhammaṃ desessāmi, evaṃ sante tuyhañca dhammappalābho mayhañca maṃsapaṭilābho bhavissatī”ti āha. Tassa kathaṃ sutvā dhammasoṇḍakamahārājā “anamatagge saṃsāre puriso hutvā adhammasamaṅgī hutvā adhammasseva atthāya pāṇātipāto adinnādāno kāmesumicchācāro sūkariko orabbhiko sākuṇi ko coro paradāriko taṃ gahetvā sīsacchinnānaṃ lohitaṃ catūsu mahāsamuddesu udakatopi bahutaraṃ mātāpituādīnampi manāpānaṃ atthāya rodantānaṃ assu catūsumahāsamuddesu udakatopi bahutaraṃ, imaṃ pana sarīraṃ dhammassa atthāya vikkiṇāmi taṃ mahapphalañca manāpañcā”ti cintetvā “sādhu mārisa evaṃ karomī”ti pabbataṃ ārūyha pabbatagge ṭhito “mama rajjena saddhiṃ mayhaṃ sajīvasarīraṃ saddhammassatthāya dassāmī”ti somanasso hutvā “dhammaṃ kathethā”ti saddhammatthāya jīvitaṃ pariccajitvā ākāsato uppatitvā dhammaṃ kathethāti āha. Atha sakko devarājā sakattabhāvena sabbālaṅkārehi paṭimaṇḍito ativiya sotuṃ somanasso ākāsato patantaṃ dibbaphassena parāmasanto urena paṭiggaṇhitvā devalokaṃ netvā paṇḍukambalasilāsane nisīdāpetvā mālāgandhādīhi pūjetvā dhammasoṇḍakamahārājassa dhammaṃ desento imaṃ gāthamāha.

Now, the turn has come for the explanation of “and also the hearing of the True Dhamma.” Therein, the hearing of the True Dhamma is indeed rare in the world. For indeed, the True Dhamma is of three kinds: the Dhamma of learning, the Dhamma of practice, and the Dhamma of penetration. Among these, the Dhamma of learning is the Tipiṭaka, the Buddha’s word. The Dhamma of practice consists of the thirteen ascetic practices, the duties of the eighty sections of the Vinaya, the rules of proper conduct, and so forth. The Dhamma of penetration is the penetration of the Four Noble Truths. Among these, the Dhamma of learning is indeed the forerunner and foundation of the other two Dhammas. Why? Because they are rooted in it. For indeed, without learning, there is no such thing as penetration. When learning disappears, practice disappears; when practice disappears, attainment disappears. What is the reason? This learning is a condition for practice, and for practice and attainment, learning alone is the standard. Herein, penetration and practice may or may not exist. For at one time, there were many bhikkhus who were bearers of penetration, to the point that one had to point out, “This bhikkhu is an ordinary person,” by snapping one's fingers. In this very island, there was a time when there was not even a single bhikkhu who was an ordinary person. Those fulfilling the practice are sometimes many, sometimes few. Thus, penetration and practice may or may not exist. But for the endurance of the Dispensation, learning alone is the standard. For a wise person, having heard the Tipiṭaka, fulfills both; just as our Bodhisatta, in the presence of Āḷāra, having generated the five higher knowledges and the seven attainments, then asked about the preliminary work for the attainment of the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception. He said, “I do not know.” Then, going to Uddaka, having compared it with the special attainment he had reached, he asked about the preliminary work for the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception. He explained it, and immediately after his words, the Great Being accomplished it. Similarly, a wise bhikkhu, having heard the learning, fulfills both. Therefore, when learning stands, the Dispensation stands. Just as it should not be said that the water of a great lake will not stand if its embankment is firm, or that lotuses and other flowers will not bloom if the water exists, so too, it should not be said that there are no noble sons who fulfill the practice, who are like the water in a great lake, if the Tipiṭaka, the Buddha’s word, which is like a firm embankment, exists. If they exist, it should not be said that noble persons such as stream-enterers, who are like lotuses in the great lakes, do not exist. Ultimately, learning alone is the standard, because when learning disappears, practice and penetration disappear. Herein, learning is the Tipiṭaka, the Buddha’s word. So long as it, the Pāli together with its commentary, stands, learning is complete. As time passes, unrighteous kings arise in the Kali Age. When those kings, ministers, and so on are unrighteous, the people of the country and districts also become unrighteous. Due to their unrighteousness, the rains do not fall properly, so crops do not flourish. When these do not flourish, supporters cannot give requisites to the Sangha of bhikkhus. Bhikkhus, afflicted by lack of requisites, cannot gather disciples. As time goes on and on, learning declines. They cannot retain the meaning, but only retain the Pāli. Then, as time goes on, they cannot retain even the entire Pāli. First, the Abhidhamma Piṭaka declines. As it declines, learning deteriorates from the top. First, the Great Treatise is lost. When that is lost, the Yamaka, Kathāvatthu, Puggalapaññatti, Dhātukathā, Vibhaṅga, and Dhammasaṅgaṇī are lost. Thus, when the Abhidhamma Piṭaka is lost, the Suttanta Piṭaka declines from the top. First, the Aṅguttara Nikāya is lost. In that, first the Book of Elevens… and then the Book of Ones; thus, when the Aṅguttara is lost, the Saṃyutta Nikāya declines from the top. First, the Great Book is lost, and then the Book of the Six Sense Bases, the Book of Aggregates, the Book of Causes, and the Book with Verses. Thus, when the Saṃyutta Nikāya is lost, the Majjhima Nikāya declines from the top. First, the Later Fifty is lost, then the Middle Fifty, then the Root Fifty. Thus, when the Majjhima Nikāya is lost, the Dīgha Nikāya declines from the top. First, the Pāthika Book is lost, then the Great Book, then the Sīlakkhandha Book. When the Dīgha Nikāya is lost, the Suttanta Piṭaka is said to be lost. They only retain the Jātaka along with the Vinaya Piṭaka. Those with a sense of shame retain the Vinaya Piṭaka. But those who desire gain, even when the Suttas are spoken, do not pay attention, and retain only the Jātaka. As time passes, they cannot retain even the Jātaka. Then, first the Vessantara Jātaka is lost. Then, in reverse order, the Puṇṇaka Jātaka and the Mahānāradakassapa Jātaka are lost. They only retain the Vinaya Piṭaka. As time goes on, even that declines from the top. First, the Parivāra is lost, then the Khandhaka, the Bhikkhunīvibhaṅga, and the Mahāvibhaṅga in order. Only the Uposatha Khandhaka is retained; at that point, learning is not yet completely lost. But as long as even a four-line verse circulates among humans, learning is not completely lost. When a faithful, devoted king, placing a bag containing a thousand coins in a golden casket atop an elephant, has a drum beaten in the city, proclaiming, “Whoever knows a four-line verse spoken by the Buddhas, let them take this thousand,” and after the first proclamation, though some hear and some do not, no one takes it, and even after the third proclamation no one takes it, and the king’s men return the bag of a thousand to the royal palace, then learning is said to have disappeared. Thus, when learning disappears, practice and penetration also disappear. The rarity of hearing the True Dhamma should be shown with the story of Dhammasoṇḍaka. It is said that our Perfectly Self-Enlightened Buddha, not long after the Dispensation of the Perfectly Self-Enlightened Buddha Kassapa, the King of Dhamma, disappeared, was born as Prince Dhammasoṇḍaka, son of the King of Bārāṇasī. After his father’s death, he was established in the kingdom. Desiring to hear the Dhamma taught by Kassapa, the Ten-Powered One, after ruling for about a month, our Perfectly Self-Enlightened Buddha, while ruling a kingdom like that of a universal monarch in this very fortunate aeon, in the city of Bārāṇasī, which was like a city of the gods, thought thus: “What enjoyment is this kingdom to me, what royal power, without the True Dhamma? Like the sky without the sun, like a night without the moon, like an elephant without tusks, like a great ocean without a shore, like a well-adorned face without eyes, like a pārichatta flower without fragrance, my kingdom does not shine without the teaching of the Dhamma limited to four syllables.” Thinking thus, having a bag containing a thousand coins placed in a golden casket on a well-adorned auspicious elephant's forehead, he had the drum beaten in the great street of Bārāṇasī: “I will give this to whoever knows a one-line, two-line, three-line, or four-line Dhamma verse.” Having thus had the drum beaten, and finding no one who knew the Dhamma, he repeatedly increased the reward to two thousand, three thousand, up to a hundred thousand, a crore, two thousand crores, a hundred thousand crores, and offered the positions of village, town, and district head, commander-in-chief, and viceroy. Finally, finding no teacher of the Dhamma, even after offering to give up his golden throne and white parasol, still finding no teacher of the Dhamma, abandoning royal glory, giving up himself, saying, “Even as a slave to a teacher of the Dhamma, I will hear the Dhamma,” and even then finding no teacher of the Dhamma, he was remorseful, thinking, “What is this kingdom to me without the True Dhamma?” Entrusting the kingdom to his ministers, King Dhammasoṇḍaka became a seeker of the True Dhamma and entered the great forest. On account of King Dhammasoṇḍaka's quest for the hearing of the True Dhamma, at the very moment he entered the forest, Sakka, the King of the Gods' Vejayanta Palace, together with its pinnacle, trembled; the Paṇḍukambala stone seat grew hot. Sakka, the King of the Gods, wondering, “For what reason has the Paṇḍukambala stone seat grown hot?” surveyed the world of gods and humans with his thousand eyes, and seeing King Dhammasoṇḍaka, who had entered the forest as a seeker of the Dhamma, thought: “Today, I must abandon my own form, create the appearance of a demon, show him the fault in this entire body, which is a manifestation of impermanence, birth, aging, sickness, and death, teach him the Dhamma, and establish him in his own kingdom.” Thinking thus, Sakka, the King of the Gods, created a demon's form and showed himself not far from the Bodhisatta, facing him. Seeing him, King Dhammasoṇḍaka thought thus: “Could such a demon know the Dhamma?” Thinking thus, standing not far away, he decided to ask. Conversing with the demon, he said, “O lord, meritorious king of the gods, O king of the gods dwelling in this dense forest, do you indeed know the Dhamma?” The deity replied, “Great king, I know the Dhamma.” “If you know the Dhamma, please deliver a Dhamma talk to me,” he said. “I will deliver the Dhamma to you, but what honor will you give to me, a Dhamma teacher?” he said. “In that case, after you have delivered the Dhamma to me, you may eat the flesh of my body,” he said. “Great king, I cannot deliver the Dhamma while I am hungry,” he said. “If you eat the flesh first, who will listen to the Dhamma?” he said. Again, that demon said, “I cannot teach the Dhamma.” Again, the king said, “Knowing that my gain is the Dhamma and your gain is my flesh, please teach me the Dhamma.” Then Sakka, the King of the Gods, saying, “So be it,” created a huge blue-black mountain about three leagues in height nearby, and said thus: “Great king, if you climb to the top of this mountain, leap from the sky, and fall into my mouth, I will teach you the Dhamma as you are falling through the sky. In that case, you will gain the Dhamma, and I will gain your flesh,” he said. Hearing his words, King Dhammasoṇḍaka thought: “In this beginningless saṃsāra, having been a person endowed with unrighteousness, for the sake of unrighteousness alone I have killed living beings, taken what was not given, and engaged in sexual misconduct. The blood shed from the beheading of those caught as pig-slaughterers, sheep-slaughterers, bird-catchers, thieves, and adulterers is more than the water in the four great oceans. The tears shed by those crying for the sake of loved ones like mother and father is more than the water in the four great oceans. Now, I sell this body for the sake of the Dhamma; that is a great and pleasing result.” Thinking thus, he said, “It is well, sir, I will do that.” He climbed the mountain, stood at the peak, and becoming joyful, thought, “I will give my living body together with my kingdom for the sake of the True Dhamma.” Having sacrificed his life for the sake of the True Dhamma, he leaped from the sky, saying, “Teach the Dhamma!” Then Sakka, the King of the Gods, in his own form, adorned with all his ornaments, exceedingly joyous to hear, as the king fell from the sky, touched him with a divine touch, received him on his chest, took him to the world of the gods, seated him on the Paṇḍukambala stone seat, honored him with garlands, perfumes, and so forth, and while teaching King Dhammasoṇḍaka the Dhamma, spoke this verse:

“Aniccā vata saṅkhārā, uppādavayadhammino;

Uppajjitvā nirujjhanti, tesaṃ vūpasamo sukho”ti.

Impermanent, alas, are formations, subject to origination and dissolution. Having arisen, they cease; their stilling is bliss.

Evaṃ sakko dhammasoṇḍakamahārājassa dhammaṃ desetvā devalokasampattiṃ dassetvā devalokato ānetvā sakarajje patiṭṭhāpetvā appamādena ovaditvā devalokameva agamāsi evaṃ saddhammasavanassāpi dullabhabhāvo veditabbo. Evaṃ pariyattiantaradhānena paṭipatti paṭivedhāpi [M 0.0090] antaradhāyanti. Ettha ca tīṇi parinibbānāni veditabbāni katamāni tīṇi parinibbānāni. Kilesaparinibbānaṃ, khandhaparinibbānaṃ, dhātuparinibbānanti. Tattha kilesaparinibbānaṃ bodhimaṇḍeyeva hoti, bhagavā hi bodhimaṇḍeyeva vīriyapādehi sīlapathaviyaṃ patiṭṭhāya saddhāhatthena kammakkhayakaraṃ ñāṇapharasuṃ gahetvā sabbo lobhadosamohaviparītamanasikāraahirīkānottappakodhūpanāhamakkhapaḷāsa- issāmacchariyamāyāsāṭheyyathambhasārambhamānāti mānamadapamādataṇhā’vijjātividhākusalamūladuccaritasaṃkilesamalavisamasaññāvitakka papañcacatubbidhavipariyesaāsavaganthaoghayogā’gatiganthu’ pādānapañcacetokhilavinibandhanīvaraṇā’bhinandana chavivādamūlataṇhākāyasattānusaya aṭṭhamicchattanavataṇhāmūlakadasākusalakammapatha dvāsaṭṭhidiṭṭhigata aṭṭhasatataṇhāvicaritappa bheda sabbadarathapariḷāhakilesasatasahassāni, saṅkhepato vā pañca kilesaabhisaṅkhārakhandhamaccudevaputtamāre asesato hatā vihatā anabhāvaṃkatā tasmā sabbepi kilesā bodhimaṇḍeyeva nibbānaṃ nirodhaṃ gacchantīti kilesanibbānaṃ bodhimaṇḍeyeva hoti ettha ca bodhīti arahattamaggaññāṇaṃ adhippetaṃ tathā hi sabbesampi buddhapaccekabuddhaariyasāvakānaṃ arahattamaggakkhaṇeyeva sabbepi kilesā asesaṃ nirodhaṃ nibbānaṃ gacchanti tepi buddhā ugghaṭitaviññūvibhajjitaññūneyyavasena tividhā honti vuttañhetaṃ jātattakīsotattakīnidāne.

Thus, Sakka, having taught the Dhamma to King Dhammasoṇḍaka, showed him the attainments of the deva-world, brought him from the deva-world, established him in his own kingdom, exhorted him to heedfulness, and returned to the deva-world. In this way, the difficulty of hearing the true Dhamma should be understood. Thus, with the disappearance of scriptural learning, practice and realization also disappear. Here, three kinds of complete Nibbāna should be understood. What are the three? The complete Nibbāna of defilements, the complete Nibbāna of aggregates, and the complete Nibbāna of elements. Among these, the complete Nibbāna of defilements occurs right at the Bodhi-maṇḍa. For the Blessed One, standing firmly on the ground of virtue with the feet of energy, took up the axe of knowledge that destroys kamma with the hand of faith and utterly annihilated all defilements—greed, hatred, delusion, wrong attention, lack of shame and fear of wrongdoing, anger, resentment, hypocrisy, malice, envy, stinginess, deceit, fraudulence, stubbornness, rivalry, pride, conceit, heedlessness, craving, ignorance; the three unwholesome roots, misconduct, corruptions, stains, wrong perceptions, thoughts and mental proliferation, the four distortions, influxes, bonds, floods, yokes, ways of going, knots, attachments, the five mental barrennesses, fetters, hindrances, delight, the sixty-two roots of contention, underlying tendencies of craving, identity-view, and doubt, the eight wrongnesses, the nine roots of craving, the ten unwholesome courses of action, the sixty-two kinds of views, the one hundred and eight modes of craving, and all vexations, fevers, and hundreds of thousands of defilements. In brief, the five Māras—defilement, volitional formations, aggregates, death, and the divine son—were utterly destroyed, eliminated, and brought to non-existence. Therefore, all defilements reach cessation and Nibbāna right at the Bodhi-maṇḍa. Thus, the Nibbāna of defilements occurs right at the Bodhi-maṇḍa. Here, 'Bodhi' refers to the knowledge of the path of arahantship. For at the moment of attaining the path of arahantship, all defilements of all Buddhas, Paccekabuddhas, and noble disciples cease entirely and reach Nibbāna. Those Buddhas are also threefold: those who understand quickly, those who understand through explanation, and those who need guidance—as stated in the Jātaka in the stories of Jātattaki and Kīsotattaki.

“Ugghaṭitaññunāmako, vibhajjitaññuno duve;

Tatiyo neyyo nāmena, bodhisatto tidhā mato.

One is called the one of quick understanding, the second is the one understanding through analysis; the third is named the one to be led—thus, the Bodhisatta is known to be threefold.

Ugghaṭitaññubodhisatto, paññādhikoti nāmaso;

Vibhajjitaññubodhisatto, vutto vīriyādhiko.

The Bodhisatta of quick understanding is foremost in wisdom; the Bodhisatta who understands through analysis is said to be foremost in energy.

Mato neyyo saddhādhiko nāma, bodhisattā ime tayo;

Kappeca satasahasse, caturo ca asaṅkhyeyye.

The one to be led is known as foremost in faith; these are the three Bodhisattas. For four incalculable aeons and a hundred thousand aeons,

Pūretvā bodhisambhāre, laddhabyākaraṇo pure;

Ugghaṭitaññubodhisatto, patto sambodhimuttamaṃ.

having fulfilled the requisites for enlightenment and received the prophecy in the past, the Bodhisatta of quick understanding attained supreme enlightenment.

Aṭṭhame [M 0.0091] ca asaṅkhyeyye, kappe ca satasahasse;

Pūretvā bodhisambhāre, laddhabyākaraṇo pure.

For eight incalculable aeons and a hundred thousand aeons, having fulfilled the requisites for enlightenment and received the prophecy in the past,

Vipañcitaññubodhisatto, patto sambodhimuttamaṃ;

Neyyo tu bodhisatto ca, soḷase asaṅkhyeyye.

the Bodhisatta of analytical understanding attained supreme enlightenment. The Bodhisatta to be led, however, takes sixteen incalculable aeons

Kappe ca satasahasse, laddhabyākaraṇo pure;

Pūretvā bodhisambhāre, patto sambodhimuttama” nti.

and a hundred thousand aeons; having received the prophecy in the past and fulfilled the requisites for enlightenment, he attained supreme enlightenment.

Suttanipātaapadānaṭṭhakathāsu pana “buddhānaṃ ānanda heṭṭhimaparicchedena cattāri asaṅkhyeyyāni kappasatasahassañca, majjhimaparicchedena aṭṭha asaṅkhyeyyāni kappasatasahassañca, uparimaparicchedena soḷasāsaṅkhyyeyyāni kappasatasahassañca. Eteca paññādhikasaddhādhikavīriyādhikavasena veditabbāti vuttaṃ tesu paññādhiko cattāri asaṅkhyeyyāni kappasatasahassañca, saddhādhiko aṭṭhaasaṅkheyyeyyāni kappasatasahassañca, vīriyādhiko soḷasaasaṅkhyeyyāni kappasatasahassañcāti veditabbaṃ. Tattha paññādhiko yonigativiññāṇaṭṭhitisattāvāsesu saṃsarantopi paññābahullavasena sampannajjhāsaya sambhavato khippaññeva tassa sambodhi. Saddhādhiko pana mandapaññattā assaddahitabbepi saddahati, tasmā tassa mandaññeva sambodhi. Vīriyādhiko pana ubhayamando assaddahitabbampi saddahati, akattabbampi karoti, rājā passenadīkosalo yathā so hi sabbaññubuddhe dharamāneyeva agamanīyampi paradāraṃ gantuṃ cittaṃ uppādetvā paraṃ jīvitā voropetuṃ āraddho nerayikānaṃ viravantānaṃ du-sa-na-soti saddampi sutvā ativimūḷho sabbaññubuddhaṃ ṭhapetvā micchādiṭṭhibrāhmaṇaṃ pucchitvā tassa vacanena sabbajanānaṃ yaññatthāya dukkhaṃ uppādesi ko panavādo anuppanne buddhe, tathā hi esa kassapabhagavato sāsanantaradhānena andhabhūte loke bārāṇasiyaṃ rājā hutvāpi nigrodharukkhadevatāya yaññatthāya ekasatarājāno mahesīhi saddhiṃ māretuṃ āraddho. Evaṃ vīriyādhiko ubhayamando, tasmā tassa sambodhi atimandoti evaṃ paññādhikasaddhādhikavīriyādhikavasena kālassāpi rassadīghabhāvo veditabboti. Pacchimanayoeva pasaṃsitabboti ayamettha amhākaṃ attanomati. Khandhaparinibbānaṃ pana kusinārāya upavattane mallānaṃ sālavane yamakasālānamantare [M 0.0092] vesākhapuṇṇamadivase paccūsasamaye ekūnavīsatiyā cuticittesu mettāpubbabhāgassa somanassañāṇasampayuttaasaṅkhārikakusalacittasadisena mahāvipākacittena abyākatena carimakaṃ katvā katthaci bhave paṭisandhiviññāṇassa anantarapaccayo hutvā kammataṇhākilesehi anupādāno sabbupadhipaṭinissaggo upādinnakakkhandhapariccāgo hotīti veditabbaṃ. Vitthāro pana dīghanikāye mahāvagge mahāparinibbānasuttavaṇṇanāyaṃ oloketabbo. Tattha videsaṃ gacchanto puriso sabbaṃ ñātijanaṃ āliṅgetvā sīse cumbitvā gacchati viya bhagavāpi nibbānapuraṃ pavisanto sabbepi catuvīsatikoṭisatasahassasamāpattiyo anavasesaṃ samāpajjitvā yāva saññāvedayitaṃ, tatopi vuṭṭhāya yāva nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ samāpajjitvā vuṭṭhāya jhānaṅgāni paccavekkhitvā bhavaṅgacittena abyākatena dukkhasaccena parinibbāyi. Pāḷiyaṃ pana “catutthajjhānā vuṭṭhahitvā samanantarā bhagavā parinibbāyī”ti vuttaṃ tattha dve samanantarā jhānasamanantarā, paccavekkhaṇasamanantarāti. Tattha jhānā vuṭṭhāya bhavaṅgaṃ otiṇṇassa tattheva parinibbānaṃ jhānasamanantaraṃ nāma. Jhānā vuṭṭhahitvā puna jhānaṅgāni paccavekkhitvā bhavaṅgaṃ otiṇṇassa tattheva parinibbānaṃ paccavekkhaṇasamanantaraṃ nāma. Bhagavā pana jhānasamanantarā apari nibbāyittā paccavekkhaṇasamanantarameva parinibbāyīti veditabbaṃ, tenevāha “bhagavā pana jhānaṃ samāpajjitvā jhānā vuṭṭhāya jhānaṅgāni paccavekkhitvā bhavaṅgacittena abyākatena dukkhasaccena parinibbāyī”ti. Ettha bhagavato parinibbānacittassa kiṃ ārammaṇaṃ kammaṃ vā hoti, udāhu kammanimittagatinimittāni, atha nibbānanti apare evaṃ vadanti.

In the commentaries to the Suttanipāta and the Apadāna, it is said: 'For the Buddhas, according to the lower reckoning, there are four incalculable aeons and one hundred thousand aeons; according to the middle reckoning, eight incalculable aeons and one hundred thousand aeons; and according to the higher reckoning, sixteen incalculable aeons and one hundred thousand aeons. These should be understood as distinguished by wisdom, faith, and energy. Among them, the one distinguished by wisdom requires four incalculable aeons and one hundred thousand aeons; the one distinguished by faith requires eight incalculable aeons and one hundred thousand aeons; and the one distinguished by energy requires sixteen incalculable aeons and one hundred thousand aeons.' Here, the one distinguished by wisdom, even though wandering through the states of existence in various wombs, destinations, stations of consciousness, and abodes of beings, due to the abundance of wisdom and being endowed with a well-developed inclination, attains enlightenment very quickly. The one distinguished by faith, however, being of dull wisdom, believes even in what should not be believed; therefore, his enlightenment is slow. The one distinguished by energy is dull in both—he believes in what should not be believed and does what should not be done, like King Pasenadi of Kosala. For he, while the Omniscient Buddha was still alive, conceived the intention to commit adultery with another's wife and even attempted to take another's life. Hearing the cries of those in hell saying 'du-sa-na-so,' he became utterly confused and, setting aside the Omniscient Buddha, consulted a heretical brahmin. Following his advice, he caused suffering to all people for the sake of a sacrifice—what more need be said about when no Buddha has arisen? Similarly, after the dispensation of the Blessed Kassapa had disappeared and the world had become blind, he was reborn as a king in Bārāṇasī. For the sake of sacrifice to the deity of the banyan tree, he attempted to kill one hundred and one kings along with their chief queens. Thus, the one distinguished by energy is dull in both, and therefore his enlightenment is extremely slow. In this way, the length or brevity of time should be understood according to whether one is distinguished by wisdom, faith, or energy. The latter view is the one to be praised—this is our own opinion here. Regarding the final passing away of the aggregates, it should be understood that in Kusinārā, in the Mallas' sāla grove at Upavattana, between the twin sāla trees, on the full moon day of Vesākha, at dawn, after nineteen death-consciousness moments, with a great resultant consciousness, indeterminate, resembling an unprompted wholesome consciousness accompanied by joy and knowledge, with loving-kindness as its forerunner, having made it the last consciousness, there was the relinquishment of all substrata and the abandonment of the clung-to aggregates, being non-clinging due to the absence of kamma, craving, and defilements, and thus not becoming an immediate condition for rebirth-linking consciousness in any existence. The details should be examined in the commentary on the Mahāparinibbāna Sutta in the Mahāvagga of the Dīgha Nikāya. There, just as a man going abroad embraces all his relatives, kisses them on the head, and departs, so too the Blessed One, entering the city of Nibbāna, attained without remainder all twenty-four trillion attainments, up to the cessation of perception and feeling. Rising from that, he attained the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, emerged, reviewed the factors of jhāna, and attained final Nibbāna with a life-continuum consciousness, which is indeterminate, by means of the truth of suffering. In the Pāli, it is said: 'Having emerged from the fourth jhāna, the Blessed One attained final Nibbāna immediately after.' Here, 'immediately after' refers to two possibilities: immediately after the jhāna and immediately after the reviewing. In the first case, emerging from the jhāna and sinking into the life-continuum, final Nibbāna occurs right there—this is called 'immediately after the jhāna.' In the second case, emerging from the jhāna, reviewing the factors of jhāna, and sinking into the life-continuum, final Nibbāna occurs right there—this is called 'immediately after the reviewing.' It should be understood that the Blessed One attained final Nibbāna immediately after the reviewing, not immediately after the jhāna. Hence it is said: 'The Blessed One, having entered jhāna, emerged, reviewed the factors of jhāna, and attained final Nibbāna with a life-continuum consciousness, which is indeterminate, by means of the truth of suffering.' Here, some ask: 'What is the object of the Blessed One's final consciousness—is it kamma, or the signs of kamma and the destination, or is it Nibbāna?' Others say thus.

Nāhu assāsapassāsā, ṭhitacittassa tādino;

Anejo santi’mārabbha, yaṃkāla’makarī muni.

There were no in-breaths or out-breaths for the steadfast one of firm mind; unwavering, intent on peace, the sage made his end.

Asallīnena cittena, vedanaṃ ajjhavāsayi;

Pajjotasse’va nibbānaṃ, vimokkho cetaso ahū’ti.

With an unshrinking mind, he endured the feeling; like the extinguishing of a lamp, was the liberation of his heart.

Imissāgāthāya “yaṃ yo muni anejo santi nibbānaṃ ārabbha kālaṃ akarī”ti yojetvā bhagavato parinibbānacittassa nibbānārammaṇanti tamayuttaṃ, paṭisandhibhavaṅgacutīnaṃ nibbānārammaṇassa anārahattā “nibbānaṃ gotrabhussa vodānassa maggassa ārammaṇapaccayenapaccayo, nibbānaṃ phalassa [M 0.0093] āvajjanāyā”ti paṭṭhānapāḷiyā javanaāvajjanānameva adhippetattā tasmā ettha “yaṃ yo muni phalasamāpattiyā anejo anejasaṅkhāto taṇhārahito santiṃ nibbānaṃ ārabbha ārammaṇaṃ katvā kālaṃ asītivassaparimāṇaṃ akari atikkamī”ti yojanā kātabbā. Keci pana evaṃ vadanti… “katthaci pana anuppajjamānassa khīṇāsavassa yathopaṭṭhitaṃ nāmarūpadhammādikameva cutipariyosānānaṃ gocarabhāvaṃ gacchati, na kamma-kammanimittādayo”ti vuttattā kammanittagatinimittāni arahato cuticittassa ārammaṇabhāvaṃ na gacchantīti tampi ayuttameva ayañhetthattho katthaci pana bhave anuppajjamānassa khīṇāsavassa arahato yathā yathā yena yena pakārena upaṭṭhitaṃ nāmarūpadhammādikameva cutipariyosānaṃ āvajjanajavanacittānaṃ gocarabhāvaṃ gacchati, puna bhavābhinibbattiyā abhāvato. Kiṃ kāraṇaṃ bhūtāni kammakammanimittagatinimittāni gocarabhāvaṃ na gacchantīti. Cuticittassa pana paṭisandhicittena gahitaṃ atītārammaṇameva gocarabhāvaṃ gacchati. “Nāmarūpādikamevā ti ettha nāma”nti cittacetasikanibbānaṃ rūpanti aṭṭhārasavidhaṃ rūpaṃ saṅgaṇhāti. Nāmañca rūpañca nāmarūpā. Nāmarūpā ca te dhammā ceti tathā. Te ādi yesaṃ teti nāmarūpadhammādi tameva nāmarūpadhammādikaṃ. Ādisaddena cha paññattiyo saṅgaṇhāti, tena nibbānampi arahato maraṇāsannakāle kriyajavanassapi ārammaṇabhāvo bhaveyyāti amhākaṃ khanti vīmaṃsitvā pana gahetabbaṃ. Bhagavato nibbānacittassa pana tusitapurato cavitvā sirīmahāmāyāya kucchimhi vasitapaṭisandhicittena gahitārammaṇameva ārammaṇaṃ hotīti daṭṭhabbaṃ tañca kho gatinimittameva, na kammakammanimittāni. Yuttitopi āgamatopi gatinimittameva yujjati tathā hi tusitapureyeva setaketudevaputto hutvā dibbagaṇanāya cattāri sahassāni, manussagaṇanāya sattapaññāsavassakoṭi,saṭṭhivassasatasahassāni ṭhatvā pariyosāne pañca pubbanimittāni disvā suddhāvāse arahantabrahmunā dasahi cakkavāḷasahassehi āgamma devatāvisesehi ca.

It is unsuitable to interpret this verse, 'Whatever sage, unwavering, having taken the peace of Nibbāna as object, completed his time,' by connecting it with the Blessed One's parinibbāna-consciousness having Nibbāna as its object. This is because rebirth-linking, life-continuum, and death consciousnesses cannot have Nibbāna as their object. Therefore, in the Paṭṭhāna-pāḷi, it is stated: 'Nibbāna is a condition for the change-of-lineage, purification, and path by way of the object condition; Nibbāna is also a condition for the adverting of the fruition.' Here, only the moments of impulsion and adverting are intended. Thus, in this context, the interpretation should be: 'Whatever sage, unwavering through fruition attainment—termed 'unshakable' and devoid of craving—having taken the peace of Nibbāna as object, passed the time measuring eighty years and went beyond.' Some, however, say this: 'For an arahant who will not arise again, at the time of death, only the phenomena of mind and matter, etc., as they appear, become the object for the concluding process, not kamma, kamma-signs, or destiny-signs.' This, too, is incorrect. The meaning here is that for an arahant who will not arise again in any existence, whatever phenomena of mind and matter, etc., appear in whatever way, those become the object for the final adverting and impulsion consciousnesses, because there is no further becoming. Why is it that kamma, kamma-signs, and destiny-signs do not become objects? Because the death consciousness takes only a past object, the same object as the rebirth-linking consciousness. In 'Only mind and matter, etc.,' 'mind' includes consciousness, mental factors, and Nibbāna, while 'matter' refers to the eighteen kinds of materiality. Mind and matter together are called 'mind-and-matter.' These phenomena are the ādi, hence 'mind-and-matter phenomena, etc.' The term 'ādi' includes the six kinds of concepts; therefore, even Nibbāna could be an object for the functional impulsion consciousness of an arahant near the time of death. This is our opinion, but it should be examined carefully. However, for the Blessed One’s parinibbāna-consciousness, the object was what was taken by the rebirth-linking consciousness when he descended from the Tusita realm and entered the womb of Queen Sirīmahāmāyā. That object was only a destiny-sign, not kamma or kamma-signs. Both reason and scripture support that only a destiny-sign is appropriate. For while still in the Tusita realm, as the devaputta Setaketu, he remained there for four thousand divine years, or five hundred seventy-six million human years. At the end, seeing the five premonitory signs, he was approached by the Arahant-Brahmās from the Pure Abodes and by distinguished deities from ten thousand world-systems.

“Kālo deva mahāvīra, uppajja mātukucchayaṃ;

Sadevakaṃ tārayanto, bujjhassu amataṃ pada”nti.

“It is time, O Deva, Great Hero, to arise in a mother’s womb; delivering the world with its gods, awaken to the deathless state.”

Yāciyamāno [M 0.0094] “kālaṃ dīpañca desañca, kulaṃ mātarameva cā”ti vuttāni pañca mahāvilokanāni viloketvā tusitapurato cavitvā āsāḷhīpuṇṇamāyaṃ uttarāsāḷhanakkhatteneva saddhiṃ ekūnavīsatiyā paṭisandhicittesu mettāpubbabhāgamassa somanassañāṇasampayuttaasaṅkhārikakusalacittassa sadisena mahāvipākacittena paṭisandhiṃ aggahesi. Tadārammaṇāsannavīthito pubbabhāge ālokitāni kāladīpadesakulamātaravasena imāni pañca paṭisandhicittassa gatinimittārammaṇabhāvena gocarabhāvaṃ gacchantīti amhākaṃ khanti tanninnatappoṇatappabbhāravasena bāhullappavattito teneva abhidhammatthasaṅgahādīsu abhidhammattha vibhāvaniyaṃ “maraṇakāle yathārahaṃ abhimukhībhūtaṃ bhavantare paṭisandhijanakaṃ kammaṃ vā taṃ kammakaraṇakāle rūpādikamupaladdhapubbamupakaraṇabhūtañca kammanimittaṃ vā anantaramuppajjamānabhave upalabhitabbaṃ upabhogabhūtaṃ gatinimittaṃ vā kammabalena channaṃ dvārānamaññatarasmiṃ paccupaṭṭhāsi, tato paraṃ tameva tathopaṭṭhitamālambaṇaṃ ārabbha vipaccamānakakammānurūpaṃ parisuddhaṃ vā, upakkiliṭṭhaṃ vā upapajjitabbabhavānurūpaṃ tatthoṇataṃva cittasantānamabhiṇhaṃ pavattati bāhullena. Tameva vā pana janakabhūtaṃ kammaṃ abhinavakaraṇavasena dvārappattaṃ hoti. Paccāsannamaraṇassa pana tassa vīthicittāvasāne bhavaṅgakkhayevā cavanavasena paccuppannabhavapariyosānabhūtaṃ cuticittaṃ uppajjitvā nirujjhati. Tasmiṃ niruddhāvasāne tassānantarameva tathā gahitamālambaṇamārabbha savatthukamavatthukameva vā yathārahaṃ avijjānusayaparikkhittena taṇhānusayamūlakena saṅkhārena janiyamānaṃ sampayuttehi pariggayhamānaṃ sahajātānamadhiṭṭhānabhāvena pubbaṅgamabhūtaṃ bhavantarapaṭisandhānavasena paṭisandhisaṅkhātaṃ mānasamuppajjamānameva patiṭṭhāti bhavantare”ti vuttaṃ. Tattha “tatthoṇataṃva cittasantānamabhiṇhaṃ pavattati bāhullenā”ti iminā kammabalena upaṭṭhitaṃ gatinimittaṃ maraṇāsannavīthito pubbe ekāhadvīhādivasena sattāhampi, sattāhato uttaripi uppajjate vāti dasseti tathā hi parisuddhaṃ vā upakkiliṭṭhaṃ vā vipaccamānakakammānurūpaṃ gatinimittaṃ cirakālampi tiṭṭhati, ariyagālatissacoraghātakādayo viya tathā hi ariyagālatisso nāma upāsako sīhaḷadīpe attano bhariyāya sumanāya saddhiṃ yāvajīvaṃ dānādi puññakammāni [M 0.0095] katvā āyūhapariyosāne ariyagālatissassa maraṇamañce nipannassa chadevalokato rathaṃ ānetvā attano attano devalokaṃ vaṇṇesuṃ. Upāsako devatānaṃ kathaṃ sutvā tusitapurato āhaṭarathaṃ ṭhapetvā avasesarathe “gahetvā gacchathā”ti āha. Sumanā pana attano sāmikassa vacanaṃ sutvā “kiṃ tissa maraṇāsanne vilāpaṃ akāsī”ti āha. Tisso attano bhariyāya kathaṃ sutvā āha… “ahaṃ vilāpaṃ na karomi, devalokato devatā cha rathe ānesuṃ tāhi devatāhi saddhaṃ kathemī”ti. Taṃ napassāmi kuhi”nti vutte pupphadāmaṃ āharāpetvā ākāse khipāpesi. Sā taṃ pupphadāmaṃ rathasīse olambamānaṃ disvā gabbhaṃ pavisitvā sayane sayitvā nāsikavātaṃ sannirujjhitvā cavitvā pāturahosi. Sā attano sāmikassa sāsanaṃ pesesi… “ahaṃ pana paṭhamaṃ āgatomhi, tvaṃ kasmā cirāyasī”ti. Ubhopi rathe ṭhatvā sabbe olokentānaṃyeva tusitapuraṃ agamaṃsu. Imasmiñhi vatthusmiṃ sāmikassa upaṭṭhitagatinimittaṃ bhariyāya pākaṭaṃ hutvā puretaratusitapure nibbattitvā sāmikassa sāsanaṃ pesesi tena ariyagālatissassa cirakālaṃ gatinimittaṃ upaṭṭhātīti veditabbaṃ. Manussaloke hi cirakālaṃ tusitapure muhuttaṃva hoti. Evaṃ parisuddhaṃ vipaccamānakakammānurūpaṃ gatinimittaṃ cirakālaṃ pavattati iminā nayena duṭṭhagāmaṇiabhayadhammikaupsakādīnampi vatthu vitthāretabbaṃ. Coraghātakassa pana mahāniraye vipaccamānakakammānurūpaṃ nerayaggijālādikaṃ sattāhaṃ upaṭṭhāti. Sāvatthinagare kira pañcasatā corā bahinagare corakammaṃ karonti. Athekadivasaṃ janapadapuriso tesaṃ abbhantaro hutvā corakammaṃ akāsi, tadā te sabbepi rājapurisā aggahesuṃ. Rājā te disvā “tumhākaṃ antare ime sabbe māretuṃ samatthassa jīvitaṃ dammī”ti āha. Pañcasatā corā aññamaññaṃ sambandhā aññamaññaṃ sahāyakāti aññamaññaṃ māretuṃ na icchiṃsu, janapadamanusso pana “ahaṃ māremī”ti vatvā sabbe māresi. Rājā tassa tussitvā coraghātakakammaṃ adāsi. So pañcavīsativassāni coraghātakakammaṃ ākāsi. Rājā tassa mahallakoti vatvā aññassa coraghātaka kammaṃ dāpesi. So coraghātakakammā apanīto aññatarassa santike [M 0.0096] nāsikavātaṃ uggaṇhitvā hatthapādakaṇṇanāsādīhi chinditabbānaṃ ūrūthanakaṭṭhānaṃ bhindantamāretabbayuttānaṃ nāsikavātaṃ vissajjetvā māretabbaṃ mantaṃ labhi. So rājānaṃ ārocetvā nāsikavātena coraghātakakammaṃ karontasseva tiṃsa vassāni atikkami. So pacchā mahallako hutvā maraṇamañce nipajji “sattadivasena kālaṃ karissatī”ti. Maraṇakāle mahantaṃ vedanaṃ ahosi. So mahāniraye nibbatto viya mahāsaddaṃ katvā dukkhito hoti. Tassa saddena bhītā manussā ubhatopasse gehaṃ chaḍḍetvā palāyiṃsu. Tassa maraṇadivase sāriputtatthero dibbacakkhunā lokaṃ olokento etassa kālaṃ katvā mahāniraye nibbattamānaṃ disvā tassa anukampaṃ paṭicca gehadvāre pākaṭo ahosi. So kujjhitvā nāsikavātaṃ vissajjesi. Yāva tatiyaṃ vissajjamānopi vissajjāpetuṃ asakkonto theraṃ atirekena virocamānaṃ disvā cittaṃ pasādetvā pāyāsaṃ therassa dāpesi. Thero maṅgalaṃ vaḍḍhetvā agamāsi. Coraghātako therassa dinnadānaṃ anussaritvā cavitvā sagge nibbatti, nirayajālādayo antaradhāyanti. So anāgatepi paccekabuddho bhavissati. Evaṃ upakkiliṭṭhaṃ vā vipaccamāna kakammānurūpaṃ gatinimittampi sattāhaparamaṃ upaṭṭhāti. Atthikehi pana sahassavatthu sagāthāvaggesu oloketabbo. Bhagavato pana parisuddhaṃ mettāpubbabhāgassa somanassa ñāṇasampayuttakusalassa kammassa vegena kāladīpadesakulamātaravasena imāni pañcagatinimittāni cirakālaṃ hutvā upaṭṭhahanti. Cirakālanti manussaloke sattāhamattaṃ, tusitapuremuhuttameva, tathāpi manussaloke sattāhamatte kāle devatānaṃ pañcapubbanimittāni paññāyanti tañca kho puññavantānaṃyeva paññāyanti, na sabbesaṃ manussaloke puññavantānaṃ rājarājamahāmattādīnaṃ viya taṃ disvā mahesakkhā devatā jānanti, na appesakkhā, manussaloke nemittakā brāhmaṇapaṇḍitādayoviya. Mahesakkhā devatā taṃ pubbanimittaṃ disvā nandavanaṃ netvā “kālo devā”ti ādīhi yācanti. Mahāsakko tattheva nandavanuyyāne kāladīpadesakulamātaravasena pañca viloketvā cavitvā tamārammaṇaṃ katvā mahāmāyāya kucchimhi paṭisandhiṃ aggahesīti sanniṭṭhānamavagantabbaṃ.

Being entreated, he contemplated the five great observations—the time, the continent, the country, the family, and the mother—and passing away from the Tusita realm, on the full moon day of Āsāḷhī, under the Uttarāsāḷha constellation, among the nineteen rebirth-linking consciousnesses, he took rebirth with a great resultant consciousness similar to the unprompted wholesome consciousness accompanied by joy and knowledge, preceded by loving-kindness. The five objects observed in the preliminary stage of the death-proximate thought-process—time, continent, country, family, and mother—become the objects of the rebirth-linking consciousness as signs of destiny. Our conviction is that due to their inclination, leaning, and weight, they frequently appear. Therefore, in the Abhidhammatthasaṅgaha and other expositions of Abhidhamma, it is explained: “At the time of death, the kamma that will produce rebirth in the next existence, or the kamma-sign (an object previously perceived during the performance of that kamma, or an instrumental object), or the destiny-sign (an object of enjoyment to be experienced in the immediately arising existence), appears at one of the six doors by the power of kamma. Thereafter, taking that very object thus presented as its basis, the stream of consciousness, whether pure or defiled, suitable for the existence to be attained, frequently and predominantly arises, inclined towards that very existence, in accordance with the ripening kamma. Or else, the generating kamma itself, as if newly performed, reaches the door.” For one nearing death, at the end of that thought-process, with the cessation of the life-continuum, the death consciousness, which is the termination of the present existence, arises and ceases. Immediately after its cessation, taking that very object thus grasped as its basis, whether with or without a physical basis, the rebirth-linking consciousness—a mental state generated by formations, enveloped by the latent tendency of ignorance, rooted in the latent tendency of craving, accompanied by its associated factors, and serving as the forerunner and support for co-nascent mental states—establishes itself in the next existence by way of rebirth-linking.” Here, the phrase “the stream of consciousness arises repeatedly and predominantly, inclined towards that very existence” indicates that the sign of destiny, presented by the power of kamma, may appear even a week or more before the death-proximate thought-process, for one, two days, etc., up to seven days, or even more than seven days. Thus, a pure or defiled sign of destiny, in accordance with the ripening kamma, may persist for a long time, as in the case of the lay disciple Ariyagālatissa and the executioner. Indeed, the lay disciple Ariyagālatissa in Sri Lanka, together with his wife Sumanā, performed meritorious deeds like giving throughout his life. At the end of his life, as Ariyagālatissa lay on his deathbed, deities from the six heavens brought chariots and described their respective heavenly realms. The lay disciple, hearing the words of the deities, kept aside the chariot brought from the Tusita realm and told the others, “Take them and go.” Sumanā, hearing her husband’s words, asked, “Tissa, why are you lamenting at the time of death?” Tissa, hearing his wife’s words, said, “I am not lamenting; deities from the heavens brought six chariots, and I am conversing with them.” When she said, “I do not see them anywhere,” he had a garland of flowers brought and thrown into the air. Seeing the garland hanging from the chariot’s pole, she entered her chamber, lay down on the bed, stopped her breath, passed away, and reappeared. She sent a message to her husband: “I have arrived first; why are you delaying?” Both stood in their chariots and, while all were watching, went to the Tusita realm. In this account, the sign of destiny presented to the husband became evident to the wife, who was reborn earlier in the Tusita realm and sent a message to her husband. Thus, it should be understood that Ariyagālatissa’s sign of destiny appeared for a long time. Indeed, a long time in the human world is but a moment in the Tusita realm. Similarly, a pure sign of destiny, in accordance with the ripening kamma, persists for a long time. In this way, the stories of Duṭṭhagāmaṇī, Abhayadhammika, and other lay disciples should also be elaborated. For the executioner, however, the fires of hell and so forth, in accordance with his ripening kamma, appeared for seven days in the great hell. It is said that in Sāvatthī, five hundred thieves committed robberies outside the city. Then one day, a man from the countryside joined them and committed a robbery, and at that time, all of them were captured by the king’s men. The king, seeing them, said, “Among you, I will grant life to whoever is able to kill all of these.” The five hundred thieves, being related to each other and friends, did not wish to kill each other, but the man from the countryside said, “I will kill them,” and slew them all. The king, pleased with him, gave him the duty of executioner. He performed the duty of executioner for twenty-five years. The king, saying he was old, had the duty of executioner given to another. Relieved of his duty as executioner, he learned from another the art of controlling the breath and obtained a mantra for killing by releasing breath on those who were to be executed by cutting off hands, feet, ears, nose, etc., and by breaking thighs and chests. He informed the king and continued performing the duty of executioner by means of the breath for thirty years. Later, having become old, he lay on his deathbed, and it was known he would die in seven days. At the time of death, he experienced great pain. He made a great sound, as if reborn in the great hell, and was distressed. Terrified by his sound, people abandoned their homes on both sides and fled. On his death day, the Venerable Sāriputta, surveying the world with his divine eye, saw him passing away and being reborn in the great hell, and out of compassion for him, appeared at his door. He, being angry, tried to release his breath. Even after trying to release it three times, being unable to do so, seeing the elder exceedingly radiant, he made his mind serene and offered milk-rice to the elder. The elder blessed him and departed. The executioner, remembering the gift given to the elder, passed away and was reborn in heaven, and the hell-fires and so forth vanished. He will also become a Paccekabuddha in the future. Thus, even a defiled sign of destiny, in accordance with ripening kamma, appears for up to seven days. Those interested should consult the Sahassavatthu in the Sagāthāvagga. For the Blessed One, however, due to the power of his pure wholesome kamma, preceded by loving-kindness, accompanied by joy and knowledge, these five signs of destiny—time, continent, country, family, and mother—appeared for a long time. “A long time” here means seven days in the human world, but just a moment in the Tusita realm. Nevertheless, during that seven-day period in the human world, the five preliminary signs appear to the deities. And indeed, they appear only to the meritorious, not to all. Seeing these signs, powerful deities know, but not those of lesser power—just as in the human world, soothsayers, brahmin scholars, and the like know the signs of meritorious people such as kings and great ministers. Powerful deities, seeing that preliminary sign, led him to Nandana Grove and entreated him with words like, “It is time, O deva.” The Great Being, having contemplated these five—time, continent, country, family, and mother—right there in Nandana Grove, passed away and, taking that object, took rebirth in Queen Mahāmāyā’s womb.
