This should be understood as the definitive account.

Tattha [M 0.0097] kālanti manussaloke ekūnatiṃsatime vasse vesākhapuṇṇamadivase buddho bhavissāmīti kālaṃ olokesi. Dīpanti jambudīpeyeva bhavissāmi, na aññadīpesūti dīpaṃ olokesi. Desanti majjhimadeseyeva, tañca kho mahābodhimaṇḍeyeva, na aññadeseti desaṃ olokesi. Kulanti dve kulāni khattiyakulabrāhmaṇakulavasena. Tattha yasmiṃ kāle khattiyakulaṃ loke seṭṭhabhāvena “ayameva loke aggo”ti sammannati, tadā khattiyakuleyeva buddhā uppajjanti. Yasmiṃ pana kāle brāhmaṇakulaṃ ayameva loke aggoti, tadā brāhmaṇakuleyeva buddhā uppajjanti. Idāni pana khattiyakulameva aggoti sammannanti. Khattiyakulepi sakyarājānova loke uttamā, asambhinnakhattiyakulattā tasmā sakyarājakuleyeva bhavissāmīti kulaṃ olokesi. Mātaranti mama mātaraṃ dasa māsāni satta ca divasāni ṭhassati, etthantare mama mātuyā arogo bhavissatīti mātarampi olokesi. Bodhisattamātā pana pacchimavaye ṭhitā. Evaṃ yuttitopi āgamatopi bhagavato nibbānacittassa paṭasandhicittena gahitanimittameva ārammaṇaṃ hotīti veditabbaṃ.

There, 'time' means: he considered the time, thinking, “I will become a Buddha in the human world on the full moon day of Vesākha in the twenty-ninth year.” 'Island' means: he considered the island, thinking, “I will be in Jambudīpa only, not in any other island.” 'Region' means: he considered the region, thinking, “I will be in the Middle Country only, and specifically at the great Bodhi terrace, not in any other region.” 'Family' means: there are two families—the warrior family and the brahmin family. At a time when the warrior family is esteemed in the world as the highest, regarded as “This is the foremost in the world,” then Buddhas are born in the warrior family. But at a time when the brahmin family is esteemed as the highest, then Buddhas are born in the brahmin family. Now, however, the warrior family is regarded as the highest. Among warrior families, the Sakyan royal family is the most excellent in the world, being of unmixed warrior lineage; therefore, he considered the family, thinking, “I will be born in the Sakyan royal family.” 'Mother' means: he also considered his mother, thinking, “My mother will live for ten months and seven days, and during that time my mother will be free from illness.” The mother of a Bodhisatta, however, is established in her final stage of life. Thus, both by reasoning and by tradition, it should be understood that for the Nibbāna-consciousness of the Blessed One, its object is precisely the sign grasped by the relinking consciousness.

Sabbaññujinanibbāna, cittassa gocaraṃ subhaṃ;

Viññeyyaṃ gatinimittaṃ, ganthehi avirodhato.

Nibbāna of the All-Knowing Conqueror, the mind's beautiful object, is to be understood as the sign of destination, without contradiction from the treatises.

Dhātuparinibbānampi bodhimaṇḍeyeva bhavissati vuttañhetaṃ uparipaṇṇāsake “dhātuparinibbānaṃ anāgate bhavissati sāsanassa hi osakkanakāle imasmiṃ tambapaṇṇidīpe dhātuyo sannipatitvā mahācetiyato nāgadīpe rājāyatanacetiyaṃ, tato mahābodhipallaṅkaṃ gamissanti. Nāgabhavanatopi devalokatopi dhātuyo mahābodhipallaṅkameva gamissanti sāsapamattāpi dhātuyo antarā na nassissanti, sabbā dhātuyo mahābodhipallaṅke rāsibhūtā suvaṇṇakkhandhā viya ekagghanā hutvā chabbaṇṇarasmiyo vissajjissanti, tā dasasahassalokadhātuṃ pharissanti. Tato dasasahassacakkavāḷesu devatā sannipatitvā ''ajja satthā parinibbāyati ajja sāsanaṃ osakkati pacchimadassanaṃ idaṃ amhāka”nti dasabalassa parinibbutadivasato mahantataraṃ kāruññaṃ karissanti ṭhapetvā anāgāmikhīṇāsave avasesā sakattabhāvena sandhāretuṃ [M 0.0098] nāsakkhiṃsu, dhātusarīrato tejodhātu uṭṭhahitvā yāva brahmalokā uggacchissati. Sāsapamattāyapi dhātuyā sati ekajālā bhavissati dhātūsu pariyādānaṃ gatāsu pacchijjissati. Evaṃ mahantaṃ ānubhāvaṃ dassetvā dhātūsu antarahitāsu sāsanaṃ antarahitaṃ nāma hoti. Yāva evaṃ na antaradhāyati, tāva anantaradhānameva sāsanaṃ. Parinibbānakālato paṭṭhāya yāva sāsapamattā dhātu tiṭṭhati, tāva buddhakālato pacchāti na veditabbaṃ dhātūsu hi ṭhitāsu buddhā ṭhitā va honti, tasmā etthantare aññassa buddhassa uppatti nivāritāva hoti. Teneva vadāma.

The relics' final extinction will also occur at the Bodhi seat, as stated in the Uparipaṇṇāsaka: “The relics' final extinction will occur in the future, when the Dispensation is declining. At that time, the relics will gather from this island of Tambapaṇṇi, from the Great Stupa to the Rājāyatana Shrine in Nāgadīpa, and then proceed to the Great Bodhi Throne. Even from the Nāga abodes and the heavenly realms, the relics will go only to the Great Bodhi Throne. Not even a mustard-seed-sized relic will perish along the way. All the relics will assemble at the Great Bodhi Throne, forming a mass like a solid golden block, emitting rays of six colors that will pervade the ten-thousandfold world-system. Then, deities from the ten-thousand world-systems will gather, lamenting, ‘Today the Teacher attains final extinction! Today the Dispensation declines! This is our last sight of him!’ They will feel greater sorrow than on the day of the Ten-Powered One's final passing. Except for non-returners and arahants, the remaining ones will be unable to bear their grief. A fiery element will rise from the relic-body, ascending as far as the Brahma-world. Even if only a mustard-seed-sized relic remains, it will become a single flame. When the relics are entirely consumed, the flame will be extinguished. After displaying such immense power, when the relics disappear, the Dispensation is said to have vanished. Until this happens, the Dispensation has not vanished. From the time of final extinction until even a mustard-seed-sized relic remains, it should not be considered ‘after the Buddha's time.’ For as long as the relics remain, it is as if the Buddhas remain. Therefore, during this interval, the arising of another Buddha is entirely prevented. Thus we declare.”

Parinibbānakālato, pacchā dharanti dhātuyo;

Anibbutova sambuddho, aññabuddhassa vāritā.

From the time of the Parinibbāna, the relics are preserved; as if the Sambuddha were not extinguished, the arising of another Buddha is prevented.

Jīvamānepi sambuddhe, nibbute vā tathāgate;

Yo karoti samaṃ pūjaṃ, phalaṃ tāsa samaṃ siyā’ti.

Whether the Sambuddha is living or the Tathāgata has passed into Nibbāna, for one who offers equal veneration, the fruit thereof will be equal.

Iti sāgarabuddhittheravicarite sīmavisodhane dhammavaṇṇanāya

Thus in the Dhamma exposition on the purification of boundaries, from the treatise of the Elder Sāgarabuddhi.

Nibbānakaṇḍo tatiyo paricchedo.

The Section on Nibbāna: The Third Chapter.

4. Samasīsikaṇḍo

4. The Chapter on Equal Heads

Idāni saṅgho ca dullabho loketi padassa vaṇṇanākkamo sampatto. Tattha saṅghoti paramatthasammutivasena duvidho hoti. Tesu paramatthasaṅgho cattāri purisayugāni, aṭṭhapurisapuggalā, cattālīsampi puggalā, aṭṭhasatampi purisapuggalāti catubbidho hoti. Tattha saṅkhepavasena sotāpattimaggaṭṭho phalaṭṭhoti ekaṃ yugaṃ, sakadāmimaggaṭṭho phalaṭṭhoti ekaṃ, anāgāmimaggaṭṭho phalaṭṭhoti ekaṃ, arahattamaggaṭṭho phalaṭṭhoti ekanti cattāri purisayugāni honti. Cattāro maggaṭṭhā cattāro phalaṭṭhāti aṭṭhapurisapuggalā honti. Cattālīsampi puggalāti sotāpattimaggaṭṭho paṭhamajhānikādivasena pañca, tathā sakadāgāmianāgāmiarahattamaggaṭṭhāpi pañca pañcāti vīsati maggaṭṭhapuggalā, tathā phalaṭṭhāpi sotapattiphalaṭṭhādivasena pañca pañcāti vīsati phalaṭṭhāti cattālīsapuggalā honti. Ettha ca maggassa ekacittakkhaṇikattā kathaṃ vīsatimaggaṭṭhā puggalā bhaveyyunti.

Now the explanation of the phrase “the Sangha is rare in the world” has been reached. Herein, the Sangha is twofold: in the ultimate sense and the conventional sense. Among these, the ultimate Sangha is fourfold: the four pairs of persons, the eight individuals, the forty persons, and the one hundred and eight persons. Briefly, there are four pairs of persons: one pair consisting of the one established in the path of stream-entry and the one established in the fruit of stream-entry, one pair consisting of the one established in the path of once-returning and the one established in the fruit of once-returning, one pair consisting of the one established in the path of non-returning and the one established in the fruit of non-returning, and one pair consisting of the one established in the path of arahantship and the one established in the fruit of arahantship. The eight individuals are the four established in the paths and the four established in the fruits. The forty persons are as follows: the one established in the path of stream-entry is fivefold in terms of having the first jhāna and so on, and likewise those established in the paths of once-returning, non-returning, and arahantship are also fivefold each—thus twenty persons established in the paths. Similarly, those established in the fruits, such as the one established in the fruit of stream-entry, are also fivefold each—thus twenty persons established in the fruits, making forty persons in total. Here, since the path lasts only a single mind-moment, how can there be twenty persons established in the paths?

Vuccatepādakajjhānasammasitajjhānapuggalajjhāsayesupi [M 0.0099] hi aññataravasena taṃ taṃ jhānasadisavitakkādiaṅgapātubhāvena cattāropi maggaṭṭhā paṭhamajjhānikādivohāraṃ labhantā paccekaṃ pañcapañcadhā vibhajiyanti, tasmā vīsati maggaṭṭhā honti. Tattha paṭhamajjhānādīsu yaṃ yaṃ jhānaṃ samāpajjitvā tato vuṭṭhāya saṅkhāre sammasantassa vuṭṭhānagāminīvipassanā pavattā, taṃ pādakajjhānaṃ vuṭṭhānagāminivipassanāya padaṭṭhānabhāvato. Yaṃ yaṃ jhānaṃ sammasantassa sā pavattā, taṃ sammasitajjhānaṃ. “Aho vata me paṭhamajjhānasadiso maggo pañcaṅgiko, dutiyajjhānādīsu vā aññatarasadiso caturaṅgikādibhedo maggo bhaveyyā”ti evaṃ yogāvacarassa uppannajjhāsayo puggalajjhāsayo nāma. Tattha yena paṭhamajjhānādīsu aññataraṃ jhānaṃ samāpajjitvā tato vuṭṭhāya pakiṇṇakasaṅkhāre sammasitvā maggo uppādito hoti, tassa so maggo paṭhamajjhānādi taṃ taṃ pādakajjhānasadiso hoti. Sace pana vipassanāpādakaṃ kiñcijhānaṃ natthi, kevalaṃ paṭhamajjhānādīsu aññataraṃ jhānaṃ sammasitvā maggo uppādito hoti, tassa so sammasitajjhānasadiso hoti. Yadā panayaṃ kiñci jhānaṃ samāpajjitvā tato aññaṃ sammasitvā maggo uppādito hoti, tadā puggalajjhāsayavasena dvīsu aññatarasadiso hoti. Evaṃ samathayānikassa puthujjanassa ariyānaṃ vā pādakajjhānasammasitajjhānapuggalajjhāsayavasena paṭhamajjhānādīnaṃ aññatarajhānasadisassa maggaṅgikassa pavattanato puggalabhedavasena vīsati maggaṭṭhā puggalā honti, tathā phalaṭṭhāpi vīsatīti cattālīsaariyapuggalā honti. Aparampi sace pana puggalassa tathā vidho ajjhāsayo natthi, anulomavasena heṭṭhimaheṭṭhimajhānato vuṭṭhāya uparūparijhānadhamme sammasitvā uppāditamaggo pādakajjhānamanapekkhitvā sammasitajjhānasadiso hoti. Paṭilomavasena uparūparijhānato vuṭṭhāya heṭṭhimaheṭṭhimajhānadhamme sammasitvā uppāditamaggo sammasitajjhānamanapekkhitvā pādakajjhānasadiso hoti, heṭṭhimaheṭṭhimajhānato hi uparūparijhānaṃ balavataranti, aṭṭhasatampi purisapuggalāti tattha ekabījī, kolaṃkolo, sattakkhattuparamoti tayo sotāpannā. Kāmarūpārūpabhavesu adhigataphalā tayo sakadāgāminoti te sabbepi dukkhapaṭipadā dandhābhiññaṃ, dukkhapaṭipadā khippābhiññaṃ, sukhāpaṭipadā dandhābhiññaṃ, sukhāpaṭipadā khippābhiññanti [M 0.0100] catunnaṃ paṭipadānaṃ vasena catuvīsati, antarāparinibbāyī, upahaccapari nibbāyī, sasaṅkhāraparinibbāyī, asaṅkhāraparinibbāyī, uddhaṃsoto akaniṭṭhagāmīti avihāsu pañca anāmigāno, tathā atappāsudassāsudassīsupi pañca. Akaniṭṭhesu pana uddhaṃsotavajjā antarāparinibbāyī, upahaccaparinibbāyī, sasaṅkhāraparinibbāyī, asaṅkhāraparibbāyīti cattāroti catuvīsati anāgāmino. Sukkhavipassako, samathayānikoti dve arahanto, cattāro maggaṭṭhāti catupaññāsa te sabbepi saddhādhurapaññādhurānaṃvasena dveguṇe hutvā aṭṭhasataṃ ariyapuggalā honti. Te sabbepi “aṭṭhasataṃ vā”ti vitthāravasena uddiṭṭhā ariyapuggalā te saṅkhepato yugavasena sotāpatti maggaṭṭho phalaṭṭhoti ekaṃ yugantyādinā cattārova honti. Tenevāha ratanaparitte “ye puggalā aṭṭhasataṃ pasatthā, cattāri etāni yugāni hontī”ti ayaṃ pabhedomaggaṭṭhaphalaṭṭhesu missakavasena labbhati jhānikavipassakapabhedo pana phalaṭṭheyeva labbhati maggassa ekacittakkhatikattā maggaṭṭhesu na labbhati. Tattha sotāpattiphalaṭṭho jhānikasukkhavipassakavasena duvidho. Tesu jhāniko puggalo tasmiṃ bhave arahattappatto parinibbāyati. Appatto brahmalokagato hoti, so jhāniko nāma. Mūlaṭīkāyaṃ pana nikantiyā sati puthujjanādayo yathāladdhajjhānassa bhūmibhūte suddhāvāsavajjite yattha katthaci nibbattanti tathā kāmabhavepi kāmāvacarakammabalena “ijjhati hi bhikkhave sīlavato cetopaṇidhi visuddhattā”ti vuttaṃ. Anāgāmino pana kāmarāgassa sabbaso pahīnattā kāmabhavesu nikantiṃ na uppādentīti kāmalokavajjite yathāladdhassa bhūmi bhūte yattha katthaci nibbattantī’ti vuttaṃ tathā hi anāgāmisseva kāmabhave nikantiyā pahīnattā sotāpannasakadāgāmīnampi nikantiyā sati kāmabhave kāmāvacarakammabalena uppattibhāvo avāritova hotīti viññāyati tasmā “appatte brahmalokagato hoti, so jhāniko nāmāti idaṃ yebhuyyavasena vutta”nti daṭṭhabbaṃ. Sukkhavipassako pana tividho ekabījī, kolaṃkolo, sattakkhattuparamoti. Tattha eko manussaloke vā hotu chadevaloke vā, ekapaṭisandhiko hutvā arahattaṃ patvā parinibbāyati, ayaṃ ekabījī nāma eko [M 0.0101] manussaloke ekā paṭisandhi devaloke ekāti dvepaṭisandhiko vā, evaṃ ticatupañcachaparamo hutvā arahattaṃ patvā parinibbāyati, ayaṃ kolaṃkolo nāma. Eko manussaloke sotāpanno hutvā devaloke ekā paṭisandhi tato punāgantvā manussaloke ekāti evaṃ manussadevalokamissakavasena tattha cattāri idha tīṇityādinā tattha cha, idha ekoti yāva sattapaṭisandhiko hutvā parinibbāyati, ayaṃ sattakkhattuparamo nāma. Kevalaṃ pana manussaloke yeva satta devalokeyeva vā sattāti evaṃ amisso hutvā paṭisandhiko gahetabbo apare pana “ito satta, tato satta saṃsāre vicarantī”ti vuttattā manussalokasattapaṭisandhidevaloka sattapaṭisandhivasena sattakkhattuparamaṃ vadanti. Taṃ ayuttaṃ kasmā “kiñcāpi te honti bhusaṃpamattā, na te bhavaṃ aṭṭhamamādiyantī”ti pāḷiyā virodhattā. Apica dvibhavaparicchinno sakadāgāmīpi dvikkhattuṃ manussalokaṃ āgato bhaveyya, tassapi tattha ekaṃ, idha ekanti vārassa labbhitabbato na panevaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Sakiṃ imaṃ manussalokaṃ āgacchatīti sakadāgāmī idha patvā idha parinibbāyī, tattha patvā tattha parinibbāyī, tattha patvā idha parinibbāyī, idha patvā tattha parinibbāyīti pañcasu sakadāgāmīsu pañcamako idha adhippeto. Yadi evaṃ idha patvā idha parinibbāyīti ādayo cattāro kathaṃ sakadāgāmīnāmāti. Te sakiṃ puna āgacchatīti vacanatthena sakadāgāmī nāma. Kāmataṇhāya sabbaso pahīnattā imaṃ kāmadhātuṃ anāgacchatīti anāgāmī, niccaṃ brahmalokeyeva paṭisandhi vasena āgacchatīti adhippāyo. Jhānikassa evaṃ hotu, sukkhavipassakassa kathanti sopi jhānikova hutvā gacchati tassa hi maggantare sīhabyagghādīhi hatassāpi lakkhaṇattayaṃ āropetvā jhāniko hutvāva maraṇaṃ hoti ayaṃ pana rūpārūpabhavena ekakkhattuparamo nāma. Sattakkhattuparamādayo ariyā rūpārūpalokesu anekakkhattupaṭisandhikāpi brahmalokasāmaññena ekapaṭisandhikā nāma honti. Arahā pana pāpakaraṇe rahābhāvā dakkhiṇaṃ arahattā puna bhavā bhinibbattiyārahābhāvātyādinā vacanatthena arahā nāma. Sopi tividho jhānika sukkhavipassaka samasīsīvasena. Tattha jhāniko maggeneva āgato[M 0.0102], so paṭisambhidāppatto nāma. Aparopi puthujjanasekkhasantāne jhāniko hutvā jhānaṃ pādakaṃ katvā maggaṃ uppādeti, sopi jhānikova. Sukkhavipassako pana kilesakkhayamattameva maggena saha anāgatajhānaṃ nāma natthi, taṃ pacchā parihāyatītipi vadanti. Samasīsī pana tividho hoti iriyāpathasamasīsī, rogasamasīsī, jīvitasamasīsīti. Tattha yo ṭhānādīsu iriyāpathesu yeneva iriyapathena samannāgato hutvā vipassanaṃ ārabhati, teneva iriyāpathena arahattaṃ patvā parinibbāyati ayaṃ iriyāpathasamasīsī nāma. Yo pana ekaṃ rogaṃ patvā antoroge eva vipassanaṃ paṭṭhapetvā arahattaṃ patvā teneva rogena parinibbāyati ayaṃ rogasamasīsi nāma. Yo pana terasasu sīsesu kilesasīsaṃ avijjaṃ arahattamaggo pariyādiyati, pavattisīsaṃ jīvitindriyaṃ cuticittaṃ pariyādiyati, ayaṃ jīvitasamasīsīnāma. Tattha terasa sīsāni katamāni terasa sīsāni. Palibodhasīsaṃ, taṇhābandhanasīsaṃ, mānaparāmāsasīsaṃ, diṭṭhivikkhepasīsaṃ, uddhaccakilesasīsaṃ, avijjāadhimokkhasīsaṃ, saddhāpaggahasīsaṃ, vīriyaupaṭṭhānasīsaṃ, satiavikkhepasīsaṃ, samādhidassanasīsaṃ, paññāpavattisīsaṃ, jīvitindriyagocarasīsaṃ, vimokkhasaṅkhārasīsanti. Ettha ca avijjā pariyādāyakaṃ maggacittaṃ jīvitindriyaṃ pariyādātuṃ sakkoti, jīvitapariyādāyakaṃ cuticittaṃ avijjaṃ pariyādātuṃ na sakkoti, avijjā pariyādāyakaṃ cittaṃ aññaṃ, jīvitindriyapariyādāyakaṃ cittaṃ aññaṃ. Kathaṃ panidaṃ sīsaṃ samaṃ hotīti. Vārasamatāya, yasmiñhi vāre maggavuṭṭhānaṃ hoti, sotāpattimagge maggaphalanibbānapahīnasesakilesapaccavekkhaṇāvasena pañca paccavekkhaṇāni, tathā sakadāmimagge pañca, anāgāmimagge pañca, arahattamagge sesakilesābhāvā cattārīti ekūnavīsatime paccavekkhaṇañāṇe patiṭṭhāya bhavaṅgaṃ otaritvā parinibbāyato imāya vārasamatāya idaṃ ubhayasīsa pariyādānampi samaṃ hoti nāma. Arahattamagge ca pavattisīsaṃ jīvitindriyaṃ pavattito vuṭṭhahanto maggo cutito uddhaṃ appavattikaraṇavasena yadipi pariyādiyati. Yāva pana cuti, tāva pavattisabhāvato pavattisīsaṃ jīvitindriyaṃ cuticittaṃ pariyādiyati nāma. Kilesapariyādānena maggacittena attano anantaraṃ viya nipphādetabbā paccavekkhaṇavārāva [M 0.0103] paripuṇṇā. Paripuṇṇavasena pahīnakilese paccavekkhaṇato kilesapariyādānasseva vārāti vattabbataṃ arahanti, teneva kilesasīsaṃ avijjāpariyādānañca pavattisīsaṃ jīvitindriyapariyādānañca samaṃ katvā imāya vārasamatāya kilesapariyādānaṃ jīvitapariyādānānaṃ apubbacarimatā veditabbāti vuttaṃ. Saṃyuttaṭhakathāyaṃ pana yassa puggalassa apubbaṃ acarimaṃ āsavapariyādānañca hoti jīvitapariyādānañca, ayaṃti puggalo samasīsī. Ettha ca pavattisīsaṃ kilesasīsanti dve sīsāni. Tattha pavattisīsaṃ nāma jīvitindriyaṃ. Kilesasīsaṃ nāma avijjā. Tesu jīvitindriyaṃ cuticittaṃ khepeti. Avijjaṃ maggacittaṃ. Dvinnaṃ cittānaṃ ekato uppādo natthi. Maggānantaraṃ pana phalaṃ, phalānantaraṃ bhavaṅgaṃ, bhavaṅgato vuṭṭhāya paccavekkhaṇaṃ paripuṇṇaṃ hoti, taṃ aparipuṇṇaṃ vāti. Tikhiṇena asinā sīse chindantepi hi eko vā dve vā paccavekkhaṇavārā avassaṃ uppajjantiyeva, cittānaṃ pana lahuparivattitāya āsavakkhayo ca jīvitapariyādānañca ekakkhaṇe viya sañjāyatī’ti vuttaṃ. Taṭṭīkāyañca “dvinnaṃ cittānanti cuticittamaggacittānaṃ. Tanti paccayavekallaṃ. Paripuṇṇaṃ javanacittānaṃ sattakkhattuṃ pavattiyā. Aparipuṇṇaṃ pañcakkhattuṃ pavattiyā. Kiñcāpi eko vā dve vā ti vuttaṃ, taṃ pana vacana siliṭṭhavasena vuttaṃ. Yāva ekaṃ vā dve vā tadārammaṇacittānīti heṭṭhimantena dve pavattantīti vadantī”ti vuttaṃ. Ettha ca “paripuṇṇaṃ javanacittānaṃ sattakkhattuṃ pavattiyā, aparipuṇṇaṃ pañchakkhattuṃ pavattiyā”ti iminā aṭṭhakathāṭīkāvacanena samasīsīnaṃ paccavekkhaṇāvasena maraṇāsannavīthiyaṃ paripuṇṇavasena sattakkhattuṃ, aparipuṇṇavasena pañcakkhattuṃ kriyajavanāni javantīti sanniṭṭhānametthāva gantabbaṃ. “Asammūḷho kālaṃ karotī”ti vuttattā ca viññāyati sattakkhattuṃ paripuṇṇavasena maraṇāsannakāle javanapavatti. Evañca sati “mandappavattiyaṃ pana maraṇakālādīsu pañcavāramevā”ti ettha “mandappavattiya”nti visesanaṃ sātthakaṃ siyā. “Pañcavāramevā”ti ettha evakārena mandappavattikāle chasattakkhattuṃ nivatteti. Asammūḷhakāle pana chasattakkhattumpi anujānāti. Kāmāvacarajavanānañca aniyataparimāṇā balavakālepi parisampuṇṇabhāvā tathā hi “kāmāvacarajavanānibalavakāle sattakkhattuṃ chakkhattuṃ vā, mandappavattiyaṃ pana maraṇakālādīsu pañcavārameva. Bhagavato yamakapāṭihāriyakālādīsu lahukappavattiyaṃ cattāri pañcavā [M 0.0104] paccavekkhaṇajavanacittāni bhavantī”ti aniyamitappamāṇavasena uppattibhāvo āgato imasmiṃ samasīsiniddesavārepi maraṇāsannavīthicittassa visuṃ alabbhanato paccavekkhaṇanteyeva bhavaṅgacittena parinibbānato paripuṇṇavasena satta javanacittāni pāṭikaṅkhitabbānīti amhākaṃ khanti vīmaṃsitvā gahetabbo ito yuttataro vā pakāro labbhamāno gavesitabbo. Evaṃ paramatthasaṅghavasena cattāri purisayugāni, aṭṭha purisapuggalā, cattālīsampipuggalā aṭṭhasatampi purisapuggalāti sabbaṃ samodhānetvā imasmiṃ loke saṅghopi dullabhoti veditabbo tathā hi ariyapuggalā atidullabhāva sāsanassa vijjamānakālepi idāni sotāpannassāpi avijjamānato ayaṃ paramatthasaṅghavicāraṇā. Sammutisaṅgho pana ñatticatutthena upasampanno puthujjanasaṅghova, sopi dullabhoyeva. Kasmā buddhuppādakāleyeva labbhanato tathā hi anuppanne buddhe paccekabuddhānaṃ satasahassādigaṇane uppajjamānepi tesaṃ santike upasampadābhāvassa alabbhamānato paccekabuddhānañhi santike pabbajitānaṃ kulaputtānaṃ saraṇagamanakammaṭṭhānassāpi dātuṃ asakkuṇeyyattā. Paccekabuddhā ca nāma mūgassa supinadassanaṃ viya attanā paṭiladdhasaccadhamme paresaṃ ācikkhituṃ na sakkonti, tasmā te pabbājetvā kammaṭṭhāne niyojetuṃ asamatthā “evaṃ te nivāsetabbaṃ, evaṃ te pārupitabba”nti ādinānayena abhisamācārikameva sikkhāpesuṃ tasmā buddhuppādakāleyeva sammutisaṅgho labbhati. Apica na te dullabhāyeva honti, atha kho tasmiṃ uddissa appamattakassāpi katākārassa asaṅkhyeyyaphalāpi honti vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā “bhavissanti kho panānanda anāgatamaddhānaṃ gotrabhuno kāsāvakaṇṭhā dussīlā pāpadhammā tesu dussīlesu saṅghaṃ uddissa dānaṃ dassanti, tadā pāhaṃ ānanda saṅghagataṃ dakkhiṇaṃ asaṅkhyeyyaṃ appameyyaṃ vadāmi na tvevāhaṃ ānanda kenaci pariyāyena saṅghagatā dakkhiṇā pāṭipuggalikaṃ dakkhiṇaṃ mahapphalanti vadāmī”ti. Tathā hi uparipaṇṇāsake dakkhiṇavibhaṅgavaṇṇanāyaṃ “kāsāvakaṇṭhānaṃ saṅghe dinne dakkhiṇāpi guṇāsaṅkhyāya asaṅkhyeyyā”ti vuttaṃ. Saṅghagatadakkhiṇā hi saṅghe cittīkāraṃ kātuṃ sakkontassa hoti. Saṅghe pana cittīkāro dukkaro yo hi “saṅghagataṃ dakkhiṇaṃ dassāmī”ti deyyadhammaṃ paṭiyādetvā [M 0.0105] vihāraṃ gantvā bhante saṅghaṃ uddissa ekaṃ theraṃ dethā’ti vadati, atha saṅghato sāmaṇeraṃ labhitvā “sāmaṇero me laddho”ti aññathattaṃ āpajjati, tassa dakkhiṇā saṅghagatā na hoti. Mahātheraṃ labhitvā pi “mahāthero me laddho”ti somanassaṃ uppādentassāpi na hotiyeva. Tassa dakkhiṇā saṅghagatā na hoti. Yo pana sāmaṇeraṃ upasampannaṃ vā daharaṃ vā theraṃ vā bālaṃ vā paṇḍitaṃ vā yaṃkiñci saṅghato labhitvā nibbematiko hutvā “saṅghassa dammī”ti saṅghe cittīkāraṃ kātuṃ sakkoti, tassa dakkhiṇā saṅghagatā nāma hoti. Parasamuddavāsino kira evaṃ karonti tattha hi eko vihārassāmikuṭumbiko “saṅghagataṃ dakkhiṇaṃ dassāmī”ti saṅghato uddisitvā “ekaṃ bhikkhuṃ dethā”ti yācitvā so ekaṃ dussīlabhikkhuṃ labhitvā nisīdanaṭṭhānaṃ opuñjāpetvā āsanaṃ paññapetvā upari vitānaṃ bandhitvā gandhadhūmapupphehi pūjetvā pāde dhovitvā makkhetvā buddhassa nipaccakāraṃ karonto viya saṅghe cittīkārena deyyadhammaṃ adāsi so bhikkhu pacchābhattaṃ vihāraṃ jagganatthāya “kudālakaṃ dethā”ti gharadvāraṃ āgato upāsako nisinnova kudālaṃ pādena khipitvā “gaṇhā”ti adāsi tamenaṃ manussā āhaṃsu “tumhehi pātova etassa katasakkāro vattuṃ na sakkā, idāni apacayamattakampi natthi, kiṃ nāmeta”nti. Upāsako “saṅghassa so ayyo cittīkāro, na etassā”ti āha. Kāsāvakaṇṭhasaṅghadinnadakkhiṇaṃ pana ko sodheti, sāriputtamoggalānādayo asītimahātherā sodhenti api ca therā ciraparinibbutā, there ādiṃ katvā yāvajjatanā dharamānakhīṇāsavā sodhentiyeva, tathā hi dāyakato paṭiggāhatopi mahapphalaṃ hoti dānaṃ. Dāyakato vessantarajātakaṃ kathetabbaṃ vessantaro hi dussīlassa jūjakābrāhmaṇassa nayanasadise dve jālīkaṇhājine putte datvā pathaviṃ kampesi. Sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ ārabbha pavattacetanāya mahantabhāvena pathaviṃ kampesi. Evaṃ dāyakatopi mahapphalaṃ hoti. Paṭiggāhakato pana sāriputtattherassa dinnacoraghātakavatthu kathetabbaṃ coraghātako nāma hi sāvatthiyaṃ eko janapadapuriso pañcavīsati vassāni coraghātakakammaṃ katvā mahallakakālepi tiṃsa vassāni nāsikavāteneva kaṇṇanāsādīni chinditvā coraghātakakammaṃ [M 0.0106] karontasseva pañcapaññāsa vassāni vītivattāni. So maraṇa mañce nipannova attano kammabalena mahāniraye nibbatto viya mahāsaddaṃ katvā dukkhito hoti, tassa saddena bhītā manussā ubhato passe gehaṃ chaḍḍetvā palāyiṃsu. Tadā sāriputtatthero dibbacakkhu nā lokaṃ volokento taṃ disvā tassa anukampaṃ paṭicca tassa gehadvāre aṭṭhāsi. So kujjhitvā tikkhattuṃ nāsikavātena vissajjamānopi vissajjituṃ asakkonto ativirocamānaṃ theraṃ disvā ativiya pasīditvā attano atthāya sampāditaṃ pāyāsaṃ adāsi. Theropi maṅgalaṃ vaḍḍhetvā pakkami. Coraghātako therassa dinnadānaṃ anussaritvā sagge nibbatti, evaṃ paṭiggāhakatopi mahapphalaṃ hoti. Ubhayatopi mahapphalaṃ, anāthapiṇḍikavisākhādayo buddhappamukhassa dinnadānaṃ veditabbaṃ.

Indeed, the four path-attainers, by virtue of one of the three—the foundational jhāna, the contemplated jhāna, or the jhāna according to the individual's inclination—and by the manifestation of jhāna-like factors such as initial application (vitakka), etc., receive the designation of 'first-jhāna-attainer,' etc., and are each divided into five types. Therefore, there are twenty path-attainers. Among these, whichever jhāna one attains and then, rising from it, contemplates the formations, and thereby develops insight leading to emergence, that jhāna is called the 'foundational jhāna' (pādakajjhāna) because it serves as the basis for insight leading to emergence. Whichever jhāna one contemplates, and thereby develops insight, that is called the 'contemplated jhāna' (sammasitajjhāna). When a meditator thinks, 'Oh, may my path be like the first jhāna, having five factors, or like one of the second jhānas, etc., having four factors, etc.,' such an inclination that arises is called the 'individual's inclination' (puggalajjhāsayo). In this regard, if one has attained any of the jhānas, from the first jhāna onwards, and then, rising from it, has contemplated various formations and thereby generated the path, then that path is said to be like that particular foundational jhāna, such as the first jhāna. However, if there is no jhāna serving as a basis for insight, but one has merely contemplated one of the jhānas, from the first jhāna onwards, and thereby generated the path, then that path is said to be like that contemplated jhāna. But when one has attained some jhāna and then contemplated another and thereby generated the path, then it is like one of the two (foundational or contemplated) according to the individual's inclination. Thus, for the person who has developed tranquility (samathayānika), whether a worldling or a noble one, due to the arising of the path with its factors similar to one of the jhānas (first jhāna, etc.) by way of the foundational jhāna, the contemplated jhāna, or the individual's inclination, there are twenty path-attaining individuals according to the classification of individuals. Similarly, there are twenty fruit-attainers, making a total of forty noble persons. Furthermore, if an individual does not have such an inclination, then the path generated by rising from a lower jhāna and contemplating higher jhāna factors in direct order (anuloma) is similar to the contemplated jhāna, without regard to the foundational jhāna. Conversely, the path generated by rising from a higher jhāna and contemplating lower jhāna factors in reverse order (paṭiloma) is similar to the foundational jhāna, without regard to the contemplated jhāna, for indeed, a higher jhāna is more powerful than a lower jhāna. Thus, there are also 108 types of individuals: among them, the three Stream-Enterers are the 'once-seeded' (ekabījī), the 'clan-to-clan' (kolaṃkolo), and the 'seven-times-at-most' (sattakkhattuparamo). The three Once-Returners are those who have attained the fruit in the sense, form, and formless realms. All of these noble ones (the three types of Stream-Enterers and three types of Once-Returners), by way of the four modes of practice—painful practice with slow comprehension, painful practice with swift comprehension, pleasant practice with slow comprehension, and pleasant practice with swift comprehension—become twenty-four. The five Non-Returners in the Aviha realm are the 'intermediate Nibbāna-attainer' (antarāparinibbāyī), the 'Nibbāna-attainer by striking' (upahaccaparinibbāyī), the 'Nibbāna-attainer with exertion' (sasaṅkhāraparinibbāyī), the 'Nibbāna-attainer without exertion' (asaṅkhāraparinibbāyī), and the 'one going upstream to the Akaniṭṭha realm' (uddhaṃsoto akaniṭṭhagāmī). Similarly, there are five in the Atappa, Sudassa, and Sudassī realms. However, in the Akaniṭṭha realm, excluding the 'one going upstream,' there are four: the 'intermediate Nibbāna-attainer,' the 'Nibbāna-attainer by striking,' the 'Nibbāna-attainer with exertion,' and the 'Nibbāna-attainer without exertion,' making twenty-four Non-Returners. The two Arahants—the 'dry-insight practitioner' (sukkhavipassako) and the 'tranquility-vehicle practitioner' (samathayāniko)—and the four path-attainers, make fifty-four. All of these, being doubled by way of those whose leading faculty is faith (saddhādhura) and those whose leading faculty is wisdom (paññādhura), become 108 noble persons. All these noble persons, thus enumerated as '108' in detail, are in brief only four by way of pairs: the path-attainer and fruit-attainer of Stream-entry, etc., forming one pair. Therefore, it is said in the Ratana Sutta: 'Those persons, praised as one hundred and eight, these are the four pairs.' This classification is found mixed among the path-attainers and fruit-attainers. However, the classification of jhāna-attainers and insight-practitioners is found only among the fruit-attainers, because the path is of a single mind-moment and is not found among the path-attainers. Among these, the Stream-Entry fruit-attainer is of two kinds: the jhāna-attainer and the dry-insight practitioner. Of these, the jhāna-attainer individual, if he attains Arahantship in that very existence, attains final Nibbāna. If he does not attain it, he goes to the Brahma-world; he is called a jhāna-attainer. However, in the Mūlaṭīkā, it is stated that when there is attachment (nikanti), worldlings and others are reborn wherever it is the realm of their attained jhāna, excluding the Pure Abodes. Similarly, even in the sense-sphere existence, by the power of sense-sphere kamma, it is said: 'Indeed, bhikkhus, the aspiration of one who is virtuous succeeds because of its purity.' Non-Returners, however, having completely abandoned sense-desire, do not generate attachment in sense-sphere existences. Therefore, it is said that they are reborn wherever it is the realm of their attained jhāna, excluding the sense-sphere world. Thus, since attachment in the sense-sphere existence is abandoned only by Non-Returners, it is understood that for Stream-Enterers and Once-Returners, if there is attachment, rebirth in the sense-sphere existence by the power of sense-sphere kamma is not prevented. Therefore, it should be understood that 'if he does not attain it, he goes to the Brahma-world; he is called a jhāna-attainer' is stated as a general rule (yebhuyyavasena). The dry-insight practitioner, however, is of three kinds: the once-seeded, the clan-to-clan, and the seven-times-at-most. Among these, one who, whether in the human world or in one of the six deva worlds, takes one rebirth and attains Arahantship and attains final Nibbāna, is called the 'once-seeded.' One who takes one rebirth in the human world and one in a deva world, thus taking two rebirths, or similarly, taking three, four, five, or six rebirths at most, and attains Arahantship and attains final Nibbāna, is called the 'clan-to-clan.' One who, having become a Stream-Enterer in the human world, takes one rebirth in a deva world, and then returns again to the human world for one rebirth, and so on, by way of mixed human and deva worlds, taking four (rebirths) there and three here, or six there and one here, up to taking seven rebirths and attains final Nibbāna, is called the 'seven-times-at-most.' However, one who takes seven rebirths only in the human world or only in the deva world, thus unmixed, should be understood as taking rebirth. Others say that since it is stated 'from here seven, from there seven, they wander in saṃsāra,' they speak of the seven-times-at-most by way of seven rebirths in the human world and seven rebirths in the deva world. That is incorrect, because it contradicts the Pāḷi: 'Even if they are extremely heedless, they do not take an eighth existence.' Moreover, a Once-Returner, who is limited to two existences, might come to the human world twice; for him too, the turn of 'one there, one here' could be obtained. But it should not be understood thus. A Once-Returner is one who comes to this human world once. Among the five types of Once-Returners—such as one who, having attained the fruit here, attains final Nibbāna here; one who, having attained the fruit there, attains final Nibbāna there; one who, having attained the fruit there, attains final Nibbāna here; and one who, having attained the fruit here, attains final Nibbāna there—the fifth type is intended here. If so, how are the four types, such as 'one who, having attained the fruit here, attains final Nibbāna here,' called Once-Returners? They are called Once-Returners by the meaning of the words 'comes again once.' A Non-Returner (anāgāmī) is one who does not return to this sense-sphere realm, having completely abandoned craving for sense-pleasures. The intention is that they always take rebirth only in the Brahma-world. Let it be so for the jhāna-attainer. But what about the dry-insight practitioner? He too goes having become a jhāna-attainer. For even if he is killed by lions, tigers, etc., on the path, by superimposing the three characteristics, his death occurs as a jhāna-attainer. This one, by way of the form and formless existences, is called the 'once-at-most' (ekakkhattuparamo). Noble ones such as the 'seven-times-at-most,' even if they take many rebirths in the form and formless worlds, are called 'one-rebirth-takers' in the general sense of the Brahma-world. An Arahant (arahā) is so called by the meaning of the words 'having no secret place for doing evil' (pāpakaraṇe rahābhāvā), 'being worthy of offerings' (dakkhiṇaṃ arahattā), and 'being unworthy of further rebirth' (puna bhavā bhinibbattiyārahābhāvā), and so on. He too is of three kinds: the jhāna-attainer, the dry-insight practitioner, and the 'equal-headed' (samasīsī). Among these, the jhāna-attainer who has come by the path itself is called one who has attained the analytical knowledges (paṭisambhidāppatto). Another, who, being a jhāna-attainer in the continuum of a worldling or a trainee, makes jhāna the basis and generates the path, is also a jhāna-attainer. The dry-insight practitioner, however, has only the destruction of defilements by the path; there is no future jhāna, and some say that it later declines. The 'equal-headed' (samasīsī) is of three kinds: the 'equal-headed in posture' (iriyāpathasamasīsī), the 'equal-headed in illness' (rogasamasīsī), and the 'equal-headed in life' (jīvitasamasīsī). Among these, one who, in the postures such as standing, etc., begins insight practice in a particular posture, and attains Arahantship and final Nibbāna in that same posture, is called the 'equal-headed in posture.' One who, having contracted an illness, establishes insight practice within that illness, and attains Arahantship and final Nibbāna with that same illness, is called the 'equal-headed in illness.' One who, among the thirteen 'heads' (sīsāni), has the Arahantship path exhaust the 'head of defilements,' which is ignorance, while the death consciousness exhausts the 'head of continuity,' which is the life faculty, is called the 'equal-headed in life.' What are these thirteen 'heads'? The 'head of impediments,' the 'head of the bond of craving,' the 'head of the clinging of conceit,' the 'head of the distraction of views,' the 'head of the defilement of restlessness,' the 'head of the inclination to ignorance,' the 'head of the exertion of faith,' the 'head of the establishment of energy,' the 'head of the non-distraction of mindfulness,' the 'head of the manifestation of concentration,' the 'head of the arising of wisdom,' the 'head of the sphere of the life faculty,' and the 'head of the formations of liberation.' Here, the path-consciousness that exhausts ignorance is able to exhaust the life faculty, but the death-consciousness that exhausts life is not able to exhaust ignorance. The consciousness that exhausts ignorance is one thing, and the consciousness that exhausts the life faculty is another. How then are these 'heads' equal? By the equality of turns. When the emergence from the path occurs, there are five reviews in the Stream-Entry path by way of reviewing the path, the fruit, Nibbāna, and the abandoned and remaining defilements. Similarly, five in the Once-Returner path, five in the Non-Returner path, and four in the Arahantship path due to the absence of remaining defilements. Thus, having established oneself in the nineteen reviewing knowledges, one descends into the life-continuum and attains final Nibbāna. By this equality of turns, the exhaustion of both these 'heads' is said to be equal. And in the Arahantship path, the path, emerging from continuity, exhausts the 'head of continuity,' which is the life faculty, by making it cease to arise after death, even if it does so. However, as long as there is death, the 'head of continuity,' which is the life faculty, is said to be exhausted by the death-consciousness by virtue of its nature of continuity. The turns of reviewing, which are to be produced immediately after the path-consciousness by the exhaustion of defilements, are complete. By reviewing the abandoned defilements in a complete manner, they are worthy of being called the turns of the exhaustion of defilements. Therefore, it is said that by making the 'head of defilements,' which is the exhaustion of ignorance, and the 'head of continuity,' which is the exhaustion of the life faculty, equal, the non-anteriority and non-posteriority of the exhaustion of defilements and the exhaustion of life should be understood by this equality of turns. However, in the Saṃyutta Commentary, it is said that the individual for whom the exhaustion of influxes and the exhaustion of life occur neither before nor after each other is called 'equal-headed.' Here, there are two 'heads': the 'head of continuity' and the 'head of defilements.' Of these, the 'head of continuity' is the life faculty. The 'head of defilements' is ignorance. Among these, the death-consciousness destroys the life faculty. The path-consciousness destroys ignorance. There is no simultaneous arising of these two consciousnesses. However, after the path, there is the fruit; after the fruit, the life-continuum; and rising from the life-continuum, the review is complete or incomplete. Even if one's head is cut off with a sharp sword, one or two turns of review must necessarily arise. But due to the swift change of consciousnesses, the destruction of influxes and the exhaustion of life occur as if in a single moment. And in the Sub-commentary, it is said: 'Two consciousnesses' refers to the death-consciousness and the path-consciousness. 'That' refers to the lack of conditions. 'Complete' refers to the sevenfold occurrence of impulsion consciousnesses. 'Incomplete' refers to the fivefold occurrence. Although it is said 'one or two,' that statement is made by way of rhetorical flourish. They say that 'one or two tadārammaṇa consciousnesses occur at the lowest limit.' Here, by the statement of the Commentary and Sub-commentary, 'complete' refers to the sevenfold occurrence of impulsion consciousnesses, and 'incomplete' refers to the fivefold occurrence, it should be concluded that in the death-proximate thought-process of the 'equal-headed,' impulsion consciousnesses occur seven times in the complete manner and five times in the incomplete manner by way of reviewing. And since it is said, 'He dies undeluded,' it is understood that in the death-proximate moment, impulsion consciousnesses occur seven times in the complete manner. If this is so, then the qualification 'in slow occurrence' in the statement 'in slow occurrence, such as at the time of death, only five times' would be meaningful. The word 'only' in 'only five times' excludes six or seven times in the case of slow occurrence. But in the case of undeluded death, it allows for six or seven times. And the impulsion consciousnesses in the sense-sphere are of indeterminate number, being complete even in strong cases. Thus, it is stated: 'In strong cases, sense-sphere impulsion consciousnesses occur seven or six times; in slow occurrence, such as at the time of death, only five times. At the time of the Buddha's Twin Miracle, etc., in swift occurrence, four or five impulsion consciousnesses of review occur.' The arising by way of indeterminate measure is thus presented. In this section on the 'equal-headed,' since a separate death-proximate thought-process is not obtained, and Nibbāna is attained by the life-continuum consciousness right after the review, seven impulsion consciousnesses are to be expected in the complete manner. This is our view, which should be examined and accepted. Or, if a more suitable explanation is found, it should be sought. Thus, by way of the ultimate Sangha, combining the four pairs of persons, the eight individuals, the forty individuals, and the 108 individuals, it should be understood that even the Sangha is rare in this world. Indeed, noble persons are extremely rare, and even in the present time, when the Dispensation exists, there are no Stream-Enterers. This is the consideration of the ultimate Sangha. The conventional Sangha, however, is a Sangha of worldlings who have received higher ordination by means of a motion followed by three proclamations (ñatticatutthena). This too is rare. Why? Because it is obtained only at the time of the Buddha's arising. For when no Buddha has arisen, even if Paccekabuddhas arise in numbers of hundreds of thousands, higher ordination is not obtained from them. Indeed, Paccekabuddhas are unable to give even the going for refuge and meditation subjects to young men who have gone forth under them. Paccekabuddhas, like a mute person's dream, are unable to teach to others the true Dhamma they have realized. Therefore, they are unable to ordain others and assign them meditation subjects. They only teach the conventional practices, such as 'thus should you wear your lower robe, thus should you wear your upper robe.' Therefore, the conventional Sangha is obtained only at the time of the Buddha's arising. Moreover, they are not merely rare; rather, even a small deed done with reference to them yields immeasurable results. For it was said by the Blessed One: 'Indeed, Ānanda, in the future there will be those of the lineage, wearing the yellow robe, who are immoral and of evil nature. Even to those immoral ones, when a gift is given with reference to the Sangha, then, Ānanda, I declare that gift given to the Sangha to be immeasurable and incalculable. But, Ānanda, I do not declare in any way that a gift given to the Sangha is more fruitful than a gift given to an individual.' Thus, in the commentary on the Dakkhiṇāvibhaṅga Sutta in the Uparipaṇṇāsaka, it is stated: 'Even a gift given to the Sangha consisting of those wearing the yellow robe (kāsāvakaṇṭha) is immeasurable in terms of its qualities.' Indeed, a gift given to the Sangha (saṅghagatadakkhiṇā) belongs to one who is able to make his mind focused on the Sangha. But focusing the mind on the Sangha is difficult. For if someone prepares a donation, saying, 'I will give a gift to the Sangha,' and goes to the monastery and says, 'Venerable sirs, give me one elder for the Sangha,' and then, having received a novice from the Sangha, thinks, 'I have received a novice,' and changes his intention, his donation is not a gift to the Sangha. Even if he receives a great elder and generates joy, thinking, 'I have received a great elder,' it is still not a gift to the Sangha. His donation is not a gift to the Sangha. But one who, having received from the Sangha a novice, or an ordained monk, or a young one, or an elder, or a foolish one, or a wise one, whatever it may be, remains free from doubt and is able to focus his mind on the Sangha, thinking, 'I am giving to the Sangha,' his donation is called a gift to the Sangha. It is said that those dwelling across the ocean do this. There, a householder who was the owner of a monastery, saying, 'I will give a gift to the Sangha,' requested from the Sangha, 'Give me one bhikkhu.' He received an immoral bhikkhu, had the sitting place swept, arranged a seat, tied a canopy above, honored him with perfumes, incense, and flowers, washed and anointed his feet, and gave the donation with his mind focused on the Sangha, as if performing an act of reverence to the Buddha. After the meal, that bhikkhu came to the householder's door to ask for a spade to maintain the monastery, saying, 'Give me a spade.' The householder, while seated, kicked a spade with his foot and said, 'Take it.' The people then said to him, 'The respect you showed him this morning cannot be described, but now there is not even a trace of reverence. What is this?' The householder replied, 'That reverence was for the Sangha, not for him.' But who purifies a gift given to the Sangha consisting of those wearing the yellow robe? The eighty great elders, such as Sāriputta and Moggallāna, purify it. Moreover, even if the elders have long since attained final Nibbāna, those who are living Arahants, starting from the elders up to the present day, purify it. Thus, a donation is greatly fruitful both from the giver's side and the recipient's side. From the giver's side, the Vessantara Jātaka should be recounted. For Vessantara, by giving his two children, Jālī and Kaṇhājinā, who were like his own eyes, to the immoral Brahmin Jūjaka, caused the earth to quake. He caused the earth to quake by the greatness of the volition that arose with reference to Omniscience. Thus, a donation is greatly fruitful even from the giver's side. From the recipient's side, however, the story of the bandit given to Venerable Sāriputta should be recounted. There was a certain man from the countryside in Sāvatthī who had engaged in banditry for twenty-five years. Even in his old age, he continued to commit banditry by cutting off ears and noses with just a breath from his nose for thirty years. Thus, fifty-five years had passed for him while he was engaged in banditry. Lying on his deathbed, he suffered greatly, making a loud noise as if reborn in a great hell by the power of his kamma. Frightened by his sound, people on both sides abandoned their houses and fled. At that time, Venerable Sāriputta, surveying the world with his divine eye, saw him and, out of compassion for him, stood at his house door. He, though angry and trying three times to expel the Elder with a breath from his nose, was unable to do so. Seeing the Elder shining brightly, he became exceedingly pleased and gave him the milk-rice he had prepared for himself. The Elder, having increased his welfare, departed. The bandit, recollecting the gift given to the Elder, was reborn in a heavenly world. Thus, a donation is greatly fruitful even from the recipient's side. A donation that is greatly fruitful from both sides should be understood as the gifts given by Anāthapiṇḍika, Visākhā, and others, with the Buddha as the chief recipient.

Vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā.

This was said by the Blessed One.

“Tathāgate ca sambuddhe, athavā tassa sāvake;

Natthi citte pasannamhi, appakā nāma dakkhiṇā.

To the Tathāgata, the Perfectly Enlightened One, or to his disciple, there is no offering that is insignificant when the mind is gladdened.

Evaṃ acintiyā buddhā, buddhadhammā acintiyā;

Acintiye pasannānaṃ, vipāko hoti acintiyo”ti.

Thus, the Buddhas are inconceivable, the Dhamma of the Buddhas is inconceivable; for those who are gladdened by the inconceivable, the result is inconceivable.

Evaṃ ubhayavasena mahapphalabhāvo veditabbo. Dāyakatopi paṭiggāhakatopi nipphalameva. Seyyathāpi makkhaligosālachasatthārādīnaṃ attano upāsakamicchādiṭṭhīhi pūjāvisesā vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā.

Thus, the state of great fruitfulness should be understood from both perspectives. For both the giver and the receiver, it is indeed fruitless, just as with the special offerings to Makkhali Gosāla and the other six teachers made by their own lay followers who held wrong views. This was said by the Blessed One:

“Māse māse kusaggena, bālo bhuñjeyya bhojanaṃ;

Na so saṅkhatadhammānaṃ, kalaṃ nāgghati soḷasi”nti.

Though month after month a fool should eat his food with the tip of a kusa grass blade, he is not worth a sixteenth part of those who have realized the Dhamma.

Tenevāha bhagavā cattārimāni ānanda dakkhiṇavisuddhiyo”ti. Imāni tīṇi buddhadhammasaṅgharatanāni sādhūnaṃ ratijananatthena ratanāni nāma.

Therefore, the Blessed One said, "Ānanda, there are these four purifications of offerings." These three jewels—the Buddha, Dhamma, and Saṅgha—are called jewels because they generate delight for the good.

Vuttañhetaṃ.

This was said.

“Cittīkataṃ mahagghañca, atulaṃ dullabhadassanaṃ;

Anomasattaparibhogaṃ, ratanaṃ tena vuccatī”ti.

Esteemed, of great value, incomparable, difficult to see, enjoyed by noble beings—therefore it is called a jewel.

Ratanañca nāma duvidhaṃ saviññāṇakāviññāṇakavasena. Tattha aviññāṇakaṃ cakkaratanaṃ, maṇiratanaṃ yaṃ vā panaññampi anindriyabaddhasuvaṇṇarajatādi. Saviññāṇakaṃ hatthiassaratanādipariṇāyakaratanapariyosānaṃ, yaṃ vā [M 0.0107] panaññampi indriyabaddhaṃ. Evaṃ duvidhe cettha saviññāṇakaratanaṃ aggamakkhāyati, kasmā yasmā aviññāṇakaṃ suvaṇṇarajatamaṇimuttādiratanaṃ saviññāṇakānaṃ hattiratanādīnaṃ alaṅkāratthāya upanīyati. Saviññāṇakaratanampi duvidhaṃ tiracchānagataratanaṃ manussaratanañca. Tattha manussaratanaṃ aggamakkhāyati. Kasmā yasmā tiracchānagataratanaṃ manussaratanassa opavayhaṃ hoti. Manussaratanampi duvidhaṃ itthiratanaṃ purisaratanañca. Tattha purisaratanaṃ aggamakkhāyati. Kasmā yasmā itthiratanaṃ purisaratanassa paricārikattaṃ āpajjati. Purisaratanampi duvidhaṃ agārikaratanaṃ anagārikaratanañca tattha anagārikaratanaṃ aggamakkhāyati. Kasmā yasmā agārikaratanesu aggo cakkavattīpi sīlādiguṇayuttaṃ anagārikaratanaṃ pañcapatiṭṭhitena vanditvā upaṭṭhahitvā payirupāsitvā dibbamānussikā sampattiyo pāpuṇitvā ante nibbānasampattiṃ pāpuṇāti. Anagārikaratanampi duvidhaṃ ariyaputhujjanavasena. Ariyaratanampi duvidhaṃ sekkhāsekkhavasena. Asekkharatanampi duvidhaṃ sukkhavipassakasamathayānikavasena. Samathayānikaratanampi duvidhaṃ sāvakapāramippattamapattañca. Tattha sāvakapāramippattaṃ aggamakkhāyati. Kasmā guṇamahattatāya. Sāvakapāramippattaratanatopi paccekabuddharatanaṃ aggamakkhāyati. Kasmā guṇamahattatāya. Sāriputtamoggalānasadisāpi hi anekasatā sāvakā paccekabuddhassa guṇānaṃ satabhāgampi na upanenti. Paccekabuddhato sambuddharatanaṃ aggamakkhāyati. Kasmā guṇamahattatāya sakalampi jambudīpaṃ pallaṅke na pallaṅkaṃ ghaṭṭento nisinnā paccekasambuddhā ekassa guṇānaṃ neva saṅkhyaṃ kalaṃ gaṇanabhāgaṃ upanenti, vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā “yāvatā bhikkhave sattā apadā vā…pe… tathāgato tesaṃ aggamakkhāyatī”ti ādi. Evaṃ “buddho ca dullabho loke, saddhammasavanampi ca, saṅgho ca dullabho loke”ti imesaṃ tiṇṇaṃ padānaṃ atthuddhāravasena sabbaso attho vuttoyeva hoti.

The jewel is of two kinds: by way of having consciousness and not having consciousness. Among these, the wheel-treasure is without consciousness, as are gem-treasures or any other gold, silver, and the like not endowed with sense faculties. The jewel with consciousness includes the elephant-treasure and horse-treasure up to the Pariṇāyaka-treasure, or any other endowed with sense faculties. Among these two, the jewel with consciousness is declared supreme. Why? Because jewels without consciousness—gold, silver, gems, pearls, and the like—are brought forth to adorn those with consciousness, such as the elephant-treasure and others. The jewel with consciousness is also of two kinds: the jewel among animals and the jewel among humans. Among these, the human jewel is declared supreme. Why? Because the jewel among animals is subordinate to the human jewel. The human jewel is also of two kinds: the woman-jewel and the man-jewel. Among these, the man-jewel is declared supreme. Why? Because the woman-jewel undertakes the service of the man-jewel. The man-jewel is also of two kinds: the householder-jewel and the homeless-jewel. Among these, the homeless-jewel is declared supreme. Why? Because even the foremost among householders, the wheel-turning monarch, pays homage to the homeless-jewel endowed with virtues like morality, reveres and attends to them, and serves them, and thereby attains divine and human prosperity, ultimately reaching the attainment of Nibbāna. The homeless-jewel is also of two kinds: by way of noble ones and ordinary people. The noble jewel is also of two kinds: by way of trainees and perfected ones. The perfected jewel is also of two kinds: by way of dry-insight practitioners and serenity-vehicle practitioners. The serenity-vehicle practitioner jewel is also of two kinds: those who have attained the disciple’s perfection and those who have not. Among these, the one who has attained the disciple’s perfection is declared supreme. Why? Because of the greatness of virtue. Higher than the jewel of one who has attained the disciple's perfection, the Paccekabuddha-jewel is declared supreme. Why? Because of the greatness of virtue. For even hundreds of disciples like Sāriputta and Moggallāna do not measure up to even a hundredth part of the Paccekabuddha’s virtues. Higher than the Paccekabuddha, the Fully Enlightened Buddha-jewel is declared supreme. Why? Because of the greatness of virtue. If all the Paccekabuddhas were seated on the entire Jambudīpa without their crossed-legged postures touching one another, they would not measure up to even a fraction, a portion, or a calculated part of the virtues of a single Buddha. As the Blessed One has said: "Monks, as far as beings are without feet… the Tathāgata is declared supreme among them," and so on. Thus, by way of extracting the meaning of these three phrases—"The Buddha is rare in the world, the hearing of the true Dhamma is rare, and the Sangha is rare in the world"—the entire meaning has indeed been stated.

Iti sāgarabuddhittheraviracite sīmavisodhane saṅghavaṇṇanāya

Thus, in the Saṅghavaṇṇanā of the Sīmavisodhana, composed by the Elder Sāgarabuddhi,

Samasīsikaṇḍo catuttho paricchedo.

The fourth chapter, the Samasīsikaṇḍa.

Pakiṇṇakakaṇḍo

The Miscellaneous Section.

Idāni [M 0.0108] sappurisāti dullabhāti imassa saṃvaṇṇanākkamo sampatto tathā hi loke sappurisāpi atidullabhāyeva. Sappurisāti kalyāṇaguṇasampannā uttamapurisā. Te nissāya jātidhammā sattā jātiyā, maraṇadhammā sattā maraṇato muccanti vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā “mamañhi ānanda kalyāṇamittaṃ āgamma jātiyā parimuccanti, jarādhammā sattā jarāya muccantī”ti ādi vacanato pana sammāsambuddhoyeva sabbākārasampanno kalyāṇamitto nāma. Taṃ alabhantena sāriputtamoggalānādayo ariyapuggalā vā. Tepi alabhantena ekantajahitaṃ sivaṭṭhikaṃ kalyāṇamittaṃ labhitabbaṃ, vuttañhetaṃ porāṇehi.

Now, regarding the phrase "good persons are rare," the turn for its exposition has arrived. Indeed, in the world, good persons are very rare. "Good persons" refers to superior persons endowed with wholesome qualities. Relying on them, beings subject to birth are freed from birth, and beings subject to death are freed from death. For it was said by the Blessed One: "Indeed, Ānanda, by relying on me as a good friend, beings subject to birth are freed from birth; beings subject to aging are freed from aging," and so on. Moreover, from such statements, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened Buddha alone, complete in every way, is called a good friend. If one cannot obtain him, then noble individuals like Sāriputta and Moggallāna should be sought. If even they are unobtainable, one should obtain a good friend who is completely abandoned, like a skeleton, as stated by the ancients.

“Piyo garu bhāvanīyo, vattā ca vacanakkhamo;

Gambhīrañca kathaṃ kattā, nocāṭhāne niyojaye”ti.

Dear, worthy of respect, fit for cultivation, a speaker and tolerant of speech, one who speaks profound discourse, and does not employ one in an unsuitable situation.

Evamādiguṇasamannāgato vaḍḍhipakkhe ṭhitapaṇḍitapuggalo loke dullabhova. Katamo paṇḍitapuggalo buddhapaccekabuddhā asīti ca mahāsāvakā aññe ca tathāgatassa sāvakā sunettamahāgovindavidhurasarabhaṅgamahosadhasutasomanimirājaayogharakumāraka akattipaṇḍitādayo ca paṇḍitāti veditabbā. Te bhaye viya rakkhā, andhakāre viya padīpā, khuppipāsādidukkhābhibhave viya annapānādipaṭilābho, attano vacanakarānaṃ sabbabhayaupaddavūpasaggaviddhaṃsanasamatthā honti tathā hi tathāgataṃ āgamma asaṅkhyeyyā aparimāṇā devamanussā āsavakkhayaṃ pattā brahmaloke patiṭṭhitā, devaloke uppannā. Sāriputtatthere cittaṃ pasādetvā catūhi ca paccayehi theraṃ upaṭṭhahitvā asīti kusalahassāni sagge nibbattāni, tathā mahāmoggalānamahākassapapabhuti sunettassa satthuno sāvakā appekacce brahmaloke upapajjiṃsu, appekacce paranimmitavasavattīnaṃ devānaṃ sahabyataṃ…pe… appekacce gahapatimahāsālakulānaṃ sahabyataṃ upapajjiṃsu, vuttañhetaṃ “natthi bhikkhave paṇḍitato bhayaṃ, natthi paṇḍitato upaddavo, natthi paṇḍitato upasaggo”ti. Apica taggaramālādigandhabhaṇḍasadiso paṇḍito, taggaramālādigandhabhaṇḍapaliveṭhanapattasadiso tadupasevī vuttañhetaṃ nāradajātake.

A wise person endowed with such qualities, standing on the side of growth, is indeed rare in the world. Who is a wise person? The Buddhas, Paccekabuddhas, the eighty great disciples, and other disciples of the Tathāgata, as well as the wise ones like Sunetta, Mahāgovinda, Vidhura, Sarabhaṅga, Mahosadha, Sutasoma, Nimi, Rāja, Ayoghara, Kumāraka, and Akattipaṇḍita—these are to be understood as wise. They are like a refuge in fear, a lamp in darkness, like obtaining food and drink when overcome by the suffering of hunger and thirst. They are capable of dispelling all dangers, calamities, and afflictions for those who follow their words. For, by relying on the Tathāgata, countless, immeasurable devas and humans have attained the destruction of the taints, been established in the Brahma-world, or arisen in the deva-world. By inspiring faith in the Elder Sāriputta and attending to him with the four requisites, eighty thousand wholesome beings were reborn in heaven. Likewise, among the disciples of the teacher Sunetta were those such as Mahāmoggallāna and Mahākassapa—some were reborn in the Brahma-world, some in the company of the Paranimmitavasavattī devas… some in the company of great householder families. For it was said: "Monks, there is no fear from the wise, no danger from the wise, no affliction from the wise." Moreover, a wise person is like fragrant tagara garlands and other fragrant merchandise, and one who associates with them is like the leaves wrapped around such fragrant merchandise—as stated in the Nārada Jātaka.

“Taggarañca [M 0.0109] palāsena, yo naro upanayhati;

Pattāpi surabhi vāyanti, evaṃ dhīrūpasevanā”ti.

The person who wraps tagara with a palāsa leaf—the leaves also smell fragrant. Such is association with the wise.

Akittipaṇḍito sakkena devānamindena vare diyyamāne evamāha

When the wise Akitti was being offered a boon by Sakka, the king of devas, he said:

“Dhīraṃ passe suṇe dhīraṃ, dhīrena saha saṃvase;

Dhīrenā’lāpasallāpaṃ, taṃ kare tañca rocaye.

May I see the wise, hear the wise, and dwell with the wise. May I converse and discuss with the wise; may I do that and delight in that.

Kiṃ nu te akaraṃ dhīro, vada kassapa kāraṇaṃ;

Kena kassapa dhīrassa, dassanaṃ abhikaṅkhasi.

What has the wise one done to you? Tell me, Kassapa, the reason. For what reason, Kassapa, do you long for a sight of the wise one?

Nayaṃ nayati medhāvī, adurāyaṃ na yuñjati;

Sanaro seyyaso hoti, sammā vutto na kuppati;

Vinayaṃ so pajānāti, sādhu tena samāgamo”ti.

The wise one leads the way, he does not engage in what is wrong; a good person becomes superior; when rightly spoken to, he does not become angry; he understands discipline; association with him is good.

Apica tepi sappurise saṅgamma assutapubbampi ubhayalokahitāvahaṃ vācaṃ suyyateva, vuttampi cetaṃ.

And further, by associating with those good people, one hears speech conducive to the welfare of both worlds, even though unheard before. Moreover, this has also been said.

“Subhāsitaṃ uttamamāhu santo, dhammaṃ bhaṇe nādhammaṃ taṃ dutiyaṃ;

Piyaṃ bhaṇe nāppiyaṃ taṃ tatiyaṃ, saccaṃ bhaṇe nālīkaṃ taṃ catuttha”nti ca.

The wise say that well-spoken words are supreme—that is the first; speak Dhamma, not non-Dhamma—that is the second; speak what is pleasing, not unpleasing—that is the third; speak truth, not falsehood—that is the fourth.

“Yaṃ buddho bhāsati vācaṃ, khemaṃ nibbānapattiyā;

Dukkhassantakiriyāya, sā ve vācānamuttamā”ti.

The speech that the Buddha speaks, which is safe, for the attainment of Nibbāna, for the ending of suffering—that indeed is the supreme of speeches.

Sappurisūpanissayasevanapaccayāyeva dānādikusalasamāyogena apāyadukkhato muccanti vuttañhetaṃ “apica nerayikādidukkhaparittāṇato puññāni eva pāṇīnaṃ bahūpakārāni, yato tesampi upakārānussaraṇatā kataññutā sappurisehi pasaṃsanīyādinānappakāravisesādhigamahetūca honti vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā, “dve me bhikkhave puggalā dullabhā lokasmiṃ, katame dve, yo ca pubbakārī, yo ca kataññu katavedī”ti. Apica vidhuramahosadhajātakādikālepi idhalokaparalokasampattiatthameva attano vacanakare sammā yojenti. Anākulakammantādhiṭṭhānena kālaññutāya patirūpakāritāya analasatāya uṭṭhānavīriyasamatāya abyasaniyatāca kālānatikkamanaappatirūpakaraṇaakaraṇasithilakaraṇāhi akusalādīhi rahitakasigorakkhavāṇijjādayo kammantā, ete attano vā puttadārassa vā dāsakammakarānaṃ vā byattatāya evaṃ payojitā diṭṭheva dhamme dhanadhaññavitti paṭilābhahetū honti, vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā.

Indeed, by the very condition of resorting to and serving virtuous people, through the practice of generosity and other wholesome deeds, beings are liberated from the suffering of the lower realms. For it is said: 'Moreover, meritorious deeds are of great benefit to beings, as they provide protection from the sufferings of hell and other miseries. Since for them, remembering the help received and gratitude are praised by the virtuous, these become causes for the attainment of various special qualities.' The Blessed One has said: 'Monks, these two persons are rare in the world. Which two? One who is a benefactor, and one who is grateful and acknowledges the benefit received.' Furthermore, even in the times of the Vidhura and Mahosadha Jātakas, they rightly guided those who followed their word for the sake of prosperity in both this world and the next. By undertaking orderly occupations with adherence to proper timing, suitability, diligence, balanced effort, and freedom from addiction—livelihoods like farming, cattle-rearing, and trade, free from unwholesome actions such as delaying, acting improperly, not acting, or acting slackly—when skillfully managed for oneself, one’s wife and children, or servants and workers, become causes for acquiring wealth, grain, and property in this very life. This was said by the Blessed One.

Patirūpakārīri [M 0.0110] dhuravā, uṭṭhānatā vindate dhananti ca;

Na divā soppasīle, rattiṃ uṭṭhānadassinā;

Niccappamattena soṇḍena, sakkā āvasituṃ gharaṃ.

One who acts appropriately, is steadfast in duty, and energetic, gains wealth. Not by one who is inclined to sleep by day and is only wakeful at night; but by one constantly diligent and resolute, is it possible to maintain a household.

Atīsitaṃ atiuṇhaṃ, atisāramidaṃ ahu;

Iti vissaṭṭhakammante, atthā accenti māṇave.

“It is too cold, it is too hot, it is too late in the evening”—thus, for the young people who abandon their work, opportunities pass them by.

Yo ca sītañca uṇhañca; Tiṇāni yo na maññati;

Sappurisakiccāni, so sukhaṃ na vihāyatī”ti ca.

He who regards cold and heat as no more than a blade of grass, and performs the duties of a virtuous person, does not fall away from happiness.

Bhoge saṃharamānassa, parasseva irayato;

Bhogā sannicayaṃ yanti, vammikovūpacīyatīti.

For one who gathers wealth as a bee gathers nectar, his riches accumulate like an anthill being built up.

Evamādippabhedā sappurisā loke atidullabhāva. Tabbigamena duppurisā bālajanā agavesantopi labbhanteva te bālā attānaṃ sevamāne parajane saṃsāradukkheyeva osīdenti bhavato vuṭṭhānaṃ na denti tathā hi pūraṇakassapādayo chasatthārā devadattakokālika-modakatissa khaṇḍadeviyāputtasamuddadattaciñcamāṇavikādayo atītakāle ca dīghadukkhassa lābhāti ime aññe ca evarūpā sattā bālā aggipadittamiva agāraṃ attanā duggahitena attānañca attano vacanakārake ca vināsenti tathā hi devadattamāgamma rājā ajātasattu-kokālikādayo tadaññepi puggalā apāye nibbattanti. Rājā ajātasattu sāmaññaphalasuttantasavanakāle yadi pitaraṃ aghāteyya, sotāpanno bhaveyya pitaraṃ ghātitattā maggaphalampi appatvā lohakumbhiyaṃ saṭṭhivassasahassāni paccitvā muccissati anāgatepi paccekabuddho bhavissati. Kokālikopi sāriputtamoggalānatthere anapacāyitvā mahāniraye padumagaṇanāya paccanokāse nirayapadese padumaniraye…pe… padumaṃ kho pana aññataro bhikkhu nirayaṃ kokāliko bhikkhu upapanno sāriputtamoggalānesu cittaṃ āghātetvā”ti āha. Tattha aññataro bhikkhūti nāmagottena apākaṭaṃ “kiṃ va dīghaṃ nukho bhante padume niraye āyuppamāṇa”nti pañhaṃ pucchitvā nisinnaṃ ekaṃ bhikkhuṃ evamāha “dīghaṃ kho bhikkhu niraye āyuppamāṇaṃ, taṃ na sukaraṃ saṅkhātuṃ ettakāni vassānīti vā ettakāni vassasatānīti vā ettakāni vassasahassānīti vā ettakāni vassasatasahassānī”ti [M 0.0111] vā sakkā pana bhante upamaṃ kātunti. “Sakkā bhikkhū”ti bhagavā avoca. Seyyathāpi bhikkhu vīsatikhāriko kosalako tilavāho hoti, tato puriso vassasatassa vassasahassassa accayena ekamekaṃ tilaṃ uddhareyya, khippataraṃ kho so bhikkhu vīsatikhāriko kosalako tilavāho iminā upakkamena parikkhayaṃ pariyādānaṃ gaccheyya, na tveva eko abbudo nirayo. Seyyathāpi bhikkhu vīsa abbudo nirayo, evameko nirabbudo nirayo”ti ādi. Vīsatikhāriko ti māgadhikena patthena cattāro patthā kosalaraṭṭhe eko pattho hoti. Tena patthena cattāro patthā āḷhakaṃ. Cattāri āḷhakāni doṇaṃ. Catudoṇā mānikā. Catumānikā khārī. Tāyakhāriyā vīsatikhāriko tilavāho. Tilavāhoti tilasakaṭaṃ. Abbudo nirayoti abbudo nāma eko paccekanirayo natthi, avīcimhi eva pana abbudagaṇanāya paccanokāso “abbudo nirayo”ti vutto. Esa nayo nirabbudādīsupi. Tattha vassagaṇanāpi evaṃ veditabbā yathāha “sataṃsatasahassāni koṭi hoti evaṃ sataṃsahassakoṭiyo pakoṭi nāma. Sataṃsatasahassapakoṭiyo koṭipakoṭi nāma. Sataṃ satasahassakoṭipakoṭiyo nahutaṃ. Sataṃsatasahassanahutāni ninnahutaṃ. Sataṃsatasahassaninnahutāni eko abbudo. Tato vīsatiguṇo nirabbudo. Esa nayo sabbattha ayañca gaṇanā aparicitānaṃ dukkarā”ti vuttaṃ taṃ na sukaraṃ saṅkhātu’nti keci pana tattha paridevanānattenapi kammakaraṇanānattenapi imāni nāmāni laddhānīti vadanti. Aparepi vānakāraṇehīti ṭīkānetti. Yathā ca dīghavidassaāghātā ca buddhantaraṃ saṭṭhiyojanamattena attabhāvena uttāno patito mahāniraye paccati yathā ca, tassa diṭṭhiabhirucitāni pañca kulasatāni tasseva sahabyataṃ uppannāni mahāniraye paccanti. Vuttañcetaṃ bhagavatā “seyyathāpi bhikkhave naḷāgāraṃ vā tiṇāgāraṃ vā aggiphuṭṭho kūṭāgārāni dahati ullittāvalittāni nivātāni phusitaggaḷāni pihita vātapānāni evameva kho bhikkhave yānikānici bhayāni uppajjanti, sabbāni bālato uppajjanti, no paṇḍitato yekeci upaddavā uppajjanti, no paṇḍitato, yekeci upasaggā…pe… no paṇḍitato. Iti kho bhikkhave sappaṭibhayo [M 0.0112] bālo appaṭibhayo paṇḍito saupaddavo bālo anupaddavo paṇḍito, saupasaggo bālo anupasaggo paṇḍitoti. Api ca pūtimacchasadiso bālo, pūtimacchabaddhapatta putisadiso hoti tadupasevī. Chaḍḍanīyataṃ jigucchanīyatañca āpajjati viññūnaṃ vuttampi cetaṃ nāradajātake.

Such virtuous people, distinguished in these ways, are exceedingly rare in the world. In contrast, foolish and wicked people, though not sought after, are easily found. These fools, indulging themselves in self-serving ways, cause others to sink into the suffering of saṃsāra, offering no means of escape. For instance, figures like Pūraṇakassapa and others, the six heretical teachers, Devadatta, Kokālika, Modakatissa, Khaṇḍadeviyāputta, Samuddadatta, Ciñcamāṇavikā, and others in times past were recipients of long-lasting suffering. Such beings, through their own wrong grasping, destroy themselves and those who follow their words, like a house set ablaze. For example, King Ajātasattu, influenced by Devadatta, and others like Kokālika, were reborn in the lower realms. Had King Ajātasattu not killed his father when listening to the Sāmaññaphala Sutta, he would have become a stream-enterer. But because he committed patricide, he failed to attain the path and fruit, and will instead be tormented in a molten copper hell for sixty thousand years before being released. In the future, he will become a Paccekabuddha. Kokālika, too, due to his disrespect toward the Elders Sāriputta and Moggallāna, was reborn in the great Paduma hell. There, a certain monk asked, “Venerable sir, how long is the lifespan in the Paduma hell?” The Buddha replied, “Monk, the lifespan in hell is long—not easy to calculate in years, centuries, millennia, or even hundreds of millennia.” “But, Venerable Sir, can a simile be given?” “It can, monk,” the Blessed One said. “Suppose there were a Kosalan cartload of twenty khārīs of sesame seeds. If a man were to remove one seed at the end of every hundred or thousand years—that cartload would be exhausted sooner than the duration of a single Abbuda hell. Twenty such Abbuda hells equal one Nirabbuda hell.” The twenty-khārī Kosalan measure is explained thus: one Kosalan pattha is equal to four Magadhan patthas. Four of these patthas make an āḷhaka, four āḷhakas make a doṇa, four doṇas make a mānikā, and four mānikās make a khārī. Twenty such khārīs make a cartload of sesame seeds. An ‘Abbuda hell’ is not a separate hell, but a term for a specific counting period within Avīci. The same applies to Nirabbuda and other hells. The counting is as follows: A hundred hundred-thousands make a koṭi. A hundred thousand koṭis make a pakoṭi. A hundred thousand pakoṭis make a koṭipakoṭi. A hundred thousand koṭipakoṭis make a nahuta. A hundred thousand nahutas make a ninnahuta. A hundred thousand ninnahutas make one abbuda. Twenty times that is one nirabbuda. This counting is difficult for the unacquainted. Some say these names arise from the difference in lamentation or the nature of kamma. Others attribute them to etymological reasons, as the subcommentary explains. And just as Dīghavida, out of hatred, fell prone into the great hell for an entire Buddha-interval, with a body sixty yojanas in size, and is being tormented, so too, five hundred families who delighted in his views were reborn into a state of companionship with him and are being tormented in the great hell. The Blessed One said: ‘Monks, just as when a house of reeds or a house of grass is touched by fire, it burns the peaked roofs, plastered and sealed, sheltered from the wind, with bolts fastened and windows shut; even so, monks, whatever fears arise, they all arise from the fool, not from the wise one. All calamities arise from the fool, not from the wise one. All misfortunes arise from the fool, not from the wise one. Thus, the fool is attended by fear, the wise fearless; the fool is beset by calamity, the wise free from it; the fool is plagued by misfortune, the wise untouched. Moreover, the fool is like a rotten fish; one who associates with him becomes like a leaf used to wrap a rotten fish. He becomes worthy of being discarded and despised by the wise. This was also stated in the Nārada Jātaka.

“Pūtimacchaṃ kusaggena, yo naro upanayhati;

Kusāpi pūti vāyanti, evaṃ bālūpasevanā”ti.

“If a man wraps a stinking fish with kuśa grass, the kuśa grass also smells putrid; so it is with associating with a fool.”

Akattipaṇḍitocāpi sakkena devānamindena vare diyyamāne evamāha

Akattipaṇḍita, even when Sakka, the king of gods, was offering a boon, said thus:

“Bālaṃ na passe na suṇe, na ca bālena saṃvase;

Bālenā’llāpasallāpaṃ, na kare na ca rocaye.

“See not the fool, hear not the fool, do not associate with a fool; engage not in talk with a fool, nor approve of it.”

Kiṃnu te akaraṃ bālo, vada kassapa kāraṇaṃ;

Kena kassapa bālassa, dassanaṃ nābhikaṅkhasi.

“What harm has the fool done to you, Kassapa, tell me the reason? Why, Kassapa, do you not wish to see the fool?”

Anayaṃ nayati dummedho, adhurāyaṃ niyuñjati;

Dunnayo seyyaso hoti, sammā vutto pakuppati;

Vinayaṃ so na jānāti, sādhu tassa adassana”nti.

“The unwise man leads to ruin, he applies himself to what is not right. Wrong guidance is preferable to him, and he gets angry when rightly spoken to. He does not understand discipline—better not to see him.”

Evaṃ bāladujjanasaṃsaggavaseneva sabbāni bhayupaddavāni uppajjanti, no paṇḍitasevanavasenātisabbaso sappurisā atidullabhāva.

Thus, all dangers and calamities arise solely from associating with foolish, wicked people, not from associating with the wise. Truly, good people are exceedingly rare.

Idāni.

Now.

“Dullabhañca manussattaṃ, buddhuppādo ca dullabho;

Dullabhā khaṇasampatti, saddhammo paramadullabho”ti.

“Difficult to obtain is human birth, difficult to encounter is the arising of a Buddha; difficult to obtain is a favorable opportunity, exceedingly difficult to encounter is the true Dhamma.”

Imissā gāthāya vaṇṇanākkamo sampatto. Tattha dullabhañca manussattanti manussabhāvopi dullabhoyeva manussassa dullabhabhāvo kāṇakacchapopamādīhi veditabbo eko kira kassako gaṅgātīre tīṇi saṃvaccharāni kasitvā kiñcimattampi alabhitvā naṅgalaphālaṃ dvedhā bhinditvā gaṅgāyaṃ khipi tena gaṅgānadiyā vāhena eko uddhaṃ eko heṭṭhāti evaṃ dve naṅgalaphālakā gaṅgāyaṃ vuyhantā cirena eko uddhaṃ eko heṭṭhāti dve ekato hutvā pākatikā yujjanti tasmiṃ khaṇe vassasatavassasahassaccayena ekavāraṃ ummujjamāno kāṇakacchapo ummujjamānakkhaṇe tassa gīvā dvinnaṃ naṅgalaphālānamantare hoti ayaṃ kālo [M 0.0113] dullabhova evameva manussattabhāvopi dullabhoyeva tathāhi manussattabhāvaṃ alabhitvāva nirayapetaasurakāyatiracchānabhūmīsuyeva saṃsaritvā sīsampi ukkhipituṃ alabhantā ekabuddhantarā dve buddhantarā tayo buddhantarā cattāro buddhantarā ekāsaṅkhyeyyavasena niraye paccanakasattānaṃ gaṇanapathaṃ vītivattā, tathā petaasurakāyatiracchānabhūmīsupi atikhuddakena attabhāvena tilabījasāsapabījassa catupañcachasattaaṭṭhakalabhāgamatteneva aṅguliyā patiṭṭhitaṭṭhānamatteyeva bhūmipadese nisinnānaṃ atikhuddakasattānaṃ gaṇanapathampi vītivattā te hi gaṇituṃ iddhimantapuggale ṭhapetvā añño ko nāma sakkhissati tehi mohandhabhāvena kaṇhasukkapakkhampi ajānantā anekesu buddhasatesu vā buddhasahassesu vā uppajjamānesupi buddhotisaddaṃ asutvā vītivattā, evaṃ manussattabhāvopi dullabhoyeva. Manussattabhāve labhamānepi buddhuppādakālo atidullabhova. Buddhuppādakālepi sammādiṭṭhi hutvā kusalūpapattisaṅkhātā khaṇasampatti dullabhāva, saddhammadesakassāpi dullabhattā, tasmimpi sati aṅgavikalabhāvena saddhammasavanassāpi dullabhattāti evamādippabhedā manussattabhāvādikā khaṇasampattiyo dullabhāti veditabbā. Dullabhā khaṇasampattīti kusalūpapattisaṅkhātā khaṇasampatti dullabhāyeva tathā hi paccantavisaya arūpaasaññasattatiracchānapetanerayikakāle vā majjhimadesepi cakkhādiaṅgavikalevā paripuṇṇaaṅgabhāvepi micchādiṭṭhibhūtā vā kusalūpapattisaṅkhātā khaṇā na honti paccantavisaye hi pavattā janā pāṇātipātādidasaakusalakammapatheyeva ramanti abhiramanti, caṇḍasabhāvā ca te honti, ratanattayaguṇampi na jānanti. Arūpinopi puggalā paratoghosavirahitattā sotāpattimaggapaṭilābhopi tesaṃ natthi, buddhadassanādīnipi na labhanti. Asaññasattatiracchānapetanerayikakālesu pana pageva pūritapāramīnaṃ sattānaṃ pavattanampi abbohārikaṃ, sammādiṭṭhikule jāyamānāpi cakkhuvikalena buddhasaṅgharatanānaṃ adassanaṃ, sotavikalena dhammasavanatopi hāyati eḷamūgādibhāvena kusalasamādānā na honti. Aṅgasampannepi micchādiṭṭhibhāvena dvattiṃsamahāpurisalakkhaṇaasītianubyañjanabyāmappabhāparikkhittaṃ sabbajanānaṃ [M 0.0114] nayanarasāyatanabhūtaṃ sabbaññubuddhampi disvā pasādasommena cakkhunā oloketabbampi na maññanti manopadosavaseneva yugaggāhā hutvā yamakapāṭihāriyakālādīsu anekepi micchādiṭṭhino mahāniraye uppajjanti. Evaṃ kusalūpapattikhaṇasampattipi dullabhāyeva.

The turn for the explanation of this verse has come. Therein, “hard to obtain is human birth” means that the state of being human is indeed hard to obtain. The difficulty of obtaining human birth should be understood through similes like that of the blind turtle and others. It is said that once a certain farmer plowed the banks of the Ganges for three years but obtained nothing. He broke his plowshare in two and threw it into the Ganges. Carried by the current of the Ganges, one piece went upstream and the other downstream. The two pieces of the plowshare, drifting in the Ganges, after a long time, naturally came together again. At that very moment, a blind turtle, surfacing once every hundred or thousand years, happened to emerge with its neck between the two pieces of the plowshare. This occasion is indeed rare. Similarly, the state of being human is indeed hard to obtain. For beings who fail to attain human birth wander through the realms of hell, hungry ghosts, asuras, and animals. They cannot even lift their heads, passing through one, two, three, or four intervals between Buddhas, or even an incalculable aeon; the number of beings boiling in hell is beyond calculation. The same applies to beings in the realms of hungry ghosts, asuras, and animals—their tiny bodies, the size of a fourth, fifth, sixth, seventh, or eighth part of a sesame seed or mustard seed, sitting on a patch of ground merely the size of a fingertip; their number is also beyond calculation. Who, except those with psychic powers, could count them? Blinded by ignorance, they do not even know the dark and bright halves of the month. Though hundreds or thousands of Buddhas arise, they pass on without ever hearing the word “Buddha.” Thus, human birth is indeed hard to obtain. Even when one attains human birth, the time of a Buddha’s appearance is exceedingly rare. And even in the time of a Buddha, to have right view and the opportune moment, which is designated as the attainment of a wholesome state, is hard to come by, due to the rarity of a teacher of the true Dhamma. Even when that is present, the opportunity to hear the true Dhamma is rare due to physical impairment. Thus, the opportune moments, such as human birth and others, are to be understood as rare. “Hard to obtain is the opportune moment” refers to the opportune moment designated as the attainment of a wholesome state, which is indeed rare. For example, opportune moments, designated as the attainment of a wholesome state, do not occur for beings in border regions, the formless realms, the realm of non-percipient beings, the animal realm, the realm of hungry ghosts, or hell; or even in the Middle Country, for those with defective faculties such as eyes, or even for those with complete faculties who hold wrong views. For indeed, people living in border regions delight and rejoice in the path of the ten unwholesome courses of action, such as killing living beings. They are also of fierce disposition and do not know the qualities of the Triple Gem. Formless beings, due to the absence of sound from another, cannot attain the path of stream-entry; nor do they obtain the sight of the Buddha and so on. As for non-percipient beings, animals, hungry ghosts, and hell-beings, how much more so is the existence of beings who have fulfilled the perfections non-conventional. Even if born into families with right view, those with defective eyes cannot see the gems of the Buddha and Sangha. Those with defective hearing are deprived of hearing the Dhamma. Being deaf and mute, they cannot undertake wholesome actions. Even those with complete faculties, if holding wrong views, do not consider the Omniscient Buddha—adorned with the thirty-two major marks and eighty minor marks, encompassed by a fathom’s breadth of light, a delight for the eyes of all beings—as worthy of being looked upon with eyes of serene faith.
